# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added
- **A `WARN` line when pushing metrics to an OpenTelemetry collector fails (b.zpx).**
  With `observability.metrics.enabled: true`, a failed push used to leave no line at the default log level. The service now logs `OTLP metrics export failed` at the first failure, then at most once a minute for the failures since the previous line, even once the collector has recovered, plus a last line at shutdown for failures not yet reported.
  Its fields are `failed_exports` (since the previous line), `failed_exports_total` and `last_export_error`, the collector's last error. No metric is added. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Collecting metrics.
- **A source and dependency inventory.**
  `oss/SOURCE-INVENTORY.md` records what the source tree, container image, API archive, conformance-test image and Helm charts each contain, how their files are licensed, their third-party code and dependencies, and the cryptography the service uses. `oss/RUST-DEPENDENCY-LICENSES.md` lists the license, source and scope of every Rust package in the `Cargo.lock` whose SHA-256 it records, as a snapshot that is not kept current.
- **Skipped backup copies of large values are now repaired automatically.**
  A large value's copy to the backup store can be skipped: the copy memory is full, the backup store refuses it, or a replica stops or crashes before sending it. Nothing sent such a copy again, so the value had no backup until a user uploaded it again. Now, every 5 minutes, one replica checks each large value in the backing store that has no confirmed backup copy, and uploads the ones the backup store lacks from the backing store. A skipped copy is normally repaired within about 3 to 8 minutes with `StorageApi` at its default timeouts, and 1 to 6 minutes with `LocalDisk`. There is no new setting, and repairs use the existing copy memory, one value at a time, so pod memory and its minimum are unchanged.
  - **One-time checks, and a daily one.** After the upgrade, the first repair checks every large value in the backing store against the backup store once, and uploads any whose copy an earlier release skipped: one existence request per value, at most 8 at a time. The same happens after the service restores from backups, and after `checkpoint.storageApiEndpoint`, `checkpoint.basePath` or `checkpoint.localDiskPath` changes; after a change of location every value the new location lacks is uploaded to it. Each confirmed copy is also re-checked about once a day, so expect about one extra existence request per large value per day. During a rolling upgrade, copies made by replicas of the earlier release are checked once, not uploaded again.
  - **New metrics.** `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_backup_repair_total{outcome}` (`already_stored`, `resent`, `resend_failed`, `probe_failed`, `value_gone`, `budget_busy`, `oversized`, `confirmed_copy_missing`) and `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_backup_mark_failed_total{op}` (`confirm`, `clear`), present at zero from the first scrape; `already_stored` includes the daily re-checks. The gauge `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_backup_owed{service}` counts the large values with no confirmed copy after the latest finished check: one replica publishes it and the others publish 0, so query it with `max()`; it appears after the first check. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values backed up?, which now also holds the `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_mirror_*` rows.
  - **A new alert, and a changed meaning.** `LiveEditLargeValueBackupsMissing` fires when large values have gone an hour without a backup copy. Each check spends at most 4 of its 5 minutes on existence checks and uploads, then still counts every value without a copy, so a slow backup store does not silence the alert. Only a check that cannot finish counting within its 5-minute limit reports nothing: watch for repeated `WARN` lines containing `backup repair pass did not complete its walk`. `LiveEditExternalValueMirrorDropping` now warns that copy capacity is short, no longer that values are left without a backup for good. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Backup alert rules.
  - **What it does not cover.** A skipped copy is still lost if the backing store loses its data before the repair reaches it. A value too large for the copy memory, possible only for one uploaded under a larger `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE`, is not repaired until that limit is restored or `resources.limits.memory` is raised so that the copy memory, `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_mirror_budget_bytes`, exceeds its size; it stays counted as missing. A value uploaded again more often than every few minutes, every one of whose copies is skipped, is repaired only once those uploads pause, and is not counted as missing meanwhile. A copy deleted from the backup store by something other than the service is found at the next daily check. A copy the service's own clean-up deletes just as it is confirmed is also found at the next daily check, and is not counted as missing until then. A repair can land just after the service's own backup clean-up deleted that value's copy, leaving an unused file in the backup store: storage, never data, is lost. Like any copy, a repaired one that no backup later names stays in the backup store.
  - **What else changes.** `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use{pool="maintenance"}` can stay above `0` for up to 5 minutes during a check. Each delivered copy is now recorded in the backing store, so a slow backing store slows the copies and can raise `reason="queue_full"` skips, each repaired later. The startup line `checkpoint ext-mirror workers spawned` is now `checkpoint ext-mirror workers and backup repair spawned` and gains `backup_store_id` and `repair_interval_secs`. A check that does anything logs one `INFO` line, `external-value backup repair pass complete`, with `work_budget_spent=true` when it ran out of time and left values for the next check. The skip and failure `WARN` lines now say the repair re-sends the value.
- **Chained writes: several `UpdateLayer` calls to one layer in flight, committed in order.**
  A client that waited for each reply before sending its next write could make one write per round trip to a layer. A write sent through the new `chained_header` arm names its predecessor, the `operation_id` of the same `client_id`'s previous write to that object, and the service commits it only directly after that write, and at most once, whichever replica serves it; re-sending a chained write unchanged is always safe. Unchained calls are unchanged, so clients that do not chain, the Kit client among them, are unaffected.
  - **Wire (additive):** `UpdateLayerRequest.chained_header = 3`, a new `ChainedUpdateLayerHeader { UpdateLayerHeader header = 1; string previous_operation_id = 2; bool no_wait = 3; }`; `PingResponse.operation_ids = 3` and `chained_writes = 4`, which describe only the replica that answered. A chained call needs a non-empty `operation_id` (so a non-empty `client_id`) and a `previous_operation_id` that is empty (a chain's first write) or a valid id other than its own. A replica without chained writes refuses a chained call `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, with no `ErrorInfo`, before reading any content, so chaining fails closed during a mixed-version rollout. Rolling back to a build without chained writes needs no data migration, and a chained call already in flight still commits only directly after its predecessor.
  - **Waiting and cancelling:** a chained call whose predecessor has not committed waits for it after its content is read, holding only its share of the upload budget. The service does not track the predecessor's call and never refuses a waiting call because that call failed: cancel the calls waiting behind a write that did not commit. A cancelled call that had not started its commit applies nothing; one that had can still commit at any later time, but only directly after its predecessor. A waiting call that is never cancelled is refused before its deadline, leaving a margin for the answer to reach the client: a tenth of the deadline the service received (the sooner of the client's `grpc-timeout` and `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS`, counted from the call's arrival), at least 50 ms. The wait's refusal is sent at least half that margin before that deadline, or, for a call whose content arrived with no more than its margin left (checked once, without waiting), at least half of what it had left when checked; the proto's `ChainedUpdateLayerHeader` comment gives these floors and their one exception. A call whose wait ended is checked again at its commit, which refuses it `ABORTED` `PREDECESSOR_NOT_COMMITTED` if another write under its `client_id` became the latest in between; the margin does not bound that refusal. A client whose round trip, delay before sending, and `grpc-timeout` round-up (the gRPC C-core library rounds it up by up to about 4%) exceed the floor can see its own deadline end the call first, leaving it unsettled. Set `no_wait` on the first re-send after a break: such a call never waits for its predecessor (when the service cannot read the client's latest write, it still keeps checking until the call's margin before its deadline and, if no check read it, refuses it `CHAIN_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE`).
  - **Refusals:** `ABORTED` with `ErrorInfo` `PREDECESSOR_NOT_COMMITTED` (metadata `last_record` = `operation`, `absent` or `no_operation_id`, and `last_operation_id`) — the only `ABORTED` the service answers, and only to chained calls; `ErrorInfo` `INVALID_UPDATE` on every `INVALID_ARGUMENT` a chained call receives, unless another reason applies (a delta too large, `DELTA_TOO_LARGE` or `CHILDREN_LIST_TOO_LONG`, see the entry under `### Changed`); and `UNAVAILABLE` with the new `CHAIN_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE` retry hint (0.5–1.5 s) for a call whose wait could not once read the client's latest write. The first two carry no `RetryInfo` and each means this call applied nothing; a chained call refused after the service retried it internally gets a plain `UNAVAILABLE`, outcome unknown. After an unchained write without an `operation_id`, that `client_id` can neither start nor continue a chain on the object (`last_record` = `no_operation_id`): to chain again, send one unchained write carrying an `operation_id` and chain from it, or use a new `client_id`.
  - **Falling back to unchained calls:** a chained call is settled once it returns `OK`, a refusal carrying an `ErrorInfo` of domain `nvidia.omniverse.live.v1alpha`, or `INVALID_ARGUMENT` without one, and also by `FAILED_PRECONDITION` or `NOT_FOUND` (no `ErrorInfo`), after which it can no longer commit on that object (resync the layer to its new object); send a write unchained only once every chained call for it and for every earlier write is settled, by finishing the chain chained. See `docs/api/docs/writing-layers.md` § Chained writes for the full client rules.
  - **Observability:** histograms `live_edit_update_layer_chain_wait_seconds{outcome}` and `live_edit_update_layer_chain_detect_lag_seconds`, present from their first observation; counter `live_edit_update_layer_chain_probes_total{result}` and gauges `live_edit_update_layer_chain_waiters` and `live_edit_update_layer_chain_waiting_bytes`, present at zero from the first scrape. Failure lines gain the stage `chain` and the fields `chained`, `previous_operation_id` and `no_wait`; a chained call's `ABORTED` (`PREDECESSOR_NOT_COMMITTED`) and `INVALID_ARGUMENT` (`INVALID_UPDATE`) log at `INFO`, and its `CHAIN_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE` at `WARN`, like every `UNAVAILABLE` a shutdown or a client-keepalive timeout did not cause. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics.
- **A bound on released large values awaiting clean-up: the release budget `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RELEASE_MAX_BYTES`, sized from the backing store's own `maxmemory` or the new `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES`; and a new gauge, `live_edit_store_memory_used_ratio`, for how full the store is.**
  A large value (an external value) an edit replaces, or a deleted or replaced layer held, stays in the backing store until the background clean-up collects it. Nothing bounded those released values: under a large-value workload (several updates a second of tens of MB) they could fill a `noeviction` store between clean-ups — measured at 10 MiB × 2 updates/s, about 1.2 GB waiting before one clean-up ran — and a full store refuses every write, for every session.
  - **The store's size, read from the store.** Unless `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RELEASE_MAX_BYTES` is set, the budget comes from the store's size. The service reads the memory limit (`maxmemory`) the store reports at startup and again about every minute, so a store resized in place moves the budget within about a minute with no redeploy, and a read that fails or reports nothing usable keeps the last good value. A limit the store reports wins over `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES`. A store that reports no `maxmemory` (Azure Managed Redis is one) takes its size from `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES`, or, with that unset, leaves the budget at the fallback below, with one startup `INFO` line saying so; one that reports a `maxmemory` of 0 (no limit configured) or a value that is not a number, or refuses or fails `INFO` to the service's user, leaves it at the fallback too, with a startup `WARN` naming the fault. No such answer is ever read as "unlimited", and each reason is logged once, not again every minute. On a first deploy to a store other than Azure Managed Redis, check that the startup budget line shows `source=info` with the value you expect, and fix what a fallback `WARN` names.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES`** (Helm `valkey.memoryBytes`, empty, so unset, by default): the backing store's **total memory as the provider states it**, in bytes, such as the SKU's size (`12000000000` for Azure Managed Redis `Balanced_B10`). Set it only on a store that does not report its own memory limit, which in practice means Azure Managed Redis; self-hosted Valkey, Amazon MemoryDB and ElastiCache report theirs, so leave it unset there. The service works out each shard's share itself and re-checks it about every minute, so a change in how the cache is split moves the budget without a redeploy; after resizing the cache, update the setting to the new total. A memory limit the store reports wins over this setting. Values below 100,000,000 (100 MB) and non-numeric values fail startup with an error naming the variable; to turn the budget off, set `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RELEASE_MAX_BYTES` to `0`. See `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Memory and `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Capacity and retention.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RELEASE_MAX_BYTES`** (Helm `valkey.extReleaseMaxBytes`, empty by default): the release budget, the level of released large values awaiting clean-up at which uploads carrying large values wait (below). Unset, the service works it out from the store's size: the memory limit the store reports, or, on a store that does not report one, `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES`; when neither is available, 8 × `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` capped at 100 MB (100,000,000 bytes at the default size cap). The capped fallback is safe on every Azure Managed Redis cache of 1 GB or more. On a larger store that does not report its size it costs throughput: released large values arriving faster than about 8 MB/s are collected at any age rather than from about 5 s, and uploads carrying large values wait once 100 MB are waiting during a clean-up stall, so for workloads of large values setting `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES` is recommended (not required). `0` turns it off (startup `WARN`). Startup logs the budget and its `source` (`override`, `info` or `fallback`) at `INFO`; on the fallback the line names the reason, at `INFO` when the store does not report its size and at `WARN` for a fault.
  - **What clients see.** While the released values awaiting clean-up are at or above the budget, a `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` carrying large values waits, up to what is left of its deadline (at most `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS`) less 5 s, and goes ahead as soon as the clean-up brings the total down. If it stays full, the upload fails the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` with `the backing store's external-value release budget (LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RELEASE_MAX_BYTES) is full: released external values awaiting garbage collection stayed at or above it for as long as the call's deadline allowed; nothing was applied, retry`: nothing was stored or applied by this call; a re-send is safe unless an earlier attempt of the same write is still in doubt — then use `operation_id`. This refusal carries the `EXT_RELEASE_BUDGET_FULL` retry hint (0.5–1.5 s; see the retry-hint entry below). A replica that starts shutting down refuses an upload waiting here as it refuses one waiting for admission: `UNAVAILABLE` `server shutting down`, with the `SERVER_SHUTTING_DOWN` hint. Uploads without large values, `DeleteLayer`, and the reads and restores that rebuild values from the backup store never wait; if the total cannot be read, the upload goes ahead. So releases from those (deleting or replacing layers, edits that drop large values without adding any) and from the background reference-count repair can still take the total past the budget. While uploads wait they keep their share of the upload memory budget, so other uploads on the replica can be refused at admission for that time.
  - **Metrics.** Gauges published by the replica holding maintenance leadership (zero from the others; query with `max()`), absent until its first clean-up pass: `live_edit_ext_release_pending_bytes`, `live_edit_ext_release_pending_count`, `live_edit_ext_release_oldest_age_seconds`, `live_edit_ext_release_settled_count` and `live_edit_ext_read_holds` (see the catch-up entry under `### Fixed`). The gauge `live_edit_ext_release_budget_bytes{service, source}`, recorded by every replica from startup: the budget its uploads wait at, under the `source` it came from (`override`: set explicitly or derived from `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES`; `info`: from the store's reported `maxmemory`; `fallback`), and 0 under the other two. Counters present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_ext_release_gate_total{outcome}` (`passed_after_wait`, `refused`, `read_failed`) and `live_edit_gc_decrement_total{outcome}` (`applied`, `replayed`, `stale`; see the entry under `### Fixed`). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly?, the alert rules in `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rules, and the sizing model in [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § Memory.
  - **How full the store is: the new gauge `live_edit_store_memory_used_ratio`**, normally 0 to 1 (above 1: past its limit). Above 0.7, plan to grow the store; above 0.85, grow it now. An alert on it is recommended, not required. Each pod reports its own reading, refreshed about once a minute. On Azure Managed Redis it needs `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_MEMORY_BYTES` set, reflects the cache as a whole, and appears about a minute after a pod starts. When the service cannot compute it, the series is absent, never 0: write alerts to tolerate a missing minute, for example over `max_over_time(live_edit_store_memory_used_ratio[5m])`. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Store memory and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?
  - **New backing-store requirement on a cluster:** keep the primaries' clocks within 15 s of each other for clean-up to progress, and none 105 s or more behind another for the at-most-once guarantee under `### Fixed` (`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements). Any NTP-synchronised fleet clears both.
- **When the backing store refuses a write for lack of memory, the service now deletes the released large values it holds at once, including when only one shard of a clustered store is full.**
  Before, a store filled by released large values (external values an edit replaced, or a deleted or replaced layer held) refused writes until an operator added memory. Now the first write the store refuses for lack of memory, on any replica, starts a clean-up within about a second. It deletes those values whatever their age, so such a store recovers within seconds. A store full of data still in use keeps refusing writes until you add memory.
  - **What clients see.** While the clean-up runs, an upload carrying large values waits, usually under a second and at most 15 s, inside the wait the release budget already uses. The clean-up never refuses an upload by itself: the upload then goes ahead or ends as before. Uploads without large values, reads and `DeleteLayer` never wait. There is no new status code, retry hint or message.
  - **New metrics.** `live_edit_gc_oom_collections_total{outcome}` counts the clean-ups, with `outcome` = `freed`, `freed_nothing`, `nothing_to_collect`, `failed` or `refused`. `live_edit_gc_oom_freed_bytes_total` counts the bytes they freed. Both are present at zero from the first scrape.
  - **New log lines.** Each clean-up logs one `WARN` with its outcome, at most one every 30 s; a clean-up that could not hold uploads back says so on that line (`held_uploads=false`, `hold_error`). The first `refused` clean-up on a replica logs one `WARN` naming the likely cause. Only one replica runs clean-ups at a time, so search every replica's logs. After `freed` or `failed` the next clean-up can start a second later; after any other outcome, 30 s later.
  - **What to do.** Alert on any increase of `outcome="refused"` (the new `LiveEditStoreCleanupRefused` rule): the store does not let the service free memory while it is full, so add memory. If writes stay refused while clean-ups report `nothing_to_collect`, or `freed_nothing` with `oom_skipped=0` on their `WARN`, the store is full of data in use: add memory. Keep paging on `live_edit_store_write_refused_total{reason="out_of_memory"}`. It can now stop by itself within seconds, but the store is still too small. The metric rows are in `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? and the runbook in `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Backing store full.
  - **The store can go slightly past its memory limit.** While the store is full, the service's clean-up writes a little past `maxmemory`: about 1.5 MB at typical release rates, more during a large backlog, for about two minutes. If `maxmemory` sits right at the container or host memory limit, that can get the store's server killed for running out of memory, so keep `maxmemory` a few MB or more below it (`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Memory).
  - **A release made while the store is full may not be recorded.** The periodic reference-count repair reclaims its memory an hour or two later at the defaults; `live_edit_ext_ref_release_enqueue_failed_total` counts such releases.
- **Retry hints on refusals: a machine-readable reason and retry delay on every `UNAVAILABLE` that refuses a call for a reason worth retrying later and provably applied nothing.**
  A client could not tell a planned shutdown from a dropped connection, or a capacity refusal from an outage, except by matching message text, and nothing told it when to retry. Each such refusal now carries, in `grpc-status-details-bin`, a `google.rpc.Status` with a `google.rpc.ErrorInfo` (domain `nvidia.omniverse.live.v1alpha`, a `reason`, `metadata`) and a `google.rpc.RetryInfo` (`retry_delay`). The code and the message are unchanged, so clients that ignore status details behave as before.
  - **Reasons:** `SERVER_SHUTTING_DOWN` (every shutdown ending and refusal; `retry_delay` under 100 ms), `SUBSCRIPTION_CAPACITY` (metadata `cause` = `saturated` / `waiter_cap`), `READER_CAPACITY`, `CATCHUP_CAPACITY` (`wait` = `slot` / `read_permit`), `UPLOAD_CAPACITY` (`cause` = `saturated` / `waiter_cap` / `growth`), `SNAPSHOT_FENCE_TIMEOUT`, `EXT_RELEASE_BUDGET_FULL` (an upload carrying large values refused at the external-value release budget, above) and `CHAIN_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE` (a chained `UpdateLayer` whose wait could not read the client's latest write, see the chained-writes entry above) (0.5–1.5 s each), `STORE_WRITE_REFUSED` (`condition` = `out_of_memory` / `misconf` / `readonly` / `noreplicas`; 2–6 s), `UPSTREAM_UNAVAILABLE` (a `CreateLayer` / `UpdateLayer` refused before its commit because a dependency was unavailable, see the backing-store entry under `### Changed`; 1–3 s), and `CLIENT_KEEPALIVE_TIMEOUT` (a `JoinSession` ended for client silence, see the `JoinSession` keepalive entry below; under 100 ms).
  - **How to use it, on an `UNAVAILABLE` carrying the hint:** `retry_delay` is the minimum wait before the next attempt; across consecutive refusals of the same call keep your own backoff and wait the larger of `retry_delay` and that backoff, with no extra jitter on top (a shutdown ending's first reconnect stays prompt). Treat an unknown reason in this domain as "retry after that wait". On `CreateLayer` / `UpdateLayer` it means this call applied nothing; a re-send is safe unless an earlier attempt of the same write is still in doubt — then use `operation_id`; recognise it by the `ErrorInfo` domain and reason, never by the message text. It is therefore never on a refusal of the `CreateLayer` commit, nor on an `UpdateLayer` refused after the server retried it internally (an earlier attempt whose reply was lost may still land): those answer plain `UNAVAILABLE`, outcome unknown, the latter as `UpdateLayer outcome unknown: …`. On other calls it means only "retry after that wait". An `UNAVAILABLE` without it keeps its usual meaning, and the hint on any other status code means only what that code's refusal states. See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Retry hints.
- **`JoinSession` keepalives: a participant whose client vanishes without closing its stream now leaves the session about 45 s after its last data, for clients that send them.**
  A client that vanished behind a proxy — a laptop that slept, a network that went silent — stayed in its session, seen by the other participants, until the proxy ended its stream or a connection failed, which behind the chart's ingress could take hours; when it came back it joined under a new connection id, so the same user showed twice. The HTTP/2 and TCP keepalives on either side reach only the nearest proxy. A message on the stream itself is the one signal that crosses every hop, so the protocol now carries one. All additive: a client that does not opt in sees no change.
  - **Wire.** `JoinSessionHeader.client_keepalive` (field 3) opts a join in; `SessionConnected.keepalive_interval_ms` (field 2) answers with the join's interval, non-zero only for a join that opted in while the keepalive is on; `JoinSessionRequest` gains a `keepalive` arm (field 3, `JoinSessionKeepalive`, an empty message), never delivered to other participants.
  - **What a client must do.** Send keepalives only if *this* join's `SessionConnected` carried a non-zero `keepalive_interval_ms`, deciding on every join: during a rolling upgrade another replica may not implement keepalives and may end the stream `INVALID_ARGUMENT` on one. From `SessionConnected`, send a `JoinSessionKeepalive` whenever nothing has been sent for the interval (any bytes of the stream count, a message still being sent included), from the transport or network thread, never behind application work. See `docs/api/docs/streams.md` § Keepalives.
  - **What the service does.** Once a join that opted in has sent no data for three intervals (45 s at the default), counted from the later of its last data and the end of its initial participant list, it is ended a moment later: the participant leaves first, so the others receive one `PeerLeft`, then the stream ends with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` (`no data from the client for three keepalive intervals; rejoin the session`) and the `CLIENT_KEEPALIVE_TIMEOUT` retry hint. Each ending logs one `INFO` `RPC failed` line and counts on the new `live_edit_presence_keepalive_timeouts_total{service}`, present at zero from the first scrape.
  - **Setting: `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_KEEPALIVE_INTERVAL_MS`** (Helm `presence.keepaliveIntervalMs`, default 15000). `0` turns the keepalive off for every join, the rollback without a new release. Any other value must be at least `1000`: the setting is in milliseconds, and a smaller one, such as `15` meant as seconds, would end every opted-in join almost at once, so it fails startup with an error naming the variable and its unit, as does a non-numeric value. The chart refuses an empty or `null` value, anything but a whole number (such as `15s`), and `1`–`999`. Behind a proxy an upload waiting for admission holds back keepalives on its connection, so keep `LIVE_EDIT_UPLOAD_ADMISSION_WAIT_MS` (2 s by default) well under two intervals.
  - **Not changed by this.** A client that sends no keepalives is still removed only when the proxy ends its stream or a connection fails, which behind a proxy can take hours, and a vanished `SubscribeReadLayer` subscription, which has no client-to-server channel for a keepalive, still holds its place until the ingress ends its stream (at most 240 minutes after it opened behind the chart's ingress). Where your ingress supports a downstream HTTP/2 keepalive, enabling it bounds both; the chart's Contour HTTPProxy has no setting for it. See `docs/api/docs/streams.md` § Keepalives.
- **Two shutdown settings: `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_STREAM_END_RATE`** (Helm `shutdown.streamEndRate`, default 25 streams per second) **and `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_STREAM_END_MAX_WINDOW_SECS`** (Helm `shutdown.streamEndMaxWindowSecs`, default 20). They pace how a shutting-down replica ends its live streams (see `### Changed`); `0` in either ends every stream at once, as before. A malformed or out-of-range value fails startup, naming the variable; the chart refuses an empty or `null` value. Startup logs one `INFO` line with the shortest termination grace the shutdown settings need (`min_termination_grace_secs`, 76.25 s at the defaults).
- **A replica autoscaler that sees queued joins and subscription occupancy: the chart's new default, `autoscaling.mode: scaler`.**
  A burst of joins costs a replica little CPU while it waits, and a replica at its subscription ceiling refuses subscribes while its CPU and memory sit idle, so the CPU-utilization HorizontalPodAutoscaler kept the fleet at its minimum while joins waited for seconds. The chart now runs a small scaler instead: one pod of the service image, run as `live-edit scaler`, that every 5 s reads each server pod's load and sets the server Deployment's replica count, within `autoscaling.minReplicas` and `autoscaling.maxReplicas`, to the largest of three needs:
  - **CPU**, by the HorizontalPodAutoscaler's rule against `autoscaling.targetCPUUtilizationPercentage` (80) of `resources.requests.cpu`, measured from the service process's own CPU time, so it needs no metrics server, metrics adapter or sidecar requests;
  - **queued joins**, enough replicas to drain the subscribes waiting for their first catch-up within `autoscaling.scaler.catchupTargetSeconds` (10 s), at the drain rate it measures on each replica. It learns that rate only while a replica has at least as many first catch-ups pending as its concurrent-catch-up limit (8 by default), so ordinary traffic does not change it: it is 7 per second per replica until a replica has been that busy, and that replica then keeps the rate it learned until it restarts or leaves;
  - **subscription occupancy**, enough replicas to keep subscriptions at or below `autoscaling.scaler.occupancyHeadroomPercent` (80 %) of each replica's ceiling, so a replica is added before replicas refuse subscribes.

  It scales up at once. It scales down one pod at a time, only after a 600 s window of lower demand (`autoscaling.scaler.scaleDown.windowSeconds`) and 60 s after its last removal (`…intervalSeconds`), only while every server pod is Ready, not terminating and reporting and the Deployment has exactly its replica count of pods (so never during a rollout), and only while no catch-up queue has built up: while more subscribes are waiting for their first catch-up across the fleet than one replica drains within the catch-up target (70 at the default target while no replica has learned a drain rate), scale-down is held. It does not size the removed pod's own reconnects: every scale-down leaves at least `autoscaling.minReplicas` pods, so `minReplicas` sets the burst of losing one pod that you accept. A restarted scaler waits a full window before its first scale-down. When the scaler is not running, the replica count stays where it is.
  - **What it does not fix.** New replicas serve the subscribes that arrive after them. They do not drain catch-ups already queued on a replica, and established streams never move to them; the scaler never ends streams to rebalance. Shortening the wait inside a join burst is a matter of how fast each replica catches its joiners up and of spreading reconnects out over time, not of adding replicas.
  - **Optional: `autoscaling.scaler.podDeletionCost`** (default `false`). `true` marks the emptiest server pod for removal just before each scale-down, so the fewest clients reconnect, and adds `patch` on pods to the scaler's Role. It changes only how many streams a scale-down ends, not how often scale-down happens.
  - **New Helm values:** `autoscaling.mode` (`scaler` | `hpa`) and `autoscaling.scaler.{pollIntervalSeconds, catchupTargetSeconds, occupancyHeadroomPercent, scaleDown.windowSeconds, scaleDown.intervalSeconds, podDeletionCost, serviceAccount.create, serviceAccount.name, rbac.create, resources, podAnnotations, affinity}`. The scaler pod inherits the server's `nodeSelector` and `tolerations` but not its `affinity` (a required anti-affinity against server pods could leave it unschedulable and freeze the replica count); `autoscaling.scaler.affinity` (default `{}`) sets its own. New scaler environment variables `LIVE_EDIT_SCALER_*`, set by the chart (`docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Autoscaling).
  - **Metrics.** On each server pod, a new gauge present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_subscribe_catchups_pending`, the subscriptions that have asked for a layer and do not have it yet. On the scaler pod's own `/metrics` (port 9090, no Service: scrape the pod), counters present at zero from its first scrape: `live_edit_scaler_decisions_total{direction, reason}`, `live_edit_scaler_scale_down_blocked_total{reason}` (`startup`, `window`, `unsettled`, `interval`, `feasibility`) and `live_edit_scaler_poll_errors_total{stage}` (`endpoints`, `scale`, `snapshot`, `deletion_cost`, `patch_scale`); gauges present after its first poll: `live_edit_scaler_desired_replicas{term}`, `live_edit_scaler_target_replicas`, `live_edit_scaler_pods{state}` and `live_edit_scaler_catchup_rate{scope}`, the catch-up drain rate in use (`scope="fleet"`, absent while no pod reports; `scope="pod"` with `pod`, each pod's learned value). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the autoscaler working? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rules.
  - **`/scaling/v1` on each server pod's metrics port**: the scaler's internal load snapshot, not an API to build on. Like `/metrics` it needs no authentication and carries only aggregate counts (subscriptions, queued catch-ups, CPU time, the replica's limits), no session, user or layer identifiers.
  - **A warning** when `autoscaling.maxReplicas` × `valkey.catchupSnapshotReadConcurrency` is above 10: every replica runs that many whole-layer reads against the backing store at once, and the defaults (10 and 1) are at the limit the store's read sizing assumes.
  - See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Autoscaling and the `### Changed` entry for the operator action.
- **`live_edit_snapshot_read_duration_seconds` gains buckets at 0.075, 0.15 and 0.2 seconds**, so a whole-layer read under a join burst, around 100–200 ms, is resolved rather than estimated across the 100–250 ms band. Every existing boundary is kept, so dashboards and `histogram_quantile` queries keep working; each series has three more buckets.
- **A per-layer byte limit on the backing store's update history: `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAX_BYTES`** (Helm `valkey.layerStreamMaxBytes`, default `134217728`, 128 MiB).
  Each layer's update stream keeps every committed update whole, large values included, and was limited only to `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN` entries (4096), so a layer of large updates could hold 4096 of them: at 10 MiB per update, about 40 GiB for one layer, filling the backing store within minutes and leaving every later write refused. Every `UpdateLayer` now trims its layer's stream once the newest entries hold more than the limit in bytes, always keeping the 2 newest, so one layer's stream holds at most about the limit plus twice `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` (about 328 MiB at the defaults). The stream keeps the smaller of the two windows; at the default a layer keeps all 4096 entries while its updates average 32 KiB or less, so layers of ordinary edits are unaffected.
  - **What a smaller window costs.** A subscriber that falls further behind than the window reconnects and catches up in full (`stream_gap`), and an incremental backup can cover only what the window still holds (`live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="refused_trimmed"}`). At the defaults one incremental backup at its own 64 MiB limit always fits.
  - **Settings.** `0` turns the byte limit off — entry-count retention only, the rollback without a redeploy. Non-numeric values and values above `9007199254740991` (2^53 − 1) fail startup. The chart refuses an empty or `null` value, and anything but a whole number of bytes (such as `128MiB`), at render time rather than turning the limit off. A new startup warning fires when `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_BYTES` is above a non-zero limit (a backup cannot carry more than the stream retains); the existing warning about a layer's worst-case stream size now counts the limit, so it is silent at the defaults.
  - **Upgrade.** No migration: a layer starts counting at its next update. During a rolling upgrade, entries written by replicas that predate the limit stay bounded by the entry count until a later trim removes them.
  - **Sizing.** [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § Memory now sizes the stream from your update sizes: per hot layer about `min(4096 × mean update size, 128 MiB) × 1.2`, plus the layer's large values, which are also held in the external-value store, and replaced values awaiting collection. A new histogram, present from its first observation, gives the update sizes: `live_edit_update_layer_relay_bytes`, one sample per commit the service saw land — its count equals that of `live_edit_update_layer_attempts_histogram`.
- **`live_edit_store_write_refused_total{stage, reason}`: uploads the backing store refused, with an alert recipe** (see the `UNAVAILABLE` entry under `### Changed`). `stage` ∈ `ext_store`, `update_commit`, `create_commit`; `reason` ∈ `out_of_memory`, `misconf`, `readonly`, `noreplicas`; all twelve cells present at zero from the first scrape. Page on any `out_of_memory` increase; alert on a sustained rate of the other three; and alert on the store's own memory (`redis_memory_used_bytes / redis_memory_max_bytes`, Azure Managed Redis **Used Memory Percentage**, MemoryDB `DatabaseMemoryUsagePercentage`) above 70 % for 15 minutes, paging above 85 %. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Recommended alerts.
- **A per-replica memory budget for uploads, with two new settings.**
  Every `CreateLayer` and `UpdateLayer` now reserves the memory it will use from one budget per replica, after it is authorized and before its content is read, and holds the reservation until the call ends. **No upload under `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` is refused for its byte size:** the budget bounds how many uploads run at once, and an upload whose processing does not fit the room left runs as the replica's one upload beyond the budget, one at a time. So a replica's uploads use at most the budget plus that one upload, including the memory a structural edit holds for the layer it edits. An edit that creates, deletes, moves, reorders or changes sections, or replaces the layer's whole content, holds memory that grows with the layer rather than the edit — the layer's cached state, which it is planned on, its catch-up of the edits made since the layer was cached, reserved before each read of them, before replaying what it read and before each edit it replays, what that plan adds to it and the rows it rewrites, or, when it reads the whole layer, that read, the rows it rewrites and the backing-store commands its commit queues — and it reserves that memory from the same budget before it uses it. When it needs more than the budget has free, it gives back what it holds between commit attempts, waits for the room within its deadline and carries on without using a retry; so many structural edits on large layers wait for room, or run one at a time beyond the budget, instead of running the replica out of memory. A small residual is still not reserved in advance: an edit whose names are much longer than the layer's others. An upload's memory grows with how many edits it carries more than with its byte size, and is measured per commit path; charges for these edits are sized for the most memory-hungry setting, including the `txn` rollback value of `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND`, so with the default setting they reserve more than needed.
  - **The budget** follows the pod's memory limit, 384 MiB at the chart's `5Gi`, and has no setting (see the pod-memory entry under `### Changed`). It never falls below about 8 MiB plus twice `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` (about 208 MiB at the default), so a larger size cap raises the budget when it needs to, rather than failing startup. A larger cap does raise the pod's minimum memory limit, below which the pod refuses to start (see the pod-memory entry under `### Changed`). It also limits each session to `budget ÷ 32 MiB` uploads still sending their content at once (12 at `5Gi`).
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_UPLOAD_ADMISSION_WAIT_MS`** (Helm `layer.uploadAdmissionWaitMs`, `null` by default): how long an upload waits for admission before it is refused. Unset, it is `min(2000, LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS / 2)` — 2000 at the default deadline — and follows the deadline if you change it. A value you set must be below `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS`, or startup fails with an error naming both. `0` means never wait.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_UPLOAD_ADMISSION_MAX_WAITERS`** (Helm `layer.uploadAdmissionMaxWaiters`, default 64): uploads that may wait at once; one more is refused immediately. Zero is rejected at startup.
  - **What clients see.** An upload refused because the replica's upload capacity is full — the budget at admission, the admission queue, the session's limit, or content arriving faster than the budget can hold it — fails `UNAVAILABLE` with a message saying nothing was applied and the `UPLOAD_CAPACITY` retry hint: this call applied nothing; a re-send is safe unless an earlier attempt of the same write is still in doubt — then use `operation_id` (`docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Retry hints); clients that retry `UNAVAILABLE` (the Kit client does) need no change. Once its content is in, an upload is never refused for capacity: it may wait, within the call's deadline, for room or for its turn beyond the budget. **If the deadline passes during that wait, the call fails `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` with nothing applied**; since a `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` does not say how far the call got, re-send it with the same `operation_id` (see the `operation_id` entry below). A shutdown during that wait fails `UNAVAILABLE` (`server shutting down`), nothing applied. The C++ client library's updates and whole scenes observed so far fit well inside the default budget. At the defaults a 100 MiB upload whose content is one external value does not: it needs about 409 MiB at the measured memory costs, so it is accepted and runs beyond the budget, one at a time, never refused for its size. The one startup `INFO` line `Upload admission budget configured` gives the largest blob that fits within the budget when sent as the one external value of a single empty-named field (`largest_in_budget_blob_upload_bytes`, about 94 MiB at the defaults); it is the blob's size, not the upload's, which adds its delta and framing, and an upload with a larger delta may not fit. Larger uploads, up to `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE`, are accepted and run one at a time beyond it, with no warning. An upload that runs beyond the budget logs one `INFO` line, `upload runs beyond the upload budget`. An `UpdateLayer` whose delta fails the per-delta checks (too many edits, a name too long or carrying control characters, a non-finite sample time, a section parented to itself) now logs its failure at stage `parse` (was `storage`); the status is unchanged, `INVALID_ARGUMENT`.
  - **The budget is counted in the pod's memory limit**, beside its waiters and the one upload running beyond it, together with every other budget the limit funds (see the pod-memory entry under `### Changed`). The one upload running beyond the budget is extra, and is bounded only by its own content: one packed with very many small edits, near the per-upload limit of 99,999 struct commands, set fields and time samples in total, can need close to 1 GiB.
  - **Metrics**, present from the first scrape: `live_edit_upload_admission_total{rpc, result}` (`create_layer`, `update_layer`; `admitted`, `refused_saturated`, `refused_waiter_cap`, `refused_growth`, `cancelled`), `live_edit_upload_reserved_bytes`, `live_edit_upload_budget_bytes`, `live_edit_uploads_in_flight{phase}` (`receiving`, `processing`), `live_edit_upload_admission_waiters`, `live_edit_upload_overflow_bytes`, `live_edit_upload_overflow_waiters` and `live_edit_upload_overflow_runs_total{rpc}`. Histograms, present from their first observation: `live_edit_upload_admission_wait_seconds{rpc}`, `live_edit_upload_processing_wait_seconds{rpc}` (which also records each structural edit's wait between commit attempts for room) and `live_edit_upload_object_state_bytes{path}`, the memory each structural `UpdateLayer` reserved for the layer it edits. A counter present at zero from the first scrape, `live_edit_update_layer_recharges_total{path}`, counts the structural commit attempts that waited for that room. A replica that starts shutting down refuses an edit waiting there `UNAVAILABLE` (`server shutting down`) with the `SERVER_SHUTTING_DOWN` hint when the wait followed its first attempt, nothing applied; after an internal retry it answers the outcome-unknown `UpdateLayer outcome unknown: …` instead. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it saturated? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rules.
- **Safe re-sends of `UpdateLayer` and `CreateLayer`: an optional client `operation_id`.**
  A client that lost the reply to an upload — on `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, on `UNAVAILABLE` after the service's own retries ran out, or on a dropped connection — could not tell whether it had committed, and re-sending it applied the edit a second time or replaced the layer it had just created. A re-send that carries the same `operation_id` as a call that already committed is now answered with that commit and applies nothing.
  - **Wire (additive; clients and servers that predate it ignore it):** `string operation_id` on `CreateLayerHeader` (field 3) and `UpdateLayerHeader` (field 6); `OperationOutcome operation_outcome` on `CreateLayerResponse` (field 2) and `UpdateLayerResponse` (field 3); new enum `OperationOutcome { OPERATION_OUTCOME_UNSPECIFIED = 0; OPERATION_OUTCOME_APPLIED = 1; OPERATION_OUTCOME_ALREADY_COMMITTED = 2; }`.
  - **`operation_id`:** empty means absent, and the call behaves exactly as before. Otherwise 1–64 bytes of `A–Z a–z 0–9 - . _ ~`, compared byte for byte; anything else is `INVALID_ARGUMENT` before any chunk is read. On `UpdateLayer` it requires a non-empty `client_id` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT` `operation_id requires a non-empty client_id` otherwise; calls without an id still accept an empty one). Recommended: a random UUIDv4 per write intent.
  - **Ordering guarantee on `UpdateLayer`:** no part of a call carrying an `operation_id` applies after the same `client_id`'s next `UpdateLayer` to the same object has committed, as long as that commit came after the server read the call's starting state (once, as soon as it has received the whole call). A call still in flight when another commit under its `client_id` lands on the object — or when the object is restored from backup — fails `UNAVAILABLE` (`this attempt applied nothing, but an earlier attempt of this call may have committed and the server can no longer tell: …`): treat its outcome as unknown, not as safe to re-send: a re-send with the same `operation_id` is a new call that finds no matching record and applies, so if an earlier attempt had landed with its response lost, the update lands twice. The code is the retryable one the service's other transient failures use, so recognise this answer by that message prefix. A caller still connected normally sees it only after such a restore, or when writers share a `client_id`, which is forbidden (below). The exception is the client's own earlier call to the same object: one it gave up on that is still running on the server can commit while the next call is in flight, and the next call gets this answer.
  - **Answers:** a re-send whose original committed gets `OK` with the original `sequence_num` and server timestamp (`UpdateLayer`) or the original `object_id` (`CreateLayer`, for 24 hours, even if the layer has since been replaced or deleted), and `OPERATION_OUTCOME_ALREADY_COMMITTED`. A re-send whose original never committed is applied normally (`APPLIED`). The same id with a different request is refused `ALREADY_EXISTS` (`operation_id already committed with a different request`). A call without an id, or served by a replica that predates the field, answers `OPERATION_OUTCOME_UNSPECIFIED`.
  - **Client obligations:** one `client_id` per writer per object, never shared between concurrent writers to one object (the server cannot detect sharing; writers that share one overwrite each other's record, so a call can fail `UNAVAILABLE` as above because another writer committed, and a re-send may then land the update twice); one id per write intent, never reused for another; a re-send keeps the id, the chunk bytes, `session_id`, `layer_name`, `object_id` and (on `UpdateLayer`) `client_id`, and goes out before that `client_id`'s next `UpdateLayer` to the same object; treat `ALREADY_COMMITTED` as success and accept its sequence even at or below sequences already seen (its server timestamp is the original commit's, not a jitter sample); read `UNSPECIFIED` in reply to a request that carried an id as "this replica ignored the id".
  - **Limits:** the record does not survive a backing-store loss followed by a restore from backup; writers sharing a `client_id` and writing to one object concurrently (forbidden, above) have their in-flight calls refused `UNAVAILABLE`, but a re-send that follows another writer's commit can still be applied again; a call the client abandoned without the server noticing, whose starting-state read ran only after the client's next write, can still apply once. `CreateSession` takes no operation id.
  - **Observability:** new counters `live_edit_operation_id_match_total{rpc, outcome}` (`rpc` ∈ `update_layer`, `create_layer`; `outcome` ∈ `already_committed`, `conflict`) and `live_edit_update_layer_operation_overtaken_total{backend}` (`lua`, `txn`; one tick per call answered `UNAVAILABLE` as above, with one `INFO` line), all cells present at zero from the first scrape; one `INFO` line per answered re-send (`UpdateLayer operation already committed by an earlier call; reporting it`, `CreateLayer operation already committed by an earlier call; reporting it`); failure lines gain an `operation_id` field. See `docs/api/docs/writing-layers.md` § Safe re-sends with `operation_id` and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **`live_edit_valkey_router_restarts_total{pool}`: a backing-store client whose internal connection task ended unexpectedly is now restarted, and counted.**
  Until now such a client kept reporting itself connected while every command sent through it timed out, until the process restarted. The service now watches that task on every pooled client (`subscriber`, `data`, `txn`, `subscriber_pool`, `maintenance`, `snapshot`) and restarts it: immediately the first time, then — for a member that ends again within 60 s — after 100 ms doubling to 5 s. Each restart ticks the counter (all six cells present at zero from the first scrape; baseline zero) and logs one `ERROR` line, `Valkey client router task ended; restarting it`, naming the pool member and how the task ended. No configuration. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **Stream-reader connection metrics: `live_edit_stream_mux_reader_connects_total{cause, result}` and `live_edit_stream_mux_readers_connected`.** Present at zero from the first scrape. The counter counts the connections stream readers open — `cause="bind"` when a reader first serves a subscription, `cause="retarget"` when a clustered reader moves its connection to another primary — by `result` (`ok`, `failed`); `failed` is expected zero. The gauge is how many readers hold a connection (zero after startup, at most `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE`). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up? and the `### Changed` entry on backing-store connections.
- **Helm value `autoscaling.behavior`**, rendered verbatim into the HorizontalPodAutoscaler's `spec.behavior` in `autoscaling.mode: hpa`; the default damps scale-down (see `### Changed`), and `null` omits it. The default scaler mode has its own damping, `autoscaling.scaler.scaleDown.*`.
- **Per-layer reference-record metrics.** Present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_ext_index_recount_total{site, outcome}` (`site` `reconcile` | `torn_commit`; `outcome` `repaired`, `clean`, `too_large`, `not_live`, `failed`, `withheld`) and `live_edit_ext_index_drift_total{site, direction}` (`site` adds `structural_settle`, structural edits committed through the structural write-planning cache; `direction` `under` | `over`), and a tenth `live_edit_ext_reconcile_total` outcome, `skipped_index_drift`, for excess the reconciler held back because a layer's record could not be corrected that pass. `direction="under"` recurring after the first pass following an upgrade means something still produces wrong records and is worth reporting. Any `outcome="withheld"` means a layer has a field whose stored large-value name is malformed (keyspace corruption): the check then corrects only upward and holds the layer's values from reclamation on every pass until the field is fixed, so find and fix it; the warning line names the layer. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly? and the entry under `### Fixed`.
- **Stream-multiplexer metrics.** Present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_stream_mux_subscriptions`, `live_edit_stream_mux_streams`, `live_edit_stream_mux_xreads_total`, `live_edit_stream_mux_wakes_total{outcome}`, `live_edit_stream_mux_keepalives_total{result}`, `live_edit_stream_mux_bind_refusals_total`, `live_edit_stream_mux_poison_keys_total`, `live_edit_stream_mux_overflow_kills_total{bound}` and `live_edit_stream_mux_head_checks_total{result}`. A histogram, so present only from its first observation: `live_edit_stream_mux_xread_reply_bytes`.
- **Capacity metrics.** Present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_subscriber_admitted`, `live_edit_subscribe_catchups_inflight` and `live_edit_subscribe_catchups_waiting`. A histogram, so present only from its first observation: `live_edit_subscribe_catchup_wait_seconds{result}` (`acquired`, `cancelled`, `timed_out`). Both capacity-wait histograms (this one and `live_edit_subscriber_admission_wait_seconds`) gain `20` and `30` second buckets, so a wait that ran out the default budget lands in the (20, 30] band; with a budget above 30 s those waits land in `+Inf`. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it saturated? and § Capacity diagnostics.
- **Helm value `valkey.subscriberMaxConcurrentCatchups`** (default 8), rendered as `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_MAX_CONCURRENT_CATCHUPS`; `valkey.subscriberAdmissionWaitMs` now defaults to 20000. (The subscription ceiling follows the pod's memory limit and has no setting; see the pod-memory entry under `### Changed`.)
- **A snapshot connection lane and the atomic whole-layer read limits.**
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_POOL_SIZE`** (Helm `valkey.snapshotPoolSize`, unset by default; the binary's default is `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_READ_CONCURRENCY` + `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY` + 4, 6 at the defaults): a dedicated set of Valkey connections for reading a layer's whole state, so those large replies never queue in front of edits. **Action for operators: each replica now opens 6 more Valkey connections**, and on a clustered store each connects to every primary; recheck your store's connection limit against [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § Connections. Every lane connection is checked at startup, so a store that cannot accept them now fails startup. The lane's startup connect phase takes the place of the stream readers', which no longer connect at startup, and the new startup check of the backing store's script support (below) adds up to one `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` (5 s) to a degraded boot. The net changes since 0.1.1, connection count and startup budget (`startupProbe.failureThreshold` 56 → 57), are in the backing-store connections entry under `### Changed`.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_ITEMS`** (Helm `valkey.snapshotAtomicReadMaxItems`, default 37554) **and `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_BYTES`** (Helm `valkey.snapshotAtomicReadMaxBytes`, default 604619): the largest layer read in one atomic server-side read. The item limit is derived from measured read cost on Azure Managed Redis, with no per-platform default; the byte limit bounds one read's reply size and memory, not its time, and its default is the item limit at 16.1 bytes per item, what real layers store, since clients give sections random 64-bit ids; raise them only with your own measurement of a faster store, and lower both if you expect sustained join storms across every replica ([`docs/service/docs/scaling.md`](docs/service/docs/scaling.md) § Keep fleet-wide layer reads at 10 or fewer).
  - All three reject `0` and non-numeric values at startup. External-value clean-up, and structural edits on the transactional path (`LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua-watch-structural` or `txn`, a layer the structural-edit cache cannot hold, or an edit too large for one commit call), read a layer's state on the lane, in windows of commands sized from the limits (at most 2,141 commands outstanding at the default, each window's replies aimed at about the byte limit); they never use the atomic read, at any layer size. A structural edit's read sends each window of the layer's records as one short read-only script call, except three kinds of window it sends as ordinary commands: its first round, which lists the layer's records; a single list of a prim's children too long for one short call; and every window after the store refused a script call because the layer's slot was moving between nodes or not served, or the cluster was down, for the rest of that read. Each script call holds the shard while it runs, and is sized to hold it no longer than one window of a fenced read may. Under the default `lua`, structural edits read a layer whole only to load it into the structural-edit cache, on the lane, through the atomic read or, for a layer over the limits, a fenced read (see the structural-edit entry under Changed). Catch-ups and backups of a layer within both limits use the atomic read on the lane (see the catch-up and backups entries under Changed); catch-ups and backups of a layer over either limit read on the lane too, through their fenced reads, whose fence requests and renewals go on the data pool. The startup check below runs on the lane. The `pool` label on `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total`, `live_edit_valkey_errors_total` and `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use` gains the value `snapshot`, present at zero from the first scrape.
- **`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_READ_CONCURRENCY`** (Helm `valkey.catchupSnapshotReadConcurrency`, default 1): how many atomic reads one replica's catch-ups run at once (see the catch-up entry under Changed); a caught-up subscription's live updates take none, and only its re-bootstrap after an overwrite, a catch-up, takes one. The default is the value the atomic-read limits were derived at; raising it raises the edit delay a join wave can cause on a shard, so raise it only with your own measurement of your store. `0` and non-numeric values are rejected at startup. New metrics: `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_reads_in_flight{class}` (`atomic`, and `fenced` for the fenced reads of layers over the limits), present at zero from the first scrape; and the histogram `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_read_wait_seconds{consumer, class}`, present from its first observation, with the capacity-wait buckets (including `20` and `30`); its `class` is the permit's (`atomic` or `fenced`), so filter on `class="atomic"` for the atomic reads' waits. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics.
- **New backing-store requirement, checked at startup: a Lua script must be allowed to read keys it does not declare, in its declared key's slot.**
  The service's read of a layer's whole state is one script that declares only the layer's header key and reads the layer's other keys, all in the same slot, without declaring them. Before it reports ready, each replica runs that script once against a layer id that is never written. **If the store refuses the undeclared read, the replica logs an error naming the requirement ([`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § Startup check) and exits**; if it refuses the script itself (an access-control denial, say), the replica exits with an error naming the script and the store's error. If the store cannot answer at that moment (still loading after a restart, busy, read-only, the cluster down or the slot migrating, no reply in time), it logs a warning and starts anyway; the check is not retried. Accepted on Valkey 9.0.3 (single node and cluster) and Azure Managed Redis; on Amazon MemoryDB a test script of the same shape was accepted, and the service's own script has not yet been run there; ElastiCache is untested. Catch-ups and backups of a layer within the atomic-read limits read through the script, and a structural edit that reads the whole layer sends its windows through a second script of the same shape, which relies on the same capability (see the snapshot-lane entry above). Platform notes: `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements and `docs/service/docs/backing-store-aws.md` § Limitations.
- **Snapshot-read metrics.** `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer, path, outcome}`, with the atomic read's cells, the `structural_update` and `ext_gc` cells on `path="unfenced"` (`ok`, `not_found`, `transient_error`, `permanent_error`) and the `catchup`, `checkpoint`, `structural_cache` and `structural_cache_audit` cells on `path="fenced"` (`ok`, `not_found`, `transient_error`, `permanent_error`, `fence_broken`; one tick per attempt of a layer's shared fenced read, not per catch-up, or of a backup's or the structural-edit cache's own fenced read) present at zero from the first scrape; histograms, present only from their first observation: `live_edit_snapshot_read_duration_seconds{consumer, path}`, `live_edit_snapshot_read_reply_bytes{consumer}` and `live_edit_snapshot_read_entries{consumer}`. Transactional-path structural edits and external-value clean-up record them on `path="unfenced"`, the structural-edit cache's loads and audits on `consumer="structural_cache"` and `consumer="structural_cache_audit"`, and catch-ups, the rollback-mode replay's reads of single updates and backups on `consumer="catchup"`, `consumer="catchup_legacy_per_commit"` and `consumer="checkpoint"`, all on `path="atomic"`; catch-ups, backups and the cache's loads and audits of layers over the limits on `path="fenced"`. `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total{key_family}` gains `children_index`, present at zero. A new unlabelled counter, `live_edit_snapshot_read_window_fallback_total`, present at zero from the first scrape, ticks once per structural edit's whole-layer read that finished on ordinary commands after the store refused one of its script calls because the layer's slot was moving between nodes or not served, or the cluster was down; expected zero outside a resharding, a scaling operation or a cluster failover. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **`UpdateLayer` commit-time and conflict metrics.** A histogram, present only from its first observation: `live_edit_update_layer_commit_duration_seconds{path, outcome}` (`ok`, `error`), one sample per commit call (an `UpdateLayer` makes one, or two when it restores its layer from a backup and commits again), failed calls included, timed from the call's start — the wait for the layer's per-object admission slot included — to its return, with buckets from 1 ms to 32 s. A call cut off before it returns (its RPC deadline elapsed, or the client cancelled) is recorded as `error`, timed to that moment. A counter, present at zero from the first scrape on all twenty-five cells: `live_edit_update_layer_conflicts_total{path, kind}`, one tick per commit attempt abandoned because the layer changed after the attempt read it: `kind="watch_conflict"` (the commit's transaction was cancelled because a key it watched — the layer's header or its update stream — changed: usually another edit's commit, but also the checkpoint loop recording its backup position on the layer's header, so a single-writer layer can show it), `kind="watch_read_moved"` (a transactional-path structural edit's read of the layer overlapped another commit), and, on the structural-edit cache's paths, `kind="seq_fence"` (another edit landed after the cached state the edit planned on) and `kind="cache_diverged"` (the cached copy disagreed with the backing store); and, on every path, `kind="snapshot_fence_wait"` (the attempt met a live snapshot fence and wrote nothing; see the fenced-read entry below). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **A structural-edit cache**: a per-replica cache of the layer state that structural edits are planned on under the default `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua` (see the structural-edit entry under Changed). Its memory budget follows the pod's memory limit, 256 MiB at the chart's `5Gi`, and has no setting (see the pod-memory entry under `### Changed`). Only structural edits use it; catch-ups, live updates, backups, `StatLayer` and external-value clean-up always read the backing store, and `lua-watch-structural` and `txn` leave it empty. The budget bounds the cached layers' state by the cache's own estimate of its memory use; with the indexes it keeps beside each layer (see the structural-edit planning entry under Changed) a full cache holds up to about 1.21 times the budget. It never caches a layer whose state alone is estimated above a quarter of the budget or above 4,294,967,295 bytes (about 4 GiB), and drops entries unused for 10 minutes. The budget is part of the pod's memory, not extra to it: the service counts the full cache, with its indexes, against the pod's memory limit. A replica that serves structural edits holds up to about 1.21 times the budget for as long as they keep arriving, and drops a layer 10 minutes after its last use. New gauges, present once the replica's in-process state is built: `live_edit_structural_cache_entries` and `live_edit_structural_cache_weight_bytes`. New metrics for the read that loads a layer into the cache: the counter `live_edit_structural_cold_start_reads_total{route}` (`atomic`, `fenced`, `uncacheable`), present at zero from the first scrape, and the histogram `live_edit_structural_cold_start_read_duration_seconds{route}`, present from its first observation; `live_edit_snapshot_read_total` gains the `consumer` values `structural_cache` (that read) and `structural_cache_audit` (the sampled audit's read, below), their cells on `path="atomic"` and `path="fenced"` present at zero. **A load chooses its read before it reads,** from the layer's record counts, which the check the cache already makes before each structural edit now also returns (ten constant-time commands added inside that one script call, no extra round trip): a layer that fits the atomic-read limits, at 18 bytes per counted item, takes the atomic read (`route="atomic"`); any other layer takes the same fenced read a catch-up of it takes, directly (`route="fenced"`), so a load reads the layer once. Only a layer denser than that (large values stored inline) reads twice, a counted miss whose first read stops at the byte limit. **A fenced load pauses an edit to that one layer that meets it for about one polling step**, typically 1–2 s, and holds no backing-store shard; it takes the replica's fenced read permit (`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY`) and reserves from the catch-up memory budget, one such load per replica at a time. If it cannot finish in the time its edit can spare, that one edit takes the slower path and the layer's next structural edit tries again; a layer so large that its load could not fit even a whole `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` is not read at all, and is marked uncacheable for 10 minutes instead (`over_deadline`, see the uncacheable entry below). See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Self-checks that should read zero.
- **Structural-edit cache metrics: how each structural edit got its cached layer state, and why a cached copy was dropped.** Before a structural edit uses its cached copy of a layer, the replica brings the copy up to date from the layer's live-update stream; if that finds anything unexpected, the copy is dropped and the layer is read again (a reload). If the copy from a fresh read fails that check twice in one edit, the edit takes the slower path that reads the backing store, for that edit only, rather than read the layer a third time. New counters, present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_structural_cache_lookups_total{outcome}` (`hit`, `repaired`, `cold`, `reload`), one per structural edit attempt, so a retried edit counts again; `live_edit_structural_cache_reloads_total{reason}`, with eleven reasons — `uncacheable` counts a layer that grew past a quarter of the cache budget while cached, which is the first sign of a layer outgrowing the cache; and `live_edit_structural_cache_divergence_total`, a structural edit's commit refused because the cached copy disagreed with the backing store, **expected to stay 0 and alertable**. New histograms, present only from their first observation: `live_edit_structural_cache_replayed_entries` (stream entries one update of a copy replayed) and `live_edit_structural_cache_repair_bytes` (stream bytes it read). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Self-checks that should read zero.
- **A sampled audit of the structural-edit cache: `LIVE_EDIT_STRUCTURAL_CACHE_AUDIT_RATE`** (Helm `layer.structuralCacheAuditRate`, default 0.001) **and `LIVE_EDIT_STRUCTURAL_CACHE_AUDIT_ABOVE_CEILING`** (Helm `layer.structuralCacheAuditAboveCeiling`, default `true`). After a structural edit commits, a replica starts an audit with the configured probability, at most one at a time: it reads the layer again, compares it with the cached copy brought up to the same version, and counts the result on the new counter `live_edit_structural_cache_audit_total{outcome}` (`match`, `diverged`, `skipped`), present at zero from the first scrape. It only measures and never writes to the backing store; on `diverged`, which is expected to stay 0 and is alertable, it drops the cached copy and logs a `WARN` naming the layer and the first difference. A running audit holds about twice the audited layer's cached state in memory, counted in the chart's memory sizing, and reserves the memory of its catch-up of its copy to the version it read from the upload budget, never waiting and giving way to uploads: while any upload waits for admission or a processing step is queued, including a session held at its upload cap, when its reservation would leave less than one upload's admission free, or when it would need more than one upload's admission (about 8.1 MiB), the audit counts `skipped`. Those skips fall mostly on busy and large layers, and on a replica whose uploads fill the budget, so those are checked least often. The rate is a number from `0` (never audit) to `1`; anything else fails startup. With the switch `true` (the default), the audit reads a layer over the atomic-read limits with the same fenced read the cache loads it with, so the cache's largest entries are checked too. That read pauses an edit to that layer that meets it for about one polling step, not for the whole read: half of such pauses last at most about 1.4 s and 95 % at most about 2.5 s on a layer of about 7,000 prims and 4,860 attributes in the Kitchen_set live layer's shape (150,211 items, 4 times the item limit), and 2.0 s and 3.1 s on one of about 17,400 prims and 12,150 attributes (375,526 items, 10 times) (measured on the same fenced read when a client joins a layer that large), so a replica starts at most one such read every 10 minutes; it never waits for the replica's read capacity or catch-up memory (an audit that would have to is skipped), and its memory counts against the catch-up memory budget. A new counter, `live_edit_structural_cache_audit_fenced_total{result}` (`read`, `paced`, `busy`, `failed`), present at zero from the first scrape, says whether each such audit read the layer and, if not, why; the edit pause it causes is on `live_edit_snapshot_fence_hold_seconds{consumer="structural_cache_audit"}`. With the switch `false`, a layer over the limits counts `skipped`, and the cache's largest entries are never checked. The switch takes exactly `true` or `false`; `1` fails startup. The chart renders both values, `0` and `false` included; an explicit `null` or empty chart value fails the render with an error naming the key. Only structural edits under the default `lua` backend start audits. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Advanced tuning and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Self-checks that should read zero.
- **Why a layer's structural edits left the structural-edit cache: `live_edit_structural_cache_uncacheable_total{reason}` and one log line per verdict.** When the cache cannot hold a layer, the layer's structural edits take the slower path that reads the whole layer on every attempt. The new counter, present at zero from the first scrape on all five reasons, has three reasons that mark the layer and two that concern one edit: `over_weight` (the layer is estimated above a quarter of the cache budget), `no_chs_complete` (a layer created before the service kept a complete index of its child-ordering lists), `over_deadline` (a layer over the atomic-read limits so large that its load and one attempt on the slower path would not fit `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS`: at the defaults, layers of about 25,825 to 56,500 placed assets, each a prim with a reference and four attributes, estimated), `reload_limit` (one edit whose loads of the layer kept finding it changed, usually during a rolling upgrade; that edit only) and `fenced_read_failed` (one edit whose fenced load of a layer over the atomic-read limits could not finish in the time that edit had left, or was broken three times; that edit only). `over_weight`, when the layer's record counts already put it over a quarter of the budget, and `over_deadline` are decided from the layer's size, with no whole-layer read. A marked layer stays marked for 10 minutes on a replica, and its structural edits in that time tick nothing, so a busy marked layer ticks about once per 10 minutes per replica; `reload_limit` and `fenced_read_failed` mark nothing and tick once per edit. Each tick logs one line, `structural cache found the object uncacheable; its structural edits take the WATCH path`, at `INFO` (`DEBUG` for `reload_limit` and `fenced_read_failed`), naming the session, layer and object, the reason and what it compared. **A layer is cacheable by weight up to that quarter of its budget:** at the default 256 MiB, about 48,200 placed assets (estimated); at the default deadline, though, a layer of about 25,825 to 56,500 placed assets (estimated) that is not already cached is marked `over_deadline` instead of loaded (see the structural-edit cache entry under `### Changed`). A layer the cache does not hold keeps its field edits unaffected, but its structural edits queue one at a time per layer and replica, each reading the whole layer, and can run out the RPC deadline. Raising the budget with the pod's memory limit helps only a layer whose fenced load also fits the deadline: at the defaults a placed-asset layer of about 25,825 to 56,500 assets (estimated) is already `over_deadline`, and a larger one would only move from `over_weight` to `over_deadline` on the same slower path. For an `over_deadline` layer, raise `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` or split the scene; a raised deadline returns a layer to the cache only if it is also under the weight limit (about 48,200 placed assets at the default budget), so a layer of about 48,200 to 56,500 assets would then pay a load and be marked `over_weight`, and also needs a larger budget, raised together with the pod's memory limit, or a split; the mark clears after its 10 minutes once the layer has shrunk or the deadline has been raised.
- **A third `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND` value, `lua-watch-structural`** (Helm `layer.updateLayerBackend`; case-insensitive, read at startup). It is the rollback for the default `lua` backend's handling of structural and reset edits (see the structural-edit entry under `### Changed`): field and time-sample edits use the same Lua fast path as `lua`, and structural and reset edits commit on the WATCH/MULTI/EXEC path, reading the layer's whole state on every attempt, admitted like any other edit (up to `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_PER_OBJECT_CONCURRENCY` at once per layer), with the structural-edit cache left empty. An unknown value still fails startup; the message now lists `lua, lua-watch-structural, txn`. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Advanced tuning.
- **`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY`** (Helm `valkey.catchupFencedReadConcurrency`, default 1) **and a catch-up memory budget.** The setting is how many fenced reads of layers over the atomic-read limits one replica runs at once, with permits separate from the atomic reads'. The budget is per replica; it follows the pod's memory limit and has no setting (see the pod-memory entry under `### Changed`).
