Catch-ups of those layers reserve the memory for their snapshot and build from the budget, granted in arrival order; a reservation larger than the budget is cut to it and runs alone, so no layer is refused for its size. A reservation starts from an estimate of the layer's size and grows as the read goes, without waiting and never past the budget, so a layer whose values are larger than estimated (big arrays, rigs) is counted in full while it is read, its first read included; the growth shows on `live_edit_catchup_build_budget_overcommit_bytes`. Every catch-up of a layer over the limits uses both, through its layer's shared fenced read (see the catch-up entry under Changed). **The default `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_POOL_SIZE` now follows `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY` too**, so raising it opens one more Valkey connection per replica for each step (unchanged at the defaults). The budget is what the pod's memory limit leaves after everything else the service counts, including about 143 MiB of read buffers per fenced read in flight: 744 MiB at the chart's `5Gi`. Other limits: `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size. Because the same limit funds the budget, the structural-edit cache and the uploads, the subscription ceiling at `5Gi`, 4096, holds while an established subscription costs at most about 54 KiB (it measured about 50 KiB). Under the default `lua` backend the structural-edit cache's fenced loads, and its sampled audit's fenced reads, of layers over the limits use both too (see the structural-edit cache entries above). The fenced-read setting rejects `0` and non-numeric values at startup. New metrics, present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_catchup_build_budget_reserved_bytes`, `live_edit_catchup_build_budget_overcommit_bytes`, `live_edit_catchup_builds_in_progress`, `live_edit_catchup_build_admission_total{result}` (`immediate`, `waited`, `refused_timeout`, `cancelled`) and `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_flight_total{role}` (`leader`, `joined_read`: the catch-ups that started, or joined, one replica's shared fenced read of a layer over the limits); and the histogram `live_edit_catchup_build_wait_seconds`, present from its first observation, with the capacity-wait buckets. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics.
- **`UpdateLayer` now honours fenced reads of a layer, on every commit path, and three metrics report it.** A catch-up's or a backup's read of a layer over the atomic-read limits, and under the default `lua` backend the structural-edit cache's load or sampled audit of one, holds a short-lived fence on the layer so that it sees one exact version (a catch-up's is one read per layer per replica, shared by every subscriber joining it there; see the catch-up and backups entries under Changed, and the structural-edit cache entries above). An edit that meets a live fence — a field or time-sample edit, a structural edit or a reset, under every `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND` — writes nothing and waits, without using its retry budget, and gives up with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` (`update_layer waited <w> ms of its <c> ms snapshot-fence wait cap on object <id> (<path>; <l> ms left of the call's deadline); nothing was written, retry`) only once its waiting would pass `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` in total or the call's remaining deadline. That message names how long the edit waited, the cap, the layer's object id, the commit path of the attempt that last met the fence and how much of the call's own deadline was left, and the service logs it once, as the call's `RPC failed` line. Edits to layers within the limits never wait, except while the structural-edit cache loads a layer whose record counts put it near the byte limit, which it reads with the fenced read too: at the defaults, from about 1,460 prims in the Kitchen_set live layer's shape, about 290 prims with a translate, rotate and scale each, or about 220 placed assets (33,590 counted items, the byte limit at the 18 bytes per counted item the cache predicts a layer's size from).
  - **Commit time includes the pause.** The wait counts in `live_edit_update_layer_commit_duration_seconds` and in the client's round trip: up to about one fence hold plus one polling step, measured in a test environment at about 141 ms for a layer just over the limits and 2.8 s for a layer of about 48,900 prims and 34,074 attributes in the Kitchen_set live layer's shape (1,053,154 items; a managed store is slower). Each fenced attempt also ticks `live_edit_update_layer_conflicts_total{kind="snapshot_fence_wait"}` on its path.
  - **Writer intents, on the transactional path only.** A structural edit or reset that reads the whole layer before it commits (the transactional path: `lua-watch-structural`, `txn`, a layer the structural-edit cache cannot hold, or an edit too large for one commit call) registers a short-lived intent on a layer over the limits, so that back-to-back fenced reads cannot starve it. Field and time-sample edits and cached structural edits register none: each commits in one server-side step, so it is either refused by a fence or done before one starts.
  - **New metrics:** `live_edit_update_layer_fence_intent_total{result}` (`registered`, `refused_fenced`; transactional-path attempts only) and `live_edit_update_layer_fence_timeout_total`, present at zero from the first scrape; and the histogram `live_edit_update_layer_fence_wait_seconds{backend}`, present from its first observation, with the snapshot-read duration buckets: the time from the first attempt a fence refused or interrupted to the end of the last wait, one observation per call that met a fence, including a call the RPC deadline cancels, its `backend` the commit path of the call's first fence wait: `lua` for edits committed by the server-side script (the fast path and the structural-edit cache), `txn` for edits on the WATCH path (every edit under `txn`; structural and reset edits under `lua-watch-structural`, on a layer the cache cannot hold, or too large for one commit call).
  - **Two new per-layer keys and their cost.** The fence and intent live on `le:{oid}:obj:snapfence` and `le:{oid}:obj:snapintent`. Every commit checks both keys' types inside its script, and reads the fence and the store's clock only while the fence key exists (it lingers for a time that grows with the layer's size after the layer's last fenced read); see [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § CPU. A foreign value on either key refuses every edit to that layer with `INTERNAL`, on every commit path, counted on `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total` like the layer's other keys.
  - **Rolling upgrade.** Replicas on an earlier build do not honour the fence, so while a rollout is under way their edits can break an upgraded replica's fenced read. Until every replica runs this build, a catch-up of a layer over the limits on an upgraded replica can end with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` after three broken reads (its re-subscribe catches up), and a backup of such a layer can fail its pass (`live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="error", kind="layer"}`) and is retried on the next one. Once the rollout completes, edits no longer break fenced reads; a read can still be broken, in any build, when its lease lapses under a backing-store or replica stall longer than the lease, or when the layer is restored or removed under it.

  No configuration change. See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Retry hints, `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Keep fleet-wide layer reads at 10 or fewer, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **Three memory metrics, present from the first scrape: `process_resident_memory_bytes`, `live_edit_jemalloc_allocated_bytes{service}` and `live_edit_jemalloc_resident_bytes{service}`.**
  The first is the process's resident memory, named and unlabelled like the standard Prometheus process metric so existing dashboards work unchanged.
  The other two are the memory allocator's view: bytes the service currently holds, and an upper bound on the resident memory the allocator has mapped, including freed memory it has not yet returned.
  Read together they tell a leak (allocated keeps growing) from memory that is not being returned (resident memory stays high while allocated is back at its baseline).
  See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it saturated? and § Capacity diagnostics.
- **An optional Helm value, `allocator.mallocConf`, for diagnosing or tuning the memory allocator** (rendered as `_RJEM_MALLOC_CONF`; empty by default, which leaves the built-in options in force).
  Nothing needs setting; see the entry under `### Fixed` below.
- **Every failed call now leaves exactly one log line naming the request, including failed subscriptions and joins.**
  Previously a failed `SubscribeReadLayer` or `JoinSession` left nothing in the log, only a `+1` on `live_endpoint_calls_total`, so when joins failed under load there was no record of which session, layer or client was affected, or which read had failed.
  Several other failures were equally silent: a subscription refused at a full replica, a call refused for a missing or malformed `Authorization` header, a request the gRPC transport rejected (undecodable, over the 4 MB message limit, or to a method that does not exist), and a call the client abandoned or timed out on.
  Each of these now logs one line in the same shape as the existing `RPC failed` line, with the same field names and levels:
  - A failed subscription logs the session, layer, client and the status message the client received, which names the read that failed, plus which catch-up it was in (`regime`), why it ended (`reason`, the same value as the `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total` label) and whether the client had caught up (`caught_up`). A failed join logs the session and its `connection_id`.
  - A subscription refused at admission logs a `WARN` at `stage` `admission` naming who was refused and why.
  - A call refused for its `Authorization` header logs a `WARN` with a `rejection` field naming which check failed; the header value is never logged.
  - A request the transport rejected logs at `stage` `transport`.
  - A call the client abandoned before an answer existed — it hung up, its own per-call timeout fired, or the connection dropped — logs `RPC abandoned before completion` at `INFO`, with `client_timeout_ms` when the client set a timeout. Such calls now also count on `live_endpoint_calls_total` under `grpc_code="CANCELLED"`; previously they were not counted at all.
  - A subscription that hits the internal consistency check that should never fire logs a `WARN`, `SubscribeReadLayer ended without a terminal status`; the client still sees a normal end of stream.

  **Server faults now carry the request.** The `ERROR` line `internal error` now includes the failed call's session, layer, client and other request fields alongside the detail, instead of the detail alone.
  An `INTERNAL` that the gRPC transport produced itself, such as for an undecodable request, logs as a `WARN` `RPC failed`, so a client still cannot raise the service's error-level log rate.
  **No failure line for:** successful calls, a client leaving a subscription or join that was already established, a subscription that ends cleanly because its session was deleted, or because the replica shut down while the subscription was idle, and successful health checks.
  Under heavy load, a Kubernetes health probe that times out now logs one `RPC abandoned before completion` line under `endpoint` `other`.
  **No configuration change and no new metric**; all new lines are visible at the default `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_LEVEL=info`, with no sampling. Status codes and messages are unchanged, except for the `SubscribeReadLayer` layer-name check under `### Changed` below.
  See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Logs, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it saturated? and § Is live delivery keeping up?
- **Join latency: a new histogram, `live_edit_join_latency_seconds{endpoint, service}`, measures how long a join takes from the request arriving to the user having the layer or session in front of them.**
  For `SubscribeReadLayer` it runs until the subscriber is caught up, and includes authorization, any wait for a free subscription slot, and the catch-up. For `JoinSession` it runs until the connection confirmation and the current membership have been sent, and includes the wait for the client's first message.
  Successful joins only; a failed join's duration stays on `live_endpoint_duration_microseconds`. Buckets run from 5 ms to 60 s.
  Previously the only timing was the catch-up histogram, which starts after the wait for a free slot, so the whole join could not be measured.
- **Valkey command timeouts are now counted, on `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{pool, kind="command_timeout"}`.**
  Previously a command that got no reply within `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` failed or retried its caller and was counted nowhere, because it does not close the connection; that counter saw connection-level errors only.
  It now counts once per command whose own deadline — usually `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` — expired, on the pool that sent it, for client requests and background work alike, so it can rise with no client-visible error. All five pool series are present at zero from the first scrape.
- **Two counters that were listed on `/metrics` but never moved now record live delivery: `live_subscriber_updates_total` and `live_presence_events_total{event_type}`.**
  The first counts live updates handed to subscribers' streams, the second live presence events (`joined`, `left`, `message`) handed to session participants' streams, each once per recipient; neither shows whether or when the client read them. The catch-up a new subscriber receives, the membership list a new participant receives, and a client's own edits and events are not counted.
  Both are recorded in small batches per stream, so they can trail the deliveries by about a second; read them as rates.
  See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving? and § Is the backing store healthy?
- **Trace export settings and lost-span metrics** (see the trace-export entry under `### Fixed`).
  New environment variable `LIVE_EDIT_TRACE_FILTER` (default `live_edit=info`). New Helm values under `observability.tracing`: `samplingRatio` (default `"1.0"`), `filter` (default `live_edit=info`), and `batch.maxQueueSize` (2048), `batch.maxExportBatchSize` (512) and `batch.scheduleDelayMs` (5000).
  New metrics, present only on a replica with trace export on, at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_trace_spans_exported_total` and `live_edit_trace_spans_dropped_total{reason}` (`queue_full`, `export_failed`).
  See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Traces and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is trace export working?
- **Live delivery lag: new metrics and a log line show whether late live updates are the service's delay or something past it.**
  - `live_edit_subscriber_delivery_lag_seconds{stage}`: how far behind its commit a live update was when the service queued it on the subscriber's stream (`enqueued`) and when the service's gRPC layer took it off that stream to send (`transport`). Buckets run from 1 ms to 120 s.
  - `live_edit_subscriber_send_stalls_total{phase}`, present at zero from the first scrape, and `live_edit_subscriber_send_wait_seconds{phase}`: sends that found a subscriber's stream full because the HTTP/2 peer had stopped taking data, and how long they waited. `phase="catchup"` stalls are normal for large catch-ups; read `phase="live"`.
  - `live_edit_subscriber_backlog_entries` and `live_edit_subscriber_backlog_bytes`: how many updates, and bytes, wait in a subscription's buffer on the replica.
  - The histograms record the worst update per subscription per window of about a second or 64 updates, not every update, so their quantiles are of each window's worst update. Catch-ups are not measured, and none of the series carries a session, layer or client label.
  - A new `INFO` line, `subscriber delivery is lagging`, names a subscription whose live updates run 1 s or more behind after it has caught up, at most once a minute per subscription. Time spent catching up does not count toward it.
  - **Behind a proxy these see only the hop between the service and the proxy.** A proxy with a large HTTP/2 window, such as Envoy on its defaults, can hold a slow client's updates for tens of seconds while every service-side signal, including the `slow_reader_deadline` and `inner_watchdog` endings, stays at zero.
  See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Edits propagate slowly.
- **`live_edit_stream_mux_reauths_total{result}`: the stream readers' periodic credential re-authentications.** Both cells (`ok`, `failed`) present at zero from the first scrape. Under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` expect one to three `ok` per connected reader around each token rotation (typically one) and none in between; under `iam` about one per connected reader every 5–6 minutes; under `static` none. `failed` is expected zero. A failed `AUTH` logs one `WARN`, `stream reader credential re-AUTH failed; the connection is kept and nothing is reconnected (the next rotation or reconnect re-authenticates it)`, naming the reader, node and error; a credential the reader could not obtain counts `failed` at debug level only, because the replica's readiness and the other connections' refresh warnings already report it. See the stream-reader re-authentication entry under `### Changed` and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up?.
- **Presence metrics: whether session participants' leases are kept alive, what the background presence sweep does, and whether a replica lost presence events.**
  - Present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_presence_lease_refresh_failures_total` (lease renewals, or rejoins after a removal, that failed on a backing-store error), `live_edit_presence_rejoins_total` (participants that were still connected, were removed, and put themselves back), `live_edit_presence_sweep_lease_total{outcome}` (`renewed`, `lost`, `refresh_error`), `live_edit_presence_stale_removed_total`, and `live_edit_pubsub_relay_lagged_total{relay}` (`presence`, `session_deleted`; see the pub/sub entry under `### Fixed`).
  - Histograms, present only from their first observation: `live_edit_presence_sweep_duration_seconds{outcome}` (`completed`, `lease_lost`, `cancelled`, `error`) and `live_edit_presence_stale_removal_delay_seconds` (from a lease's expiry to its removal, on the backing store's clock).
  - A gauge, absent until the replica first publishes it (at the earliest one sweep interval after it starts): `live_edit_presence_sweep_oldest_stale_age_seconds`, the most overdue removal of the latest completed sweep. Aggregate it with `max()`, never `sum()`.
  - The sweep's series are reported by whichever replica ran the sweep, so sum the counters across replicas. Healthy `live_edit_presence_rejoins_total`, `live_edit_presence_sweep_lease_total{outcome="lost"}` and `live_edit_pubsub_relay_lagged_total` are zero.
  See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?, and the presence entries under `### Changed` and `### Fixed`.
- **New backing-store requirement: a Lua script must be allowed to `PUBLISH`, and every node must deliver those messages in the order the publishing node ran them.**
  The service now announces a participant joining or leaving a session (`PeerJoined`, `PeerLeft`) from inside the same script that changes the session's membership, so an announcement can never be separated from the change it announces. The backing store must allow `PUBLISH` inside a server-side script and deliver those messages to subscribers on every node, in the order the publishing node executed them, including when script publishes and ordinary `PUBLISH`es from other connections are interleaved on one channel. Unlike the script-read requirement above, this one is not checked at startup.
  See `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements.
- **`live_edit_presence_resyncs_total{service}`: `JoinSession` streams the service ended so their client rejoins with a fresh participant list.**
  Present at zero from the first scrape; one tick per stream ended because its connection fell too far behind and missed a participant joining or leaving (see the presence entry under `### Changed`). Healthy is zero or rare. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?
- **`live_edit_userinfo_fallbacks_total` and a `WARN` line: callers the userinfo service has no user for.**
  With the userinfo service and authorization both on, each time the service names such a caller from its token's claims (see the userinfo entry under `### Changed`) it ticks this unlabelled counter, present at zero from the first scrape in every configuration and 0 unless both are on, and logs one `WARN`, `userinfo service has no user for the caller; using the display name from the bearer's JWT claims`, whose `principal_id` field is the token's `oid` (else `sub`); never the token or the name. Lookups are cached per token, so expect one tick per token per cache entry on each replica, not one per call. A fallback rate close to the userinfo call rate means the userinfo service is not the directory your callers live in. See `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Caller identity and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are credentials and auth services healthy?

### Changed
- **Action for deployers: every Helm value name is now camelCase.**
  The 46 chart values whose names held underscores were renamed, one capital letter per underscore: `valkey.memory_bytes` is now `valkey.memoryBytes`, and `rpc.deadline_ms` is now `rpc.deadlineMs`. The environment variables did not change.
  - **Old names fail the render.** While a values file or `--set` still uses an old name, `helm template` and `helm upgrade` fail before any pod changes. One message lists every old name found, each with its new name; rename them and upgrade again.
  - **`helm upgrade --reuse-values` fails on this upgrade**, because it carries the previous chart's values, old names included. Upgrade with your renamed values files (`-f`). `--reset-then-reuse-values` (Helm 3.14 or later) works too, unless the release's own earlier values used an old name.
  - **Rolling back:** `helm rollback` works, because it restores the earlier chart with that revision's own values. Installing an earlier chart with your renamed values does not fail, but that chart ignores the renamed values and uses its own defaults for them; give an earlier chart your values files from before the rename.
  - `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` lists every value under its new name; see also `docs/service/docs/operations.md` § Renamed settings.
- **An OpenTelemetry collector URL that is not `http://` or `https://` now stops startup, as does an exporter the service cannot build for a valid one (b.zpx).**
  This covers `observability.tracing.endpoint.grpc` (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`), also read by `live-edit scaler`, and `observability.metrics.endpoint.grpc` (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_ENDPOINT`). A URL with no scheme, another scheme such as `grpc://` or `grpcs://`, or no host is refused.
  The pod prints `configuration error:` naming the variable, exits and never becomes Ready, and the previous pods keep serving. Before upgrading, change a `grpc://` URL to `http://`; the other refused forms already failed every export.
  An `https://` URL whose CA bundle cannot be read also stops startup, naming the cause. So does any value, `none` included, of `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_COMPRESSION` or `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_COMPRESSION` while trace export is on, and of `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_COMPRESSION` or `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_COMPRESSION` while metrics push is on. Exports are always sent uncompressed, so the error names the variable and the fix is to unset it. Such a value used to turn trace export off silently, or metrics push off with a `WARN`.
  Without the chart, an empty or blank `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` now turns trace export off; it no longer falls back to `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_ENDPOINT`.
- **The chart's default CPU request is now one core** (`resources.requests.cpu: 1`, was `500m`).
  The autoscaler's CPU target is a percentage of the request, in either autoscaling mode. At the default 80 it now adds replicas when they average about 0.8 cores, not 0.4. A replica uses about 0.6 to 0.8 cores in join bursts while the replica count is below `autoscaling.maxReplicas`, so the old request added replicas that relieved nothing. Each replica now reserves half a core more on its node. See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size.
- **Every per-replica memory budget now follows the pod's memory limit, and four memory settings are removed.**
  The subscription ceiling, the memory for catching up subscribers to very large layers, the upload memory and the structural-edit cache were each set for the chart's `5Gi` memory limit. Changing `resources.limits.memory` meant re-deriving them by hand, and a pod given another limit without re-derived values could run out of memory under load. The service now reads its container's memory limit at startup and derives every one of them from it. **At the chart's default `5Gi` nothing changes:** 4,096 subscriptions, 744 MiB of catch-up memory, 384 MiB of upload memory and a 256 MiB structural-edit cache, as before. Other limits get less or more: at `4Gi`, 3,276 subscriptions, 433 MiB, 307 MiB and 204 MiB; at `8Gi`, 6,553, 1,607 MiB, 614 MiB and 409 MiB (`docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size).
  - **Removed settings:** `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_MAX_SUBSCRIPTIONS` (Helm `valkey.subscriber_max_subscriptions`), `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_BUILD_MEMORY_BUDGET_MIB` (Helm `valkey.catchup_build_memory_budget_mib`), `LIVE_EDIT_UPLOAD_MEMORY_BUDGET_MIB` (Helm `layer.upload_memory_budget_mib`) and `LIVE_EDIT_STRUCTURAL_CACHE_MAX_MIB` (Helm `layer.structural_cache_max_mib`). With them go an unlimited subscription ceiling and turning the structural-edit cache off on its own; `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua-watch-structural` remains the way to stop using the cache.
  - **Pods smaller than their settings need refuse to start.** A pod whose memory limit cannot carry its configuration exits at startup with `configuration error: this pod's memory limit, <L> MiB (<source>), is below the <N> MiB this configuration needs, <D> MiB short; …; raise resources.limits.memory to at least <N> MiB or lower those settings`, naming the settings that take the most memory, and never becomes ready, so a rolling upgrade keeps the old pods serving. At the chart's defaults the minimum is 2,709 MiB (`2709Mi`) on any CPU count without a backup store and 2,809 MiB (`2809Mi`) on any CPU count with one, and every limit from it up starts; a larger limit never gives any budget less memory. Larger connection pools, more catch-ups or fenced reads at once, more waiting uploads, a larger `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` or a larger atomic-read byte limit raise it, and each also takes memory from the catch-up budget. A configuration that no limit can fit is refused with `no pod memory limit can fit this configuration`; in practice that is `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY` above 61 at the chart's other settings, and the message names the largest value that fits.
  - **More startup refusals.** Any removed variable set, to any value, stops startup: `<VARIABLE> is no longer read: the <budget> is derived from the container's memory limit at startup (<value> at this pod's <L> MiB). Unset it; to change the <budget>, change the pod's memory limit.` `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_MAX_CONCURRENT_CATCHUPS=0`, which meant unlimited, is now refused (`must be greater than 0`).
  - **Chart.** `resources.limits.memory` is now required: the render fails with `resources.limits.memory must be set` without it. On Kubernetes 1.27 and later, the versions with in-place resize, the server container gets `resizePolicy` memory `RestartContainer`, so resizing a running pod's memory in place restarts its container, since the budgets are sized once, at start; on older clusters the field is not rendered. A removed Helm value set to anything but its old default (`4096`, `744`, `384`, `256`) fails the render, naming it; at its old default it renders nothing, so values carried over from an install that never changed it do not fail on it. The memory limit in `helm/live-edit-service/values-local.yaml`, the local-development values file, rises from `512Mi` to `4Gi`.
  - **Without a readable limit** (no memory limit on the container, a limit it cannot read, or a run outside Linux), the service sizes itself as a 5 GiB pod and, on Linux, logs the `WARN` `no readable pod memory limit: every memory budget is sized for the 5,120 MiB reference pod …`. Its `reason` says why and the message says what to fix: `unlimited`, set a memory limit; `not_found`, mount `/sys/fs/cgroup` in the container with its memory controller enabled; `unreadable`, fix the file and error the reason names. A limit above the host's memory is treated as the host's memory.
  - **Metrics and logs.** Four new gauges are present from the first scrape. `live_edit_pod_memory_limit_bytes{service, source}` gives the limit read: of its `source` series (`cgroup_v2`, `cgroup_v1`, `host_memory`, `reference`), the one in use carries the limit and the others read 0. A non-zero `reference` series means the replica read no limit; the new optional `LiveEditPodMemoryLimitUnread` alert fires on it. `live_edit_catchup_build_budget_bytes` and `live_edit_subscriber_max_subscriptions` have no labels, like the usage gauges they are read against (`live_edit_catchup_build_budget_reserved_bytes` and `live_edit_subscriber_admitted`). `live_edit_structural_cache_budget_bytes{service}`, labelled like `live_edit_structural_cache_weight_bytes{service}`, carries the cache's budget with its indexes, about 1.21 times the budget and the bound of that weight gauge; `live_edit_upload_budget_bytes` and `live_edit_catchup_build_budget_bytes` carry the budget alone. A startup `INFO` line, `pod memory budgets derived from the pod memory limit; …`, gives the limit, where it came from and every amount derived. Dashboards and alerts that compare usage with a fixed 4096, 744 MiB, 384 MiB or 256 MiB should read these gauges instead. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it saturated? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rules.
  - **Refusal messages** no longer name the removed variables: the subscription-ceiling refusal reads `this replica is at its concurrent-subscription ceiling (its memory budget, sized from the pod's memory limit); retry`, and the upload and large-layer catch-up refusals name "its memory budget, sized from the pod's memory limit" where they named a variable. Status codes, reasons and retry hints are unchanged.
  - **Upgrade note.** If you set any removed variable in the pod's environment, or any removed Helm value in your values files to anything but its old default, remove it: the service now derives this from the pod memory limit, and to change the amount you change `resources.limits.memory`. Keep `resources.limits.memory` set, and at or above the minimum for your settings. See `docs/service/docs/operations.md` § Upgrade and its Removed settings table.
- **With backups on, the memory for large values waiting to be copied to the backup store now follows the pod's memory limit, with no setting; with incremental backups on, a pod with a backup store needs at least 2,809 MiB to start.**
  Each replica could hold up to `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_EXT_MIRROR_QUEUE_CAPACITY` (1,000) large values of any size waiting for the backup store, far more memory than a pod has when the values are large.
  - **The memory.** It is 2.5 % of `resources.limits.memory`, and never less than one `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` value: 102 MiB at `4Gi`, 128 MiB at `5Gi` and 204 MiB at `8Gi`. It comes out of the 20 % of the limit the service holds back, so no other budget changes.
  - **Minimum limit.** With a backup store and incremental backups on, the smallest limit that starts is 2,809 MiB (`2809Mi`) on any CPU count with the chart, and at 1 to 64 CPUs with the binary's defaults. Without a backup store, or with `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED=false`, it stays 2,709 MiB on any CPU count at the chart's defaults. A larger `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` raises it.
  - **The refusal names what sets the minimum.** When the backup terms set it, the `configuration error:` names `the terms of the 20 % memory margin that settings size`, each with the variables that turn it on and size it. It then says to raise `resources.limits.memory` first, which costs nothing else. Failing that, it says to lower `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` or, with incremental backups on, turn them off (`LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED=false`), at the cost it states. It ends `do not turn off LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ENABLED or LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ADAPTER for this: that removes backups`.
  - **Skipped copies.** A large value that finds no room is not copied, and its upload still succeeds; uploads never wait for this memory. A skipped copy, or one the backup store refuses, is uploaded again a few minutes later by the new backup repair (see its entry under `### Added`). Until then, if the backing store's data is lost, requests that open a restored layer using such a value fail with `INTERNAL`. The documentation said such values were fetched back on demand; they are not (`docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Backups of large values).
  - **Metrics.** `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_mirror_dropped_total` gains a `reason` label: `queue_full`, `budget_full`, `oversized` (a restored value larger than the current upload size limit) or `shutdown`. Every `reason` is present at zero from the first scrape, and `sum()` and `rate()` queries keep working. New gauges `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_mirror_bytes{state}` (`queued`, `in_flight`) and `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_mirror_budget_bytes`, `0` with backups off, show the memory in use and its limit (`docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values backed up?).
  - **Alert rule.** Update `LiveEditExternalValueMirrorDropping` to leave out `reason="shutdown"`, which a replica that stops with values still waiting now counts, so that rollouts do not fire it (`docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Backup alert rules).
  - **Logs.** The drop and failure `WARN` lines are reworded and carry a `reason` field. A new `WARN` counts the values still waiting when a replica stops, which were dropped silently before. The startup line `pod memory budgets derived from the pod memory limit` gains `ext_mirror_budget_bytes`, and `checkpoint ext-mirror workers spawned` gains `budget_bytes`.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_EXT_MIRROR_WORKERS` (Helm `checkpoint.extMirrorWorkers`) above 16 now fails startup**, with an error naming the variable. More workers only add concurrent uploads to the backup store.
- **The default `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_COUNTER_SAFETY_MARGIN` (Helm `checkpoint.counterSafetyMargin`) is now 1,000,000, up from 1,000.**
  When the service recovers from a backing-store data loss, it starts new layer ids this far above the last id its backups recorded, so a new layer never takes the id of a layer a backup holds and overwrites that backup. Backups record the latest id only every minute or so, and not at all while the backup store is down, so the margin must exceed the layers created across the deployment in that time. 1,000 could be used up by a few layer creations a second; 1,000,000 covers a backup-store outage of about 11 days at one layer creation a second.
  - **What to do.** If your values set `checkpoint.counterSafetyMargin` to `1000`, remove it or raise it. Raising it is safe; lowering it below the default risks reused layer ids after a backing-store data loss. Values above `1000000000`, like `0`, now fail startup with an error naming the variable.
  - **What clients see.** Layer ids (`object_id`) can jump by up to the margin each time a pod starts, so expect large ids with gaps. They stay unique and increasing.
  - **New log line.** A `WARN`, `checkpoint counters-sync: live le:counter:object_id is below the stored counters sidecar; not lowering the sidecar`, repeats about every minute while the backing store's id counter is below the latest id the backups recorded: the store went back to older data. Check the backing store for a data loss or a restore from an older copy; see `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Keyspace lost.
- **Backup errors for the layer-id record, `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="error", kind="counters_sidecar"}`, can now rise on a healthy backup store for a short time, and point at a deleted record when they persist.**
  Once a replica has read or written the record (`{BACKUP_PREFIX}/.live-edit/counters.json`), it treats a "not found" answer for it as an error and writes nothing on that check (about once a minute). This stops a stale answer from letting it record a lower id. Each such check logs a `WARN` containing `answered absent, but this process has already seen the counters sidecar` and counts one error.
  - **Expected after the first layer on a new backup prefix.** A Storage service that caches "not found" answers can keep answering "not found" for up to that cache time after the record is first written, so expect these errors during backup validation; they stop by themselves.
  - **What to do if they persist.** The record was deleted from the backup prefix. Restart the replicas (`kubectl rollout restart`); a replica writes it again within about two minutes. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Backups behind or stopped.
- **A layer whose content is over the 99,999-entry limit of one upload is not rebuilt from its backup after a backing-store data loss, and the service never falls back to an older backup of it.**
  This was already true: such a layer's backup could not be read back, and its requests failed with `INTERNAL`. The service now refuses it before restoring from it, so requests for the layer fail with `INTERNAL` and never fall back to an older backup. A replica logs a `WARN` containing `a backup over the per-delta entry limit cannot be restored on access` (for an older backup, sometimes only an `internal error` line). A layer deleted or replaced during the data loss still answers `NOT_FOUND`.
  - **What to do.** A user who still has the layer's content creates the layer again, split across several uploads; otherwise the service cannot recover it. Creating it again replaces the layer, and the service then deletes the old layer's backups, so copy these files out of the backup prefix first if you want to keep them. They are the files under `{BACKUP_PREFIX}/.live-edit/layers/` whose names start with the layer's object ID followed by a dot, and the `{BACKUP_PREFIX}/.live-edit/ext/{HASH}.blob` files named in the `ext_hashes` list of its `.ext-refs.json` files, which the service deletes too once no remaining backup names them. Take the object ID from the `WARN`, or, for an older backup, from the `ERROR` `internal error` line whose `error` field starts `checkpoint recovery failed for object {OBJECT_ID}:` (`layer-state recovery failed for object {OBJECT_ID}:` for a subscription). See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost.
- **The chart refuses a negative or fractional number, or text, at install time for most of the server's whole-number settings, naming the value; a million or more now reaches every server setting as written.**
  A values-file number of a million or more rendered as `1e+06` on some server settings, which the service refused at startup; every server setting now renders it as plain digits. Write whole numbers as plain digits.
  - **Not checked at install:** `valkey.memoryBytes`, `valkey.extReleaseMaxBytes`, `valkey.snapshotAtomicReadMax*`, `valkey.subscriberAdmissionMaxWaiters`, `layer.maxSize`, `layer.uploadAdmission*`, `shutdown.streamEnd*`, `observability.tracing.batch.*`, `checkpoint.tail.max*` and the `autoscaling.*` scaler settings. On the server settings, anything but plain digits can be read as a different number, such as `0`; the scaler settings are passed as written, so a values-file number of a million or more there still renders as `1e+06`, and you should quote it.
  - **Upgrade note.** A `helm upgrade` or `helm template` whose values hold such an invalid number now fails before any pod changes, with an error naming the value, for example `presence.keepaliveIntervalMs must be a non-negative whole number`. Fix the value it names.
- **An upload whose delta is too large is refused with a clear reason; the per-upload limit is 99,999 entries in total.**
  The limits were documented as 100,000 each of struct commands, set fields and time samples per delta. One delta could never carry more than 99,999 of them in total. A larger one, such as 60,000 sections plus 60,000 fields, was refused `INVALID_ARGUMENT` with `OODF error: FlatBuffers verifier failed: …`, naming no count or remedy. Now a `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` over a limit, chained or not, is refused `INVALID_ARGUMENT` with an `ErrorInfo` (domain `nvidia.omniverse.live.v1alpha`) and no `RetryInfo`:
  - **`DELTA_TOO_LARGE`**: more than 99,999 struct commands, set fields and time samples in total. Metadata `struct_commands`, `set_fields`, `time_samples` and `max_entries` (99999), each a decimal integer. The message names the counts, the limit and the remedy.
  - **`CHILDREN_LIST_TOO_LONG`**: a `ReorderChildren` naming more than 100,000 children. Metadata `limit` (100000) and `actual`.

  Either means this call applied nothing, and re-sending the same delta always fails. On `DELTA_TOO_LARGE`, split the content into several smaller deltas (for a new layer, create it with the first and add the rest with `UpdateLayer`). On `CHILDREN_LIST_TOO_LONG`, name at most 100,000 children in one `ReorderChildren`. The limit applies to each upload, not to a layer. No upload accepted before is refused now, and nothing refused before is accepted. A client that matched the old message text must match the reason instead. On a chained `UpdateLayer` the size reason replaces `INVALID_UPDATE`. During a rolling upgrade, a replica of an earlier version answers the same upload with the old error; rolling back needs no step. See `docs/api/docs/validation.md` § Deltas that are too large.
- **Structural edits no longer copy the layer, and their replica CPU no longer grows with the layer's size (deletes on layers holding time samples excepted).**
  Under the default `lua` backend each structural edit used to plan on a copy of the layer's cached state and rebuild lookups over the whole layer on every attempt, and each replica repeated those lookups for every other replica's structural edit it caught up; on a development deployment's growing layer, structural commits slowed from about 4–5 ms to about 45 ms at the median as the layer grew to about 60,000 prims and attributes, and edit conflicts between replicas rose about tenfold with them. Now the edit is planned on the cached layer itself, against indexes the cache keeps beside each layer, and undone there if it does not commit (a conflict, a refusal, a cancelled call), so the cached layer is always left exactly as one stored version. Measured on a local store with three replicas, a placement of a 32-prim-and-attribute asset costs about 1.3–1.7 ms of replica CPU and a rename about 0.1 ms from 10,000 to 190,000 prims and attributes (before: 9.2 → 168.5 ms and 4.3 → 75.8 ms), deleting such an asset about 0.7–0.9 ms on a layer without time samples, and the share of edits that conflict stays flat. Edits, their results, what subscribers receive and the commit's checks are unchanged. No configuration change. Rolling upgrades and rollbacks need no ordering or extra step: nothing stored or sent between replicas changes.
  - **Memory.** The cache's indexes add about a tenth of each layer's state, and the cache's budget is raised for them by 21 %, so every layer keeps the room for its state it had: a full cache now holds up to about 1.21 times the budget (about 310 MiB at the 256 MiB of the chart's `5Gi` limit), and `live_edit_structural_cache_weight_bytes` includes the indexes. A layer whose indexes weigh relatively more (a very small one, for instance) is counted in full, so it, or other layers, are only dropped a little sooner; the cache never holds more than about 1.21 times the budget, whatever a layer's indexes weigh, and a layer whose indexes alone have grown it past that whole figure (they keep room that earlier edits to the layer made) is dropped from the cache after an edit to it and loaded again, with fresh indexes, at its next structural edit. Which layers can be cached is unchanged: the per-layer limit counts the state alone, and building the indexes after a layer's load does not count against the load's deadline. A structural edit no longer reserves room for a copy in the upload budget, so the same edit reserves 0.38–0.47 times what it did on layers of 10,000–190,000 prims and attributes (a layer of a few prims receiving a large edit reserves up to about 21 kB more). The chart's 5 GiB memory limit still covers everything the service counts, now with about 17–30 MiB to spare, and the catch-up memory budget stays 744 MiB there (other limits: see the pod-memory entry above). The subscription ceiling of 4096 at that limit now holds while an established subscription costs at most about 54 KiB (it measured about 50 KiB).
  - **Known limits.** An edit that deletes prims on a layer holding time samples (animation) still scans every time sample of the layer, on its own replica and on each replica that catches it up: with 20 time samples on every placed asset, deleting one cost about 3.2 ms of replica CPU at about 60,000 prims and attributes and 7.5 ms at 190,000, most of it that scan. On a layer whose prims carry very many distinct attribute names, deleting prims costs at most one scan of the layer's attributes. An edit under a parent with very many children costs in proportion to those children (about 12–13 ms to place an asset under a parent of 25,000). A structural edit can now wait for upload memory more than once, each wait counted on `live_edit_update_layer_recharges_total`.
- **Known limit: structural edits on very large layers can run past the default RPC deadline.**
  A structural edit (creating, deleting, moving, reordering or renaming a prim) that reads the whole layer (every structural edit with `txn` or `lua-watch-structural`; with the default `lua`, those on layers too large for the structural-edit cache) now reads it without transferring stored values, which keeps its memory bounded and lets it reserve that memory before it reads (see the upload memory budget entry under `### Added`), sending most windows of the layer's records as one short script call each (the snapshot-lane entry under `### Added` names the windows it sends as ordinary commands): about 1.4 s for a layer of 5,000 placed assets on a local store, against 1.6 s reading whole rows as the previous release did (measured). On a store with about a 1.4 ms round trip, such an edit on a layer of more than about 69,000 placed assets (each a prim with a reference and four attributes) no longer finishes within the default 30 s `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` and fails `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` with nothing applied; reading whole rows, that point is about 83,000. If another edit lands on the layer during the read it is about 34,000 (whole rows, about 41,500), and another such edit on the same replica lowers it further. While the backing store is moving the layer's slot between nodes, a read sends its remaining windows as ordinary commands and reaches the deadline sooner, from about 47,000 placed assets (about 23,600 with another edit landing during the read). With the default `lua` backend this affects layers too large for the cache (about 25,825 placed assets and up). Heavier placed assets reach these limits at smaller counts. Estimated from a cost model, not measured on a managed store. See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Limitations.
  **Concurrent structural edits through that read no longer slow down at 6 CPUs:** four editors at once took about 0.56 s at the median with a 2-CPU limit and 0.53 s with 6 (measured on a shared local store). The previous release was not measured this way.
  - **Rolling upgrade and rollback.** No migration, no operator step, no ordering for this read: a replica of an older release never sends the per-window script, and a replica of this release loads it into the store the first time it needs it. Replicas of 0.1.x, or of any other build that does not honour fenced reads, can break an upgraded replica's fenced reads until the rollout completes (see the `UpdateLayer` fenced-reads entry's **Rolling upgrade** under `### Added`). Between releases that both honour them, each replica uses its own release's estimate of a structural edit's cost, so they can differ in how long a large layer's load and edits to that layer wait for each other, and in which layers they mark `over_deadline`; edits, their results and what subscribers receive are unaffected.
- **The external-value clean-up runs continuously, collecting each released large value about 5 s after its release; `LIVE_EDIT_GC_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS` is removed.**
  The clean-up ran every `LIVE_EDIT_GC_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS` (60 s by default) and collected everything queued, so a released value waited anywhere from 0 to 60 s. It now runs one pass about once a second on the replica holding maintenance leadership and collects each released value, oldest first, once it is at least 5 s old. The released values the store holds at any moment are about the release rate × 6 s. When they reach half the release budget, each pass also collects the excess over that half at once, oldest first, so they hold near half the budget, and uploads carrying large values wait at the budget (`### Added`). While a catch-up holds the clean-up off after finding a value collected under it (see the catch-up entry under `### Fixed`), newer released values wait for it.
  - **Removed: `LIVE_EDIT_GC_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS` and the Helm value `gc.poll_interval_secs`.** The service no longer reads the variable, so a value you set is ignored; there is no replacement setting.
  - **Startup no longer moves half-processed clean-up work back onto the queue;** the leading replica retries work older than 30 s on every pass, in place, without putting it back on the queue, so a crashed replica's work is picked up within a pass of another replica leading.
  - **Logs.** The per-run `GC sweep complete` line is gone (a quiet pass logs nothing above `DEBUG`), and a pass skipped because another replica leads logs at `TRACE`. A pass that fails logs `GC pass failed` at `ERROR` and counts on `live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total{kind}`, as before; a failure on one value's own keys now skips only that value, with an `ERROR` naming it, instead of ending the pass.
  - **Rolling upgrade and rollback.** No migration, no operator step: values queued by an older release without a time are collected too, by the same age rule as any other release (from about 5 s, or at any age while released values are at half the budget or more), counted from when this release first sees them, instead of at once. Upgrades from, and rollbacks to, any release from 0.1.0 on are supported side by side (see the upgrade-safety entry under `### Fixed`, which lists what remains).
- **Structural edits use much less memory, and larger layers, a flattened Kitchen_set among them, stay on the cached structural-edit path.** Each replica's structural-edit cache now keeps only a layer's structure — its sections and their order, and per attribute and time sample its name or time and, for a large value, its hash — never the values. A cached layer is about a quarter of its former size on layers of placed assets or prims with a few attributes each, and far smaller on layers of large inline values such as recorded animation, so a replica keeps about 4× as many layers in the same budget. The per-layer weight limit (a quarter of the budget) now falls at about 48,200 placed assets (each a prim with a reference and four attributes; heavier assets reach it sooner) at the default budget, up from about 12,000, and a flattened Kitchen_set, which was over it, takes about a fifth of it, so its structural edits no longer read the whole layer from the backing store on every edit. From about 25,825 to 56,500 placed assets, though, a layer's first load does not fit the default edit deadline, so such a layer, even one cacheable by weight, is marked uncacheable (`over_deadline`; above that, `over_weight`) and keeps that slower path; loading the largest layer that still fits takes up to about 15.2 s, and edits to that layer wait while it loads (all three figures estimated, not measured at those sizes). Loading a layer within the atomic-read limits into the cache no longer transfers its stored values from the backing store; the read's cost there, and `live_edit_snapshot_read_reply_bytes`, are unchanged, because every value is still read. Edits, their results and what subscribers receive are unchanged. No configuration change; `live_edit_structural_cache_weight_bytes` reads lower for the same layers.
- **Backing-store connections give back the memory a large call used once it ends.** A connection to Valkey kept the largest command batch, write and reply it had ever carried for as long as it stayed open, so a replica's idle memory grew with its history: tens to hundreds of MB after a few large uploads. Now each Valkey client keeps at most about 0.5 MiB once its traffic drains, so what a replica's connections keep follows its pool sizes: at most about 116 MiB at the chart's defaults on a standalone store, and on a cluster about 118 MiB at one primary plus about 1 MiB per additional primary. Nothing to configure.
- **A shutting-down replica ends its live streams gradually, bounds its wait for connections to close, and needs a longer termination grace; rollouts take at most 3 steps of about a third of the pods each.**
  A replica that shut down ended every `SubscribeReadLayer` and `JoinSession` stream at the same instant, so all its clients reconnected to the remaining replicas at once, and after telling its clients to go away it waited for their connections to close with no limit of its own, so a client that had stopped reading could hold it until the kubelet's SIGKILL.
  - **Paced stream endings.** After its drain the replica now closes its listening socket (a new connection attempt is refused at once and retried elsewhere, instead of waiting unaccepted), sends GOAWAY, and a quarter of a second later ends its live streams a few at a time in random order: at `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_STREAM_END_RATE` per second, over at most `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_STREAM_END_MAX_WINDOW_SECS` (500 streams take 20 s at the defaults, 50 take 2 s). Above 500 streams the window cap governs and the rate rises to the streams divided by 20 s — about 55 per second at 1,100 streams, which on a 3-replica deployment puts about 27.5 reconnects per second on each of the 2 remaining replicas, close to what one can catch up at; at 10 replicas each takes about 6. Every ending is still `UNAVAILABLE` `server shutting down`, now with the `SERVER_SHUTTING_DOWN` retry hint. A stream not yet ended keeps delivering until its turn, and one whose session is deleted meanwhile ends as a deletion. From the moment the endings start, a new stream and an upload waiting for admission or for room to process its content are refused `UNAVAILABLE` (`server shutting down`), exactly when they were refused before.
  - **Bounded wait.** The replica waits for its connections to close for at most 5 s past the later of the end of the stream-ending window and `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` from its start, then logs one `WARN` with `open_connections` and carries on; a connection still open finishes on its own or ends with the process.
  - **Termination grace: 40 s → 80 s.** The chart now refuses to render a `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` below `shutdown.timeoutSecs` + the larger of `rpc.deadlineMs` (in whole seconds) and `shutdown.streamEndMaxWindowSecs` + 6 + `shutdown.overallBudgetSecs` + 6 (77 s at the defaults), naming the values. **Action for operators who set their own grace, or raise `rpc.deadlineMs` or a shutdown setting: raise `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` to at least that sum.** A stuck pod can now take up to 80 s to be killed; a healthy shutdown takes about the drain plus the stream-ending window plus a few seconds.
  - **Rollouts: `maxSurge: "33%"`, `maxUnavailable: 0`, `minReadySeconds`.** The chart now sets a rollout strategy (Helm `strategy.rollingUpdate`, defaults `maxSurge: "33%"` / `maxUnavailable: 0`) and `minReadySeconds` (Helm `minReadySeconds`, `null` by default, which renders `shutdown.timeoutSecs` + `shutdown.streamEndMaxWindowSecs` + 5, 30 s at the defaults). Each step adds about a third of the replica count in new pods (rounded up, so one at 3 replicas or fewer), waits for them to be ready that long, then drains as many old pods. So at most that third of the pods hands its clients to the rest at once, capacity never drops, and a rollout takes at most 3 steps up to 99 replicas: about 2.5 minutes with a 20 s pod startup, inside Helm's default 5-minute `--wait` (arithmetic from the chart defaults, not a measurement). **Action for operators: leave room for the surge.** During a rollout the namespace needs node capacity, ResourceQuota and backing-store connections for the extra pods (1 at up to 3 replicas, 2 at 4–6, 4 at 10, 7 at 20; `docs/service/docs/operations.md` § What to expect during a rollout). A new pod that cannot be scheduled holds the rollout. **The backing store's connection limit must now hold twice `autoscaling.maxReplicas` replicas** (`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Connections): on self-hosted Valkey set `maxclients 20000` (the default 10,000 is exceeded during a rollout at 10 replicas); on Azure Managed Redis `Balanced_B10` and larger qualify, and on the 15,000-connection Balanced SKUs (`Balanced_B0`–`B5`, including the dev `Balanced_B1`) keep `autoscaling.maxReplicas` at 7 or less; Amazon MemoryDB qualifies as is. Without that headroom, set `strategy.rollingUpdate.maxSurge: 1`: pods then move one at a time, and a rollout takes about replicas × (startup + 30 s), so size `helm upgrade --wait --timeout` to that.
  - **Metrics and logs.** `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total{reason="shutdown"}` and the per-stream `INFO` `server shutting down` lines now spread over the stream-ending window instead of arriving at one instant. New lines: one `INFO` when the drain ends and the listening socket closes, one when the replica starts ending streams (`streams`, `window_ms`, `rate_per_sec`) and one when it has ended them all.
- **The chart's default autoscaler is now the replica autoscaler (`autoscaling.mode: scaler`), which adds a scaler Deployment, a ServiceAccount, a namespaced Role and a RoleBinding to every install** (see the entry under `### Added`). In the default mode the chart renders no HorizontalPodAutoscaler.
  - **The new Role's rules:** `get` and `patch` on `deployments/scale` in the `apps` group, limited by `resourceNames` to the server Deployment; `list` on `endpointslices` in `discovery.k8s.io`; and, only with `autoscaling.scaler.podDeletionCost: true`, `patch` on `pods`. No rule on Deployments themselves, no access to Pods, Secrets or ConfigMaps by default, and no ClusterRole.
  - **Prerequisite in every environment, before upgrading:** in the default `autoscaling.mode: scaler`, upgrading to this version creates a Role and a RoleBinding in the release namespace. So the identity that applies the chart (the account Helm or your GitOps controller runs as) needs the right to create Roles and RoleBindings there. Kubernetes also lets it create the Role and bind it only if it already holds the rules the Role grants, or is granted the `escalate` verb on `roles` (to create the Role) and the `bind` verb on that Role (to create the RoleBinding). Where it cannot, set `autoscaling.scaler.rbac.create: false` and grant the rules above to the scaler's ServiceAccount yourself (`autoscaling.scaler.serviceAccount.name` names a pre-provisioned one, which must mount its token), or set `autoscaling.mode: hpa`. Otherwise the upgrade fails.
  - **Action for operators: to keep the HorizontalPodAutoscaler, set `autoscaling.mode: hpa`.** Your `autoscaling.behavior` and memory target then apply as before.
  - **Action for operators who upgrade with `helm upgrade --reuse-values`:** from a release before this chart version, `--reuse-values` renders with the old release's values in place of this chart's defaults, and those have neither `autoscaling.mode` nor `autoscaling.scaler`. Alone, it fails with `autoscaling.mode must be "scaler" or "hpa" (got "")`. With `--set autoscaling.mode=scaler`, whether or not some `autoscaling.scaler.*` settings are also set, it fails with `autoscaling.scaler is missing or incomplete`. Upgrade with `--reset-then-reuse-values` (Helm 3.14 or later) **in place of** `--reuse-values`, because Helm ignores it when both are given; on older Helm, upgrade with your values files. To keep the HorizontalPodAutoscaler, set `autoscaling.mode: hpa` in your values. From any release before this one, `--reuse-values` also fails on the renamed values (see the camelCase entry above). A failed render stops the upgrade before Helm records it, so the running release is untouched.
  - **Settings the scaler cannot run with fail rendering, rather than leaving a scaler that refuses to start.** In scaler mode the chart refuses `autoscaling.targetMemoryUtilizationPercentage` (the scaler has no memory signal), a CPU target with an empty `resources.requests.cpu`, `autoscaling.minReplicas` above `autoscaling.maxReplicas`, `autoscaling.scaler.serviceAccount.create: false` with no `name`, any `autoscaling.scaler` setting left missing, null or empty other than `resources`, `serviceAccount.name`, `podAnnotations` and `affinity` (`serviceAccount.create` only while `serviceAccount.name` is empty), and a `podDeletionCost` that is not an unquoted `true` or `false`. `autoscaling.scaler.resources: null` removes the scaler's requests and limits; `{}` does not, because Helm merges it with the chart's default. **Render each of your overlays once with `helm template` in scaler mode before rolling out.**
  - **Network policies.** With `networkPolicy.enabled`, the chart now admits the scaler pod to the server pods' metrics port. If you write your own policies, allow it too, or the scaler sees no pod and holds the replica count where it is.
  - **During the upgrade that introduces it**, server pods of the previous release do not answer the scaler, so it decides nothing until the first new pods are Ready, and scales down only once every server pod runs the new release. The count the HorizontalPodAutoscaler left stays in place meanwhile, because the chart leaves the Deployment's replica count unset in both modes.
  - `autoscaling.behavior` now applies only in `hpa` mode; the scaler's damping is `autoscaling.scaler.scaleDown.*`, with the same defaults.
- **A new replica opens far fewer backing-store connections, and the autoscaler no longer drains replicas on short lulls.** Every autoscaler scale-up and every rolling deploy used to open each new replica's whole connection set at once — in 0.1.1 about 1,158 TLS connections, each with its own authentication, per replica on a two-primary clustered store at the Helm production defaults — and the autoscaler's default scale-down drained replicas during short lulls in load, ending their clients' live streams, then added them back minutes later.
  - **Stream readers connect when first used, and on a clustered store to one primary only.** Each of the `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` stream readers now opens its connection the first time it serves a subscription, not at startup, and on a clustered store (Azure Managed Redis, Amazon MemoryDB) connects only to the primary that owns the layer it serves, instead of to every primary. A replica now opens 136 clients at startup at the Helm production defaults (386 in 0.1.1, all opened at startup): on two primaries, 408 sockets instead of about 1,158. With every reader in use it holds at most 920 sockets on two primaries; at 3 shards and 10 replicas the cluster-wide figure is about 10,560 (about 15,440 in 0.1.1). The worst case for a single node, every backchannel and reader on one node, rises from 7,720 to 7,840 at 10 replicas: the readers' change leaves it as it was, and the 120 added are the new snapshot lane's (see `### Added`). Recheck the figures for your store in [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § Connections.
  - **A node that refuses reader connections now shows at the first subscribe, not at startup.** Startup still connects to every node with the same credential and TLS settings, through the other pools. A reader that cannot connect logs a `WARN` beginning `stream reader could not connect to the backing store` and counts on `live_edit_stream_mux_reader_connects_total{result="failed"}` (see `### Added`); its subscriptions are retried after a back-off, or, on a credential, TLS or configuration error, end with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`.
  - **A reader follows its layer when the cluster changes.** A read redirected with `MOVED`, or a failover that moves the layer's slot, moves the reader's connection to the new primary instead of retrying the old one. If the layer's primary moves again before any read has succeeded, the reader first backs off and logs a `WARN` (`stream reader's hash slot owner moved again (MOVED) before any read succeeded; backing off before retargeting its connection`), and each such move counts as one failure on the reader's retry budget, past which its subscriptions end with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`.
  - **A reader's single connection is re-authenticated by the reader itself, between its reads**, on clustered and non-clustered stores alike, so the credential refresh never interrupts or waits behind a reader's read and `live_edit_valkey_consumer_unblocked_retries_total` stays at or near zero in every auth mode (see the stream-reader re-authentication entries under `### Changed` and `### Fixed`, and `live_edit_stream_mux_reauths_total` under `### Added`).
  - **Reader health metrics.** `live_edit_stream_mux_keepalives_total` counts one `PING` per tick on the reader's one connection, and a reader that has not connected yet does not tick. `live_edit_valkey_consumer_forced_reconnects_total{phase}` also counts the verdicts where a clustered reader, finding its primary no longer serves its layer, moves or closes its connection instead of rebuilding it.
  - **Startup budget: `startupProbe.failureThreshold` 56 → 57** (285 s, was 280 s in 0.1.1). A degraded startup connect still costs at most 150 s at the default `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`: five phases, the snapshot lane's in place of the stream readers', but each is now cut off hard at that timeout (a connection attempt still running at the budget no longer overruns it). The other 5 s is the new startup check of script support (see the snapshot-lane entry under `### Added`); the 40 s margin is unchanged. The budget matters only for a degraded boot; a normal boot takes seconds. 285 s leaves only 15 s of Helm's default 5-minute wait for scheduling and image pull, so a boot that uses the whole budget, which happens only when the backing store or the credential sidecar is degraded, fails a default `helm upgrade --wait`: the upgrade fails without serving capacity ever dropping, `--atomic` rolls it back (including any pods already replaced), and you retry it once the dependency has recovered. Raise `startupProbe.failureThreshold` with either timeout (`docs/service/docs/operations.md` § Health probes and startup).
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS=0` is now rejected at startup.** It bounds every startup connect phase and every stream reader's connect, so `0` would cut each of them before it could finish. **Action for operators:** if you set it to `0`, set a positive value (the default is 30).
  - **Autoscaler scale-down is damped by default**: a replica is removed only after 600 s of lower demand, then at most one per 60 s; scale-up is immediate. Idle replicas now linger about 10 minutes, plus a minute per replica, after load falls. In `autoscaling.mode: hpa` this is the new Helm value `autoscaling.behavior`; **action for operators who want the previous HPA behaviour:** set `autoscaling.behavior: null`, which restores the Kubernetes defaults; `{}` does not, because Helm merges it with the chart default. The default scaler mode damps the same way (`autoscaling.scaler.scaleDown.*`). See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Autoscaling.
- **An upload the backing store refuses before applying anything now fails the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, not `INTERNAL`.**
  When the store is out of memory, cannot persist to disk, or the write reached a node that does not accept writes (a read-only replica, or a primary with too few replicas), a `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` whose write it refused now fails `UNAVAILABLE` with `the backing store refused a write this request needed because <condition>; nothing was applied, retry`. Before, it failed `INTERNAL` with the redacted `internal error`, so a client could not tell that nothing had been applied. It carries the `STORE_WRITE_REFUSED` retry hint: this call applied nothing; a re-send is safe unless an earlier attempt of the same write is still in doubt — then use `operation_id` (`docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Retry hints). A refusal of the `CreateLayer` commit itself answers `… because <condition>; retry` with no hint, and an `UpdateLayer` commit refused after an internal retry answers `UpdateLayer outcome unknown: …` with no hint: neither can prove the call applied nothing. The Kit client and standard gRPC retry policies retry it. The service does not retry it itself. A replacing `CreateLayer` refused after it has already retired the layer it replaces fails like the other writes below.
  - **Every other write the store refuses this way also fails `UNAVAILABLE` now, not `INTERNAL`:** on `CreateSession`, `DeleteSession`, `DeleteLayer` and `JoinSession`, and every write of an RPC other than the upload steps above, the call fails a plain `UNAVAILABLE`, `<command>: the backing store refused a write this request needed because <condition>; retry`, with no retry hint and no promise about what was applied, so treat its outcome as unknown, as you treated the `INTERNAL`. The service does not retry it itself, and it is not counted on `live_edit_store_write_refused_total`. A replacing `CreateLayer` retries its commit first, and can still take effect after answering (see the `CreateLayer` entry below). A `DeleteLayer` that fails this way may already have removed the layer. That happens on a clustered store, where a layer's entry and its update stream usually sit on different nodes and only the stream's node refused (or stayed loading or busy past the internal retries). It also happens on any store that starts refusing writes, or restarts, partway through the delete. Its retry can answer `NOT_FOUND`. The layer's subscribers are told it was deleted once the service finishes the delete, usually within minutes of the store accepting writes again.
  - **Action for operators: dashboards and alerts that read the `INTERNAL` rate to detect a full store now see these calls as `UNAVAILABLE`**, on every RPC; use `live_edit_store_write_refused_total` (under `### Added`) for the uploads, and the store's own memory metrics.
- **A backing store that is still loading its data after a restart, or busy running a long script, no longer fails requests `INTERNAL`; requests ride it out or fail the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`.**
  Such a store refuses every command for a while and then clears by itself. Every call that met it failed `INTERNAL` (`internal error`), which a client could not tell from a server fault, and a live `SubscribeReadLayer` ended at once. Now:
  - **Requests** answer `UNAVAILABLE`, the message naming the failed command and quoting the store's reply. `CreateSession` first retries internally, within its budget (about 0.65 s on average, at most about 1.3 s, at the default `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS`). `UpdateLayer` and `DeleteLayer` retry internally (about 3 s at the default `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS`) only when the store stops serving while they are committing, so a short window that opens mid-commit is absorbed without the client re-sending; a call that arrives while the store is already loading or busy fails at once, before an upload's content is read. A window longer than the budget ends the call `UNAVAILABLE` with the retry-exhaustion message.
  - **A `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` refused before its commit started now carries a new retry hint, `UPSTREAM_UNAVAILABLE`** (`retry_delay` 1–3 s, a minimum wait: keep your own backoff and wait the larger of the two). It covers every `UNAVAILABLE` such a call is refused with before its commit, when no more specific reason applies: the backing store still loading, busy, or failing at the connection level, the authorization or user-identity service unavailable, or the backup store failing while the service restores a layer or session. Like every hint in this domain, it means this call applied nothing; a re-send is safe unless an earlier attempt of the same write is still in doubt — then use `operation_id`. So a plain `UNAVAILABLE` the service answers on these two calls now always comes from a write whose commit had started, and its outcome is unknown (one raised outside the service, by the client's own transport or a proxy in front of it, leaves the outcome unknown too): retry exhaustion, the overtaken answer, any failure of the `CreateLayer` commit, and an `UpdateLayer` refused after an internal retry carry no hint. During a rolling upgrade, replicas still on the previous build answer these pre-commit refusals without the hint: a plain `UNAVAILABLE` for a connection-level or authorization outage or a failed restore from backup, and `INTERNAL` for a store that is loading, busy, or refusing the write; treat both as outcome unknown, as before.
  - **`JoinSession`** ends `UNAVAILABLE`, not `INTERNAL`, when the store is restarting or busy, or refuses a write the join needs, so clients rejoin in these cases. A client that re-sends, after rejoining, the message it was sending when the join ended delivers that message to the other members twice whenever the first send had already been relayed: session messages are not stored, and the service cannot tell the two apart. Write message handling to tolerate a repeat.
  - **A live `SubscribeReadLayer`** pauses instead of ending, and ends `UNAVAILABLE` only if the store stays that way longer than a few minutes; a subscribe or catch-up that meets it fails `UNAVAILABLE`, and the client re-subscribes as usual.
  - **Clients.** The Kit client retries `UNAVAILABLE` as it retried `INTERNAL`, and standard gRPC retry policies retry `UNAVAILABLE` (they never retried `INTERNAL`), and the service still answers gRPC `ABORTED` only to a chained `UpdateLayer`'s predecessor refusal. A client that reads the retry hints should treat `UPSTREAM_UNAVAILABLE` as it treats any hint in this domain (an unknown `reason` means "retry after `retry_delay`"), and should tolerate the repeated session message above.
  - **Metrics.** A backing-store restart no longer moves `live_edit_snapshot_read_refused_total{kind="backend_unavailable"}` or `live_edit_valkey_consumer_permanent_exits_total`; the former now moves only when the store refuses the background clean-up's write because it cannot persist, is a read-only replica, or has too few replicas. Snapshot reads that meet such a store count as `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{outcome="transient_error"}`, and the external-value clean-up's failed passes as `live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total{kind="transient_io"}`. While the store is busy running a script, `live_edit_storage_watch_voided_total{disposition="witness_unavailable"}` ticks once per attempt of each watched write. During a rolling upgrade, replicas still on the previous build keep the old behaviour, so expect those alerts from them only. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? and § Is live delivery keeping up?
- **A reconnecting subscriber's catch-up no longer reads the layer's whole update history at once.** It read up to 4096 retained updates in one reply to decide how to catch up; with large updates that is gigabytes in one reply. It now reads at most two entries; under `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false`, the per-update replay reads the window in pages, and if the stream is trimmed while a replay spanning several pages runs, that reconnect is served the full coalesced catch-up instead (counted `out_of_window`). Backups taken between full backups also read the stream in pages. Each page aims at about 1 MiB, sized from the largest update the read has seen so far, so a page is a target, not a limit: after a run of small updates, one page can carry up to 512 large ones. The worst case is bounded only by what the stream retains, about `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAX_BYTES` plus two updates of `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE`, and by no byte limit when `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAX_BYTES` is `0`. At the default settings, what a subscriber receives is unchanged.
- **The structural-edit cache now loads layers over the atomic-read limits with the fenced read, up to its per-layer limit by weight and when that read fits the edit's deadline; metric labels and two defaults changed from earlier builds of this release.** Earlier pre-release builds loaded a layer over the atomic-read limits with one larger atomic read (6 × the item limit, 8 × the byte limit), which held its backing-store shard for up to about a quarter of a second and left layers over those larger limits (about 1,850–1,900 prims with three attributes each) uncached, their structural edits on the slower path. Such a layer is now loaded with the same fenced read a catch-up of it takes, which holds no shard and pauses edits to that one layer only, so a layer is cacheable by weight up to a quarter of the cache's budget, and is loaded when its fenced read fits the edit's deadline (see the structural-edit cache entries under `### Added` and `### Changed`). If you built dashboards or alerts against an earlier build:
  - `live_edit_structural_cold_start_reads_total{route}` and its duration histogram lose `atomic_raised`; their routes are `atomic`, `fenced` and `uncacheable`.
  - `live_edit_structural_cache_uncacheable_total{reason}` loses `above_cold_start_ceiling` and gains `fenced_read_failed` (one edit whose fenced load could not finish in time; that edit only, logged at `DEBUG`) and `over_deadline` (a layer too large for its fenced load to fit the RPC deadline; marked for 10 minutes, logged at `INFO`); its verdict line loses the `item_bound`, `raised_max_items`, `raised_max_bytes` and `byte_tally` fields and gains `cause`, `attempts`, `size_items`, `size_bytes`, `waited_ms` and `cap_ms` for `fenced_read_failed`, and `size_items`, `size_bytes`, `rpc_deadline_ms` and `reserve_ms` for `over_deadline`.
