  - `live_edit_snapshot_read_total` and `live_edit_snapshot_read_duration_seconds` gain `consumer="structural_cache"` and `consumer="structural_cache_audit"` on `path="fenced"`; `live_edit_snapshot_fence_hold_seconds` and `live_edit_snapshot_fence_reader_wait_seconds` gain those consumers, and `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_read_wait_seconds` gains them with `class="fenced"`. A load that read twice (a layer denser than 18 bytes per counted item) now shows as a `structural_cache` atomic `too_large` followed by a fenced read.
  - New counter `live_edit_structural_cache_audit_fenced_total{result}` (`read`, `paced`, `busy`, `failed`), present at zero.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_STRUCTURAL_CACHE_AUDIT_ABOVE_CEILING` now defaults to `true`** (was `false`): the sampled audit checks layers over the atomic-read limits too, with the fenced read, at most once per replica every 10 minutes, and skips rather than wait for read capacity or memory. Set it to `false` to keep the audit off those layers.
  - **Edits too large for one commit call still take the transactional path.** A cached edit commits in one server-side script call, which holds the layer's backing-store shard for its whole run. In one call, a reset of a large layer would run for seconds (measured on a test store, scaled to Azure Managed Redis: about 7.8 s to clear 11,000 placed assets, each a prim with a reference and four attributes, the shape every placed-asset count in this entry uses), and past 5 s the store answers `BUSY` to every other client of the shard and the edit's own call can fail although its commit landed. So an edit over a fixed work limit — in placed assets, clearing, resetting or deleting about 3,000 or more at once; about 3,800 or more prims with three attributes each — commits on the transactional path, which reads the whole layer but keeps other clients waiting rather than refused. Edits within the limit stay on the one-call path and can hold the shard for about 1.5 s at the median on Azure Managed Redis (measured and scaled the same way), with single calls up to about twice that; clears of 300 and 1,000 placed assets stay there, at about 0.1 s and 0.4 s on the same scale. A reset of a layer the replica has not cached, whose size, counted before any read, already puts it over the limit, goes straight to the transactional path, without loading the layer first. The limit is not configurable.
    - `live_edit_update_layer_structural_fallbacks_total{reason}` gains `over_work_limit` and `reset_over_work_limit`, present at zero, which tick for every such edit and are not alertable; **an alert on this counter must select `reason="repeat_divergence"`**. Each such edit logs one `INFO` line, `structural edit is over the structural commit's work limit; committing this call through the WATCH path instead of one long script call`, with the edit's work and the layer's size.
    - New histogram `live_edit_update_layer_structural_commit_seconds`, present from its first observation: the time of each one-call structural commit, with buckets at 2, 2.5 and 5 s. A call over 2 s logs one `WARN`, `structural commit script ran long: …`, with the edit's work and the layer's size. **Report calls over 2.5 s**: the limit is set from measurement and comes down if real stores hold longer.
  - **`LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_BYTES` now defaults to `604619`** (was `449938`). Clients give sections random 64-bit ids, so real layers store about 16.1 bytes per item, not the 12 of the short-id layer the old default was set on; the old default stopped real layers at about three quarters of the item limit. The byte limit bounds a read's reply size and memory, not its time, so the item limit is unchanged. The memory figures derived from it moved with it: each catch-up of a layer within the limits can hold up to about 24 MiB, and the default subscription ceiling of 4096 now holds while an established subscription costs at most about 54 KiB. `live_edit_snapshot_read_reply_bytes`'s `le="1048576"` bucket now brackets the default limit.
- **Under the default `lua` backend, structural and reset edits are planned on the replica's structural-edit cache and committed in one server-side call, without a transaction connection.**
  A structural edit (creating, moving, deleting or reordering sections) or a reset used to read the layer's whole state on every attempt and commit through a WATCH/MULTI/EXEC transaction on a dedicated connection, so on a large layer under concurrent edits any commit landing during that read voided the attempt. Now a replica keeps the layer's state in its structural-edit cache (see `### Added`), brings it up to date from the layer's update stream before each edit, plans the edit on it, and commits with one call of the edit commit script. That call writes nothing if the layer changed since the state the edit planned on — the edit is retried after catching its copy up — or if a key the edit rewrites does not hold what the cache expected — the edit is retried after reading the layer again, and if the fresh copy disagrees too, it is committed through the transactional path instead, so it still succeeds. A layer the cache cannot hold (over a quarter of the cache budget, or without a complete index of its child-ordering lists), and an edit too large for one commit call (see the structural-edit cache entry above), are edited through the transactional path. The layer is read whole only to load it into the cache: once per layer per replica, again after it drops out, and on a reload ([`docs/service/docs/scaling.md`](docs/service/docs/scaling.md) § Keep fleet-wide layer reads at 10 or fewer).
  - **No wire, proto or status-code change.** The backing store still decides which edits commit and in what order, every committed edit leaves the layer's content as the transactional path would, and re-sends carrying an `operation_id` and the service's own lost-reply handling answer as before.
  - **Measured effect.** On a single-node Valkey 9.0.3, a structural edit on a layer of 1,250 prims with three attributes each (5,000 sections) now costs about 7–8 ms of replica CPU and about 19 (rename) to 130 (creating a prim with three attributes) store commands, the same command count as at 250 such prims (1,000 sections), against about 390–430 ms and 24,000–26,000 commands when every attempt read the whole layer. In a load test of a deployment (3 replicas, 8 writers at 2 edits per second each, the layer grown to about 1,375 prims with three attributes each, 5,500 sections) the layer held about 16 commits per second at every size, edits were confirmed at p95 141–150 ms as clients measured them, most of it their network round trip, structural edits took 1.05 attempts each, and each replica used at most 8 % of its 2 CPUs. Two costs still grow: creating or deleting a section rewrites its parent's whole children list (about 20 bytes per child), and a structural edit replays every field edit made to the layer since the replica's last structural edit on it (about 600 at 60 field edits per second and one structural edit every 10 seconds, about 20–22 ms of replica CPU per structural edit). See [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § CPU.
  - **Memory.** Replicas that serve structural edits now hold up to the cache budget (256 MiB at the chart's `5Gi` limit); the chart's memory request and the pod's memory limit count it (see the Helm defaults entry below).
  - **Rollback:** `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua-watch-structural` restores the transactional path and writer-only admission for structural and reset edits, and leaves the cache empty.
  - **What moves on your dashboards.** Structural edits and resets now carry `path="structural"`, `"structural_full"` (the attempt first read the whole layer) or `"reset"` on the `UpdateLayer` commit metrics; under `lua`, `path="watch"` now counts only the edits the cache could not plan or commit in one call. `live_edit_update_layer_conflicts_total` moves on `kind="seq_fence"` and `kind="cache_diverged"` on those paths, and much less on `watch_read_moved`. The structural-edit cache's gauges and counters, `live_edit_structural_cold_start_reads_total` and `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer=~"structural_cache|structural_cache_audit"}` start moving, while `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="structural_update"}` and the transaction pool's occupancy and connection checks (`live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use{pool="txn"}`, `live_edit_valkey_txn_dirty_checkouts_total`, `live_edit_storage_watch_voided_total{path="update_layer"}`) fall with your structural-edit volume. A structural edit's refusals now count on the `mode="structural"` cells of `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total` and `live_edit_stream_id_collision_total`, and its lost replies on `live_edit_update_layer_commit_already_landed_total{backend="lua"}`. Its commit log line reads `UpdateLayer committed (structural)`.
  - **New counter** `live_edit_update_layer_structural_fallbacks_total{reason}` (`repeat_divergence`, `over_work_limit`, `reset_over_work_limit`), present at zero from the first scrape: structural edits committed through the transactional path, by why they left the one-call path. `repeat_divergence`, the cached copy disagreeing with the backing store twice, is **expected to stay 0**; alert on that cell, beside `live_edit_structural_cache_divergence_total`, and each one logs a `WARN` naming the layer. The other two count edits too large for one commit call (see the structural-edit cache entry above) and are expected.
  See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **Under the default `lua` backend, a replica runs structural and reset edits to one layer one at a time.** Each such `UpdateLayer` waits for the layer's single structural slot on the replica before taking its usual per-layer admission permit (`LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_PER_OBJECT_CONCURRENCY`), and holds both until it returns. Field and time-sample edits never wait for that slot, so a queue of structural edits does not hold them up. Edits through other replicas are not affected, and which edits commit, and in what order, is still decided by the backing store. The wait counts in `live_edit_update_layer_commit_duration_seconds`. `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua-watch-structural` or `txn` admits structural and reset edits as before (see `### Added`). The gauge `live_edit_update_layer_admission_entries` keeps its name and meaning, one entry per layer written on the replica; its help text now says "admission entries", and each entry takes roughly 160–190 bytes rather than 80.
- **`CreateLayer` makes its new content the layer in one atomic step, never re-sent automatically, and the service finishes a replacement it left unsettled.**
  When that step fails transiently (a dropped connection, a lost reply, or a cluster redirect during a reshard), the service checks whether it landed. If it did not, the service tries again, on a clustered store after refreshing its view of the cluster. If another `CreateLayer` or a `DeleteLayer` changed the layer before this call's content took effect, the call replaces the layer as it is then, or creates it again after a delete, so this call's content becomes the layer. If this call's content had already taken effect, the call answers `OK` and the later change stands.
  - **What a call's answer means.** Once the service has seen a call's content take effect, it answers `OK`. Any error this version of the service answers, other than a plain `UNAVAILABLE` (one without a retry hint), `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` or `CANCELLED`, means the content had not taken effect when the call was answered, except as below. An answer from an older replica during a rolling upgrade, from a proxy or your own transport, or a connection lost after you finished sending leaves the outcome unknown too (`docs/api/docs/errors.md` § When an upload's outcome is unknown). After a plain `UNAVAILABLE`, `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` or `CANCELLED` the outcome is unknown: re-send the same request with the same `operation_id`. If the earlier call's content took effect less than 24 hours before, the re-send changes nothing and answers `OPERATION_OUTCOME_ALREADY_COMMITTED` with that call's `object_id`. A `CreateLayer` replacing a layer that fails `INTERNAL`, or `ALREADY_EXISTS` when its `operation_id` was also sent for another layer at once, may also still take effect later: read the layer before acting on the assumption that it did not.
  - **A replacement can take effect after its call answered**, with no further call from the client. The service completes it, normally within a minute and otherwise within minutes. Until then, `UpdateLayer` calls naming the layer's previous `object_id` fail `FAILED_PRECONDITION`. This also covers a replica that crashed partway through a replacement. When the service completes it, subscribers still reading the old content are told of the replacement unless the store refuses that notice for lack of memory, and the old content is reclaimed.
  - **Always send an `operation_id` on `CreateLayer`.** A replacing `CreateLayer` re-sent without one may replace the layer again. An `operation_id` identifies one request: sent for two layers at once, a replacing `CreateLayer` can fail `ALREADY_EXISTS` yet leave its layer refusing edits for up to a day and then replace it, unless the layer is deleted, or replaced by a `CreateLayer` that does not carry that id, first.
  - See `docs/api/docs/writing-layers.md` § Creating and replacing layers.

  Related metric changes:
  - `live_edit_checkpoint_base_bootstrap_total{site="creation_base"}` now also counts every `CreateLayer` re-send answered from an earlier commit, so its rate is no longer creations alone; a re-send's run for a layer already backed up ends `skipped_generation_present` or `unstamped_generation_present`.
  - `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total{backend="txn", site="create"}` gains new sources. A creation's operation record holding a foreign value is refused before anything is stored. At the creation's final step, a foreign value on the layer's own entry, on the session's list of layers, or on one of the creation's own records is refused after the new content was stored. On a new layer, the background clean-up reclaims that content. On a re-upload, every `UpdateLayer` to the layer fails `FAILED_PRECONDITION` until the key is removed; the service then completes the re-upload. A foreign value on the layer's own entry stops the background clean-up for the whole deployment until it is removed, and the layer must then be created again. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Damaged layer keys.
- **Live Edit is now licensed under the Apache License 2.0, replacing the NVIDIA proprietary license.** `LICENSE.txt` is the Apache License 2.0 text, and the crate manifests declare `license = "Apache-2.0"`. Every source file carries the SPDX `Apache-2.0` license header, except fred's own files in the vendored Valkey client (see the fred entry below). In the API archive (`live-edit-api-<version>.zip`), `OSS_LICENSE.txt` is now the Apache License 2.0 text instead of a copy of the NVIDIA Software License Agreement. No service behavior, API, configuration, or default changes, and the third-party notices in `ATTRIBUTIONS.md` are unchanged apart from fred's entry (see the fred entry below).
- **`LICENSE.txt` and the API archive's `OSS_LICENSE.txt` are now the unmodified Apache License 2.0 text, and NVIDIA's copyright attribution is in a new `NOTICE` file.**
  `NOTICE` ships with every copy of the license: in the service image's `/licenses/`, at the root of the API archive and in its `native-codec/` directory, and in the conformance-test image's `/usr/share/doc/live-edit-conformance-tests/`.
- **The NVIDIA-written files in the vendored Valkey client, fred, now carry the Apache-2.0 license header, and the copy records which files are upstream's and how each part is licensed.**
  Fred's own files, and NVIDIA's edits to them, stay under the MIT License; NVIDIA's added files under `vendor/fred-10.1.0/src/live_edit/` are under the Apache License 2.0. `vendor/fred-10.1.0.SOURCE.md` records this split, and `ATTRIBUTIONS.md`'s fred entry states it. The new `vendor/fred-10.1.0.SHA256SUMS` lists every file of the published fred 10.1.0 crate with its SHA-256 checksum.
- **Markdown and data files now carry license information.**
  Each published file carries the NVIDIA Apache-2.0 header, keeps a recorded upstream notice, or is classified in the new `tools/license-classification.json`. Markdown files carry the header in an HTML comment at the top, which renders as nothing. The classification records each data file's owner, license and origin.
- **The OODF schema file, `oodf-ffi/schema/OmniObjectDelta.fbs`, now carries the Apache-2.0 license header in place of its original NVIDIA notice; the schema itself is unchanged.**
  `oodf-ffi/schema/SOURCE.md` records the original file's hash, the new file's hash and the hash of the unchanged schema body.
- **Live subscriptions no longer hold a Valkey connection each. `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` now sets the number of shared reader connections; subscriptions are capped per replica by a ceiling that follows the pod's memory limit (4096 at the chart's `5Gi`), catch-ups by the new `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_MAX_CONCURRENT_CATCHUPS` (default 8), and every wait for capacity by one budget, `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_ADMISSION_WAIT_MS` (default now 20000).**
  A fixed set of reader connections (`LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE`, Helm `valkey.subscriberPoolSize`) now reads live updates for every `SubscribeReadLayer` subscription on a replica, one blocking read per reader, and hands each update to every subscriber of that layer.
  The backing store serves one read per layer instead of one per subscriber, and the replica's connection count no longer grows with its subscribers.
  - **Action for operators: the subscription ceiling is now memory-based.** It was `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` (Helm default 256). An established subscription now holds no connection, only replica memory, so the ceiling is derived from the pod's memory limit: 4096 at the chart's new `5Gi` limit (see the Helm defaults entry below). It holds with the catch-up memory budget and the structural-edit cache full, and with the uploads and the backing-store connections' buffers counted. It has no setting and no "unlimited" (see the pod-memory entry above; `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Subscriber capacity). It counts subscriptions, not users. Watch the new `live_edit_subscriber_admitted` against `live_edit_subscriber_max_subscriptions`.
  - **Catch-ups are bounded separately.** A catch-up — reading the layer's state and building the full state or diff — is where joining costs CPU and memory (about 13 MiB measured for a flat 2,150-section test layer, 2,150 sections under the root with one 16-byte field each and short section ids; about 18 MiB derived for the Kitchen_set reference scene's live layer, 1,241 prims, with the random 64-bit section ids clients use; at most about 24 MiB for any layer within the atomic-read limits, and about 0.16 CPU-seconds when the layer is over the atomic-read limits; much less within them, see the catch-up entry below). `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_MAX_CONCURRENT_CATCHUPS` (Helm `valkey.subscriberMaxConcurrentCatchups`, default 8; `0` is refused at startup) caps how many read and build at once per replica. **The default is sized for the chart's 2-CPU limit (4 per CPU), so live delivery to existing viewers does not stall for seconds during a large join burst:** a catch-up build is CPU-bound, so on 2 CPUs more slots would only make every catch-up slower and take CPU from edits and live delivery, without catching a burst up any faster. If you raise `resources.limits.cpu`, raise this with it. A catch-up holds its slot only while it reads and builds, never while it sends, so a slow client cannot hold one, and a subscriber that is already up to date takes none. The re-bootstrap after a layer is overwritten counts as a catch-up. Past the limit a catch-up **waits its turn** with its stream open and nothing delivered yet. This keeps a reconnect storm from pushing a replica into an out-of-memory kill, which would drop every subscription on it at once. If your largest layers are much bigger than that test layer, size the limit against your pod memory limit.
  - **One capacity budget per subscribe.** `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_ADMISSION_WAIT_MS` now bounds, together, every wait for capacity a subscribe makes: admission past the ceiling, on a Valkey Cluster a free stream reader, each catch-up slot, and each snapshot read permit (see the catch-up entry below). Only waiting is charged, and a waiting subscription buffers nothing. When it runs out the subscribe is refused with a retryable `UNAVAILABLE`: an admission refusal before the stream opens, a reader or catch-up-slot refusal in the stream before any content (new stream-exit reasons `reader_capacity` and `catchup_capacity`). The one refusal that can follow content is a layer overwritten under an already caught-up subscription, whose re-bootstrap starts a fresh budget. `0` still means never wait. **A subscribe at a full replica now waits up to 20 s** before it is refused (the default rose from 2000 to 20000, derived from the inspected clients' retry windows); a shorter budget refuses sooner, so a retry may land on a less-loaded replica.
  - **Valkey Cluster (Amazon MemoryDB, Azure Managed Redis):** a reader serves one hash slot — in practice one layer — at a time, so `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` is also the number of distinct layers a replica can serve at once. A subscribe to one more layer waits within its capacity budget and is then ended with `UNAVAILABLE` (`reason="reader_capacity"`) before any content, counted on the new `live_edit_stream_mux_bind_refusals_total`. Keep the Helm default of 256 on a cluster unless you know your replicas' peak distinct-layer count.
  - **Non-clustered Valkey:** the reader count sets read parallelism only; a small value serves any number of subscriptions and reduces the replica's connections.
  - `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_ADMISSION_MAX_WAITERS` now defaults to the subscription ceiling instead of the pool size. `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_MAX_CONCURRENT_CATCHUPS` values above the largest supported limit are rejected at startup.
  - **Rollback mode (`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false`):** an in-window reconnect's per-commit replay now computes every commit's diff before sending any, so it never waits for capacity after content; the per-commit diffs are unchanged, but a layer overwritten while the replay runs no longer sends the old layer's commits ahead of the new layer's full state.
  - **Metric meanings changed — revisit dashboards and alerts.** `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use{pool="subscriber_pool"}` now counts reader connections that are reading at least one layer, not subscriptions (use `live_edit_subscriber_admitted` for the subscription count). `live_edit_valkey_pool_waiters{pool="subscriber_pool"}` counts calls waiting in the admission queue, which happens only at the ceiling; joins queued for a catch-up slot are on the new `live_edit_subscribe_catchups_waiting`. `live_edit_valkey_pool_checkout_seconds` is no longer recorded for `subscriber_pool`. On `live_edit_valkey_consumer_forced_reconnects_total` the `phase="checkout_probe"` series is gone and the phases are now `xread`, `wake_ack`, `probe`, `keepalive` and `permanent`; on `live_edit_valkey_consumer_permanent_exits_total` they are `xread` and `probe`, and it counts one per reader give-up rather than one per subscription. `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total` gains `reader_capacity`, `catchup_capacity` and `stream_gap`. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics.
  - **A silently dead reader connection affects every subscription on that reader, and is now looked for.** A subscribe gets a check of its reader's connection unless that connection answered in the last 3 s; a connection that fails it is reconnected, at a limited rate while it keeps failing. Idle reader connections are kept alive with a `PING` every 200 s, below Azure Managed Redis's 10-minute idle timeout. A busy reader that no subscription joins is still caught by its 35-second read timeout. A reader whose failure ended every subscription on it now rebuilds its connection and backs off, so re-subscribes do not loop onto the same broken connection. `live_edit_valkey_consumer_forced_reconnects_total{phase}` counts every rebuild.
  - **Slow subscribers:** a subscriber that stops reading is torn down (`UNAVAILABLE`, `reason="inner_watchdog"`) once, after it has started reading, its backlog has stayed at 4,096 undelivered updates or 8 MiB for 5 seconds and another update arrives for it, or at once when the next update would take it past 16,384 updates or 32 MiB (for a client that has stopped reading entirely, that ending is recorded as `reason="slow_reader_deadline"` up to 5 seconds later; `live_edit_stream_mux_overflow_kills_total` counts every one). An empty backlog always accepts one update, so a single oversized edit never ends a caught-up subscriber. The byte limits are new; Kit-sized traffic still reaches the update-count limits first. A catch-up waiting for capacity is never torn down this way.
  - **A subscription whose position the layer's update stream has trimmed away now ends `UNAVAILABLE` (`layer stream was trimmed past this subscription's position; reconnect to catch up`, `reason="stream_gap"`) instead of silently skipping the trimmed edits;** its re-subscribe catches it up in full. A trim the subscription's catch-up already covered never ends it. Subscribers whose layer stream was removed and recreated now reconnect with a full catch-up instead of skipping the missed edits.
  - A server-side subscription ending with no recorded reason now reaches the client as a retryable `UNAVAILABLE` (`layer stream reader ended without a recorded reason; reconnect`) rather than a normal end of stream.
  - Graceful shutdown still ends every subscription `UNAVAILABLE` (`server shutting down`) and closes its Valkey connections without waiting out their read timeouts; the step that released subscription reads still blocked on Valkey is now done by the shared readers themselves. A subscribe waiting for capacity ends the same way as any other stream: `UNAVAILABLE` on shutdown, `NOT_FOUND` for a deleted session, the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` for a session missing from Valkey.

  No wire or proto change. See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Subscriber capacity and `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Connections.
- **An upload over `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` now fails with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` instead of `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`.**
  The Kit client retries `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, so an oversized `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` was re-sent up to 10 more times, each re-uploading and being re-buffered up to the 100 MB limit before the same refusal. `INVALID_ARGUMENT` is not retried, so it now fails once. The message is unchanged (`layer content exceeds maximum size of N bytes`).
  **Action for client code** that recognised this refusal by its code: match `INVALID_ARGUMENT` instead. The service's own handlers no longer return `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` at all; one a client still sees comes from the HTTP/2 transport (`docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Transport errors). The proto comments on `CreateLayer` and `UpdateLayer` say so.
- **A cached authorization or identity result now ends when the bearer token expires.**
  A successful permission check or userinfo lookup is cached for `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_TTL_SECS` (600 s by default). For a JWT bearer token it now also ends at the token's own `exp`, whichever comes first. Previously a result cached shortly before a token expired kept being served for up to the full 600 s afterwards, so the client was never told to refresh its token; now, with authorization on, the first call after expiry is checked with the permissions service again and is answered `UNAUTHENTICATED`. Opaque (non-JWT) tokens keep the configured TTL. An entry that ends this way counts as a cache miss on `live_edit_auth_cache_misses_total`. No configuration change.
- **Structural edits and external-value clean-up now read a layer's state on the snapshot lane, so their read failures move to `pool="snapshot"`.** (Under the default `lua` backend a structural edit reads a layer's whole state only to load the structural-edit cache; see the structural-edit entry above.) A command timeout in either read counts on `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{pool="snapshot", kind="command_timeout"}` instead of `pool="data"`, and reads in flight show on `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use{pool="snapshot"}`. Catch-ups and backups moved to the lane too, at every layer size (see the entries below). Update dashboards or alerts that read a structural-edit or catch-up stall from the `data` cells.
  **Which consumers a foreign value on a child-ordering key stops now depends on the key**, on most layers (those created, re-uploaded or rebuilt from a backup by this release or later, which keep a complete index of their child-ordering lists): a foreign value on that index stops structural edits that read the layer's state (under the default `lua` backend, only those that load the layer into the structural-edit cache; a cached edit's commit refuses it on `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total` when it writes a child-ordering list), external-value clean-up, catch-ups and backups, at every layer size, counted on `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total{key_family="children_index"}` rather than on `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total`; a foreign value on an ordering under a live section or the root that the index does not list stops nothing (no edit, catch-up or backup reads it), and an edit that rewrites that ordering replaces it. The error log line's remediation text says which consumers stop for the key it names. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?
- **Backups of a layer within both atomic-read limits are now proven complete in one read, so a continuously-edited layer of that size gets a full backup every time it is due.**
  A backup used to be written only on a pass where no edit landed during its multi-round-trip read, so a continuously-edited layer could go pass after pass without a full backup and relied on incremental backups for its recovery point. A layer within `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_ITEMS` and `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_BYTES` is now read for its backup in one server-side read on the snapshot lane, which is complete by construction however hot the layer is. A layer over either limit is backed up through a fenced read (next entry).
  - **What moves on your dashboards, for hot layers within the limits:** `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="success", kind="layer"}` ticks on every due pass; the `captured`, `rebased` and `refused_chain_full` cells of `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total` fall to zero; and their restores resolve on `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_lineage_total{outcome="gathered_base"}` instead of `gathered_composed`. `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="checkpoint"}` moves on `path="atomic"`, and its `too_large` cell counts the backups of layers over the limits.
  - **`live_edit_checkpoint_stable_cut_total{outcome="unverified"}` now means only that the layer's edit counter went backwards between two reads**, at every layer size (with the next entry), which only a disaster-recovery restore with too small a guard band causes, so any increase is worth an alert; the backup loop's warning names the layer and both sequence numbers.
  - **Cost:** those layers now write full backups where they used to write incremental ones, so writes to the backup store grow in bytes and retention turns full backups over faster. Each due layer's backup holds its Valkey shard for one read (up to about 38–39 ms at the default limits on Azure Managed Redis); the loop runs on one replica and backs layers up one at a time, so at most one such read runs at once across the fleet, a load the default limits are already sized for.
  - **A layer whose index of sections names a section with no record is now refused** instead of backed up without that section, at any layer size (with the next entry): `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="error", kind="layer"}` ticks on every pass until the index is repaired, with a warning naming the layer and up to eight of the bad index entries. Only something writing to the keyspace outside the service produces that state.

  No configuration change. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Validate backups and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current?
- **Backups of a layer over the atomic-read limits are now proven complete too, read under a fence, so a continuously-edited layer of any size gets a full backup every time it is due.**
  A backup of such a layer used to need a pass on which no edit landed during its multi-round-trip read; on a large, continuously-edited layer that rarely happened, so its full backup aged until the editing stopped and incremental backups carried its recovery point. It is now read through a **fenced read** of its own, like a catch-up of such a layer but not shared with catch-ups: the read holds off that layer's edits while it reads (edits wait, see the `UpdateLayer` entry under Added) and is proven when it gives the fence up. Nothing unproven is ever backed up.
  - **What it costs: a pause for the layer's editors on every backup of it.** Every pass on which such a layer is due — about once per `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS` while it is being edited — pauses each edit that meets its fence for whole polling steps of about 1.1 s, as many as the fenced read lasts (plus, for a structural edit, the attempt it lost), whether or not anyone is joining it. Edits wait rather than fail, without using their retry budget, and give up with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` only past `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` of total waiting. `live_edit_update_layer_fence_wait_seconds` now has observations at backup cadence on such layers, beside `live_edit_snapshot_fence_hold_seconds{consumer="checkpoint"}`. A large layer's backup also takes longer, so it lengthens the backup loop's pass; the loop renews its leader lease while the read runs, so no layer is skipped for its size, and a lease lost during the read stops it, writes nothing, and ends the pass like any other lease loss.
  - **Rolling deploys do not leave a large layer's editors paused.** A replica that shuts down stops its fenced reads at once, a backup's or a catch-up's, and waits up to 2 s for their fences to be given back before it closes its Valkey connections, so the editors of that layer on other replicas are not left waiting until the fence's lease runs out. A backup stopped this way writes nothing and is not counted as an error; the layer stays due for the next pass.
  - **When the read gives up.** A backup's fenced read waits for its fence for at most a time sized to the layer (about 29 s just over the limits) and retries a broken read up to three times; past either, that layer's backup counts as an error for that pass (`live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="error", kind="layer"}`, warning `layer checkpoint assembly failed`) and the next pass reads it afresh. A wait that would pass its cap also logs `fenced snapshot read gave up waiting for the snapshot fence`.
  - **Incremental backups are now a fallback only for a pass skipped after a sequence regression**, and there they succeed only once the full backup the disaster-recovery restore writes for itself has landed; until then the capture is refused (`refused_seq_gap`) and the next due pass's full backup covers the layer. With every pass writing a full backup at any size, `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total` reads at or near zero on a healthy fleet, and restores resolve on `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_lineage_total{outcome="gathered_base"}`. A fenced read's broken attempts count on `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="checkpoint", path="fenced", outcome="fence_broken"}`; the backup retries counter is removed (see Removed).
  - **A dropped automatic full backup after a restore now heals on its own.** A layer whose post-restore full backup was shed under load gets an ordinary full backup at its next due pass at any layer size, which also clears the incremental-capture refusal (`refused_seq_gap`); re-restoring the affected layers is now only the way to close that gap sooner. The shed alert recipe stays, warn-level.
  - **What moves on your dashboards:** `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="checkpoint", path="fenced"}` (five outcome cells, present at zero from the first scrape, one tick per attempt), and `consumer="checkpoint"` observations on `live_edit_snapshot_read_duration_seconds`, `live_edit_snapshot_fence_hold_seconds` and `live_edit_snapshot_fence_reader_wait_seconds`; `live_edit_snapshot_fence_acquire_total` counts backups' fence requests too. A backup's read failures and command timeouts count on `pool="snapshot"` (its fence requests and renewals on `pool="data"`).

  No configuration change, no wire or proto change. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Validate backups, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? and § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **A subscriber's catch-up of a layer within both atomic-read limits is now an exact copy of the layer at one version, and its live updates start right after it, so no edit is delivered twice however continuously the layer is edited.**
  A catch-up used to read the layer's state in several round trips and prove afterwards that no edit had landed meanwhile; on a continuously-edited layer that proof failed and the subscriber could receive an edit both in its catch-up and live, which for a structural edit (creating a prim, then moving an older prim under it) could lose that prim on the client. A catch-up of a layer within `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_ITEMS` and `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_BYTES` now reads it in one server-side read on the snapshot lane, in every catch-up kind and in both catch-up modes (the per-commit replay of `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false` reads each replayed update that changed the layer that way; see below). The catch-up's acknowledgement and content carry that read's version and timestamps. A catch-up of a larger layer is exact too, through a fenced read (next entry).
  - **New limit, new refusal cause.** Each such read holds its Valkey shard while it runs, so a replica runs at most `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_READ_CONCURRENCY` of them at once (new, default 1; see Added). A catch-up waits for its turn inside its catch-up slot, charged to the same capacity budget; when the budget runs out it ends `UNAVAILABLE` (`reason="catchup_capacity"`, `this replica is at its concurrent catch-up snapshot-read limit (LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_READ_CONCURRENCY); retry`) before any content, which clients retry. At the default a replica's catch-ups take these reads one at a time, so a large join burst on one replica also queues here. A layer over the limits takes its turn too, because the same read checks its size, before it joins its layer's shared fenced read; a catch-up that finds that shared read already under way on its replica does not.
  - **Live updates take no read at all, an empty edit included; rollback-mode replay takes the same read as a catch-up.** A caught-up subscription never reads the layer for its live updates (only a re-bootstrap after the layer is overwritten does, as a full catch-up): each live update is relayed as the writer sent it, and an empty edit (an update with no content) is sent as an empty update at its place in the sequence, built with no read, at every layer size. It is still sent, because clients apply updates strictly in sequence, and it is counted on `live_subscriber_updates_total` as before; what a subscriber receives for it is unchanged. Rebuilding such an update from the layer's state is gone, and with it that read's cost per subscriber; `live_diff_case_total{case="incremental"}` and `live_diff_build_duration_seconds` no longer count empty edits, live or replayed. With `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false`, a reconnect's replay of retained updates reads each update that changed the layer atomically under its own permit (an empty edit takes no read), inside its catch-up slot and before any content, so under a join burst a long replay can now run out its capacity budget and end `catchup_capacity` (retried). On a layer over the limits the replay stops at the first update it reads (or before it, when the layer's shared fenced read is already under way on the replica) and the reconnect is served one combined diff through that shared read instead of one diff per update, the way a reconnect outside the retained window is served; `live_subscribe_regime_total` still counts it `in_window`, and `live_subscribe_stable_cut_total` counts it under `regime="out_of_window"`. `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="catchup_legacy_per_commit"}` and `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_read_wait_seconds{consumer="catchup_legacy_per_commit"}` move only for that replay.
  - **A catch-up that finds the layer's sequence number gone backwards now ends `UNAVAILABLE`** (`the layer's sequence moved backwards while this subscription caught up (the layer was restored); retry`, `reason="error"`) before any content, instead of being served from the version it first saw. Only a disaster-recovery restore with too small a guard band causes it; the client's retry catches up from the restored layer.
  - **What moves on your dashboards:** `live_subscribe_stable_cut_total{outcome="unverified"}` now means only that sequence regression, at every layer size (in a rollback-mode replay too, see Fixed), so any increase is worth an alert. All its cells are now present at zero from the first scrape. The catch-up retries counter is removed (see Removed). `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="catchup"}` moves on `path="atomic"`, and its `too_large` cell counts the catch-ups of layers over the limits that made an atomic attempt. A catch-up's read failures and command timeouts count on `pool="snapshot"` instead of `pool="data"`. On a layer that keeps a complete index of its child-ordering lists, a foreign value on that index now also stops catch-ups, at every layer size (see the snapshot-lane entry above).
  - **Configuration.** `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_POOL_SIZE`'s default is derived from `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_READ_CONCURRENCY` and `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY` (their sum + 4, 6 at the defaults), and the Helm value `valkey.snapshotPoolSize` is unset by default so that derivation applies. **If you set `valkey.snapshotPoolSize`**, your value does not follow the read concurrencies: keep it at `valkey.catchupSnapshotReadConcurrency + valkey.catchupFencedReadConcurrency + 4` or above. An explicit `0` is rendered and rejected at startup.
  - **Measured** against a local Valkey on a 2,150-section layer: every brand-new catch-up was exact at 25 and at 250 edits/s across two replicas (before, 42 of 344 and 0 of 344 were), and in 400-join bursts on a replica limited to the chart's 2 CPUs with 275 edits/s on it (before, none were), with no refusal where 114–119 of 400 joins used to be refused and retried; catch-up content is byte-identical to before. A replica catches up about 28–29 joiners per second, paced by the read permit (an estimated 19–22 on Azure Managed Redis), so a 400-join burst on one replica is caught up in about 14 s. **Edits slow slightly during small join bursts:** each atomic read holds its shard, so edits sent while 1–10 joiners catch up showed a p99 of about 10–50 ms, against 3–30 ms before, and somewhat more on Azure Managed Redis; this is within the edit delay the read limits are sized for. During bursts of 25–50 joiners edits are faster than before (p99 26–54 ms against 92–384 ms). Heavy structural editing of another large layer on the same replica slows catch-ups (about 11 joiners per second under 16 writers restructuring non-stop).

  No wire or proto change. See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Subscriber capacity, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics.
- **`live_edit_update_layer_retries_total` and `live_edit_update_layer_attempts_histogram` gain a `path` label — revisit dashboards and alerts that read them unaggregated.**
  `path` is the commit path the `UpdateLayer` call took: `fast` (field and time-sample edits on the `lua` and `lua-watch-structural` backends); `structural`, `structural_full` or `reset` (structural edits and resets on the default `lua` backend's structural-edit cache; see the structural-edit entry above); or `watch` (the WATCH/MULTI/EXEC path: structural edits and resets under `lua-watch-structural`, the ones the cache cannot plan under `lua`, and every edit under `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=txn`). A query such as `rate(live_edit_update_layer_retries_total{outcome="exhausted"}[5m])` now returns one series per path, and `histogram_quantile` over `live_edit_update_layer_attempts_histogram_bucket` without `sum by (le)` now returns one quantile per path: wrap them in `sum(...)`, `sum by (outcome)` or `sum by (le)` for the totals they used to give. The retry counter is present at zero from the first scrape on all ten `path` × `outcome` cells. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
- **The `backend="lua", site="update"` cell of `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total` and of `live_edit_stream_id_collision_total` gains a `mode` label — revisit dashboards and alerts that read that cell unaggregated.**
  `mode` is the mode of the edit commit script that refused the write: `fast` (field and time-sample edits) or `structural` (structural edits and resets on the default `lua` backend's structural-edit cache). Only that cell carries `mode`; the `backend="txn"` cells keep exactly their `backend` and `site` labels. Each family now has four series present at zero from the first scrape, up from three. Queries that sum, such as `sum by (backend, site) (rate(...))` or `sum(increase(...)) > 0`, are unchanged; a selector such as `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total{backend="lua", site="update"}` without `sum` now returns one series per `mode`. The refusal's `ERROR` (wrong-kind key) or `WARN` (stream-ID collision) log line on `backend="lua"` gains a `mode` field with the same value. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?
- **`UpdateLayer` commit log lines, and a failed `UpdateLayer`'s log line, carry new fields; a commit whose call was cut off after it landed is now counted.**
  Every commit log line (`UpdateLayer committed`, `UpdateLayer committed (lua)`, `UpdateLayer committed (structural)`, and the already-landed, unwatched-commit and lost-reply lines) now carries `attempt`, `path`, one count per conflict kind (`watch_conflict`, `watch_read_moved`, `seq_fence`, `cache_diverged`, `snapshot_fence_wait`), `records` and `bytes`; a failed `UpdateLayer` that reached the backing store carries the same fields on its one `RPC failed` / `internal error` line. `records` are counted differently on each path, so compare them within one path; `bytes` compare across `fast` and the structural-edit paths, but not against `watch`.
  A commit that landed while its call was then cut off by the deadline or by the client was counted on neither `live_edit_update_layer_attempts_histogram` nor `live_edit_update_layer_retries_total{outcome="committed"}`, and logged nowhere; it is now counted and logged. Such a call is an `outcome="error"` sample on `live_edit_update_layer_commit_duration_seconds`, so that histogram's `outcome="ok"` count can read below the attempts histogram's count. It can also read above it: an `UpdateLayer` carrying an `operation_id` that is answered `ALREADY_COMMITTED` from an earlier call's commit is an `outcome="ok"` sample but made no commit, so it is not on the attempts histogram. Count commits with the attempts histogram. A commit that lands while its call is cut off before the service sees it land is still counted nowhere.
  **When you compare commit counts with the log lines**, measure the counter per pod from its last sample at or before the window's start; a difference taken between the first and last samples inside the window undercounts. No wire or proto change.
- **A subscriber's catch-up of a layer over the atomic-read limits is now exact too, read once per layer per replica under a fence, so no edit is delivered twice at any layer size.**
  A catch-up of such a layer used to read it in several round trips and prove afterwards that no edit had landed meanwhile; on a continuously-edited layer that proof failed and the subscriber could receive an edit twice, which for a structural edit could lose a prim on the client. It now joins its layer's **shared fenced read**: one read per layer per replica, which holds off that layer's edits while it reads (edits wait, see the `UpdateLayer` entry under Added) and is proven when it gives the fence up. Every catch-up of that layer on the replica that arrives while the read is waiting to start or holding the fence shares it, and each builds its own full state or diff from the shared copy, in every catch-up kind and in both catch-up modes. The acknowledgement and content carry that read's version and timestamps, and a catch-up that finds the layer's sequence number gone backwards ends `UNAVAILABLE` before any content, as within the limits.
  - **Capacity.** The shared read is one unit of catch-up work: it holds one catch-up slot from its read until the last of its catch-ups has built, the fenced read permit (`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_FENCED_READ_CONCURRENCY`) while it reads, and one reservation from the catch-up memory budget; the catch-ups sharing it hold none of their own and are charged, against their own capacity budget, for the time the shared read waits for those. When a catch-up's budget runs out there it alone ends `UNAVAILABLE` (`reason="catchup_capacity"`, with a message naming the three limits) before any content.
  - **New ending, `reason="fenced_read_wait_cap"`.** A catch-up whose shared read takes longer than a limit sized to the layer (about 29 s just over the limits) ends `UNAVAILABLE` (`… did not complete within the time a catch-up waits for one (reader_wait_max); retry`) before any content, which clients retry. Expected zero; any increase means some layer's fence was held off far longer than it should be. The new `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total` cell is present at zero from the first scrape.
  - **What it costs.** Builds are not shared: when no extra slot and memory are free, joiners of one large layer on one replica build one after another, so the N-th waits about N × one build, and only the client's own timeout bounds that wait. Where extra builds do fit, as on the smaller layers measured on a CPU-bound 2-CPU replica, every joiner of a burst was caught up within about 40–60 ms of the last, so early joiners wait for the end of the burst's builds rather than for their own place in the queue; the cause is inferred and under investigation. Each large layer being joined holds one catch-up slot on that replica, so 8 (the default slot count) at once make every other catch-up there wait for a slot. The joins per second a replica serves for a given layer size follow from the measured build time: about 0.09–0.14 µs per item on layers shaped like the Kitchen_set reference scene, and about 0.8 µs per item on layers whose values are mostly inline.
  - **What moves on your dashboards:** `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_flight_total{role}`, `live_edit_catchup_builds_in_progress`, `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_reads_in_flight{class="fenced"}`, the catch-up memory budget's series and `live_edit_catchup_snapshot_read_wait_seconds{class="fenced"}` now move. `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="catchup", path="fenced"}` and `live_edit_snapshot_fence_acquire_total{result}` (`started_epoch`, `joined_epoch`, `retry_after_epoch`, `retry_after_gap`, `retry_after_intent`) are present at zero from the first scrape and count once per attempt or fence answer of a shared read, however many catch-ups share it. Two histograms, present from their first observation, report the shared read too: `live_edit_snapshot_fence_hold_seconds{consumer}` and `live_edit_snapshot_fence_reader_wait_seconds{consumer}`, the latter one observation per read attempt (its wait for the fence); a catch-up that ends at the wait cap is counted on the stream-exit reason above, not there. `live_subscribe_stable_cut_total{outcome="verified"}` counts each catch-up that shares a read. A catch-up that finds its layer's shared read already under way makes no atomic attempt, so it ticks no `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{consumer="catchup", outcome="too_large"}`.
  - **A foreign value on a layer's fence or writer-intent key** (`le:{oid}:obj:snapfence`, `le:{oid}:obj:snapintent`) can now also stop catch-ups of that layer when it is over the limits: a foreign value on the fence key stops every such catch-up, since each fence request reads it; one on the writer-intent key stops those whose fence request comes just after an earlier fenced read's gap, the only time a fence request reads it. A foreign value on a child-ordering list the layer's index does not list no longer stops catch-ups at any size.

  No configuration change, no wire or proto change. See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Subscriber capacity, `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Status codes, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics and § Is live delivery keeping up?
- **The log text for a token the userinfo service rejects changed** from `invalid or expired token` to `userinfo service rejected the bearer (HTTP 401)` / `(HTTP 403)`, or `(UNAUTHENTICATED)` with `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL=grpc`, because such a rejection is not proof the token is invalid or expired. Update any log search on the old text. The client still sees `INTERNAL` with `internal error`.
- **`CreateSession`, `CreateLayer` and `JoinSession` now succeed, with authorization on, for a caller the userinfo service has no user for; app identities are labelled as apps.**
  With `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=true`, a caller the permissions service had allowed but the userinfo service had no user for (a service account, automation or app identity) failed these three calls with `INTERNAL`, which the Kit client retries, and every attempt logged an `ERROR`.
  - **With authorization on**, the userinfo service's "no such user" answer (HTTP `404` with its `not_found` error body, or gRPC `NOT_FOUND`) now names the caller from its token's claims, as `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` does, and the call proceeds. So any principal the permissions service allows on a base path can create sessions and layers and join there, whatever the userinfo directory holds; the directory never decided access, and still does not. A `404` without that body still fails `INTERNAL`, as an endpoint misconfiguration. Other userinfo failures are unchanged.
  - **With authorization off**, that answer still fails the call `INTERNAL`; the logged detail is now `auth backend: userinfo service has no user for the caller` (was `auth backend: auth service returned an invalid response: auth backend returned HTTP 404`, or, with `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL=grpc`, `auth backend: auth service returned an invalid response: auth backend returned Some requested entity was not found`). Update any log search on the old text.
  - **`live_edit_auth_errors_total` no longer counts that answer**, in either mode: a userinfo `404` with the `not_found` body, or gRPC `NOT_FOUND`, stops ticking `{backend="userinfo", code="invalid_response"}`.
  - **App label, in both modes.** A token with no `name` or `preferred_username` claim that is app-only (`idtyp` is `app`, or `oid` equals `sub`) is now shown as `<app name> (app)`: its `app_displayname` claim with surrounding whitespace trimmed, else its client ID (`azp`, else `appid`), else its `oid`; an app-only token with none of these claims keeps its `sub`. `app_displayname` is not used when it is missing, when it contains a control character (C0, DEL or C1), a Unicode format character (category Cf, which includes the bidirectional-text controls U+200E/U+200F, U+202A–U+202E and U+2066–U+2069) or a line or paragraph separator (U+2028/U+2029), so that no character in the name can split, hide or reorder the ` (app)` marker, or when, after trimming, it is empty or longer than 64 characters (Unicode scalar values). It was shown by its bare `sub` (or `oid`). The ` (app)` marker keeps the name an app's owner chose from passing for a person's. Other tokens keep their label. Names stored before the upgrade (session and layer creators) are not rewritten.
  No configuration change, no wire or proto change. See `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Caller identity and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are credentials and auth services healthy?
- **Documentation only: the Amazon MemoryDB deployment guide is corrected against a real MemoryDB for Valkey 7.3 cluster.**
  No behavior or configuration change.
  - The default parameter group `default.memorydb-valkey7` already sets `noeviction`; a custom parameter group is now optional, needed only to tune other parameters.
  - The custom-group command no longer sets `reserved-memory-percent`, which MemoryDB does not have (it is an ElastiCache parameter), so the command as previously written would fail. Sizing is now against each node type's published `maxmemory`, with the `DatabaseMemoryUsagePercentage` metric as the alert signal.
  - The eviction policy can be checked from a client with `INFO memory`; only `CONFIG` is refused.
  - The prerequisites now state that the cluster's security group must admit TCP 6379 from the service's pods, with an example rule.

  See `docs/service/docs/backing-store-aws.md` § 1. Check the eviction policy and § 4. Create the cluster.
- **`SubscribeReadLayer` now rejects an invalid layer name immediately with `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, using the same rules as every other layer operation.**
  Previously it rejected only an empty name; any other name that no layer can have (over 255 bytes, control characters, `..`, a `\\` or `//` prefix, a Windows drive prefix such as `C:`, or whitespace only) opened the stream and then ended it `NOT_FOUND`.
  It now fails before the stream opens, and a blank name reads `layer name must not be blank` (previously `layer_name must not be empty`), the same message the other layer operations return.
  No existing layer is affected, because no other operation can create a layer with such a name. The Kit client retries neither `INVALID_ARGUMENT` nor `NOT_FOUND`, so its retry behaviour is unchanged.
- **`live_subscribe_catch_up_duration_seconds` and `live_diff_build_duration_seconds` gain buckets at 2.5, 10, 30 and 60 seconds.**
  The top finite bucket was 5 s, so the slow catch-ups seen under load (around 8 s) all fell into `+Inf`. Every existing bucket boundary is kept, so dashboards and `histogram_quantile` queries keep working and gain resolution; each series has four more buckets.
- **Helm defaults: the autoscaler no longer scales on memory, the server's memory limit is `5Gi` (was `1Gi`), and its memory request is 70 % of the limit (`3584Mi` at `5Gi`; was `256Mi`).**
  `autoscaling.targetMemoryUtilizationPercentage` now defaults to `null`, and the new default scaler mode has no memory signal at all (see the replica autoscaler entries). Load shows up as CPU; memory peaks from joins last well under a minute. A memory target at 80 % of a steady-state request also held idle replicas above the scale-down threshold.
  The request follows the limit when you change it, rounded down to whole MiB (`2867Mi` at `4Gi`, `5734Mi` at `8Gi`), so that nodes are not over-committed. A request you set is used as written, and the chart refuses to render one above the limit. At the `5Gi` limit, the structural-edit cache, overlapping joins of large scenes, the subscription ceiling, the uploads in progress and what the backing-store connections keep at rest all fit at their worst at the same moment, on every supported store topology. The service derives every one of these budgets from whatever limit the pod has (see the pod-memory entry above).
  **Action for operators:** each server pod now reserves `3584Mi` of node memory instead of `256Mi`. Before upgrading, check that your nodes, and any memory ResourceQuota on the namespace, have room for your replica count (3 to 10 under the default autoscaler) plus the extra pods of a rollout, or set `resources` yourself. `helm upgrade --reuse-values` from 0.2.0-rc.84 or any later release before this release's 70 % default keeps that release's `1Gi` request; to get the default, upgrade with your values files or `--reset-then-reuse-values`. A memory limit you set sizes the replica's budgets by itself (`docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size); below 2,709 MiB (`2709Mi`) at the chart's defaults, on any CPU count, the pod does not start. Deployments that run the chart's defaults get the `5Gi` limit on their next deploy.
  To keep the previous behaviour, set `autoscaling.mode: hpa` and `autoscaling.targetMemoryUtilizationPercentage: 80`, and size the memory request so idle replicas sit below it. Every server-pod container then needs a memory request.
  See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size.
- **`live_edit_valkey_errors_total` has a new `kind` value, `command_timeout`,** alongside the connection-error names that come from the Valkey client library (see `### Added`). A connect timeout is still reported under the library's `"Timeout Error"`, so the two can be told apart. An alert written as `rate(live_edit_valkey_errors_total[5m]) > 0` now also fires on command timeouts; add `kind!="command_timeout"` to keep it on connection errors only.
- **A reorder that names a prim another user just deleted or moved elsewhere, or names one prim twice, no longer fails the whole edit with `INVALID_ARGUMENT`.** The stale or repeated name is skipped and the rest of the reorder applies. See `docs/api/docs/validation.md` § Delta content.
- **Stream readers re-authenticate their own connections, between reads, under `accessTokenFile` and `iam`.** The Valkey client library's periodic credential refresh no longer runs on a stream-reader connection. Each reader re-presents its credential itself, on the same cadence and token-rotation rules as the service's other connections: when the refresh falls due during a read, the reader wakes the read and sends the re-authentication before its next read, so it never interrupts a live read or waits behind one. Nothing changes under `static`. What moves on dashboards and logs:
  - **`live_edit_valkey_consumer_unblocked_retries_total` now reads at or near zero in every auth mode.** In released builds it ticked only on **non-clustered** stores under `accessTokenFile` and `iam`, once per busy subscriber connection per credential refresh that sent a credential, and read zero on clustered stores (Azure Managed Redis, Amazon MemoryDB), where the refresh waited for the read instead (see the 0.1.0 entry and the correction under `### Fixed`); a non-clustered passwordless deployment sees that periodic rate stop. The readers' re-authentications are now counted on the new `live_edit_stream_mux_reauths_total` (see `### Added`). It now ticks only when something outside the service interrupts a reader's live read. (When a read times out on the service's side while the backing store still holds it, the reader's next command still interrupts that stale read first, but the reader had already given up on it: that costs a backchannel connection and is not counted.) **Action for operators:** the per-mode rate expectations and alert rules previously published for it no longer apply; replace them with the guidance in `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up?
  - **`live_edit_stream_mux_wakes_total` counts a fourth kind of wake-up, `credential`**: one per busy reader per re-authentication that falls due during a read, each costing one wake-up write and one extra read round trip. Its failure `WARN` (`stream multiplexer wake failed; …`) gains the `reason` value `credential`, and a credential wake-up whose read does not return in time counts on `live_edit_valkey_consumer_forced_reconnects_total{phase="wake_ack"}` like any other woken read, so every re-authentication of a busy reader also checks its connection.
  - The Valkey client library's `Failed to refresh credentials` warning now comes only from the service's other connections, never from a stream reader; a reader's failed `AUTH` logs its own `WARN` (see `### Added`).
  - One startup `INFO` line under a passwordless mode: `Periodic Valkey credential re-AUTH on the stream readers is sent by each reader between its reads, not by fred's refresh task`.

  No configuration change. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up?
- **The presence sweep, which removes session participants whose replica crashed, now does bounded work, re-proves its leadership as it goes, and is about 15 times faster.**
  Every `LIVE_EDIT_SWEEPER_INTERVAL_SECS` (default 15 s) the replica holding the sweep's leader lock removes participants whose lease has lapsed and tells the other participants they left. It used to read each session's whole participant list at once, check and remove each participant in separate requests, take its lock once without renewing it, and wait for every command as its own request.
  - **Bounded.** A sweep now reads the list of active sessions, and each session's participants, a page at a time, removes stale participants in batches of up to 100, keeps one request to the backing store in flight, and holds at most one page of sessions and one page of participants in memory. A sweep that takes longer than the interval delays the next one instead of running sweeps back to back.
  - **Correct per removal.** Each batch re-checks, in the same atomic step that removes a participant and announces its `PeerLeft`, that the participant's lease is still absent and that it is still in the session. A participant that renewed its lease meanwhile is left alone (previously one that renewed between the sweep's check and its removal was removed while still connected), and each removal is announced exactly once, even with two replicas sweeping at once.
  - **Fenced.** The sweeping replica re-proves before each batch that it still holds the lock, renewing it about every 10 s, and stops before its next batch if it cannot, with one `WARN` and a count on `live_edit_presence_sweep_lease_total{outcome="lost"}`. A sweep that stops early, or that a shutdown ends, is not resumed: the next sweep starts from the beginning. Previously a sweep longer than its 30 s lock could overlap another replica's.
  - **No session drops out of the sweep.** A session leaves the list of active sessions only once it has no participants, re-checked after the removal, so a participant that joins at that moment is never left out. After a rare combination of failures (a replica crashing mid-join while the session's last other participant leaves, say), the background clean-up that runs about every 5 minutes now puts the session back on the list, logging one `INFO` line, `presence index backstop: re-indexed a session …`, per session, and its stale participants are removed within about 5 minutes plus one sweep. The same line also follows ordinary disconnects: most occurrences are a session whose last participant left while the clean-up was scanning, which the next sweep takes off the list again, so they need no action. If that step fails, it logs one `WARN`, `presence index backstop failed (non-fatal) …`, counts on `live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total`, and retries on the next run.
  - **Measured** on a single-node Valkey about 0.1 ms per request from the service, with 300,000 participants in sessions of 200, 30,000 of them stale: a sweep takes about 2.2 s, against about 32 s before, with about 8.5 times less backing-store CPU, although it runs about 19 % more commands (most of them inside its batch scripts, so it sends 54 times fewer requests). At the default interval, stale participants were removed a median of about 7 s, and at most about 15 s, after their lease expired. A sweep waits for its requests one at a time, so on a backing store across a network it is longer by its request count times the round-trip time, and many small sessions are the slow case.
  - **During a rolling upgrade,** a sweep run by a replica on an older version still removes and announces in separate steps, so participants can briefly see another participant's events out of order.
  No configuration change; the new metrics are under `### Added`. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?
- **A `JoinSession` participant now receives the list of participants already in the session in pages, so joining a large session is faster, and a join ended mid-list stops promptly.**
  The joiner's list of the other participants is now read from the backing store 100 participants per round trip, instead of one participant per round trip; the commands sent are unchanged. Measured locally on joins into a session of 400 participants, `live_edit_join_latency_seconds{endpoint="JoinSession"}` p50 / p95 fell from 37.6 / 48.9 ms to 8.4 / 22.3 ms.
  While the list is being sent, a session deletion, a replica shutdown or the client going away now ends the join before its next read or send, instead of after the whole list, with the same endings as before (`OK` for a deleted session, `UNAVAILABLE` `server shutting down` for a shutdown).
  The list is read in one step taken just before the joiner registers, so a failure to read it now ends the join during setup, before `SessionConnected`, with `UNAVAILABLE` naming `SMEMBERS presence` (`INTERNAL` for a permanent error). A session deleted while a join is registering ends that join with `NOT_FOUND` as the stream's only item, before `SessionConnected`.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **A `JoinSession` participant that falls too far behind and misses another participant joining or leaving is now made to rejoin, instead of being left with a wrong participant list; `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY` is now applied exactly.**
  A connection more than `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY` (default 4096) presence events behind loses the oldest events it has not read, as before, counted on `live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total`. If they were only peer messages, the stream carries on, as before. If another participant's join or leave was among them, the client's participant list would stay wrong with nothing later to correct it; until now the stream carried on regardless. Now the service ends the stream with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` and the message `this connection fell too far behind and missed a participant joining or leaving; rejoin the session to restore the participant list`, logs one `WARN` `RPC failed` line and counts it on `live_edit_presence_resyncs_total`; the client's rejoin gets a fresh participant list.
  - **The other participants see that user leave and then join again, under a new connection id.**
  - **On the Kit client, the rejoining user's view can show participants who are still connected as having left the session,** from about 10 s after the rejoin; among them is every participant that rejoined in the same wave. The other participants' views stay correct: they see that user leave and join again, as above.
  - **What clients must do:** treat a mid-stream `UNAVAILABLE` on `JoinSession` as "rejoin". The Kit client library and the desktop application's clients we inspected already retry it.
  - **When to expect it:** a burst of more than the setting's worth of presence events in one session can make several slow connections rejoin together, a wave of reconnects. Each rejoin adds a leave and a join to that session's events, so in a session whose membership changes approach the setting the rejoins can push other slow connections past it and keep the wave going: **keep `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY` well above twice the participant count of your largest session**; it costs nothing while connections keep up. That rule has been measured with sessions of up to 100 participants, and connections that read too slowly under sustained message traffic can keep rejoining whatever the setting. A join into a busy session whose participant list is slow to arrive can be ended this way just after it succeeds.
  - **The setting is now applied exactly.** It was rounded up to the next power of two, so 5000 behaved as 8192; 5000 now means 5000, plus up to 7 events a connection has already taken from the queue but not yet sent. It now only bounds how far a connection may fall behind and reserves no memory (see the presence memory entry under `### Fixed`).
  No wire change.
- **Documentation only: the measured cost of a structural edit on a layer of heavier placed assets too large for the structural-edit cache.**
  No behavior or configuration change. On a layer just over the cache's per-layer limit at the default budget (about 6,050 placed assets of about 4.5 prims and 6 attributes per prim, with one deforming mesh), each structural edit reads the whole layer and took about 10 s, growing in proportion to the layer's stored keys (measured on a test host, not on a managed store); by extrapolation such an edit passes the default 30 s deadline at about 18,000 such assets (estimated). On a managed store such an edit is slower, and the deadline is reached at fewer assets: the read sends its roughly 3,200 windows one at a time, and each pays the store's network round trip; on a store with about a 1.4 ms round trip, a cost model of that read gives about 16 s per structural edit at about 6,050 such assets, and the deadline near 11,000 (estimated from the model, not measured). Value edits are unaffected. See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Limitations.
- **Upgrading from a release before 0.1.0: keep the smoke tests enabled for that one upgrade.** Smoke-test pods carry no `app.kubernetes.io/name` label, even when custom pod labels supply one, so they never match the server Deployment's selector; custom labels cannot override the server's selector labels or `app.kubernetes.io/component: server`. The upgrade deletes the previous smoke-test Job and its pods and runs the replacement with the new labels, but only with `tests.enabled: true` and Helm hooks enabled: changing values with the old chart, or turning the tests off, leaves the old Job's pods in place. `helm test` with the upgraded chart also replaces it. Keep the release name and naming overrides unchanged; the Deployment selector itself is unchanged, so the Service keeps routing to the server pods.

### Removed
- **Two retries counters are gone from `/metrics`: `live_subscribe_stable_cut_retries_total` and `live_edit_checkpoint_stable_cut_retries_total`.**
  They counted the extra passes of the multi-round-trip read that used to prove a catch-up's or a backup's copy of a layer after the fact. That read is removed: every catch-up and every backup now reads a layer once, in one atomic read within the atomic-read limits or through a fenced read over them (see the catch-up and backups entries under Changed), so nothing could move either counter.
  **Action for operators:** a dashboard panel or alert on either name now finds no series; remove it. A fenced read's broken attempts are on `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{path="fenced", outcome="fence_broken"}`, and `live_subscribe_stable_cut_total` and `live_edit_checkpoint_stable_cut_total` are unchanged.
- **Two log fields are gone:** `attempts` on the backup loop's warning `checkpoint loop could not prove a stable cut for this layer`, and `stable_cut_attempts` on `layer checkpoint assembled and persisted`. Every read is now one pass, so neither had anything left to report. Update any log query that reads them.
- **`OTLP metrics push exporter enabled` no longer carries `endpoint`**, which could hold credentials (b.zpx). Update any log query that reads it.

### Fixed
- **Deleting or replacing a large layer no longer reads the whole layer into memory, and these clean-ups now share a per-replica memory allowance (b.fsz).**
  To find the large values a deleted or replaced layer referenced, its clean-up read and copied the layer's whole state, and nothing limited how many clean-ups ran at once. On large layers each could hold tens of MB or more, outside every memory budget, so a burst of deletes could run a replica out of memory.
  - **What changes.** Each replica now runs these clean-ups a few at a time, within memory that grows with `resources.limits.memory`, so a burst of deletes can no longer run it out of memory. Each clean-up's share grows with its layer and its large values, so clean-ups of very small layers run many at a time. There is no new setting, no default changes, and the smallest memory limit that starts is unchanged.
  - **What clients see.** Clean-ups on one replica start in arrival order, so any `DeleteLayer` can wait behind earlier deletes, replacements and background clean-ups, for seconds, before it changes anything. A `DeleteLayer` still waiting at its deadline fails `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` with nothing changed, and a retry deletes the layer. A `DeleteSession` of several large layers can reach its deadline partway: the layers already deleted stay deleted, and a retry finishes the rest. A `DeleteLayer` that races a replacement of the same layer deletes the replacement once its wait ends, and the layer's subscriptions end `NOT_FOUND`. `CreateLayer` answers as before; only the replaced layer's clean-up waits. An `UpdateLayer` that replaces or removes more than 64 large values at once answers slightly later, by a fraction of a second at 20,000 values.
  - **What operators see.** When many layers that hold many large values are deleted or replaced at the same time, each replica frees their storage a few at a time. Layers of about 1,000 large values each are freed two at a time at `5Gi` and one at a time at the smallest limit, so a burst of deletes answers more slowly, and replaced layers' storage is freed later, after `CreateLayer` has answered. At `5Gi`, a layer with more than about 5,100 distinct large values is cleaned up beside very small layers only, and at the smallest limit so is every layer of more than a handful of prims that holds any. Above about 7,600 such values at `5Gi`, or 2,500 at the smallest limit, it runs alone and briefly uses about 1 KB per further value beyond the memory set aside for clean-ups. A replica that stops while replaced layers' clean-ups wait leaves them to the background clean-up, about 5 minutes later. A clean-up stops before it changes anything when one of its size checks fails for any reason but a foreign value, and a replaced layer is then left to the background clean-up. A `DeleteLayer` then fails with the layer unchanged: `UNAVAILABLE` on a transient error, which a retry clears; otherwise `INTERNAL`, and retries fail the same way until the cause is fixed.
  - **New metrics.** The gauges `live_edit_retirement_budget_bytes` (the allowance, from startup), `live_edit_retirement_budget_reserved_bytes` and `live_edit_retirement_budget_overcommit_bytes` (present at zero from the first scrape), and the histogram `live_edit_retirement_wait_seconds{outcome}` (`immediate`, `waited`, `cancelled`), present from its first observation.
