The gauge `live_edit_jemalloc_dirty_bytes{service, arenas}` (`automatic`, `fanout`) shows memory the allocator freed but has not yet returned. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it saturated? and § Capacity diagnostics.
  - **Changed metrics.** `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total{key_family}` gains `ext_index`, present at zero, for a foreign value on a layer's large-value reference index. It also counts a foreign value on a deleted or replaced layer's section, deleted-section or children index (`sections_index`, `deleted_sections_index`, `children_index`) when the clean-up checks the layer's size. `live_edit_snapshot_read_duration_seconds{consumer="ext_gc"}` now times the clean-up's reference read, which is far smaller than a whole-layer read.
  - **A damaged key on a deleted or replaced layer.** A foreign value on the layer's header or large-value reference index is now counted (`key_family` `header` or `ext_index`, which fires `LiveEditLayerKeysDamaged`). The layer is still deleted and the key removed with it; the reference-count repair frees the layer's large values later. A foreign value on one of the layer's other indices is counted under that index's `key_family` too, except in rare cases on the background clean-up. It stops the clean-up after the layer is gone, leaving its other keys in the store to delete by hand: see `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Damaged layer keys.
  - **Changed log text.** The failure text of a failed clean-up no longer names `UNLINK old object keys` or `UNLINK orphaned object keys`. It names the failed step, `UNLINK object keys (the teardown's first)`, `UNLINK object rows`, `UNLINK object indices (the teardown's last)` or the index read that failed, followed by `on <key>`. `SSCAN obj sections index`, `SSCAN obj deleted sections index` and `SSCAN obj children index` replace the `SMEMBERS` forms, `HSCAN obj:ext` replaces `HKEYS obj:ext`, and the `ZRANGE` forms keep their names. A replaced layer's `failed to clean up old object keys` carries it in a new `failure` field, beside `old_object_id` and `error`. The background clean-up's `failed to clean up orphaned object` carries it in its `error` text, as `orphan sweep error: <step> on <key> failed (<kind>): …`. A failed `DeleteLayer`'s failure line names the failed step but not its key, which the new `WARN` below names; one that fails while sizing its clean-up names `retirement size read`. Update log queries keyed on the old text. The remediation text on type-error and refused-read `ERROR` lines now gives separate steps for a deleted or replaced layer's clean-up.
  - **New log lines.** A `WARN` when a deleted or replaced layer's header, reference index or one of its other indices holds a foreign type, naming the layer's `object_id`. A `WARN` for each failed attempt to delete a deleted layer's keys, `could not delete a deleted layer's object keys; …`, with `object_id`, `failure` (the failed step and its key) and `error`: it is where to find the keys a failed `DeleteLayer` left. An `INFO`, `the layer was replaced while its delete waited for the retirement allowance; deleting the replacement`. A `WARN` when a replaced or orphaned layer's clean-up cannot size itself, which leaves it to the background clean-up.
- **Traces and pushed metrics now reach an `https://` OpenTelemetry collector, over TLS (b.zpx).**
  An `https://` value in `observability.tracing.endpoint.grpc` (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`) or `observability.metrics.endpoint.grpc` (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_ENDPOINT`) was accepted at startup, but every export to it failed, so TLS-only collectors received nothing.
  The service now checks the collector's certificate against the CA bundle in its container image, read once at startup, and sends no client certificate.
  For a collector signed by a private CA, mount a bundle of the public roots plus your CA over the image's bundle; without the chart, `SSL_CERT_FILE` or `SSL_CERT_DIR` can name it instead.
  `http://` endpoints work as before. The startup lines `OpenTelemetry trace export enabled` and `OTLP metrics push exporter enabled` gain a `transport` field, `plaintext` or `tls`. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Logs, traces and metrics export.
- **A write still waiting to be sent when its call failed, timed out or was cancelled no longer takes effect later, after the connection to the backing store comes back (b.1qo).**
  A write the service had already sent to the backing store before its call ended can still take effect, so every answer that leaves an upload's outcome unknown keeps the handling `docs/api/docs/errors.md` gives it. On a clustered backing store, a write the service had started to send before its call ended no longer waits while the service connects to another node, which could delay it until tens of seconds after its call failed (b.gbt). Such a write also no longer waits behind several nodes' stalled connections in turn, which could delay it a minute or more after its call failed (b.bo2). Some transactions cut off partway through being sent now close their connection; each such close counts once on `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{pool="txn",kind="Canceled"}` and `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total{pool="txn"}`. No configuration or wire change.
- **With `LocalDisk`, writing a large value's backup copy no longer holds a second copy of the value in memory, and a copy write the service stops waiting for now finishes instead of leaving a partial file.**
- **A `StorageApi` backup prefix with no layer backups no longer reads as a failing backup store.**
  On a prefix that holds no layer backups (a new one, or one whose layers were all deleted), a request for a layer the backing store has lost now answers `NOT_FOUND` (no backup) instead of `INTERNAL`. Backup clean-up no longer logs a `WARN` that it could not list the backup store, nor counts `live_edit_checkpoint_reconcile_total{outcome="live_set_failed"}`, on every pass, so `LiveEditBackupCleanupBlocked` no longer fires for it. An incremental backup refused for lack of a full backup counts as `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="refused_no_base"}` instead of an error. No configuration or wire change.
  - **Clean-up never deletes on the strength of an empty listing, on every adapter.** Clean-up deletes large values no backup names only when the same listing it read the backups through listed at least one backup. It decides a deleted layer has no backups left only when a listing in that pass named at least one backup and none of that layer's. When two listings disagree, it keeps waiting and logs a `WARN` containing `two checkpoint listings in one reconciliation pass disagreed about a dead object`. So a backup store at the wrong path, or one the service cannot list, is never read as empty and cleaned out. As a result, once every layer has been deleted, their large values stay in the backup store until a layer is backed up again. Layers deleted while the store holds no layer backups wait too: `live_edit_checkpoint_dead_object_backlog` can grow and fire `LiveEditBackupStorageGrowing`, and drains after the next layer backup.
  - **A file listed and then missing stops one pass's large-value clean-up.** Clean-up deletes no large values in a pass where a backup file it listed then reads as missing, and the next pass tries again. When another replica took over backups during the pass, its routine pruning explains the missing file and nothing is counted; otherwise it counts `live_set_failed` with a `WARN` naming the file. If that recurs every pass, the Storage service is listing files it then answers as not found; take the `WARN` lines to its owner.
  - **New `WARN`: `the post-write checkpoint listing is empty`.** The service wrote a layer's backup but the backup store listed none of that layer's backups, so old backups are not deleted and a restore would find no backup. Check that the pods reach the Storage service's list endpoint through any gateway in front of it, and that the list endpoint resolves `checkpoint.basePath`, in the form you set it, to the same prefix that writes use (`docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Backups behind or stopped).
- **A time sample at time `-0.0` is now the same sample as one at `0.0`, so late joiners, backups and restores no longer get back a time-0 sample the user erased or replaced (b.8jc).**
  `-0.0` and `0.0` are one USD time code, but the service stored a sample written at `-0.0` as a second sample beside one at `0.0`, and readers chose between the two by storage order. A client that replaces a whole time-sample map and changes the sign of its time-0 key sends, in one update, an erase at one spelling and a set at the other. Late joiners, backups and restores could then get the erase instead of the value the user set.
  - **What clients see.** `-0.0` is still accepted. The service stores it, and serves it to late joiners and in restored content, as `0.0`; live subscribers still receive each update exactly as its writer sent it, `-0.0` included. One section holds one sample at time 0: within one update the last one at either spelling is kept, and a later write at either spelling replaces it. Field values, including a whole `timeSamples` dictionary set by `SetField`, are stored and served byte for byte, as before. No wire change. See `docs/api/docs/validation.md` § Time samples at `-0.0`.
  - **Existing data.** Where a layer already holds both spellings, readers now serve the most recent write, and on a tie the value rather than the erase. The reference-count reconciler merges each such pair, and each lone `-0.0` sample, into one sample at `0.0` on every pass, with no operator action and no change to what readers serve. Samples old pods write during a rollout are merged within about one `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS` (an hour at the default) after the last old pod stops. With the reconciler off (`0`), nothing is merged, but reads stay correct. Rolling back needs no step: merged samples stay merged and earlier releases read them correctly; while a rollback rolls out, with old and new pods side by side, the self-check increases below can occur as during the upgrade. Backups taken before the upgrade keep what they captured, so restoring one can still bring back the erase.
  - **Expected self-check increases during the upgrade or a rollback.** On layers that held time samples at `-0.0`, `live_edit_structural_cache_divergence_total`, `live_edit_structural_cache_audit_total{outcome="diverged"}` and, rarely, `live_edit_update_layer_structural_fallbacks_total{reason="repeat_divergence"}` can increase and fire the `LiveEditSelfCheckFired` warning. On an upgrade, expect any number of them until the merge counter's `merged` and `rekeyed` cells stop rising after the rollout. After that, each affected layer can add at most one more increase on each replica. On a rollback, expect them while old and new pods run side by side. After the rollback completes, each affected layer can still add at most one increase on each replica. Each late increase comes at that replica's next reset of the layer or deletion of the affected attributes or their prims, or a sampled self-check of its cached copy. These need no support case. Each increase's `WARN` line identifies the layer by its `object_id`. See `docs/service/docs/operations.md` § Upgrading from a release that stores time samples at `-0.0` as written.
  - **Not expected, so open a support case as usual:**
    - an increase of any other self-check metric, `live_edit_ext_index_drift_total{direction="under"}` included;
    - after a rollback completes, more than one increase for the same layer on the same replica;
    - after an upgrade (an earlier release has no merge counter, so this and the next check apply to upgrades only), any increase after the first reconciler pass that follows the rollout, if `merged` and `rekeyed` have not risen since the rollout began;
    - after an upgrade, once `merged` and `rekeyed` stop rising, more than one further increase for the same layer on the same replica.
  - **New metric.** `live_edit_time_sample_signed_zero_merge_total{outcome}` (`merged`, `rekeyed`, `changed`, `fenced`, `not_live`, `undecodable`, `failed`), present at zero from the first scrape: samples the reconciler merged (`merged` for a pair, `rekeyed` for a lone `-0.0` sample) or could not merge on that pass. A burst after an upgrade is the migration; `merged` or `rekeyed` recurring once every replica is upgraded, or recurring `undecodable` or `failed` (each with a `WARN` naming the layer), is worth a support case. The reconciler's pass-complete `INFO` line gains `time_sample_rows_merged`. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly?
- **A deleted or replaced layer's subscribers are told, and on Valkey or Azure Managed Redis a full store can still delete layers.**
  A `DeleteLayer` cut short after removing the layer, whose retry answers `NOT_FOUND`, no longer leaves the layer's subscriptions waiting. The service finishes the delete in the background, and the subscriptions end `NOT_FOUND` (`layer '<name>' was deleted`). That is usually within minutes of the store accepting writes again. The background clean-up likewise tells any subscriber still reading an abandoned layer before it removes it. On a full self-hosted Valkey or Azure Managed Redis store, a `DeleteLayer` now completes and frees the layer's own records at once. The background clean-up there still removes abandoned layers too. Large values a removed layer referenced are reclaimed later, by a background repair. No configuration, metric or wire change.
- **Large layer clears and creates no longer time out and retry at the default command timeout.**
  A `CreateLayer` of a large layer, or an `UpdateLayer` that clears or resets one, could run past `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` (5 s) while writing to the backing store, then retry the whole write and take far longer. Such a write now gets more time, growing with its size, up to the larger of that timeout and 45 s. It still applies all or nothing. No configuration change; `0` still turns the timeout off.
- **The memory a whole-layer read holds no longer grows with the replica's CPU limit.**
  A catch-up of a large layer, a structural-edit cache load or audit of one, a backup's read of one, the read a structural edit makes of the whole layer (on the `txn` and `lua-watch-structural` backends, and on the default backend for layers the structural-edit cache cannot hold) and the clean-up of released large values' reads read the layer in windows of many commands. Each window's working memory was freed into the memory allocator's area for whichever of the service's worker threads had run it, and stayed there until that area was cleaned up in the background, so the memory such a read held beyond what it was using grew with the number of worker threads, which follows the pod's CPU limit: on a layer of about 17,400 prims (10 times the atomic-read item limit) about 43 MiB per worker thread, about 190–237 MiB with 6 against 43–73 MiB with 2, measured on a pre-release build of this change whose per-window working memory was about twice the previous release's (the previous release was not measured; about half that per worker is expected there, estimated). These reads now run on six dedicated threads of their own — one for the exact reads of large layers, four for structural edits' whole-layer reads and the clean-up's reads, and one that parses their replies — and all six share one allocator area that returns freed memory after about a second. Measured at 2 and 6 CPUs with four structural editors and a large join at once, that memory no longer grows with the CPU limit (it was lower at 6 than at 2), and the memory sizing counts it once, as one fixed term (about 143 MiB at the defaults) shared by all those reads, the structural edits' whole-layer reads included. That term was measured with four structural editors and one large join at once; up to 64 structural edits' reads can run at once at the chart's pool size (one per transaction connection), and the term is expected to cover them because the shared area's leftover memory follows how much the reads free per second, not how many run (estimated, not measured). Against the same pre-release build, a lone catch-up or cache load of a large layer finished about a third sooner (measured); the previous release was not compared. No setting changes; a replica runs six more threads, and its startup `global allocator options` line reports the new area (`fanout_arena`, `fanout_arena_dirty_decay_ms`, `fanout_arena_muzzy_decay_ms`, `fanout_arena_decay_overridden`), with a warning if it could not be set up.
- **The clean-up of released large values can no longer give one reference back twice and delete a value a layer still uses.**
  Each released reference owes exactly one give-back, but the command that gives it back can run twice: the backing-store client re-sends a command whose connection dropped after the store ran it, and work left half-processed by a failure or a crash is queued again. A second give-back of a value other layers still referenced lowered its count again and, at zero, deleted it while a layer still pointed at it. The service had treated a repeat as harmless.
  Now each give-back is recorded beside the value it applies to, so a repeat changes nothing and counts as `live_edit_gc_decrement_total{outcome="replayed"}`; a give-back that runs more than 15 s after the clean-up took the reference is refused with nothing changed (`outcome="stale"`, logged at `WARN`) and retried later. Both only ever keep a value longer. `live_edit_gc_underflow_total` therefore now has a zero baseline: a repeat no longer reaches it, so **any increase pages** (an older replica's own known defect during an upgrade or rollback, a queue entry written by a much older release, or a bug); the series is now present at zero from the first scrape, so an `increase()` alert sees its first tick.
  **The same holds when a deleted or replaced layer's give-backs are queued and the reply is lost.** Before, a layer's deletion whose request to queue its give-backs ran but whose reply was lost left them for the orphan sweep (every 5 minutes) to queue again, and once the first copies had been given back the second copies gave each reference back a second time. Now the deletion records its give-backs, and each attempt to queue them, on the layer before it sends anything; the sweep asks the backing store whether each recorded attempt ran, queues them again only once the store has settled that none did, and clears them without queuing anything when one did. Each reference cleared that way counts on the new `live_edit_ext_ref_release_already_enqueued_total`, present at zero from the first scrape: a second give-back that did not happen. No configuration or wire change.
  **Limits:** a give-back can still be applied twice when a copy of its request reaches the backing store very late: a layer edit's give-back, or a deleted or replaced layer's whose first request was answered, re-sent onto the queue more than 2 minutes after its first copy was fully given back — which needs a disabled command timeout (`LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS=0`) or a request that went away before its reply; and any other request to queue a deleted or replaced layer's give-backs that reaches the store more than 24 hours less 30 s after the service sent it. While older and newer releases run side by side, older releases keep their own known over-release defects, which upgrading closes, and the mixed window adds one narrow case of its own: a layer restore performed by an older replica can leave a stale record behind that an edit of this release already in flight across the restore can act on, giving a reference back once too often (see the upgrade-safety entry below). On a cluster this relies on the node-clock requirement under `### Added`.
- **During rolling upgrades and rollbacks between this release and any release from 0.1.0 on, running the two side by side adds only one narrow way to give a released large value's reference back twice,** a known restore-path issue (below); older releases' own known defects remain until you upgrade.
  The clean-up of released large values changed in this release, and every earlier release (0.1.0, 0.1.1 and the earlier pre-releases of this version) cleans up in a way that cannot recognise this release's work. While replicas of both ran side by side — a rolling upgrade, or a rollback — one could give back a reference the other had already given back, deleting a stored value a layer still used; catch-ups could fail `INTERNAL`; and the byte account of released values could drift. No operator step, chart setting or deployment order is needed:
  - **One clean-up leader across releases.** The first new replica takes the clean-up over from the older ones within about a second, logging one `WARN` (`took over the GC leader lock from an older build`), and older replicas start no clean-up, orphan sweep or reference-count repair of their own while any new replica is running its clean-up and can reach the backing store: every new replica renews the leadership once a second while its clean-up is making progress, even in the middle of a long clean-up run, so it can pass back to an older replica after 30 s with no new replica both running a clean-up that makes progress and reaching the backing store: all of them losing the backing store, or every new replica's clean-up having stopped or hung (each then logs the `ERROR` or the `WARN` below). A new replica whose clean-up stopped while the replica kept running logs one `ERROR` (`GC loop ended before shutdown; stopped refreshing the GC leader lock so a peer takes it over. …`); another replica, if there is one, takes the clean-up over about 20 s later, but nothing restarts the first one, so restart that pod. A new replica whose clean-up has made no progress for 20 s logs one `WARN` (`the GC loop has made no progress for too long …`) and stops renewing, so another replica takes over about 40 s after its last progress; the stalled replica logs one `INFO` (`the GC loop is making progress again; …`) if its clean-up moves again. A very slow backing store, whose commands each take close to the command timeout yet still succeed, can also trip it; that only hands the clean-up to another replica. A failing renewal logs one `WARN` when it starts failing and one `INFO` when it succeeds again; `live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total{kind="leader_lock_error"}` counts every failure.
  - **This release repeats no give-back an older replica started.** It leaves nothing it has started where an older release would repeat it, and never repeats a give-back an older replica left unfinished: it drops it instead (with a `WARN`), leaving at worst a reference standing that the reference-count reconciler reclaims (unless it is turned off, `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS=0`, when the reference stays). A released value an older replica queued without a time is now collected by the same age rule as any other release (from about 5 s, or at any age while released values are at half the budget or more), instead of at once. A deleted layer's note an older replica wrote is cleared when one of its recorded attempts landed, and otherwise dropped (with a `WARN`), never paid. The byte account of released values corrects itself with the first audit this release runs after taking the clean-up over, which starts at once.
  - **New counter `live_edit_gc_older_build_total{event}`** (`lock_taken_over`, `claim_dropped`, `release_stamped`, `carrier_dropped`), present at zero from the first scrape. Its baseline outside an upgrade or rollback is zero, so any other increase means a replica of an older release is using the same backing store. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly? and the alert rule in `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rules.
  - **What remains**: a catch-up can still fail `INTERNAL` while an older clean-up pass that was already running finishes (or one an older replica started after 30 s with no new replica both running a clean-up that makes progress and reaching the backing store), and on an older replica throughout the mixed window; older releases have their own known over-release defects, which upgrading closes; the mixed window adds one narrow case of its own, a known restore-path issue to be fixed later: a layer restore performed by a replica of 0.2.0-rc.47 or older during the mixed window can leave a stale record that an edit already in flight across the restore gives back the references its own earlier commit owed from, which can delete a stored value a layer still uses; and **0.2.0-rc.48 is not a recommended stop**: do not run it beside 0.2.0-rc.47 or older.
  - **A rollback to 0.2.0-rc.48 or earlier is meant to be temporary.** While rolled back, the reference-count reconciler is paused and stored memory is not reclaimed, for as long as the rollback lasts: give-backs this release had in progress stay unfinished, and while any note this release left for a deleted layer remains, the older release's reconciler ends every pass early (`live_edit_ext_reconcile_total{outcome="enumeration_failed"}`) and its orphan sweep logs a `WRONGTYPE` failure on every run. Find those notes with `SCAN … MATCH le:{*}:obj:extret TYPE hash`. Return to this release or a later one to resume.
- **An `UpdateLayer` whose reply was lost no longer leaves the large values it released stranded until the hourly reconciler.**
  When a commit's reply was lost and the call's next attempt found the commit already landed, the releases that commit owed were lost with the reply, so the values it replaced stayed stored until the reference-count reconciler's next pass (hourly by default) — on layers of large updates, the large ones. Each commit that releases values now also records them with the commit, under that call's own marker, and the call gives them back: on its next attempt, or, when the call ends without learning whether the commit landed (its retries exhausted, or, after a retry, a chained call whose next write committed first, an overtaken call, or a refusal such as a shutdown), by looking its own record up before it answers (b.pgs). The reconciler is left only an edit whose record about 256 later releasing edits of the same layer displaced first (fewer when each released more than 8 values), an edit whose record expired before its call checked (only while replicas run with different `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` or `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` values, or an older release, or after a jump in the backing store's clock), a call cut off before its lookup (its deadline passed, or the client disconnected), a call that ends in a way that makes no lookup (the layer deleted or replaced, an internal error), and a lookup that could not read the backing store. The new counter `live_edit_update_layer_lost_reply_release_lookup_total{outcome}` (`enqueued`, `absent`, `failed`; present at zero from the first scrape) counts the lookups, and a `failed` one also logs a `WARN`. What clients receive is unchanged. The record is a new per-layer key in the backing store, bounded per layer at about 190 KB of value names plus at most one larger edit's record (see the sizing model in [`docs/service/docs/backing-store.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store.md) § Memory) and kept only while the layer is being edited: it is removed `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` + `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` + 60 s (95 s at the defaults) after the layer's last edit that released values, or when the layer is deleted or replaced. It is checked like the layer's other keys: if it holds the wrong kind of value, an `UpdateLayer` to that layer is refused `INTERNAL` with nothing written, `update_layer keyspace type error: WRONGTYPE_KEY: <key> holds a <type>, expected hash (commit release record)`, counted on `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total{site="update"}`. Every value of `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND` gives that same message and reports it ahead of a layer at its sequence limit or a damaged update stream.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **An `UpdateLayer` committed again after the service restored its layer from backup can no longer delete a large value a layer still uses.**
  When an `UpdateLayer` finds its layer gone from the backing store, the service restores the layer from backup and commits the edit once more. That second commit reused the claim the first commit attempt had taken on each large value (external value) the edit carries. When the first attempt had already committed, its claim was spent, so the second commit could leave a value's reference count one below the layers that use it, and a later release could then delete the value while a layer still referred to it. Now the second commit takes claims of its own: the service stores the edit's large values again before it (without checking them a second time) and hands back only the second commit's own claims (b.5wb). When the first attempt's commit did not land, its claims are left for the reference-count reconciler to reclaim.
  - **What clients see.** Nothing new on success. If storing the values the second time fails, the call ends before the edit is committed again, with the answer the second commit's failure would have had: the refusal as is, or, when the service had retried the first commit internally, a plain `UNAVAILABLE`, outcome unknown.
  - **The cost.** While the values are stored the second time, the call holds room for one more copy of them in the replica's upload memory budget, given back before the second commit starts (if taking it made the call the replica's one upload running beyond the budget, it stays so until it ends), and may wait for it, recorded as one more sample on `live_edit_upload_processing_wait_seconds`; a shutdown during that wait ends the call as above. The second store does not wait at the external-value release budget again.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **A catch-up no longer fails because the clean-up removed a large value its read referenced.**
  A subscriber's catch-up reads the layer, then the large values (external values) it references. An edit landing in between could replace one of those values and the clean-up then remove the old one before the catch-up read it; the catch-up then tried to restore the value from the backup store and otherwise failed `INTERNAL`. Now it reads the layer again while holding the clean-up off — over the atomic-read limits, through a fresh shared read of the layer — and delivers that newer version of the layer with no error and no edit delivered twice. It ends with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, before any content and with no retry hint, only when it could not keep the clean-up held off across the second read (taking or renewing the hold failed, or the backing store answered that it had lapsed, as after a failover). The second read is subject to the same capacity limits as a first, and ends the same way when it meets one. If the second read finds the layer's data gone from the backing store, the catch-up does what it does when its first read finds that: with backups on, it restores the layer from the backup store when it can and catches up from the restored layer, within the same capacity limits; otherwise it moves on to a layer that replaced it, or ends `NOT_FOUND`. A value still missing after the second read is restored from the backup store as before, or fails `INTERNAL`.
  - **Metrics.** `live_edit_catchup_ext_value_miss_total{outcome, read}`, all four cells present at zero from the first scrape: `outcome="stale_retried"` per catch-up that read the layer again, `outcome="genuine"` per value still missing after it; `read="atomic"` for a catch-up's own read of a layer within the atomic-read limits, `read="shared_fenced"` for a layer's shared read over them. `live_edit_ext_read_holds`, published like the other clean-up gauges (`### Added`): catch-ups holding the clean-up off at its last pass. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly?
  - **The cost.** While a catch-up holds the clean-up off — for its second read and build: within the atomic-read limits usually well under a second, longer while other catch-ups on the replica queue for their reads; over them, the fresh shared read and the builds queued ahead of it, typically seconds — no newer released value is collected, across the whole store; if the released values are at the release budget meanwhile, uploads carrying large values wait that much longer. Over the atomic-read limits, each wave of catch-ups that finds a value removed normally costs the layer one more shared read, which pauses edits to that layer that meet it for about one polling step, as any shared read does.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **A backing-store connection that stops answering while it keeps receiving commands is now closed within seconds, and a pooled client that keeps failing is replaced.**
  The service's check for a silent connection measured the time since the newest command sent on it, so a connection that kept receiving commands was never closed, however long its oldest command had waited. One such connection could fail a share of a replica's backing-store commands, each after the 5-second command timeout and answered with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, for minutes at a time, and rebuilding its connection did not cure it.
  - **Detection.** A connection whose oldest command has had no reply for 2 seconds, while the store kept answering the replica's other connections for the same data, is closed and rebuilt, and the commands it was carrying are sent again on the new connection, inside the command timeout (`verdict="fast"`). Commands that must never run twice, such as a layer update's commit, are not sent again; they fail at once and are handled as after any lost connection. A connection with no reply for 45 seconds, whatever the others see, is closed too (`verdict="slow"`); stream-reader connections, which have their own faster checks, use only this rule. A short pause of the whole store closes nothing.
  - **Replacement.** A pooled client (data, transactional, maintenance or snapshot) whose connection is closed `fast` twice within an hour is swapped for a brand-new client: one swap at a time per pool, with a client that comes due meanwhile swapped as soon as the previous swap is made, and at most `max(pool size, 4)` per pool until the replica restarts. Requests already on the old client finish there; a swap never sends anything twice. Several old clients may be finishing at once, at most four per replica, whatever their pool; a swap past that ends the oldest other one early and logs a `WARN`, and a request still on it fails with `INTERNAL`, its outcome unknown. Background clean-up work cut off this way is picked up again later, nothing lost; a give-back of a large value's reference cut off this way also logs one `ERROR` containing `failed on its own keys` and counts on `live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total`.
  - **No wait on one backing-store connection lasts more than 45 seconds**, including the wait a credential refresh makes for every reply in flight, which could stall a client for as long as one node stayed slow. A connection that is quiet for 45 seconds after a credential refresh is no longer dropped as unresponsive (see the correction to 0.1.0 below). Once one wait on a connection has run out, the client does not wait on that connection again: whatever it still sends or reads there fails at once and is handled as after any lost connection, and closing a connection found dead no longer waits on it either, so one silent connection holds up its client for at most one 45-second wait at a time, its closing included.
  - **Two related fixes in the backing-store client.** A command written but not yet sent is always sent before the client waits for more work. A reply to an ordinary command that happens to look like a pub/sub message (an array starting `message`, `subscribe`, and so on) now reaches its command instead of being dropped, which had left every later reply on that connection one command behind.
  - **A connection lost mid-command can no longer make the background clean-up of large values delete one still in use.** The clean-up's give-back of a reference may still be sent again on the new connection, and a repeat now changes nothing (see the clean-up entry above).
  - **What clients may see.** Fewer and shorter bursts of `UNAVAILABLE` when a connection goes silent. A `JoinSession` message can reach the session's other members twice when the connection relaying it is lost after the store accepted it; this was already possible on any lost connection, and closing silent connections sooner makes it more likely (see `docs/api/docs/streams.md` § Joining a session).
  - **Metrics and logs**, all cells present at zero from the first scrape: `live_edit_valkey_unresponsive_total{pool, verdict}` (`fast`, `slow`) counts connections closed as silent, and `live_edit_valkey_member_replacements_total{pool, outcome}` (`pool` ∈ `data`, `txn`, `maintenance`, `snapshot`; `outcome` ∈ `replaced`, `connect_failed`, `budget_exhausted`) counts swaps. A wait past 45 seconds during a cluster-wide command, such as a credential refresh on a clustered store (the wait for the replies in flight before it, or its own writes and reply reads), or while a client is being closed, is logged but not counted; during a cluster-wide command, whichever wait ran out, the client's connections are all closed and rebuilt, none of them counted. Each counted close also ticks `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{kind="IO Error"}`. Every close and every wait past 45 seconds logs one `WARN` containing `Unresponsive connection to <node>` with a snapshot of where the stuck bytes were; keep those lines. A connection that went silent for over a second and recovered by itself logs an `INFO` line containing `Near miss on the connection to <node>`. Both come from the backing-store client library, which puts the client's id in front of the message (`<client id>: Unresponsive connection to <node> …`), so match them on the text they contain. Each swap logs a `WARN`; `member <N>'s replacement did not cure it` means the new client failed again within 10 minutes and is worth reporting. A swap is not counted on `live_edit_valkey_router_restarts_total`. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics.
  - **A large value a layer still used could be deleted if a backing-store connection was replaced or restarted while the background reference-count check walked the store.** The walk could end early with no error and be read as complete. Such a walk is now discarded and retried at the next pass (it counts on `live_edit_ext_reconcile_total{outcome="enumeration_failed"}`), and the checkpoint backlog gauges (`live_edit_checkpoint_oldest_pending_age_seconds`, `live_edit_checkpoint_layers_never_written`) are no longer published from a walk cut short that way. No operator action.
  - **One startup check is stricter.** Closing the backing-store connections at shutdown now takes up to 11.5 seconds when every step of it is stuck, so the service refuses to start unless `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS` is at least `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS` + 15 (it was + 10). The defaults (5 and 30) and the Helm chart's values pass. **Action for operators:** a deployment that sets the overall budget 10 to 14 seconds above the per-task timeout now fails at startup with `invalid configuration: LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS (…) must be at least LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS (…) + 15 s of headroom …`; raise `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS` (and `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` with it: the chart refuses a grace below the minimum the shutdown settings need, see the termination-grace entry under `### Changed`) or lower `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`.
  - No other configuration change. Rolling back means deploying the previous image.
- **A write refused because a Valkey key held the wrong kind of value is now always counted and logged with the key to repair.**
  On a field or time-sample edit under `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=txn`, a `CreateLayer`, or a rebuild of a layer from a backup, a key holding the wrong kind of value refused the write with nothing written, but depending on timing the refusal could skip `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total` and the error log line naming the key, its kind and the remediation, so an alert on that counter could miss it.
  Every such refusal now ticks the counter (`backend="txn"`, `site="update"` or `site="create"`) and logs that line.
  A rebuild from backup over a wrong-kind index of the layer's external values is now refused as permanent, like any other wrong-kind key, instead of being retried on every attempt while clients saw `UNAVAILABLE`.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **Under `accessTokenFile`, a new replica no longer re-authenticates every connection about a minute after it starts.**
  The service treated the first token it saw at startup as a token rotation, so every connection re-authenticated 60–90 seconds after every pod start — on a clustered store, once to every primary per connection — and on a busy store some of those re-authentications timed out, logging `Failed to refresh credentials: Timeout Error`. The first token is now recorded as the starting point, since every connection authenticated with it when it opened; only a real rotation sends a re-authentication. Under `iam`, which re-authenticates on a fixed cadence by design, nothing changes. No configuration change.
- **A busy stream reader's credential refresh no longer waits behind its blocking read under `accessTokenFile` and `iam`.**
  The refresh could queue behind a reader's read of up to 30 seconds, time out (`Failed to refresh credentials: Timeout Error` from a reader connection) and reach the connection up to 30 seconds late, outside the window the token-source refresh-lead recommendation accounts for. Each reader now re-authenticates its own connection between reads (see `### Changed`).
  **Correction to 0.1.0.** The 0.1.0 entry for the passwordless-mode warning and delivery pause says clustered deployments were never affected. They were: on a clustered store a subscriber connection's credential refresh waited for the blocking read instead of interrupting it, its caller timed out (`Failed to refresh credentials: Timeout Error`), and a subscriber connection that then wrote nothing more to that node for 45 seconds after the read it waited for — for example because its subscription had ended — was declared unresponsive and rebuilt: a subscriber-connection reconnect on `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total{pool="subscriber_pool"}`. Any refresh that sent a credential could set this off: under `iam` every refresh (every 5–6 minutes); under `accessTokenFile` each token rotation and also the refresh 60–90 seconds after every pod start (the first-token defect fixed above, which 0.1.0 shipped with its token-rotation gating). This applies to Amazon MemoryDB (`iam`) as well as Azure Managed Redis (`accessTokenFile`). Stream readers no longer run that refresh. **Still possible on a clustered store:** the service's other connections still refresh this way, so a refresh can still log `Failed to refresh credentials: Timeout Error` when a node is slow; under `accessTokenFile` only around a token rotation, under `iam` on any refresh. The other effect is gone: a connection to a node that stays quiet for 45 seconds after a refresh is no longer dropped as unresponsive, and a refresh now waits at most 45 seconds on any one connection (see the entry on silent backing-store connections above).
- **Startup warnings about the configuration are now logged.**
  Warnings the service raised while reading its configuration were lost, because logging was not set up until the whole configuration had been read. Among them: `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS=0` switching the reference-count reconciler off, `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_VALUE_VERIFY=observe`, `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_ENTRIES` above `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN`, and a large worst-case live-stream footprint. The service now sets up logging from `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_LEVEL`, `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_FORMAT` and `LIVE_EDIT_TRACE_FILTER` first, so every such warning appears at startup, before the service binds any port or connects to Valkey, including the `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_BYTES` warning under `### Added`.
  The live-stream footprint warning stays silent at the default settings, which bound a layer's stream by `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAX_BYTES` (see `### Added`). It appears once that bound passes about 4 GiB per layer: for example with `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAX_BYTES=0`, which leaves only the entry count. It describes a theoretical worst case, every retained stream entry a maximum-size upload, and needs no action unless your edits approach that size.
  Configuration errors are unchanged: a `configuration error: …` line on stderr and exit 1, never through the log output, so no log-level setting can hide them.
  No configuration change.
- **`LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ADAPTER=StorageApi` without `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE` is now refused as a configuration error, and the Helm chart refuses to render it.**
  The service already refused to run that combination, but only after binding its ports and connecting to Valkey, with an error logged as `failed to construct checkpoint store`; while Valkey was unreachable the refusal never appeared. It now exits 1 at once with `configuration error: LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE is required (non-empty) when LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ADAPTER=storageapi. …` on stderr. An empty or whitespace-only value is refused the same way.
  **Helm:** `helm template` / `helm install` / `helm upgrade` now fail when `checkpoint.adapter` is `StorageApi` (in any letter case) and `serviceIdentity.accessTokenFile.enabled` is `false`, with an error beginning `checkpoint.adapter=StorageApi requires serviceIdentity.accessTokenFile.enabled=true`. Values that select `StorageApi` without that flag rendered a pod that could not start; they now fail at render instead. Set `serviceIdentity.accessTokenFile.enabled: true` and either configure its credential-provisioner sidecar or supply the token file at `checkpoint.credentials.accessTokenFile.path` through your own `volumes` / `volumeMounts`, or choose another adapter.
- **The `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_CLIENT_CREDENTIALS_ENABLED=true` warning no longer says outbound calls will be unauthenticated.**
  The setting only logs that warning and has no other effect: outbound storage-api calls authenticate only with the token read from `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE`. The warning now says so and logs right after the configuration loads, before the service binds any port or connects to Valkey. Its opening text is unchanged. The setting accepts exactly `true` or `false` (case-sensitive).
- **A bad upload can no longer replace a large value that other layers share: uploads now verify each large value's size and content hash, and only a verified upload can replace a stored value, and only one that is damaged.**
  Large values (external values) are stored once under the hash the client sends for them and shared by every layer that refers to that hash. The service stored whatever bytes an upload carried under that hash without checking them, and each store overwrote the copy already there. So one upload with wrong or truncated bytes, such as an upload cut off part-way through its last value, replaced a good shared value for every layer that used it.
  Now, on `CreateLayer` and `UpdateLayer`, every large value must be exactly its declared `extValueSize` long and hash to its `valueOrExtHash`, which is SHA-256 over the SHA-256 digests of the value's 1 MiB blocks: the hash the Kit client, the C++ client library and the desktop application's client already send. By default a mismatch refuses the whole upload with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` and stores, applies and broadcasts nothing; the message names the failing value's `ext_value_index` and whether its size or its hash was wrong. A value already stored under a hash is kept: a restore from backup never overwrites it, and a later upload overwrites it only under the default `enforce`, after the upload's bytes passed the check, and only when the stored copy's length differs from them or it is held as the wrong Valkey key type. A stored copy of the right length is never overwritten.
  **A wrong-length `valueOrExtHash` on an `UpdateLayer` that only sets fields is now refused** with `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, where it was previously accepted and committed with the hash padded or cut to 32 bytes. `CreateLayer` and structural `UpdateLayer`s already refused it.
  **Limits:** a value stored corrupt before this release, such as one cut off part-way, is replaced when a client uploads that value again under the default `enforce` and the stored copy's length differs from the upload's (or it is held as the wrong Valkey key type). Until then, and for a corrupt copy of the right length, it stays until no layer refers to it. The copy in the backup store is not repaired, so a restore from backup, or the recovery of a value missing from Valkey, can return a copy stored corrupt before this release. During a rolling upgrade, replicas still on the older release do not check.
  **New operator-visible surface:** `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_VALUE_VERIFY` (default `enforce`; Helm `layer.extValueVerify`) and the counter `live_edit_ext_value_verify_total{result}` (`ok`, `size_mismatch`, `hash_mismatch`), counted in both modes. `observe` stores a mismatched upload instead of refusing it and logs a `WARN` naming the first failing value: a rollout setting for clients not yet confirmed to compute the hash. A value already stored is still kept, but a mismatched value stored first is what every reader of that hash gets, and clients that check content refuse it. Selecting `observe` logs a startup `WARN`. Check that `ok` climbs and both mismatch counts stay flat under real client traffic before returning to `enforce`. A value other than `enforce` or `observe` fails startup. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Requests and uploads, `docs/api/docs/validation.md` § External values, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly?
  **No action required** for clients that send the standard hash. No wire change; the upload chunk messages' proto comments now state the rule.
- **A cancelled upload could be applied.**
  When a client cancelled a `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` before it finished sending — or its stream was reset, or the service's own keepalive dropped the connection — the service could treat whatever had arrived as the whole upload and commit it: an `UpdateLayer` cancelled right after its header committed an empty update at a new sequence number, a `CreateLayer` cancelled the same way created an empty layer, and an upload cancelled partway through its content could commit that content, or an external value, cut short. Behind Envoy 1.36 every client cancel took this path, because Envoy passes a cancel on to the service as a reset the service read as a normal end. An upload is now applied only if its stream ends normally; any other ending applies nothing and is answered `CANCELLED`, logged as one `INFO` `RPC failed` at `stage` `body` (or `header`, when the stream ended before its first message) with a `status_message` beginning `stream receive error:`, and counted once as `CANCELLED` on `live_endpoint_calls_total`. A header followed by a normal end is still a valid empty upload. A cancel that arrives after the client finished sending may still apply, as before; re-send such an upload with the same `operation_id`. A stream cancelled before its first message now logs `CANCELLED` (it was logged as `INVALID_ARGUMENT` `stream closed without sending any messages`, which now means only a stream the client ended normally without sending anything). No wire change, and no client sees a different answer: a client that cancelled has already stopped listening. See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Status codes.
- **An upload's whole content was read before the call was authorized, and nothing bounded the memory concurrent uploads used.**
  A `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` without write permission, naming a session or layer that does not exist, or (on `UpdateLayer`) carrying a stale `object_id`, had its full content — up to 100 MB — read into memory before it was refused, and ten near-100 MB uploads at once could use about 1 GiB of a replica's memory before any parsing. The service now authorizes the call, and on `UpdateLayer` checks its layer and `object_id`, from the header alone, and reads the content only once the upload is admitted into the new upload budget (see `### Added`). Such a request now fails without its content being read; when a request has both a header-level problem and content over a limit, the header-level error is the one reported. An `UpdateLayer` also no longer keeps a second copy of its parsed edit, and an upload with many external values no longer copies them all at once (it stores at most 8 at a time and copies each value only as its store starts, so only about that many copies are alive at once), so an upload uses less memory than before. A failed upload's log line now reports content problems — over `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE`, too many chunks, a second header, a connection dropped mid-content — at the new `stage` `body`, where the size-cap refusal was previously logged at `header`. No wire change; the proto comments on `CreateLayer` and `UpdateLayer` state the new order.
- **A `CreateLayer` whose session was deleted while it ran could leave its layer behind in the deleted session.**
  It now fails `NOT_FOUND` (`session was deleted`), which clients do not retry, and nothing of it remains in the session; the content it had already stored is cleaned up in the background. A `CreateLayer` that completes while a `DeleteSession` is still deleting the session's layers can still leave its layer behind.
- **A section sent under a parent that did not exist yet now appears under that parent for everyone once the parent is created.**
  A `CommandDiffChangeSection` can place a section under a parent id that has no section yet; the service keeps such a section but shows it under no parent until the parent exists.
  Previously, once the parent was created, users who joined or reconnected later, and layers restored from a backup, could see the wrong children under it: sections that had since been deleted or moved elsewhere, or not all of the sections sent under it, depending on what happened in between.
  Now, when the parent is created, every section waiting under it becomes its child, ordered by `sectionOrder` and then section id, before any later command in the same update runs, and every later reader sees the same children.
  Users already connected when the waiting section was sent see it under the new parent only after they rejoin, because current clients do not keep a section whose parent they do not have.
  A `CommandReorderChildren` whose `sectionId` is a section that does not exist now changes nothing.
  Creating the parent also brings the waiting sections' subtrees under the existing tree-depth limit: a `CommandCreateSection` or new `CommandDiffChangeSection` whose waiting subtree would then sit deeper than `MAX_SECTION_TREE_DEPTH` (10,000) now fails the whole update with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` (`SectionTreeTooDeep`).
  On a layer an earlier version wrote, a section still waiting for its parent is handled the same way when this version creates the parent; a parent an earlier version already created is not repaired. Backups taken by earlier versions restore under the new rule.
  No configuration or API change.
- **With trace export on, the service now exports one span per RPC instead of every HTTP/2 library span, and spans it loses are counted and reported.**
  Trace export (Helm `observability.tracing.enabled`) sent every span of every library at every level. The HTTP/2 library opens spans for every frame it reads or writes, so on a development cluster about 800 spans and 1 MB of pod network traffic per edit were trace export, and there the exporter's 2,048-span queue overflowed all the time, not only in join bursts. It reported that once, at the first lost span, so a trace view could be incomplete with nothing saying so.
  - **What is exported now:** one span per `LiveService` RPC, named after the method, ending with the call and carrying its gRPC status code (an error status for server-side failure codes), with the service's own `INFO`-and-above log lines from its handler attached as span events. A streaming RPC's span is exported when the stream ends. Nothing from any library, and no spans for health checks, reflection or the CapabilitiesService.
  - **Joining client traces:** a request carrying a W3C `traceparent` header joins the caller's trace and follows its sampling decision; other requests are sampled at the new `observability.tracing.samplingRatio` (default `"1.0"`, every RPC). An inbound `tracestate` header is ignored.
  - **Choosing what is exported:** the new `LIVE_EDIT_TRACE_FILTER` (Helm `observability.tracing.filter`, default `live_edit=info`), independent of the log level. An empty or unparseable value fails startup.
  - **The export queue** is now set from Helm (`observability.tracing.batch.*`); the defaults are unchanged.
  - **Lost spans are visible:** counted on `live_edit_trace_spans_dropped_total{reason}` (`queue_full`, `export_failed`) beside `live_edit_trace_spans_exported_total`, and reported by a `WARN` `trace spans dropped` at most once a minute while it continues, plus a final one at shutdown. A failed export no longer logs an `ERROR` for every batch.
  - **Log lines no longer carry tracing span context.** Before, a `DEBUG` line logged inside an HTTP/2 library span carried that span's details; no line does now, at any level, and the log format is otherwise unchanged. A span-scoped directive in `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_LEVEL` or `RUST_LOG` (such as `[name]=debug`) now matches nothing.
  - With trace export on, the service no longer keeps every library's most detailed (`TRACE`) logging calls switched on; they are evaluated only as far as the log level and the trace filter need.

  **No action required**: the new defaults apply on upgrade. Raise `batch.maxQueueSize` or lower `samplingRatio` if `live_edit_trace_spans_dropped_total` keeps rising (`docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rules). No wire, API or client change.
- **During a cluster slot migration, a stream-reader connection could stop delivering live updates until the replica restarted.**
  On a clustered backing store, a reshard or scale-out that moved a layer stream's slot could end the Valkey client library's internal connection task on the stream-reader connection reading it. The connection kept reporting itself healthy and its own reconnect check never answered, so every subscription routed to that reader received nothing more until the replica restarted; the same could happen to a connection used for edits. The service now builds the Valkey client library (fred 10.1.0, MIT) from a vendored copy with a one-line fix, so such a redirect fails only the command it hit, and it restarts any connection task that ends anyway (`live_edit_valkey_router_restarts_total`, under `### Added`). The vendored copy ships in the OSS source archive and is attributed in the license notices; `vendor/fred-10.1.0.SOURCE.md` records the change. No configuration change.
- **A transaction connection rebuilt while the backing store was not answering could escape later rebuilds and the service's connection-task supervision.**
  The service rebuilds one of its dedicated transaction connections (used by transactional-path structural `UpdateLayer`s, `CreateLayer`, session create and delete, and the ID counters) when its pre-use check fails. When that rebuild's close of the old connection got no answer within its 2 s budget, the old connection's internal task kept running beside the new one. The next rebuild of that connection then left the task actually serving it in place, unrepaired and unwatched, so a connection left carrying leftover transaction state or no longer answering could stay that way, failing or timing out the calls routed to it, until the replica restarted. A rebuild now stops the old task before it starts the new one. This adds at most 0.5 s to a rebuild, so the worst case for a connection that fails every step is now 8.8 s, still well inside the 30 s default RPC deadline (`docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics). If the old task does not stop within that 0.5 s, the service logs a `WARN` (`txn slot recycle: the slot's previous router did not end within its abort budget; reconnecting anyway`) and rebuilds anyway; after that `WARN` the connection can again escape later rebuilds until the replica restarts. A rebuild does not count on `live_edit_valkey_router_restarts_total`. No configuration change.
- **Without an `operation_id`, an `UpdateLayer` the client had abandoned could be applied again after the client's next write when a backing-store connection reconnected.**
  When a client gave up on an `UpdateLayer` (its deadline passed or it disconnected) while the commit was in flight and the connection then dropped, the Valkey client library could re-send that commit after reconnecting — at any later time, possibly after the same client's next edit, which it then overwrote out of order. The commit is now sent to the backing store at most once; a dropped connection fails that attempt and the service's own retry checks whether it landed. No configuration change.
  **Still possible without an `operation_id`:** a commit still waiting to be sent while a backing-store connection reconnects is sent once the connection is back, even if the client has given up on the call, so it can still run once, late, and possibly after the client's next edit. With an `operation_id` that late commit applies nothing once the client's next edit has landed (see the `operation_id` entry under `### Added`).
- **A base path the storage service refuses as malformed now answers `INVALID_ARGUMENT` instead of `INTERNAL` when the client sent it.**
  When the permission check's request to the storage service came back `400` or `414` (`INVALID_ARGUMENT` over gRPC) — seen for a base path without a scheme — `CreateSession` and `ListSessions` answered `INTERNAL`, which clients retry, and the service logged an `ERROR`.
  They now answer `INVALID_ARGUMENT` with `base_path is not a resource address the storage service accepts (storage service answered HTTP 400)` (or `HTTP 414`, or `INVALID_ARGUMENT`), logged at `INFO`; `ListSessions` returns that error, not an empty list.
  On every other RPC the path is the session's stored one, which the client did not send in that call, so a refusal still answers `INTERNAL` and logs an `ERROR`, now with a detail naming the refusal: it needs an operator (a storage service that changed which addresses it accepts, or a session created while authorization was off).
  The refusal is not retried, and is cached like a denial for `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_NEGATIVE_TTL_SECS` (30 s by default).
  **Metrics:** a permission-check `400` or `414` (gRPC `INVALID_ARGUMENT`) no longer counts on `live_edit_auth_errors_total{code="invalid_response"}`; it shows on `live_endpoint_calls_total` under its `grpc_code`. Every other unexpected status, `405` and `422` included, is unchanged.
  No configuration change. See `docs/service/docs/deployment.md` § Prerequisites.
- **A layer created by a build older than 0.1.0 can no longer lose a large value it still uses through its incomplete per-layer record, from the first reconciler pass on this build.**
  Each layer keeps a record of how many of its fields and time samples hold each large value, and an edit trusts that record to decide when the layer has given up its last use of one. Layers created before 0.1.0 have no such record; an edit that changed how many of their fields held a value started one counting only the fields it touched, so the record could count too few. Once the reference-count reconciler handed back the excess that was keeping the value alive, a later edit could give up the layer's last counted use while another field still held it, and the value's stored bytes were deleted under a live layer.
  Each reconciler pass now checks every layer's record against the layer's contents and corrects any that disagree in one atomic step, before it hands anything back; where it cannot correct a layer's record (the layer has more fields and time samples than half `LIVE_EDIT_SNAPSHOT_ATOMIC_READ_MAX_ITEMS`, is being deleted or replaced, or has a field whose stored large-value name is malformed), it holds back the excess on that layer's values instead. The correction itself never gives anything back, and storage it frees is reclaimed on later passes. Edits on the transactional commit path that the backing store applied only in part now also re-check their layer's record straight away.
  A field written before the service stopped leaving a stale large-value marker beside a small inline value no longer counts as holding that large value when it is next edited; previously that edit subtracted the marker from the layer's record, which could make the record count too few and give up a reference another field still needed.
  **Limits:** the protection starts with the first reconciler pass on this build. On a deployment that still holds layers from before 0.1.0, the 0.1.x reconciler may already have handed back the excess behind such a layer's short record, and an edit before that first pass can still lose the value; the same holds during a rolling upgrade while a replica on an older build holds maintenance leadership. The service is pre-release, so such layers exist only in dev and test deployments. With `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS=0` nothing is checked on a schedule and nothing is handed back: a pre-0.1.0 layer's wrong record then holds storage but cannot lose a value (unless an earlier reconciler already handed its excess back), while a record gone wrong any other way stays wrong and an edit that trusts it can still give up a reference a field holds. A key of the wrong type in a layer the check has to correct stops every reconciler pass until it is cleared, and nothing is corrected or reclaimed meanwhile.
  **No action required** and no configuration change. Expect a burst of corrections on the first reconciler pass after upgrading a deployment that holds layers from before 0.1.0 (`live_edit_ext_index_drift_total`, below). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly?
- **In rollback mode (`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false`), a reconnect's update-by-update replay no longer delivers an update rebuilt from a layer whose sequence number has gone backwards.**
  After a disaster-recovery restore with too small a guard band, the read the replay takes for one update could return the layer at a sequence below that update, and the replay would deliver a rebuild of it that lacked its changes, labelled as that update. The replay now ends `UNAVAILABLE` (`the layer's sequence moved backwards while this subscription caught up (the layer was restored); retry`, `reason="error"`) before any content, as a catch-up already does, and the client's retry catches up from the restored layer. Each one logs a `WARN` naming the layer's `object_id`, the update's sequence (`upper_version`) and the one the read returned (`cut`), and counts once on `live_subscribe_stable_cut_total{outcome="unverified", regime="in_window"}`, which you may already alert on (`docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore). A replay that succeeds adds nothing to that counter. No configuration change.
- **An `UpdateLayer` call no longer applies the same edit twice through the service's own retries.**
  When the backing store applied an edit but its reply was lost — a timeout, or a dropped connection after which the Valkey client re-sends the command — the service's internal retry could apply the edit a second time at the next sequence number: subscribers received it twice, and an edit that creates a section was refused on the second attempt, so the client was told an edit failed that had landed. Each call now records a one-time marker with its commit, and a retry that finds its own marker reports that commit instead. This holds while a client has at most one `UpdateLayer` in flight per layer and `client_id`; writers sharing a `client_id`, including the empty one, can still have an edit applied twice after a lost reply. New metric `live_edit_update_layer_commit_already_landed_total{backend}` counts each duplicate avoided.
  **Still possible without an `operation_id`:** a client that retries the call itself after losing the reply — on `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, on `UNAVAILABLE` after the service's retries ran out, or after a dropped connection — makes a new call, which applies the edit again. With an `operation_id` that re-send is answered from the earlier commit instead (see the `operation_id` entry under `### Added`).
- **A structural `UpdateLayer` no longer computes its edit from a layer state read across another edit's commit.**
  A structural edit (one that adds, moves, deletes or re-orders sections) on the transactional path (`LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua-watch-structural` or `txn`, or a layer the structural-edit cache cannot hold) reads the layer's state in several round trips before applying itself. An edit landing during those round trips could leave it holding part of that other edit, and the service applied the delta to that mixed state before the commit's conflict check refused it; under concurrent updates this was reproduced as a debug-build panic, from an internal consistency check that release builds, including the shipped image, compile out. The edit now checks, before applying anything, that the layer's sequence number did not move while it read, and otherwise retries from fresh state like any other edit conflict, within `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS`. A torn read that reached the commit was refused there anyway, so for it the check only retries sooner. A torn read that made the edit fail before the commit — the debug-build panic, or a refusal such as `INVALID_ARGUMENT` judged against a state the edit never watched — never reached the commit; that edit is now retried against fresh state and may succeed. The check costs one extra read per structural edit. No configuration change.
- **An `UpdateLayer` whose delta refers to an external value by a hash that is not exactly 32 bytes is now refused with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` on both update backends.**
  With the default backend (`LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=lua`), an update made only of field and time-sample edits used to be accepted with the hash zero-padded or cut to 32 bytes. That stored a hash the client never sent, and a disaster-recovery restore could not rebuild the layer from its incremental backups past that update. Structural updates, `CreateLayer` and the `txn` backend already refused such a hash. Every path now refuses it with `delta error: invalid external-value hash length: got N bytes, expected 32` before anything is stored, so a refused `CreateLayer` or `UpdateLayer` no longer stores the uploaded external value first. A client that sends the SHA-256 hash of each external value, as the protocol requires, is unaffected.
  A field or time sample whose stored hash was changed outside the service is no longer read as a different hash, and no longer crashes the read. A catch-up or structural edit that reads it fails with `INTERNAL`. Backups of that layer fail on every pass and are logged, counted on `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="error", kind="layer"}`, instead of backing up a different value. The clean-up of its external values when it is deleted, overwritten or swept as an orphan fails with a `WARN` instead of releasing a different value, so that clean-up does not release that layer's external-value references; the periodic reference-count reconciler reclaims them later (`LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS`). No configuration change.
- **A replica's memory now returns to near its post-start level within a few minutes of load ending, instead of staying at its highest peak.**
  Previously each busier burst raised the level a replica idled at, until a pod could be killed for running out of memory on a later burst, and the memory-based autoscaler could stay at its maximum replica count because idle pods still looked full.
  This was the memory allocator keeping freed memory, not a leak.
  The service now allocates through jemalloc, with options built into the binary that return freed memory while the replica is idle, whether the node's transparent-huge-page setting is `always`, `madvise` or `never`, provided the service can read that setting (it warns at startup if not).
  Throughput is unchanged or better and new subscribers catch up faster.
  Peak memory during a burst is **not** lowered, so keep memory limits sized for your busiest bursts; memory **requests** can now be sized from steady-state use, which lets the memory-based autoscaler scale down.
  At startup the service logs `global allocator options`, and warns if something stops memory being returned: an override (`_RJEM_MALLOC_CONF`, Helm `allocator.mallocConf`), or a node whose transparent-huge-page setting the allocator cannot read from `/sys/kernel/mm/transparent_hugepage/enabled` (normally readable from containers; if it is hidden, make it visible or set the node's transparent huge pages to `madvise` or `never`).
  **No action required**, and no wire, API or client change.
  Building the service on Linux now also needs `make` and a C compiler; the release image already has them.
  See `docs/service/docs/scaling.md` § Starting size.
- **A backing-store hiccup while a viewer joins a layer no longer leaves that layer blank until the user rejoins: the service never returns gRPC `ABORTED` any more, and reports transient failures as the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`.**
  When the backing store stalled or timed out while a new subscriber's initial catch-up was being read, the subscription ended `ABORTED`.
  The Kit client library retries `UNAVAILABLE`, `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` and `INTERNAL`, but treats `ABORTED` as final, so one store stall during a join left that viewer's layer empty with no visible error, and only leaving and rejoining the session recovered it.
  Under load this affected most of the subscriptions opened during a stall.
  Now every transient failure on any read a `SubscribeReadLayer` makes — the catch-up for a new, rejoining or re-bootstrapped subscriber, the replay of retained updates, external-value reads, and the live feed — ends the subscription `UNAVAILABLE`, and the client re-subscribes from where it left off.
  A permanent failure on the catch-up or on another read of the layer's stored state still ends it `INTERNAL`; a permanent failure on the live feed still ends it `UNAVAILABLE`, as before, so the client re-subscribes; and a deleted layer still ends it `NOT_FOUND`.
  The same rule applies to every other RPC:
  - `UpdateLayer` and `CreateSession` still retry conflicts and store blips internally, and `DeleteLayer` retries store blips; when that internal budget runs out, the call now fails `UNAVAILABLE` (previously `ABORTED`), with the same message naming the attempt count and the last cause.
  - A `CreateSession` whose internal budget ran out normally leaves nothing behind, so a retry with the same name succeeds; the rare cases where the name stays held are described in the `CreateSession` entry below.
  - A `DeleteSession` that fails part-way through deleting the session's layers answers `UNAVAILABLE` when the failure was transient (retrying resumes the deletion), and `INTERNAL` when it was permanent (previously `ABORTED` for both).
  - Two subscription reads that answered a transient store failure with `INTERNAL`, which the service documents as non-retriable, now answer `UNAVAILABLE`: the read of a rejoining subscriber's retained updates, and the batch read of large attribute values (external values) when a subscriber's update is built.

  **Operator-visible effects.** `live_endpoint_calls_total{grpc_code="ABORTED"}` drops to zero, and those calls appear under `grpc_code="UNAVAILABLE"` instead, including on `SubscribeReadLayer`.
  The `RPC failed` log line for an exhausted retry budget or a transient `DeleteSession` failure now carries `grpc_code` `UNAVAILABLE`, still at `WARN`.
  A permanent `DeleteSession` cascade failure logs `internal error` at `ERROR` and no `RPC failed` line, like any other `INTERNAL`.
  Each such subscription failure is logged once and counted under the `error` reason of `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total`; see the `### Added` entry on failed-call log lines.
  Status message text is unchanged, with two exceptions: the two subscription reads above now carry a message naming the failed read instead of the redacted `internal error`, and a permanent `DeleteSession` cascade failure now carries the redacted `internal error` instead of the per-layer progress message (the progress counts are still in the server's `WARN` log).
  No configuration change and no new environment variable.
  See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Status codes.
- **A brief backing-store failure on `CreateSession`, `DeleteSession`, `ListSessions` or `CreateLayer` now answers the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, or is retried inside the service, instead of answering `INTERNAL`.**
  Several steps of these calls still reported a dropped connection, a cluster reconfiguration or a timed-out command as `INTERNAL`, which the service documents as a server fault that is not worth retrying.
  Clients whose retry policy follows that rule gave up on a failure that a retry would have cleared, and each occurrence logged an `ERROR` line that looked like a server bug.
  The affected steps:
  - `DeleteSession`: reading the session, and removing it from its base path's session lists.
  - `ListSessions`: reading the session list and each session's details. At the default settings, a backing store that stalls rather than failing fast still ends the call `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, because the `ListSessions` deadline (`LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_LIST_TIMEOUT_MS`, 2 s) is shorter than the backing-store command timeout (`LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS`, 5 s).
  - `CreateLayer`: checking whether the layer already exists, and, when it replaces an existing layer, the steps that retire the old one.
  - `CreateSession`: saving the new session's details after it is created, and checking the outcome of a create whose reply was lost. Here the service now removes that attempt's entries and tries again under a new session id, so the call normally succeeds with no visible error. If that removal also fails, the call answers `UNAVAILABLE` at once, and a retry with the same name can answer `ALREADY_EXISTS` until the next `ListSessions` of that base path removes the incomplete entry. In the rare case where the backing store saved the session's details despite reporting the write as failed, the entry is instead a complete session that `ListSessions` returns.
