Rarely, the backing store applies a create whose reply was lost only after the service has cleaned it up; the call itself is not affected, but a later same-name create then answers `ALREADY_EXISTS` until the next `ListSessions` of that base path removes the incomplete entry. No session is ever duplicated.

  A permanent backing-store refusal, such as running out of memory, still answers `INTERNAL`.

  **Operator-visible effects.** These calls move from `grpc_code="INTERNAL"` to `grpc_code="UNAVAILABLE"` on `live_endpoint_calls_total`, and each logs a `WARN` `RPC failed` line instead of an `ERROR` `internal error` line.
  The client now sees a status message naming the step that failed and the kind of error, instead of the redacted `internal error`.
  A `CreateSession` whose cleanup failed, in whole or in part, logs a `WARN` line naming the session id and each error.
  Command timeouts are still counted on `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{kind="command_timeout"}`.
  No configuration change and no new metric.
  See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Status codes.
- **Closing many live subscriptions at the same moment can no longer hang a replica.**
  Each `SubscribeReadLayer` stream that ended used to hold one of the service's runtime threads while its cleanup finished.
  When enough streams ended together, for example in a burst of client disconnects, the replica ran out of threads and hung permanently: gRPC calls and `/metrics` stopped answering, logging stopped, and only a forced kill or a failed liveness probe restarting the pod recovered it.
  The hang was possible when `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` was set at or above roughly 512 plus the number of CPUs the replica sees, because the pool size caps how many subscriptions can end together; the Helm chart's default of 256 is below that.
  Below that size, the same burst briefly started one extra OS thread per closing subscription.
  Subscription cleanup now never waits, so any number of subscriptions can end at once without tying up a thread.
  Each ending subscription still releases its connection slot and its admission, and a new subscriber is still admitted only once a slot is actually free.
  No configuration, wire or proto change, and no operator action.
- **The service no longer closes a client's connection, and every stream on it, when as few as about 100 small messages the client sent are waiting to be read.**
  The HTTP/2 library the service uses (h2) closed any connection on which as few as about 100 of the smallest DATA frames sat unread (a frame under 256 bytes costs more of the limit the smaller it is), whatever the connection's flow-control window; the client then saw every stream on that connection fail with `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`.
  A client sending small presence messages faster than the service read them could hit this, and behind a proxy that shares one upstream connection among many clients, their unread messages counted together.
  The service now uses h2 0.4.19, where that limit scales with the connection's window: at least about 2,000 unread small messages on the service's connections.
  **Client requirement.** The same limit applies inside Rust clients built on h2 (tonic or hyper): on h2 0.4.16–0.4.18 such a client closes its own connection once as few as about 120–150 presence events sit unread across its streams, and sees `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` on every stream of that connection. In that case the status comes from the client's own library, not the service; `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Transport errors explains how to tell which side closed a connection.
  Rust clients need h2 0.4.19 or later; C++ gRPC clients, including Kit, are not affected.
  See `docs/api/docs/index.md` § Client requirements.
  No configuration, wire or proto change.
- **A rolling restart no longer silently stops live updates or presence for connected users: a replica that shuts down now ends every live stream with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, so clients reconnect.**
  A replica shutting down used to end its live streams through the same signal a session deletion uses. A `SubscribeReadLayer` that was idle at that moment, and every running `JoinSession`, ended with a clean end of stream, which the Kit client treats as final: after an ordinary rolling deployment or scale-down those users stopped receiving layer updates or presence events, with no error, until they rejoined.
  Now a shutdown ends every `SubscribeReadLayer` and `JoinSession` stream with `UNAVAILABLE` and the message `server shutting down`, whether the subscription was idle, catching up or delivering, and whether the join was registering or running. The Kit client retries it: a subscription re-subscribes from the last version it saw, so updates made while it was detached are delivered, and a join reconnects.
  The replica also keeps serving for its whole drain window (`LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`, default 5 s): live streams keep delivering and new joins and subscriptions are accepted instead of refused. Only after the drain does it stop accepting connections, tell connected clients to go away, and end the streams, so a client's reconnect is not served by the replica that is leaving.
  **Deleting a session is now distinguishable from a shutdown.** A subscription on a deleted session ends `NOT_FOUND` in every case (previously a subscription that was idle ended with a clean end of stream, and one caught mid-delivery ended `UNAVAILABLE`); the message is `session was deleted`, or `layer '<name>' was deleted` when the deletion reached that subscription through its layer first. A subscribe refused because its session was deleted is now `NOT_FOUND` too, instead of `UNAVAILABLE`. A `JoinSession` on a deleted session still ends with a clean end of stream.

  **Operator-visible effects.** `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total` gains a sixth reason, `shutdown`, pre-initialised to zero; `session_cancel` now counts session deletions only, and a deletion that reached the subscription through its layer counts under `error`. A rolling restart therefore moves `shutdown`, not `session_cancel`. `live_edit_subscriber_admission_total{result="cancelled"}` still counts both a draining replica and a deleted session.
  Every stream a shutdown ends, and every join or subscription it refuses, logs one `RPC failed` line at `INFO` (previously `WARN`, or nothing for an idle subscription), with `status_message` `server shutting down` and, for a subscription, `reason` `shutdown`. A subscription ended by a session deletion logs one `INFO` `NOT_FOUND` line (previously nothing when it was idle).
  Shutdown now also waits, within its existing time budget, for every subscription to finish, and then releases any subscription read still waiting on Valkey, before closing its Valkey connections. Closing them used to take the full 2-second limit and log `subscriber pool quit timed out` at `WARN` whenever a subscription was live at shutdown; that warning now appears only when something is actually stuck.
  The wait for connections to close after telling clients to go away is now bounded; see the paced stream endings entry under `### Changed`.
  See `docs/api/docs/streams.md` § How streams end and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up?
  No configuration, proto or Helm value change.
- **Live streams now recover from a backing-store loss instead of stopping for good, and a deleted session can no longer be brought back from its backup.**
  The previous entry's deletion endings were also used when the service merely found a session missing from Valkey, which is what a lost keyspace looks like. After a backing-store loss that checkpoint-based disaster recovery could repair, every connected `SubscribeReadLayer` and `JoinSession` stream was ended as if its session had been deleted, and neither the Kit client nor the desktop application's client reconnects after that, so users had to rejoin by hand.
  Now a session found missing from Valkey that was **not** deleted ends its live streams, and refuses a subscribe still waiting for admission, with the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` and the message `session is missing from the live store; retry so it can be recovered`. The client's retry restores the session from its backup when disaster recovery is on, and carries on; a session with no backup is refused `NOT_FOUND` on that retry. A retry that lands on another replica is restored the same way. A new subscribe or join that finds its session missing when it starts is handled at setup: the session is restored there, or the call is refused `NOT_FOUND` if it cannot be, or `UNAVAILABLE` if the restore fails transiently or runs past its time limit.
  A deliberately deleted session keeps the endings described in the previous entry. `DeleteSession` now leaves a small marker in Valkey for 7 days, and a restore refuses a marked session, so a lookup made just after a deletion, before the session's backup has been removed, no longer restores the session into `ListSessions`. The marker also lets a replica that has not yet heard about a deletion give its streams the deletion endings rather than the retryable one.
  **Deleted sessions and the Kit client.** A subscription on a deleted session ends `NOT_FOUND`, as the previous entry says; the inspected Kit client reports that as a failed live read rather than a normal finish, and does not reconnect.

  **Operator-visible effects.** `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total` gains a seventh reason, `session_absent`, pre-initialised to zero; each such ending logs one `RPC failed` line at `WARN`, since outside a backing-store loss or a disaster-recovery drill it should not happen. `session_cancel` counts deliberate deletions only. `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_attempts_total{scope="session", result="not_recoverable"}` also counts restores refused because the session was deleted. Valkey holds one extra small key per session deleted in the last 7 days.
  No configuration or proto change and no new environment variable: when a `SubscribeReadLayer` or `JoinSession` finds, just after it has looked its session up, that the session has gone missing, how long it waits for the restore (7.5 s by default) is derived from `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` and `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS`; a restore the call's own lookup runs keeps its existing time limit.
  See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § Status codes, `docs/api/docs/streams.md` § How streams end, `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up? and `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements.
- **Users who join a layer late, or open it after a restore, now see prims in the same order, and the same prims, as users who were connected throughout.**
  The service now orders a parent's children by the rules the clients follow:
  - **A newly created prim, or a prim moved under a new parent, now appears last for users who join later, as it does for connected users.** Previously, under a parent that already had children, it could appear first or in the middle for users who joined later, while connected users saw it last.
  - **Kit's reorder of one kind of child (for example a prim's child prims) now keeps the parent's other children in place for everyone.** Kit reorders each kind of child separately, and the service used to treat each reorder as the parent's complete list, so the children it did not name, such as the prim's properties, disappeared for anyone who joined or reconnected afterwards, and a reconnecting client was told to delete them.
  - **Reorders now follow USD's own ordering rule.** When a reorder names only some children of one kind, the unnamed ones before the first named one stay first, and each of the others moves along with the named child it followed. A reorder of one kind of child never moves children of another kind.
  - **Reorders now reach reconnecting clients, and a restore from a backup keeps them.** Previously a client that reconnected after a reorder kept the old order, and a restore put the children back in their order before the reorder.
  - **A prim moved by two people at about the same time is no longer left attached to the parent it came from**, so deleting that parent later no longer deletes the moved prim for everyone who joins afterwards.
  - With the default `layer.catchupSnapshotEnabled: true`, a reconnecting client is now sent every child of each parent with an added, renamed, moved or reordered child, each at its position: the same order users who join later receive.

  **Known limits:**
  - A Kit reorder can race another user's new prim of the same kind and commit after it. Kit clients connected at the time then order those prims by their own rule, and can differ from users who join later, until the next reorder of all the children of that kind. A client that reconnects is also sent the service's order for that parent once a child of it was added, renamed, moved or reordered while the client was away.

  **Existing layers are not migrated:** children stored before this release keep their stored order, and only edits made from this release on follow the new rules. A prim left attached to its old parent by an earlier move stays attached until that parent's children are reordered or the prim is moved again. In such a layer, a prim that an earlier move or reorder left recorded only under the wrong parent is no longer sent to users who join, and disappears for clients that reconnect. A restore that replays edits recorded in a backup before this release applies them under the new rules, which brings the restored layer closer to what connected users saw; existing backups stay restorable.
  No configuration, API or schema change. See `docs/api/docs/streams.md` § Subscribing to a layer.
- **A burst of presence traffic, such as about 50 clients joining one session at once, no longer makes a replica silently lose presence events** (Issue `b.171`).
  Each replica receives presence events and session-deletion notices over one pub/sub connection, whose client queued at most 32 messages for the two replica-wide relays that pass them on. A relay more than 32 messages behind lost the oldest for every participant on that replica, so a client could go on not showing a participant who was present, or showing one who had left, until it rejoined. The loss was logged at `WARN` only, and `live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total` did not move. In a test of 50 clients joining one session at once, about 1 run in 6 lost events this way.
  The queue now holds 4,096 messages, and no run of the same test lost any (0 of 40). This reserves about 2.9 MiB of memory per replica up front. Any loss that still happens is counted on the new `live_edit_pubsub_relay_lagged_total{relay}` (healthy is zero; see `### Added`): it remains possible in much larger join storms, and was seen once in a local test of 30,000 connections joining.
  No configuration change. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?
- **When a session is deleted, participants connected through other replicas are now disconnected within seconds even if their replica missed the deletion notice; previously they could stay connected for up to a minute.**
  A replica learns that another replica deleted a session from a notice sent over its backing-store pub/sub connection. If the replica fell behind on those messages, or its connection dropped and reconnected, it could miss the notice: the session's `JoinSession` streams on it stayed open, renewing their presence and exchanging messages, until the periodic session sweep found the session gone, up to `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_TOKENS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SECS` (60 s by default) later. Subscriptions to the session's layers were rarely affected, since they end as each layer is deleted.
  Now a replica that may have missed notices re-checks its sessions within seconds (after a reconnect, after a delay of up to a few seconds, spread so that replicas do not all check together), and ends the streams of any deleted session exactly as a delivered notice would: `JoinSession` with a plain stream end. The periodic sweep remains for the rare notice no replica can know it missed, or one missed while re-subscribing after a reconnect fails (logged at `WARN`). The re-check logs `session_tokens sweep: requested pass for deleted sessions done` with `probed`, `cancelled` and `deferred`, at `INFO` when it ended a session's streams or had to leave an entry to the periodic sweep, otherwise at `DEBUG`.
  A replica also keeps up better with bursts of presence and deletion messages: it now takes everything waiting each time it reads them, where it previously stopped after a fixed number and lost the rest once 4,096 were waiting, and it logs one `WARN` per burst of loss instead of one per few messages. In a test with 1,000 participants in 10 sessions on one replica, under about 1,000 messages a second, with 5 of the sessions deleted through another replica, no message was lost and every participant of a deleted session was disconnected within 212 ms. Loss in much larger bursts, such as tens of thousands of connections joining or leaving at once, has not been re-measured since this change.
  No configuration or wire change. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?
- **A `JoinSession` participant is no longer announced as gone while it is still connected, comes back by itself if it is removed, and never comes back after it has left.**
  - **Slow joins.** A connection's lease was renewed only after the joiner had been sent the whole list of other participants, so a list that took longer than the lease (`LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS`, 60 s by default) to deliver, to a slow client in a large session for example, let the presence sweep announce a `PeerLeft` for a participant who was still connected. The lease is now renewed from registration until the connection ends. Measured locally with a list that took longer than a shortened lease: a false `PeerLeft` in 5 of 5 runs before, none after.
  - **Removed while still connected.** A connection that could not renew its lease for a whole lease period (a backing-store outage, or a pause longer than the lease) was removed by the sweep and then stayed hidden from the other participants and from later joiners until it disconnected, while its renewals went on giving it a lease. It now registers itself again at its next renewal, under the same `connection_id`, user name and application: the others see `PeerLeft` and then one `PeerJoined`, and each such rejoin is counted on `live_edit_presence_rejoins_total`.
  - **Late writes.** A registration or renewal that reached the backing store after its connection had ended (a timed-out request that still ran, or one the backing-store client re-sent after reconnecting) could bring the departed participant back. It now writes and announces nothing, however late it arrives. Valkey holds one small marker key per departed connection for one `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS`.
  - **Event order.** `PeerJoined` and `PeerLeft` are now published in the same atomic step as the membership change they announce, and every presence event of a session is published from the backing-store node that owns that session, so participants on any replica see each participant's events in the order they happened: a rejoin's `PeerJoined` after the removal's `PeerLeft`, and a participant's messages after its `PeerJoined` and before its `PeerLeft`. A registration or removal that changes nothing announces nothing. This relies on the new backing-store requirement under `### Added`.
  - **Joins arriving together on one replica.** A participant joining a session while another joiner on the same replica was still setting up the replica's subscription to that session's events skipped its own subscription: it missed announcements published before that subscription took effect, and received no presence events at all if it failed. Joiners now wait for the subscription in progress, and a failed subscription ends every joiner waiting on it with `UNAVAILABLE`, which clients retry.
  No configuration or wire change. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?
- **A participant joining a busy session no longer receives `PeerJoined` twice for the same peer.**
  A peer that registered while a join was being set up could reach the joiner both in its list of current participants and as a live `PeerJoined`: in a test of 50 clients joining one session at once on a development cluster, 3,325 `PeerJoined` were delivered against 2,450 expected. The service now drops a live `PeerJoined` for a peer the list already delivered, until the joiner's own first presence event comes back to its replica (normally straight away) or for at most 60 s. Such a duplicate is still possible, always for a peer the client already shows as present, when the joiner falls behind its session's events (`live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total`), when events are lost before they reach the replica (`live_edit_pubsub_relay_lagged_total`, or a backing-store reconnect), and in a few rarer cases such as a rolling upgrade; the Kit client ignores it. Dropped duplicates are not counted on `live_presence_events_total`.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **`LIVE_EDIT_SWEEPER_INTERVAL_SECS=0` is now rejected at startup, with an error naming the variable.**
  The 0.1.0 entry that introduced the variable said so, but `0` was accepted. The presence sweep's background task then crashed as soon as it started and the replica ran on without it, so with `0` on every replica the participants of a crashed replica were never removed from their sessions; the crash showed only on `live_edit_background_task_panics_total{task="presence_sweeper"}`, when the replica shut down. If you set `0`, set a positive value.
- **Replicas that share one `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_LABEL` (Helm `serviceLabel`) no longer all lead the background maintenance jobs at once.**
  The external-value GC, orphan sweep, reference-count reconciler, backup (checkpoint) loop and presence sweep each run on one replica at a time, chosen by a leader lock in the backing store. Each lock held only the replica's `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_LABEL`, and a replica that found its own value in a lock treated it as its own, so when one label was pinned across replicas, as the chart's `serviceLabel` comment invited, every replica led every one of those jobs at once.
  - **New lock value.** Each lock (`le:gc:leader`, `le:checkpoint:leader`, `le:presence:sweeper:leader`) now holds `<service label>:<ID generated at process start>`, unique per process; `le:gc:leader` holds it behind a release marker, `le-gc/2|<service label>:<ID>` (see the upgrade-safety entry above). The 0.1.0 entry for `LIVE_EDIT_MAINTENANCE_POOL_SIZE` shows the old value (`SET le:gc:leader <replica_id> …`), which no longer applies. The `service` metric label, the `replica_id` log field and a backup's `creator_replica_id` keep the label alone.
  - **New log field.** Every line in which one of these jobs reports on its own lock now carries `lock_value`, the exact value this process writes to that lock, beside `replica_id`: its start line, a run skipped because another replica holds the lock, a failure to take the lock, and every warning that it went too long without renewing the lock, lost it, or could not renew it or take it again. `lock_value` names the process that wrote the line, not the lock's holder.
  - **A restarted leader no longer inherits its own lock.** A process restarted under the same label used to pick its predecessor's lock up at once; now each job the old process led pauses across the fleet until that lock expires, for up to 30 s (the external-value jobs only until another replica takes their lock over, about 20 s). A rolling upgrade from an earlier version pauses the backup and presence jobs the same way, once, when the last old leader stops, because the new replicas cannot renew a lock written by an older version; the external-value jobs move to the first new replica at once.
  No configuration change. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Service ports and labels.
- **Each session with a `JoinSession` participant on a replica no longer reserves about 0.4 MB of that replica's memory up front.**
  Each such session reserved room for `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY` (4096) presence events as soon as its first participant joined, whether or not any event was ever sent, so a replica's memory grew with its number of sessions rather than with its traffic, and many small sessions could exhaust a pod. A session's presence memory now follows only the events its connections have not read yet: a few hundred bytes for an idle session, at any setting. Measured on one replica of a release build with 1,000 sessions of five idle participants: about 394 KB less allocated memory per session (275.0 KB per session, against 669.5 KB before), and about 401 KB less resident memory per session.
  A session with a stalled connection can still hold up to the setting's worth of unread events, about 112 bytes each plus the event's own data, so raising `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY` now costs nothing while connections keep up but raises that worst case in proportion.
  No configuration change; the default stays 4096. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Advanced tuning and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?
- **A participant whose name contains `|` is now shown with the same full name to everyone in the session.**
  A name containing `|`, as Auth0-style `auth0|…` subjects do when a caller is named by its token's `sub` claim (for example with `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` and no `name` or `preferred_username` claim), was shown to the participants already in the session cut at its first `|`, with the rest of it added to the participant's application; participants who joined later saw the full name. Every participant now sees the same full name and application, whatever characters they hold.
  While older and newer releases run side by side during a rolling upgrade or rollback, a name or application containing `|` or `%` can show altered to some participants until the replicas of the release being replaced have drained.
  The `WARN` `failed to parse presence event` no longer carries the event itself (`payload`), which holds participant names; it carries its length, `payload_bytes`, and its `error` quotes none of it. Update any log query that reads `payload`.
  No configuration or client wire change.
- **On a clustered backing store, a slot migration (a resharding, a scale-out, or a managed cache rebalancing its data) no longer turns calls on the layers whose data is moving into `INTERNAL`, and a structural edit's read of such a layer, when it reads with individual commands, follows the move** (Issue `b.vn4`).
  - **Edits to a layer whose data is mid-move answer the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, not `INTERNAL`.** While a slot moves, the store refuses a command naming several of a layer's keys when only some of them have moved, before running it; the service treated that refusal as permanent and answered `INTERNAL`. It is now transient. An `UpdateLayer` to a layer whose slot is moving is retried by the service for about 3 s (the default retry budget) and, if the move still blocks it, answers `UNAVAILABLE` without the nothing-applied hint; resolve it with `operation_id`, as for any `UNAVAILABLE` after the commit started. One refused before its commit started (an external value whose slot is moving, or an unchained call with an `operation_id` that meets the move at its first read of the layer) carries `UPSTREAM_UNAVAILABLE`: nothing was applied. A chained call meeting the move can wait until its margin before its deadline and answer `CHAIN_CHECK_UNAVAILABLE`. Every other call that meets the move answers the retryable `UNAVAILABLE` (for the split-keys refusal, instead of `INTERNAL`), after the internal retries of the calls that have them, `CreateSession` for a base path whose data is moving among them, until the move completes. **Known limit:** a key-by-key move (the classic `valkey-cli --cluster reshard`) refuses writes that touch a moving slot for that slot's whole move, not only while its keys are split. Edits, `CreateLayer`, `DeleteSession` and `JoinSession` joins in that slot answer `UNAVAILABLE` until the move completes. Presence renewals in that slot fail too, so a move longer than the presence lease (`LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS`, 60 s by default) can show other participants a participant leaving and then rejoining. A `CreateSession` whose base path's slot is moving can answer `UNAVAILABLE` too, after its internal retries. To avoid this, move slots with atomic slot migration (Valkey 9.0 or later; `valkey-cli` 9.1 or later drives it with `--cluster-use-atomic-slot-migration`), or reshard off-peak. (The `DeleteSession` `INTERNAL` this entry listed here is fixed below, Issue `b.cyu`.)
  - **Most single-key commands now follow a moving slot.** While a slot moves, the store answers a command on a key that has already moved with a redirect to the slot's new node. The backing-store client never completed that redirect: it sent the command back to the old node until its attempts ran out, so every such command failed for as long as the move lasted (the 0.1.0 entry on cluster maintenance said redirects were followed; for a moving slot they were not). It now sends the command to the new node, so a structural edit's read of the layer, once it falls back to individual commands, carries on through the move. An edit's commit itself does not follow the redirect (it is sent at most once), which is why such an edit can still answer `UNAVAILABLE` above. A command that runs a server-side script never follows the redirect either: on the new node such a script could read part of the layer that has not arrived yet as missing, so it fails retryably, as before, until the move completes. That includes the catch-up and backup reads of a layer within the atomic-read limits, and a structural edit's read once the layer's entry itself has moved; a live subscription to such a layer pauses. A slot moving to a node the client has no connection to yet, as in a scale-out, gets a connection opened for it. If that connection cannot be opened, the redirected command fails retryably, a backing-store certificate the service does not trust included; only a configuration or credential fault (an invalid TLS server name, a rejected password) fails it `INTERNAL`.
  - **Metrics:** `live_edit_snapshot_read_refused_total` no longer counts that refusal (under `kind="unknown"`): it is retried as transient, and a call it ends answers the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, or `UPSTREAM_UNAVAILABLE` before an upload's commit started, counted on `live_endpoint_calls_total{grpc_code="UNAVAILABLE"}` and, for a read, on `live_edit_snapshot_read_total{outcome="transient_error"}`. During a migration, `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total{pool}` can tick once per pooled client per node receiving a slot that the client does not yet route to, as it opens that connection: not a connection fault (`docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?). A failure to open it ticks `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{pool, kind}` under the failure's own kind.
  - **Rolling upgrade:** a replica of an earlier version still answers the refusal `INTERNAL`; treat it as outcome unknown and re-send with the same `operation_id`.
  No configuration or wire change.
- **On a clustered backing store, `DeleteSession` and `CreateLayer` no longer answer `INTERNAL` when their data's slot has moved (or, for `DeleteSession`, is moving), and a slot move can no longer leave an edit, or a background clean-up step, half-applied** (Issue `b.cyu`, with `b.qtk` and `b.bm5`).
  - **`UNAVAILABLE`, not `INTERNAL`.** A `DeleteSession` whose session sits in a slot that moved or is moving, and a `CreateLayer` whose new layer's data lands in a slot that moved since the service's connection last learned the cluster's layout, could answer the non-retryable `INTERNAL`. (A `CreateLayer` whose new layer's data lands in a slot still moving already answered the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, before writing anything of the layer, and still does.) Both now answer the retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, log at `WARN` (`RPC failed`) instead of `ERROR`, and count on `live_endpoint_calls_total{grpc_code="UNAVAILABLE"}` instead of `INTERNAL`. So does a `CreateLayer` whose reply from the backing store was lost, and whose automatic re-send by the backing-store client met the content it had already written: it answered `INTERNAL` too, and now answers `UNAVAILABLE` with one `WARN` naming the two causes it cannot tell apart (that re-send, or something outside the service writing the layer's update stream). In every case no layer was made by that call; re-send it with the same `operation_id`, which answers `ALREADY_COMMITTED` with the original `object_id` if an earlier attempt became the layer, and otherwise creates it (`APPLIED`), so the layer exists once.
  - **No stale routing after a move.** A transactional backing-store connection that met a moved slot kept sending that slot's `DeleteSession`, `CreateLayer` and session-restore writes to the slot's old node, failing each, until something else refreshed its view of the cluster. It now refreshes that view at once, holding the connection for at most `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` (5 s by default); a refresh that opens a connection ticks `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total{pool="txn"}`, and one that fails logs a `WARN` beginning `Cluster routing sync`.
  - **No half-applied edit.** An `UpdateLayer` that ran while its layer's slot was moving, in the rare state where the store let it start, could be stopped part-way by the store after its update had already gone out to live subscribers. The layer then refused every later write with `INTERNAL` (a stream-ID collision, counted on `live_edit_stream_id_collision_total`) until an operator deleted its update stream, and a reorder or reparent under an existing prim also lost that prim's list of children. Replacing a layer's only large value with another, and reordering or reparenting prims, could trigger it. Such an edit is now refused before it starts, so a slot move leaves every edit whole or unapplied; edits to a slot under a key-by-key move answer `UNAVAILABLE` for that slot's whole move, as before.
  - **Background clean-up.** Two passes of the large-value clean-up and the repair of a layer's large-value reference counts could be stopped part-way the same way, leaving bookkeeping that held storage longer than needed until a later pass healed it; they are now refused before they start while their slot moves, and run on a later pass. Three more steps (re-storing a large value whose stored copy was damaged, a catch-up's hold on the clean-up, and a large-layer edit's registration before it reads the layer) now write before they prune, with the same stored result, so a move cannot stop them part-way either.
  - Each `UpdateLayer` commit names one more backing-store key, about 30 bytes more per commit. The key is never written and costs no memory.
  - **Rolling upgrade:** a replica of an earlier version still answers these `DeleteSession` and `CreateLayer` cases `INTERNAL`; re-send a `CreateLayer` with the same `operation_id`.
  - See `docs/api/docs/errors.md` § While the service's storage is rebalanced for what each call answers during a move, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store-self-hosted.md` § Adding shards for moving slots without refusing writes.
  No configuration or wire change.

## [0.1.1] - 2026-09-22

### Added
- **The service image now ships its open-source notices and sources.** `/licenses/` holds `ATTRIBUTIONS.md` and the supporting license notices.
  `/vendor.tar.gz` holds the sources of the locked Cargo dependencies, FlatBuffers, the Rust runtime and the exact Debian GCC source package, with a copy of the notices and a manifest identifying the build inputs.
  No API, configuration or runtime behaviour change.

## [0.1.0] - 2026-09-20

### Changed
- **Cold starts are now survivable: the service answers on its gRPC port from the moment the process starts, instead of leaving the port closed until every dependency was connected** (Issue `b.rgs`). Previously the listening socket did not exist until Valkey, the credential sidecar, the backup store, and everything else had been wired — so for the whole of startup a health check against the port got a connection refusal, which Kubernetes cannot tell apart from a hung process and treats as a reason to kill and restart the pod (`failed to connect service ':50001' within 1s`). Any boot slower than the liveness window therefore risked a restart loop on cold start or rollout, even though the process was healthy and making progress. The service now binds the port as the **first** thing it does and immediately serves health checks, server reflection, and the Discovery capabilities endpoint on it, swapping in the full API over the **same already-listening socket** once startup completes — so there is no moment at which connections are refused, not even a narrow one. The practical effect: a slow dependency (cold Valkey, DNS, TLS, backup-store recovery, a credential sidecar that has not written its first token) now delays only the replica's *readiness*, which takes it out of load-balancer rotation as intended, rather than putting the pod at risk of being killed. `/metrics` also binds early, so a slow boot is diagnosable while it is happening. Two further consequences: a port conflict is now reported immediately at startup instead of after all dependency setup, and a replica no longer briefly advertises itself as ready before its dependencies have actually been checked — readiness starts out `NOT_SERVING` and is raised by the health supervisor's first successful check, which also now always logs its verdict, so a replica that never becomes ready names the dependency holding it back instead of failing silently. Readiness is additionally held down until the **full API** has taken over the socket: during startup the port answers health, reflection, and capabilities only, so without that gate a replica could pass its readiness probe while the editing API was still unregistered and answer real requests with a terminal "unimplemented" error that no client retries — and a boot that wedged after the health supervisor started would have sat there indefinitely, in rotation, with nothing to restart it. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/operations.md` § Health probes and startup. Operators deploying via the Helm chart also get a new `startupProbe` — see the `### Added` entry below.
- **Documentation only: the customer connection-sizing model for the Valkey backing store is corrected for clustered deployments, and the recurring credential-refresh command load is now documented** (Issue `b.u54`). **No behavior change, no configuration change, no new or changed defaults** — the service opens exactly the connections it always did; what changed is that the guides now describe them correctly. The previous model published a single per-replica connection total and multiplied it by replica count, which is right only on a **non-clustered** store — while cluster mode is mandatory on Amazon MemoryDB and Azure Managed Redis, where every pooled client connects to **every primary shard**. The sizing pages now give the formula (`P = 2 × LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_POOL_SIZE + LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE + 2` pooled clients per replica, 386 at the Helm production values) with separate per-primary and cluster-wide arithmetic and worked examples, and they add a **cached backchannel** term that was missing everywhere: the subscriber pool may open a second connection per subscriber slot to interrupt a blocking read, and a clustered deployment opens one on every client. On the self-hosted single-primary chart that raises the worst case from 386 to up to 642 sockets per replica, so headroom against the Valkey OSS `maxclients` default of 10,000 at ten replicas is ~1.6×, not the ~2.6× previously stated. Each platform limit is now labeled with the quantity it governs — self-hosted `maxclients` (10,000) and MemoryDB (65,000) are **per node**, compared against the per-primary figure — and the previously published "Azure Managed Redis ≥ 15,000" figure is **removed**: it was uncited and its per-node-vs-per-instance meaning could not be established, and at the HPA ceiling the cluster-wide figure lands close enough to it that a blanket "comfortable headroom" claim is not safe. The Azure guide now directs you to the connection limit documented for your selected tier and to establish whether it is per node or per instance before comparing. A new section documents the recurring per-connection credential-refresh `AUTH` load under the passwordless auth modes (`accessTokenFile` / `iam`; `static` runs no refresh at all) — roughly 1.2 `AUTH`/s per replica, per primary on a clustered store — which is negligible against a store sized for the edit rate and is deliberately **not** a tuning knob. *(That figure still holds for `iam`. For `accessTokenFile` it is superseded later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.7td`, which sends a credential only on a real token rotation and cuts the recurring load by roughly two orders of magnitude; see the `### Fixed` entry below.)* The `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use` gauge is now documented as counting pool slots rather than sockets: it sees neither the per-shard fan-out nor the backchannels, and your platform's own `connected_clients` is the authority on socket count. See `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Connections and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Capacity diagnostics.
- **Calls to the backup store are now bounded by an enforced time ceiling: a backup store that stops responding produces a retryable failure instead of an unbounded stall** (Issue `b.gg7`). The worst-case time one call to the `StorageApi` backup store could take — the request timeout across its retry and re-authentication attempts, `30 s × 3 × 2 = 180 seconds` at the defaults — was previously only a description of the retry budget, and the retry waits and token refresh sat *outside* it, so nothing actually stopped a call from running longer. That number is now enforced: a call is abandoned once it reaches the ceiling and reported as retryable, so the next cycle retries rather than one wedged call outliving every schedule in the backup subsystem. The retry backoff and the token refresh now count *inside* the budget rather than on top of it, and `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_MS` is part of the same budget rather than additive. The multi-request listing operations the housekeeping pass depends on are bounded the same way at three times the per-call ceiling (540 seconds at the defaults), and they give up **whole** rather than returning a partial listing — a partial "what is still in use" answer is precisely the input that would turn housekeeping into a wrong deletion. That 540 seconds is the listing's actual cost rather than a figure it can overshoot by one request: the deadline applies to the request in flight as well as between requests, so the last request a walk admits cannot outlive the walk. The housekeeping pass additionally bounds each of the two listings it opens with by what remains of its own time budget, so a listing can never run *on top of* the time the pass was given. Restoring a layer or a session on demand after state loss is bounded separately and more tightly — at the larger of `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` and twice `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`, **60 seconds at the defaults** — so a slow backup store can no longer hold a subscribed client, a joining client, or the other clients waiting on the same layer or session, for the full listing ceiling. That bound is deliberately not the RPC deadline alone: restoring a layer downloads the whole backup payload, and the per-request timeout is sized so that download alone may legitimately consume all of it, so a one-deadline budget would have timed out deterministically on exactly the large layers backups exist for. The pass's own reclamation time budget is now **split evenly between its two jobs** (cleaning up leftovers from failed backups, and deleting external-value blobs nothing references), so a large leftover backlog can no longer consume the whole budget and leave blob deletion unable to keep pace with what retention produces. *(The pass gains a third job later in this same unreleased set — reclaiming deleted layers' backups, Issue `b.vn6` in the `### Fixed` entries below — so the shipped split is a quarter to leftover cleanup, a quarter to reclaiming deleted layers' backups, and the guaranteed half to blob deletion, with the store-wide listing that opens the pass bounded separately at half the budget.)* **No new configuration and no changed defaults**: the 30-second request timeout and the 3 × 2 attempt budget are unchanged (the multi-megabyte-payload rationale for that timeout stands, and shrinking it would trade away legitimate recovery from transient failures), and the housekeeping reclamation grace stays 540 seconds at the defaults — the same value, now derived from a ceiling the service actually holds to. Raising `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` continues to raise all of these bounds together. See `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Advanced tuning.
- **The backup store now cleans up after itself, and external-value blobs are deleted in two stages instead of one** (Issue `b.9qd`). Two long-standing gaps in how the service manages storage on the backup store are closed, both of them cost-and-hygiene rather than data-loss issues, and neither requiring operator action. **First**, a backup that failed part-way through — a crash, an aborted upload, a write that exhausted its retry budget — could leave a partial set of files behind that no ordinary cleanup path could ever reach, because every path that enumerates backups keys off the main payload file. Such leftovers were not merely untidy: one of them pinned every external-value blob it referenced as "still in use" indefinitely, which progressively stopped the service from reclaiming *any* external-value storage for that layer. A new periodic housekeeping pass now finds and reclaims those leftovers, including leftover temporary write files. **Second**, when retention removed an old backup generation, the service used to compute which external-value blobs were now unreferenced and delete them immediately, in one step. That had a narrow window in which a blob belonging to a backup still being written elsewhere could be deleted, and — more importantly in practice — every failure along the way leaked storage permanently, because the file naming the blobs was deleted before anything else recorded them. Blob deletion is now **two-stage**: retention *records* the blobs a pruned generation referenced before deleting it, and a later housekeeping pass deletes them only if they are still unreferenced after a grace period; a blob found to be referenced again is simply kept. **Nothing is deleted until it has been observed twice, a grace period apart** — the service dates its own observations rather than trusting file timestamps, which a backup store does not reliably provide. That grace is **derived from your configured backup store rather than configurable** (three times that adapter's own worst-case time to publish one file, with a 60-second floor; for the `StorageApi` adapter it tracks `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` and its retry budget, about 540 seconds at the defaults) — deliberately not a knob, because a value set too low would delete a backup that was still being written with no signal that it had happened. **Also changed, and invisible to clients and operators alike:** the order in which the files of one backup are published is now identical across the `LocalDisk` and `StorageApi` adapters. This closes a case where a backup could become visible without its accompanying metadata, which would both hide the previous good backup and restore with empty session identity. Restores, the gRPC API, the on-disk and on-store layouts, and every existing configuration value are unchanged. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Incremental backups and retention for the operator-facing description and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the two new alert recipes.
- **The disaster-recovery sequence guard band now self-sizes per layer, and `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_SEQ_SAFETY_MARGIN` becomes its floor rather than its value** (Issue `b.vnw`). When a layer is rebuilt from a backup after a catastrophic Valkey loss, its sequence numbers resume above a guard band that stands in for the edits acknowledged between the backup and the failure — the mechanism that keeps a restore from re-issuing numbers clients already saw against different content. Previously that band was a single configured constant the operator had to size against predicted peak traffic. Every backup now records how many edits the layer accumulated since the previous backup that landed — normally one checkpoint interval's worth, and more if a run of backups was missed, in which case the larger count is used as measured rather than trimmed, since it is an honest count of the edits such a restore would lose — and the restore uses **the larger of that measurement and the configured margin** — so a layer that demonstrably edits faster than the configured value carries a band sized from its own observed traffic, and the operator's setting only has to cover the restores with no larger measurement behind them: a layer's first backup (which deliberately records no measurement rather than a misleading count of every edit since the layer was created), the narrow cases where the service cannot read a trustworthy baseline off the layer, backups written by a service version predating the measurement, and any layer whose measured edit count falls below the configured value. **No operator action required, and nothing regresses**: the configured margin is a floor, so cold layers restore exactly as before, and backups written by earlier service versions carry no measurement and also restore exactly as before. No new environment variable, no configuration change, no wire or API change. **Sizing guidance is now stated two-sided** in the Helm chart's `checkpoint.seqSafetyMargin` comment: the existing lower bound (at or above peak commits-per-second × `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS`) is unchanged, and an upper bound is now documented — every sequence number the band skips is synthesized by every reconnecting client as a no-op gap-fill callback in one uncapped loop, so an over-sized band is charged to clients as a reconnect burst. The magnitude of that burst has not yet been measured empirically.
- **The Valkey passwordless auth mode `entra` was renamed to `accessTokenFile`, and its three environment variables lost their `_ENTRA` infix** (Issue `b.ha9`). `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE` now accepts `static` \| `accessTokenFile` \| `iam` (the value `entra` is no longer accepted). The mode is described generically — the service reads a bearer token from a sidecar-written file and uses it as the Valkey `AUTH` credential; the token is still typically minted by an Azure Entra managed-identity sidecar, but the service side is issuer-agnostic. The three env vars are renamed verbatim: `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_ENTRA_TOKEN_FILE` → `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_FILE`, `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_ENTRA_AUTH_USERNAME` → `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_USERNAME`, `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_ENTRA_TOKEN_REFRESH_LEAD_SECS` → `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_REFRESH_LEAD_SECS`. The AWS `iam` mode and its `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_IAM_*` variables are genuinely AWS-specific and are **unchanged**. This is a pre-release service with no released versions, so the rename carries **no backward-compatibility guarantee** — operators using the old mode value / env-var names must switch to the new ones. Operator-facing Helm values keys for Valkey passwordless auth are being canonicalized to a `valkey.credentials.accessTokenFile.*` shape in the same change; see the chart's `values.yaml`.
- **The daily authentication window under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` is gone: the service now authenticates to its backing store with a cached token for the token's whole real lifetime, instead of refusing to use it for the last few minutes before expiry** (Issue `b.d6b`). Under the file-based Valkey credential mode the service treated a cached token as unusable once it came within `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_REFRESH_LEAD_SECS` (default 180 seconds) of expiring. Nothing on the backing-store side asked for that — Microsoft's "refresh and re-authenticate at least 3 minutes before token expiry" is guidance about how early a client should *fetch* its next token, not a rule against presenting a token that is still valid — but the service turned it into a refusal, so for roughly the last three minutes of every token's life it had no credential it was willing to present even though a working one sat in memory. Anything that had to establish a connection in that window failed: pod starts, reconnects after a backing-store blip, and the self-heal that rebuilds a wedged subscriber connection. On a deployment whose sidecar rotates its token later than that window opens, the result was a recurring outage — typically once a day, once per token lifetime — in which authentication failed and the replica reported **NOT_SERVING** until a fresh token landed. The service now presents the cached token whenever it is genuinely unexpired, and refuses only in the two states where the credential is really gone: no token has ever been read, or the cached one has actually passed its expiry. The setting keeps its other, real job — it is how early the service starts re-reading the token file looking for the replacement — and is unchanged at 180 seconds; **no configuration change and no operator action are required**. This change is never worse than the old behavior: if a backing store really would reject a token close to expiry, presenting it fails exactly as withholding it did. The 60-second equivalent on the separate service-identity token used for outbound checkpoint calls is **unchanged** by this release. *(Superseded later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.e49`, which applies the identical correction to the service-identity token — see the `### Fixed` entry below.)* **The sidecar sizing rule changes from a requirement to a recommendation, and the number changes.** The previously published "the sidecar must refresh at least ~195 seconds before expiry" requirement is **withdrawn** — it was derived from the withholding behavior this change removes. In its place: the token source should refresh **at least 360 seconds before expiry, plus the service's token-file poll interval (`LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS`, 15 seconds by default) — so at least 375 seconds at the defaults**, and deployed values should leave margin above that. *(Superseded later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.7td`, which relaxes this to **195 seconds at the defaults** — see the `### Fixed` entry below. A value sized against the 375-second figure still clears the new one.)* Meeting it means every pooled connection re-authenticates with the replacement token before the old one expires and is renewed in place. Falling short of it is **not** an outage and not an authentication failure: the fraction of connections that miss that renewal grows roughly in proportion to the shortfall, and in the worst case every pooled connection takes one brief reconnect per token lifetime, visible as a small burst on `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total` and a short pause on any subscriptions those connections carried. (One internal connection — the one carrying presence notifications — is rebuilt at every token expiry by design, whatever the lead; that reconnect is invisible to clients.) **New operator-visible surface**: a Prometheus gauge `live_edit_service_token_expires_in_seconds{role}` (`role` is `valkey` or `storage-api`) reporting the signed seconds left on each reader's cached token. It is the direct way to read your deployment's actual sidecar refresh lead — under a healthy sidecar the series is a sawtooth once per token lifetime, and **the trough of that sawtooth is the lead** to compare against the recommendation above; a dip below zero means a token expired before its replacement arrived. Also improved: after a cached token does expire with nothing to replace it, the service keeps re-reading the token file four times a second for a further 20 seconds, so recovery from a total credential gap is about a second rather than up to a poll interval. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Token refresh, `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Access-token file, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are credentials and auth services healthy? for the gauge, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for its alert rule.
- **When a credential sidecar rotates its token later than it should, the service now picks up the replacement about a second after it lands on disk instead of up to a full poll interval later** (Issue `b.qam`). At the time of this change the service treated a cached credential as unusable once it was within a set window of expiring — 180 seconds for the Valkey token under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile`, 60 seconds for the service-identity token used for outbound checkpoint calls. (That rule still applies to the service-identity token; on the Valkey token it was **removed** later in this same release by Issue `b.d6b`, described above.) The service only noticed a replaced token file on its next scheduled poll — `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS`, 15 seconds by default. A sidecar that refreshed late therefore cost up to another 15 seconds during which the service had no credential it was willing to use: on the Valkey path that meant failing authentication and a replica reporting **NOT_SERVING**, on the outbound path unauthenticated checkpoint calls. The service now re-reads the token file roughly four times a second once a cached token is inside that window plus one poll interval of expiring, until a replacement arrives or the token expires — the same fast re-read it already used at startup, applied to rotations as well. **This narrowed the gap but did not remove it**; a separate change in this same release (Issue `b.d6b`) removes the underlying window on the Valkey path by serving a cached token for its whole real lifetime, and replaces the sidecar sizing *requirement* with a recommendation. See `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Token refresh for the current rule. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, no new metric, and no wire or API change. **New operator-visible surface**: an informational log line when a cached token is approaching expiry with no replacement yet — not by itself a sign of misconfiguration, since a correctly configured deployment can emit it on every rotation; the sizing signal is the headroom warning added later in this release under `b.is1` — and a warning if that token then expires with nothing to replace it, naming which token reader was affected (`valkey` or `storage-api`), its file path, and how long it had been waiting. Neither carries any token material.
- **The shipped `ATTRIBUTIONS.md` is now generated from the exact dependency artifacts the service is built from, so its contents changed substantially in this release** (Issues `b.sv5`, `b.tcm`). **No service behavior, API, configuration, or default changes** — this affects only the third-party license document that accompanies the service. Previously each dependency's license text was fetched from that project's public source repository at its current development state, which is not the released version we redistribute: the document could pick up unreleased upstream edits, and copyright years in a small number of fallback notices were filled in from the current date rather than taken from the dependency. License text is now read out of the locked, published dependency archive for the exact version in `Cargo.lock`. Four visible consequences: dependencies offering a choice of licenses now carry **every** license text they ship rather than one chosen on your behalf (including, for the handful of dependencies that ship no license file, the canonical text of every license branch they declare rather than a single one, each marked "SPDX canonical text" so substituted standard text is always distinguishable from text the dependency itself ships); each entry is labeled with the license expression the dependency declares; copyright years are reproduced as published and never synthesized; and every license text is now enclosed in a fenced code block, so license prose containing `#` characters renders as text instead of as document headings. The document covers the same set of dependencies as before, with one entry per dependency.
- **`UpdateLayer` now reports `NOT_FOUND` instead of `FAILED_PRECONDITION` when the layer's stored content is gone** (Issue `b.f1v`). Previously this case shared a status code with "your edit was based on a version that has been replaced", which is a different situation calling for different handling. There are two ways to reach it. The first needs no fault at all and happens on a perfectly healthy service: another client deletes the layer, or replaces it with a new `CreateLayer`, while your update is already in its commit window — the cleanup removes the stored content out from under the in-flight edit. The second is a partial loss of the backing store, where the layer is still registered in its session but its content is gone; that is the case the service now attempts to repair from a backup before answering. **This narrows what `FAILED_PRECONDITION` means for a concurrent `CreateLayer` overwrite**, which can be observed at two moments, both of them inside an already-in-flight update's commit window: one that reaches its commit window after the old content is gone now gets `NOT_FOUND`, while one that reaches it while the old layer is merely marked as replaced still gets `FAILED_PRECONDITION`. An update that simply *arrives* after an overwrite is unaffected — it fails the object-generation check up front and still gets `FAILED_PRECONDITION`, as does a mismatched object generation generally. Both codes remain final — neither is worth retrying unchanged — and both call for the same client action: stop, re-resolve the layer, and either re-issue against the new object generation or treat the layer as replaced. A client already doing that needs no change. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no other wire or API change.**
- **Three per-replica load gauges now report what each replica is holding in memory, so a memory graph can be read as "bytes per session" instead of a bare number.** `live_active_sessions` (sessions with at least one participant connected to this replica — each one holds a preallocated presence buffer, so this is the main per-replica presence-memory driver), `live_active_connections` (open JoinSession connections on this replica), and `live_edit_update_layer_admission_entries` (per-object write bookkeeping entries, which are only released when a layer is deleted or overwritten). The first two names already existed on `/metrics` but were never wired to anything and read zero forever; all three are now collected from live in-process state on every scrape, so a value that fails to return to zero after every client has disconnected is itself the finding rather than a stale reading. **One visible difference from before:** the two pre-existing names used to appear as `0` from the very first scrape; all three now appear only once the replica has finished connecting to its backing store, so for the first seconds of a replica's life they are absent from `/metrics` rather than zero — alerting rules should tolerate that startup gap. **No configuration change and no change to how the service handles requests.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving? and § Capacity diagnostics for the three gauges.
- **A layer made unreadable by a foreign value on one of its Valkey keys is now visible on a metric, instead of only in the log** (Issue `b.atj`). The service already refused, loudly and safely, when it found a value of the wrong kind on a key it reads while loading a layer's current state — but no metric moved, so the existing alert on the write-side counter could not fire for it, and the metrics guide told you to fall back to the log. That was the wrong way round for the most likely shape of the problem: the write-side check deliberately does not cover a layer's child-ordering keys, so a foreign value there is caught only on the read, and the read is what every part of the service goes through — edits, subscriptions and catch-up, backups, and external-value clean-up all stop for that layer together. **New operator-visible surface:** a Prometheus counter `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total{key_family}`, where `key_family` names which part of the layer's stored state was unreadable (ten values, all pre-initialised to zero, so a flat zero is a positive statement rather than an absent series). A new **error** log line accompanies each increment and names the offending key in full, along with the layer's object id and what to do about it — for the external-value clean-up, which is a background job with no client to report to, that line and this counter are the only evidence it has stalled. **The shipped alert recipe now covers both sides as one rule** (`increase(live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total[15m]) > 0 or increase(live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total[15m]) > 0`) — one alert, one remediation, since the condition and the fix are identical and only the moment of detection differs. Two cases were deliberately left uncounted at the time and documented as such: a refusal on the same reads for a different permanent reason (a protocol, parse, credential or client-configuration failure, whose remediation is the opposite one), and the reads the service issues on a layer's header and tombstone records outside the shared state load — which, because every part of the service reads the layer's header before loading its state, left the `header` cell effectively unreachable. *(Both are addressed later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.muu` — see the entry below — which gives the first case a counter of its own and brings the backup subsystem's direct reads and the background clean-up's header touches into this one. A narrower version of the second case remains: five touches of a layer's header record in the part of the service that serves edits, subscriptions, `StatLayer` and external-value clean-up are still uncounted, and `StatLayer`'s has no counted read behind it at all.)* **Nothing about the service's behaviour changed** — the same reads are refused in the same way, with the same status code and the same message to the client; only the reporting is new. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? for the counter, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Damaged layer keys for its triage steps, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the updated alert rule.
- **A layer can also be made unreadable by a backing store that refuses the read for a reason that has nothing to do with a foreign value, and that case now has a metric of its own; separately, the counter added above stops being blind to the backup subsystem's and the background clean-up's own reads** (Issue `b.muu`). The counter shipped with `b.atj` deliberately covered one cause only — a value of the wrong kind on a key. Everything else that permanently refuses those same reads (a protocol or parse failure, a rejected credential, a TLS or client-configuration fault, a routing fault, or a backing store that answers but declines to serve) wedged exactly the same parts of the service — edits, subscriptions and catch-up, backups, external-value clean-up — and moved nothing at all. **New operator-visible surface:** a Prometheus counter `live_edit_snapshot_read_refused_total{kind}` with twelve pre-initialised cells — `protocol`, `parse`, `auth`, `config`, `tls`, `invalid_argument`, `invalid_command`, `routing`, `not_found`, `backend_unavailable`, `backend_out_of_memory`, and `unknown` — each with its own remediation, because the triage for a rejected credential, a cluster-routing fault and a service bug are three different investigations. **Eleven of them are expected to read zero forever and ship a page-on-first-occurrence recipe; `backend_unavailable` is the deliberate exception** — a primary replaying its dataset after a restart answers "still loading" to everything until it finishes, so that cell has an expected non-zero rate, takes a sustained-rate warning rule instead, and **logs at warning level where the other eleven log at error**. **Two shapes of that cell, worth knowing before you read the rate:** of the five conditions it covers, only two — the store still loading, and a long-running script — refuse a *read*, and nearly everything counted here is a read. The other three (writes blocked after a failed background save, an unpromoted replica, too few replicas) refuse writes only, so they reach this counter solely through the single header write the background clean-up makes per abandoned layer: they appear as a thin trickle of at most one increment per layer per 300-second pass, not as a burst, and a failover on its own produces no burst on this cell at all. The sustained-rate rule fires on that trickle too, which is intended — do not add a rate floor to it. Most of these conditions need no operator, but one can: writes blocked after a failed background save last until a save succeeds, which on a full disk means until someone frees space, and the 15-minute duration rule is what tells that apart from the ordinary transient case. A runbook that greps the error stream for read refusals will therefore correctly ignore an ordinary restart and will equally correctly miss every such burst; search the warning stream for those. Note that while the store is declining to serve, the affected layers are *failing* rather than waiting — the service currently treats these refusals as permanent and does not retry them, which is a pre-existing defect tracked separately and which this counter only makes visible. **A store that has run out of memory is one of the eleven, not a second exception**: `backend_out_of_memory` also comes from the backing store, but because this service requires the store to be configured never to evict, it will refuse every memory-growing write until an operator raises `maxmemory` or frees memory — waiting does not clear it, so it pages like the rest and its remediation is the sizing model in `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Memory. (Deletes are not memory-growing and keep working, so the service's background clean-up can still remove data.) **One case has a wider effect than the single layer named in the log line, and the log line's `op` field is what identifies it:** when the refused touch is the background clean-up's *discovery* read of a layer's header, it ends the rest of that clean-up pass — clean-up of abandoned layers stalls for every layer the pass had not yet reached (the ones it had already reached were cleaned), and clean-up of orphaned layer mappings and the drain of deferred external-value releases do not run at all, repeating on every pass — one every 300 seconds — until the condition is fixed. The same exception applies to `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total` and is documented on both. **Second change, to the existing counter:** `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total` now also covers the four reads the backup subsystem issues directly on a layer's header and its deleted-section records, and the two touches the background clean-up makes on a layer's header when it retires an abandoned layer. Its zero baseline is unchanged — a zero still means "no foreign value on the service's keyspace" — and its error log line now names the failing command alongside the key. **Third, the `header` cell is now honest about what a zero means, which differs by consumer:** it counts on first contact for backups and for the background clean-up; it stays race-only for edits, subscriptions and external-value clean-up, which each read the header on an uncounted path first; and it is **blind** for `StatLayer`, which has no counted read behind it at all — so for a `StatLayer` call failing with `INTERNAL`, "check the counter" is the wrong instruction, and the service's error log (or the header key itself) is where to look. Five header touches in total, plus the reads the service performs when rebuilding a layer from a backup, remain outside both counters and are tracked separately. **Nothing about the service's behaviour changed:** the same touches are refused in the same way, with the same status code, the same message to the client, and the same retry behaviour; only the reporting is new. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Store refusing reads or writes, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rules.
- **Incremental backups between full backups are now on by default** (Issue `b.3n1`). `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED` — and the Helm chart's `checkpoint.tail.enabled` — now default to **`true`** rather than `false`, so a deployment takes incremental backups without being configured to, and a continuously-edited layer keeps advancing its recovery point instead of stalling at its last full backup. The feature itself is unchanged; only its default is. **To keep a deployment on full backups only, set `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED=false`** (or `checkpoint.tail.enabled: false` in your Helm values), which gives exactly the previous behaviour. **A deployment that already sets the value explicitly sees no change at all** — an explicit `true` or `false` still means what it said. The three sizing settings are unchanged at their existing defaults (`LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_CHAIN` `8`, `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_ENTRIES` `4096`, `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_BYTES` `67108864` / 64 MiB), and there is **no wire or API change and no other changed default**. What to expect the first time a fleet runs with the new default: a bounded transition in which layers that already existed wait until each falls quiet once and takes a full backup for incremental backups to extend — visible as a `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="refused_no_base"}` population that appears at once and drains over the following hours while `outcome="captured"` rises to replace it. Layers created from then on skip that wait, and a restored layer is covered as soon as its restore completes. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Incremental backups and retention, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? for `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total`.
- **A large structural update that gives its sections explicit ordering no longer costs CPU and memory-allocation work that grows with the square of how many children end up under one parent** (Issue `b.sao`). When one update created many sibling sections under the same parent and assigned each an explicit order, the service rebuilt an internal ordering summary of that parent from scratch for every section it added — so the work of adding the last sibling was proportional to all the siblings before it, and the total grew quadratically. The summary is now refreshed only when something actually invalidates it, so the same update does that work once instead of once per section. **Nothing about the result changes**: the same sections are created in the same order, the same data is written to the backing store, the same errors are returned in the same cases, and clients see no difference. Two related shapes benefit the same way — an update that deletes many ordered sections one at a time, and one that moves many sections between parents while changing their order. Uploading or restoring a layer goes through the same code and benefits identically. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire, API, or storage-schema change.** This is a change to how the work is done, not to how much a layer may contain: the existing per-update limits (100,000 structural commands, 100,000 entries in a supplied child ordering) are unchanged, and an update is still applied on one thread with no CPU admission control, so a large update is still large.

### Added
- **The Helm chart now exposes the incremental-backup settings, so turning the feature on no longer requires forking the chart** (Issue `b.uw9`). The four `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_*` variables introduced with incremental backups (see the entry below) had no chart keys and the chart has no generic environment passthrough, so a Kubernetes deployment always ran with the shipped defaults whatever the operator wanted. They are now `checkpoint.tail.enabled`, `checkpoint.tail.maxChain`, `checkpoint.tail.maxEntries`, and `checkpoint.tail.maxBytes`, documented in `values.yaml` alongside the other backup settings and defaulted to exactly the binary's values — so **upgrading the chart changes nothing**, and `--set checkpoint.tail.enabled=true` (or the equivalent single line in a values overlay, which preserves the other three defaults) is the whole flip. *(The chart default became `true` later in this same unreleased set — see **Incremental backups between full backups are now on by default** under `### Changed` — so `--set checkpoint.tail.enabled=false` is now the opt-out.)* All four are written into the pod's settings whether or not the feature is on, because the service validates the three limits at startup either way: an unusable limit is therefore rejected when you set it, rather than lying in wait until the day you enable the feature. No service behaviour, API, or default changes.
- **Continuously-edited layers can now be backed up incrementally between full backups, so a layer that never falls quiet keeps advancing its recovery point instead of stalling at its last full backup** (Plan `b.dfh`). The service writes a full backup only when it can prove it read a complete point-in-time state of the layer, and a layer under near-continuous editing never gives it that proof — so such a layer deferred its backup pass after pass and its protection stopped advancing until the editing stopped, which was the only exit available. With the new `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED=true`, a pass that cannot prove a complete read of a layer writes a much smaller **incremental backup** instead, carrying that layer's committed edits since its last durable backup on top of that backup rather than in place of it.
