Recovery consumes them: a layer is restored from the newest recovery point the backup store holds — the full backup that point rests on, plus the run of incremental backups above it whose links recovery can verify, replayed in order — so a hot layer's recovery point advances at the backup loop's evaluation cadence instead of waiting for a quiet period it may never get. Recovery reads only that verified run, not every file the store happens to hold. **A chain that reaches its limit is composed into a fresh full backup instead of freezing.** When a layer's chain reaches `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_CHAIN`, the next pass for that layer composes the full backup and its whole chain into a new full backup covering everything the chain covered — built from files the backup store already holds rather than from a fresh read of the layer, so it needs no quiet period. The limit is therefore a bound on how many files one recovery has to fetch, not a ceiling on how much of a layer is protected, and the era in which a permanently hot layer reached the limit and then stopped advancing until it fell quiet is over. Cleanup runs on that same pass, so whatever the new full backup pushes out of the retained window is deleted immediately rather than at the layer's next write. **A chain that does not verify makes recovery fall back, not fail:** every incremental backup records, inside the file itself, which backup it continues, and recovery checks each link against that record rather than trusting the backup store's index — so a missing, unreadable or inconsistent member makes recovery restore from the newest point it can prove instead. That is older state, but internally complete and correct, and it is never silent: each fallback logs a warning naming the layer and the link that failed and increments `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_lineage_total`. **Cleanup now counts recovery points, not files.** `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_RETAIN_GENERATIONS` (default `2`, unchanged) keeps that many recovery points per layer, and a full backup together with the incremental backups resting on it counts as **one**, however many files that is. Deletion is oldest recovery point first and, within one, the incremental backups go before the full backup they rest on — so a cleanup interrupted part-way can leave a full backup with nothing on top of it, but never an incremental backup without the full backup it needs. One bounded case remains: when two replicas publish for the same layer at the same moment, a later recovery can land on a full backup newer than the chain it was about to use while the coverage above that point is older, so relative to that chain the recovery point moves **backwards**, not forwards; what it never does is fall below the newest full backup the store holds, or land on a backup that cannot be restored. Because a restored layer is given a fresh full backup of its own and that consumes one of these slots, **set the value to N + 1 if you need N recovery points to still be there after a restore.** **New metric family:** the Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome}` reports every incremental-backup attempt, with ten `outcome` values — `captured`, `captured_capped`, `rebased`, `refused_trimmed`, `refused_missing_bytes`, `refused_sentinel`, `refused_seq_gap`, `refused_no_base`, `refused_chain_full`, and `refused_lineage_unrootable`. All ten cells are pre-initialised at zero **unconditionally** — a replica running with the feature off still exports all ten, all reading `0` — which matters because a flat zero is then a positive statement that nothing was ever refused, available from the very first scrape, rather than a series that materialises only on first occurrence and is indistinguishable from a feature nobody wired. **Two of those cells read differently from what earlier documentation described, so existing dashboards should be re-read.** `rebased` is **live**, counting chain compositions — it was previously documented as reserved and always zero — and a steady trickle of it on a fleet with permanently hot layers is the feature working rather than a fault. `refused_chain_full` no longer means "this layer hit the chain limit and stopped advancing forever"; it now means the limit was reached **and the composition that should have re-rooted the chain did not land**, which makes it partly a backup-store-health signal whose remediation is the accompanying warning and the store's health rather than the chain setting. `refused_no_base` is narrowed to mean only "this layer has no durable backup of any kind yet" — the bulk-then-draining population you see after enabling the feature, and what a rollout-tracker query should count — because the backup-store-damage readings that used to share that cell now have their own, `refused_lineage_unrootable`: durable backups exist for the layer but none of them can serve as the foundation a chain rests on. That cell is expected to read zero forever on every deployment whatever the feature flag is set to, so any increment is worth investigating, and its remediation is backup-store repair rather than any setting. **New environment variables — four, all defaulted so that adding them changes nothing:** `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED` (default `false`; accepts exactly `true` or `false`, lower-case, anything else rejected at startup), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_CHAIN` (default `8`; zero rejected at startup, since a chain bound of zero would leave the feature inert while reporting itself enabled), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_ENTRIES` (default `4096`; zero rejected, as is any value above `9007199254740991`, and a value above `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN` logs a startup **warning** rather than being rejected — an incremental backup is built from the layer's retained update stream, so it can never carry more edits than that stream holds, which makes the larger value inert rather than wrong; the two defaults match, so the warning is silent unless you change one of them), and `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_MAX_BYTES` (default `67108864`, 64 MiB; zero rejected, as is anything below 66 bytes — the smallest file that can hold a single edit — because a lower limit would leave the feature unable to write anything while reporting itself enabled). **One operator-visible accounting change to an existing metric:** a pass that composes a chain moves **two** `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{kind="layer"}` cells — `{result="success", kind="layer"}` for the full backup it wrote, beside the `{result="skipped", kind="layer"}` the same pass had already recorded for the ordinary full backup it did not take — as well as `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="rebased"}`, so a success-rate or adapter-error-share ratio computed over `kind="layer"` double-counts such a pass; read the error share against `sum by (result)` rather than as a bare ratio during a composition-heavy window. Both cells are true and neither is suppressed: the ordinary full backup — the one taken from a fresh read of the layer — genuinely was skipped, and a composed full backup genuinely did land. Relatedly, `live_edit_checkpoint_lease_total{outcome="lost"}` rising **together with** `rebased` is now a benign shape — a composition can legitimately consume the leader lease — while `lost` rising **without** it stays the fault shape. **The retained update stream is the coverage floor:** an incremental backup can only cover edits still held in the layer's live update stream when the pass runs, so `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN` has to comfortably exceed what a hot layer commits per checkpoint interval, with headroom — turning that window down now costs recovery point, not only memory, and `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="refused_trimmed"}` rising is the signal that it has been turned down too far. **Additive and non-breaking:** the feature is off by default *(that default became `true` later in this same unreleased set — see **Incremental backups between full backups are now on by default** under `### Changed`)*, the four new variables leave behaviour exactly as before at their defaults, no existing default changed, and there is **no wire or API change, no new backup store or adapter, and no change to how layers, sessions or subscriptions behave** — clients cannot tell whether it is on, and recovery consults no flag, so a fleet that turns it back off keeps recovering from already-written incremental backups until they age out. Enabling it has a bounded transition: layers created afterwards get a full backup at creation and are covered right away, a layer that is restored gets one automatically, and layers that already existed wait until each falls quiet once — after which it never has to again, because a chain that reaches its limit is composed into the next full backup. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Incremental backups and retention, `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? for the counter, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule that ships for `refused_lineage_unrootable`, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Memory for sizing the retained window.
- **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_base_bootstrap_total{outcome, site}` reports whether the layers owed an automatic full backup — one when a layer is created, one after a layer is recovered — actually received it** (Plan `b.dfh`). Those two writes had almost no observability: five of their nine possible endings moved no metric anywhere, so a dropped or unrecorded first backup was visible only in the log stream, if at all. `site` says which of the two writes an increment describes — `creation_base` (the full backup taken when a layer is created) or `restore_rebase` (the one written after a successful disaster-recovery restore) — and the same two spellings ride the accompanying log lines, so a metric cell and a log line join on one string. `outcome` is one of `persisted`, `shed`, `skipped_header_absent`, `skipped_generation_present`, `skipped_untrusted_cursor`, `unstamped_generation_present`, `unstamped_foreign_restore`, `unstamped_write_error`, and `failed`. All **eighteen** cells this change introduces — every outcome × site pair; a tenth outcome and its two cells arrive with the deleted-layer fix under **Fixed** below, taking the grid to twenty — are pre-initialised at zero, including the two pairs that cannot occur (`skipped_untrusted_cursor` and `unstamped_foreign_restore` arise only at the restore site), so a dashboard selecting the family by name gets a rectangular result and a permanently-zero cell costs less than a query that has to know which pairs are structurally impossible. The zero-seeding matters more here than for its sibling families: "no first backup has ever been dropped on this fleet" is readable from the very first scrape, before any layer has been created and before any restore has run, and the condition this counter exists to catch concentrates in a mass restore against a degraded backup store — exactly the moment an operator opens this dashboard for the first time, under pressure, when a missing series cannot support the assertion a zero one can. **The counter increments once per run, on every exit, including the exits that write nothing.** That identity is load-bearing rather than tidy: `sum by (outcome)` equals the number of times the writer ran, which is what turns `shed` into a **saturation ratio** rather than a bare count, and what makes a zero total a usable "the writer never ran at all" statement — a question a refusal-only counter cannot express. **The site split is the operator-relevant dimension and the two sites are not equally serious.** A problem at the creation site delays a brand-new layer's first backup, and the periodic backup loop normally covers that layer within an interval or two anyway. A problem at the restore site is the one to watch: for a permanently hot layer, the backup written after its restore is realistically the only full one it will get, because it never falls quiet long enough for an ordinary one and has no chain to compose — so a write dropped there leaves that layer without backup coverage after its own recovery, at the worst possible time, with nothing routine to bring it back. **What to do about a non-zero `shed` at the restore site**, which is where those writes are dropped rather than queued when the writer's in-flight admission bound is saturated (queueing would keep a layer's whole state resident in memory, which is precisely what the bound exists to cap): read it as a ratio against the sum across all outcomes at the same site rather than as a count, then **re-restore the affected layers**, and raise the **pod memory limit and `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE` together** — the bound is derived against the product of those two, so raising the layer-size limit on its own moves the arithmetic the wrong way. The bound itself has no environment variable and is deliberately not tunable, because no observable signal would tell you how to set such a knob. The three `unstamped_` outcomes are one grouping rather than three unrelated cells — the backup landed on the store but the layer's record does not point at it — and nothing is lost in that state: every later reader still sees a layer with unsaved edits and backs it up again on the next eligible pass. What it costs is the coverage between now and that pass, plus, at the restore site, an outstanding restore marker that keeps incremental capture refusing on `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="refused_seq_gap"}` until it clears. **Additive and non-breaking:** the family is purely additional — `persisted` pairs one-to-one with the `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="success", kind="layer"}` those runs already moved and `failed` with `{result="error", kind="layer"}` on every reachable cause, and `unstamped_write_error` splits an existing error tick into an actionable sub-case rather than adding a second error family — so **no configuration change, no new environment variable, no existing metric name, label, default or alert recipe changes, and no change to how or when either backup is written.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore.
- **Rejected Valkey authentication under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` is now self-diagnosed: the service says which of the four known causes fired instead of leaving you with the backing store's one opaque error** (Issue `b.cbe`). A managed backing store answers *every* distinct cause of a rejected `AUTH` with the same string — `WRONGPASS invalid username-password pair` — and the four causes have entirely different fixes, so an operator previously had to eliminate them by hand against a cache they may not administer. The service holds the two halves the backing store does not expose: its own configuration, and the token it is about to send. It now reads the **non-secret** claims of that token — audience, object ID, the client-id claim, tenant, issuer, expiry — and cross-checks the identity against `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_USERNAME`. **The token itself is never logged and no signature is verified**: this is a diagnostic, never an authorization decision, and the check degrades silently to the generic advice when the credential is not a JWT, or when there is none cached to inspect. Three new operator-visible log surfaces, all carrying a structured `identity_verdict` field: **(1)** on the first rejected connection at startup, a **warning** rendering a four-cause checklist — username set to the identity's client ID instead of its Object ID; wrong token audience/scope; token identity not matching the configured username (federation misbinding, most often a missing workload-identity pod label); and no data-plane access-policy assignment for the identity, or one not yet `Succeeded` — with each cause annotated by what the check actually proved. That warning is emitted **once per startup connect** — one line per boot, covering all five of the service's connection phases and every retry within them, not one line per retry — and it is a warning rather than an error because the connect is still retrying and the rejection may yet clear; a boot that never authenticates exhausts its connect budget, exits, and is restarted by Kubernetes, which is the escalation rather than the log level. When the username *is* the client ID the service names that as the cause and prints the Object ID to substitute; when the token checks out clean it states by deduction that the first and third causes are ruled out from its own data and that the access-policy assignment is the most likely one left, which is the case that is otherwise invisible from inside the service. **(2)** The same checklist, at **error** level, from a *running* replica that starts being refused mid-life — an access-policy assignment revoked or moved to `Failed` under a live pod, which previously produced nothing but a silent reconnect loop — rate-limited to at most one every five minutes across the whole process. This one fires only on a `WRONGPASS`, which is how a persistent rejection presents: the other authentication error a managed endpoint can send mid-connection (`NOAUTH`) is a request to re-authenticate rather than a refusal, the service's automatic reconnect absorbs it, and it deliberately does not raise the checklist. **(3)** On a successful connect, one INFO recording the verdict and the same non-secret claims, so a healthy deployment leaves a baseline to compare a later failure against. The audience is **reported, never judged**: this mode is issuer-agnostic, so the service carries no list of expected audiences and prints the one it saw rather than accusing a correct non-Azure deployment; the expected value is in `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Token refresh. The `iam` and `static` modes get their own shorter hints naming the variables that feed those credentials. A token file that is *missing or unparseable* is a different fault — the service has no credential to send rather than one the server refused — and is still reported separately, so this checklist never fires at an operator whose actual problem is a sidecar that stopped writing. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no new metric, and no change to how tokens are served or authentication behaves.** The new **Troubleshooting AUTH failures (WRONGPASS)** section (now [`docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md`](docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md) § Troubleshooting `WRONGPASS`) is the canonical operator reference for the four causes and what to check for each; `docs/service/docs/troubleshooting.md` § Startup and readiness says where to look in the logs.
- **New Prometheus gauge `live_edit_service_token_refresh_headroom_seconds{role}` and a paired warning tell you whether your credential sidecar's refresh lead is actually big enough** (Issue `b.is1`). *(The numbers in this entry — the 375-second recommendation and the 360-second warning threshold — are superseded later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.7td`, which relaxes them to **195** and **180** seconds respectively; see the `### Fixed` entry below. The gauge, the warning, and everything else described here are unchanged.)* Under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` the documented recommendation is that the token source refresh at least 375 seconds before expiry (see the `b.d6b` entry under `### Changed`), but the service could never check it: that lead is a setting on your sidecar, not on this service, and is invisible to it at startup. It now measures the outcome instead. Every time a replacement token is picked up, the new gauge records how much life the token being replaced still had at that moment — the window in which the service's periodic re-authentication can still carry the new credential onto connections holding the old one, with the real pickup delay included rather than assumed. `role` is `valkey` or `storage-api`. It is **signed**: a negative reading means the replacement did not arrive until after the old token had already expired, so every pooled connection was dropped and rebuilt rather than renewed in place. On the Valkey reader a reading below **360 seconds** additionally logs a **warning** naming the headroom observed, the value recommended, and the two ways to close the gap — raise the sidecar's own refresh lead, or raise `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_REFRESH_LEAD_SECS` to at least the sidecar's lead minus one token-file poll interval, which moves pickup onto the service's fast 250 ms re-read and tightens the recommendation from 375 seconds to about 360. (That second lever is newly documented: the Helm chart previously described raising this setting as having no effect, which was wrong.) Both the reading and the warning happen once per token rotation, so expect at most one of each per token lifetime — typically one a day — not a log flood. The outbound service-identity reader records the gauge but never warns: it re-reads on demand after an upstream rejection and holds no long-lived authenticated connection, so it has no equivalent window to miss. **Also changed: `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_REFRESH_LEAD_SECS` now rejects absurdly large values at startup** (anything too large for the timestamp arithmetic the setting feeds — a representability bound, not an operational ceiling). Previously a value past that limit crashed the service on startup instead of reporting a configuration error, and a value at or above `2^63` silently wrapped to a *negative* lead, which switched off the early re-reading of the token file altogether without any error. Zero is still rejected. There is deliberately **no practical upper limit** beyond representability — an oversized lead withholds no credential and only re-reads the token file over a wider window. **No behavior change to how tokens are served, no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no changed defaults**: the re-authentication band, the 375-second recommendation, and the 180-second default are all unchanged. See `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Token refresh, `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Access-token file, and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are credentials and auth services healthy? for the gauge, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for its alert rule.
- **The Helm chart now ships a `startupProbe`, and the liveness and readiness probes carry explicit timeouts and thresholds** (Issue `b.rgs`). **Operators upgrading the chart get this by default; no values change is needed**, and per-environment values need no companion change unless they override the probes. The new `startupProbe` gives a booting replica a **280-second budget** (`periodSeconds: 5` × `failureThreshold: 56`, starting at `initialDelaySeconds: 0`) to become ready before the pod is restarted, and suspends the liveness and readiness probes until it first succeeds — so a slow-but-progressing boot cannot be killed as if it were a wedged one, while a boot that genuinely wedges still gets restarted rather than sitting out of rotation forever. It deliberately targets the **readiness** health name (`nvidia.omniverse.live.v1alpha.LiveService`) rather than the overall (`""`) one: with the early bind described in the `### Changed` entry above, the overall name answers within milliseconds of process start, so a startup probe pointed there would pass on its first attempt every time and protect nothing — and because the liveness probe also targets that name, it would leave a wedged boot with no restart path at all. The 280 seconds is the **sum** of the startup costs that can actually reach tens of seconds, because they run one after the other rather than instead of each other: the worst-case Valkey connect budget across the service's five sequential connect phases — four connection pools plus the dedicated pub/sub connection — (five times `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, so 150 seconds at the 30-second default), then the counter-recovery retry tail on a cold start (about 90 seconds), then the 5-second handoff to the full API, rounded up for scheduling jitter. A cluster-wide restart is exactly the case that pays both of the large terms, since it brings up a cold Valkey and a cold credential sidecar together. It does **not** cover a browning-out backup store, where the counter-recovery tail can run to about 91 minutes; that is a sick dependency rather than a slow boot, and restarting the pod is the right answer. **Trade-off if you deploy with `helm upgrade --wait`:** at 280 seconds the startup budget leaves only about 20 seconds of a 5-minute wait, so a boot that actually spends its full budget will time out the release wait even though the pod goes on to become ready — raise `--timeout` rather than lowering `startupProbe.failureThreshold`, which would restart pods that were still making progress. **If you raise `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, raise `startupProbe.failureThreshold` to match** — the two are not cross-validated, because one lives in the chart and the other in the service's environment. Set `startupProbe: null` to omit the probe entirely. The liveness and readiness probes additionally gain an explicit `timeoutSeconds: 2` (the kubelet's implicit default is 1 second, tight for a gRPC dial on a loaded node) and explicit `failureThreshold` / `successThreshold` values matching what the kubelet previously applied implicitly. The liveness probe deliberately gains **no** `initialDelaySeconds`: the startup probe already suspends liveness for the whole of boot, so a startup delay on liveness could only take effect after the replica was already ready. `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` and the shutdown sequence are unchanged. See `docs/service/docs/operations.md` § Health probes and startup.
- **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_stream_id_collision_total{backend, site}` reports edits refused because the layer's update stream and its edit counter had diverged** (Issue `b.ph9`). `backend` (`lua` \| `txn`) names which of the two internal commit paths refused the write and `site` (`update` \| `create`) whether it was an edit to an existing layer or the creation of a new one; three cells are reachable and all are pre-initialised at zero, which matters because zero is the expected reading forever on a healthy deployment and a series that only materialises on first occurrence is indistinguishable from one that never fired. **Any increment is actionable**: on `site="update"` writes to that layer are failing closed and will keep failing until an operator clears its update stream, while reads and subscriptions continue to work. Read it alongside `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_attempts_total{scope="layer"}` and `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_stream_refused_total` to confirm whether a recent restore is the cause. Routine races — a write arriving as the same layer is deleted or re-uploaded — hit the same internal refusal for a harmless reason, and the service re-checks the layer before counting, so they surface to the client as the ordinary "layer is gone / was replaced" errors and leave this counter flat; if that re-check cannot be completed because of a backing-store blip, the write is retried against fresh state rather than guessed at, which also leaves the counter flat. An increment therefore really does mean an unexplained divergence. Per-layer identifiers are deliberately not labels (unbounded cardinality) — the accompanying warning log names the session, layer, and stream ID, and carries remediation specific to the site (clearing the layer's stream for an edit refusal; checking the object-id counter's high-water mark for a creation refusal). Additive and non-breaking: no configuration change, and no existing metric name, label, or recipe changes. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?
- **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_adapter_ceiling_elapsed_total{phase, service}` and two additions to the `live_edit_checkpoint_config_*_seconds` gauge family report the backup store's enforced time ceilings** (Issue `b.gg7`). The counter's `phase` values are `call` (one request to the backup store ran for the full per-call ceiling — 180 seconds at the defaults — and was abandoned) and `enumeration` (a multi-request listing walk was abandoned at a listing deadline — either the adapter's own ceiling, 540 seconds at the defaults, or the tighter deadline the housekeeping pass imposes from its own time budget — always whole rather than returning a partial listing). Both cells are pre-initialised at zero, which matters more than usual here: this counter is expected to read zero forever on a healthy deployment, so without zero-seeding "the ceiling never fired" would be indistinguishable from a missing series. It gives the existing `live_edit_checkpoint_lease_total{outcome="lost"}` triage its first direct signal — "the backup store is slow" was previously an inference from the retry arithmetic, and is now confirmable. The two new gauges, `live_edit_checkpoint_config_adapter_call_ceiling_seconds` and `live_edit_checkpoint_config_reconcile_grace_seconds` (the family is now six), publish the ceiling and the reclamation grace this replica actually derived — adapter-dependent, `180` / `540` on `StorageApi` at the defaults and `5` / `60` on the local adapters — so alert thresholds can be written against them instead of recomputed from `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` by hand. The service deliberately does **not** warn when the per-call ceiling exceeds `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS`: that is true at the shipped defaults and is not a misconfiguration, so a warning would fire on every default deployment; the gauges let each fleet choose its own threshold on that ratio instead. The startup `checkpoint loop spawned` log line also now carries the derived call ceiling, layer-publish ceiling, reclamation grace, and housekeeping interval, none of which are readable off a deployment manifest because each is a product of settings rather than a setting. Additive and non-breaking — no new configuration, and no existing metric name, label, or recipe changes. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current?
- **New `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS` environment variable (default `3600`, or the derived reclamation grace period if that is longer) sets how often the backup store's housekeeping pass runs** (Issue `b.9qd`). The pass reclaims leftovers from backups that failed part-way through and deletes external-value blobs no remaining backup references (see the `### Changed` entry above for what it does and why). Hourly is the deliberate default: the work is pure storage reclamation on a cold path with nothing waiting on it, and each pass lists the backup store's whole layer namespace, which costs real money on a metered blob backend. Raising the frequency reclaims storage sooner; lowering it reduces listing traffic. **Zero is rejected at startup**, and the value is **cross-validated at startup to be at least the derived reclamation grace period** — below that, no candidate could ever be observed twice a grace apart, so every pass would pay for the full listing and reclaim nothing, forever, with no error. The grace itself is deliberately not configurable (see above). **The default obeys that same rule rather than being a flat hour**: leaving the variable unset resolves to the larger of `3600` and the derived grace, so a deployment that raises `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` far enough to push the grace past an hour still boots without having to discover and set this knob. An explicitly set value is taken as given and still validated. The pass is leader-elected on the same lease the periodic backup loop already holds, so it adds no new coordination key and a second replica does not run it in parallel with retention cleanup by design (the lease is a renewed lock rather than hard mutual exclusion — one slow backup-store call can outlast it — which is why nothing in the pass deletes anything it has not observed twice a grace apart, and why both remedies are idempotent). Each pass also bounds how long it spends reclaiming (a quarter of the interval, capped at the grace) so housekeeping cannot crowd out the backups themselves; a large accumulated backlog drains over several passes, each resuming where the last stopped without restarting any candidate's grace. Most operators have no reason to change the default. Not yet exposed as a Helm chart value; operators who need to override can set it via the pod's raw `env:` block.
- **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_reconcile_total{outcome, service}` and gauge `live_edit_checkpoint_ext_tombstone_backlog{service}` report backup-store housekeeping** (Issue `b.9qd`). The counter's `outcome` values are `orphan_deferred` (a leftover seen but not acted on this pass — either still inside its grace period, which is the healthy shape while a backup is in flight and not a fault, or aged out but past the pass's reclamation time budget, which a non-zero `orphan_deleted` alongside it distinguishes), `orphan_deleted`, `temp_deleted`, `ext_tombstoned` (blobs recorded as deletion candidates — ticked by both the housekeeping pass and ordinary retention cleanup, because both are the same act), `ext_collected` (**the only cell representing storage actually reclaimed**), `ext_spared` (a recorded blob found referenced again and kept — **non-zero here is the guard working**, and is precisely the wrong deletion the previous one-step cleanup would have committed), and `live_set_failed`. Every cell is pre-initialised at zero, which matters more than usual on an hourly pass: a series that materialises only on first occurrence is indistinguishable from a pass that never ran. The gauge counts blobs currently awaiting their grace period or a successful deletion; it is sampled by the leader and published as `0` by every other replica, so **aggregate it with `max()`, never `sum()`** — and read it as a derivative rather than a level, since a backlog that rises and falls with retention churn is the two-stage protocol working normally. Two new alert recipes ship with them: sustained backlog growth, and a wedged-fleet-wide condition where one malformed reference file on the backup store stops external-value reclamation everywhere (the live-set read is deliberately fail-closed, because skipping a file it cannot read would under-count what is still in use and turn the difference into a wrong deletion). Additive and non-breaking — no existing metric name, label, or recipe changes. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current?
- **New `live_edit_checkpoint_config_*_seconds` gauge family echoes the checkpoint pacing configuration so alert thresholds can be written relative to it** (Issue `b.ijf`). The `/metrics` scrape now carries four gauges labelled `service` — `live_edit_checkpoint_config_interval_seconds`, `live_edit_checkpoint_config_quiesce_seconds`, `live_edit_checkpoint_config_tick_seconds`, and the derived `live_edit_checkpoint_config_stuck_threshold_seconds` (`interval + quiesce + tick`) — reporting the values this replica booted with. They exist so the two checkpoint alert recipes that carry a numeric threshold can be expressed as a multiple of your configuration (e.g. `> 5 * max(live_edit_checkpoint_config_interval_seconds)`) instead of the literal `300` that is only correct at the default 60/30/10 pacing. **Both rewritten recipes evaluate to exactly 300 at the defaults, so adopting them is a no-op on a default-configured fleet**; a fleet running a non-default interval no longer has to rescale the thresholds by hand. The stuck-threshold gauge is computed by the same code the service uses for its own stuck-pending WARN, so the bound you alert on and the bound the service logs cannot drift apart. **The family is exported only by replicas that actually run the checkpoint loop** (`LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ENABLED=true` with a real adapter) — its absence is itself the "this replica does not checkpoint" signal, and the consequence for alerting (an expression with an empty side never fires) is documented with the recipes. Additive and non-breaking: no new configuration, no existing metric name, label, or recipe changes, and the previous absolute-threshold rules keep working. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? for the metric entry and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Recommended alerts and § Additional alert rule definitions for the updated recipes.
- **New `catchup_mode` label and `live_subscribe_catch_up_delivered_bytes` histogram on the `/metrics` SubscribeReadLayer catch-up series.** The `/metrics` scrape now carries an additive `catchup_mode` label (values `snapshot` and `legacy`) on `live_subscribe_regime_total` and `live_subscribe_catch_up_duration_seconds`, plus a new histogram `live_subscribe_catch_up_delivered_bytes` reporting the OMPF byte count delivered during catch-up. `catchup_mode="snapshot"` marks the from-empty faithful full-state catch-up served to a brand-new / late-join subscriber (under the `snapshot` catch-up mode); every other catch-up reports `legacy` — so read `catchup_mode` together with `regime`, not as a fidelity verdict (a faithful out-of-window reconnect still reports `legacy`). This is an additive, non-breaking observability addition: existing metric names, the `regime` label values, and every existing dashboard/alert/PromQL recipe keep working unchanged, and no configuration changes. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving? for the label semantics.
- **Corrected faithful full-state catch-up is now ON by default for clients joining or rejoining a session mid-stream, gated by the new boolean `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED` environment variable (default `true`, accepts `true` or `false`)** (Plan `b.msb`, Issue `b.pev`). `true` (the default) serves a corrected faithful full-state snapshot (authoring order, net-live state, no tombstones) to late-joining / rejoining subscribers — the fix for the late-join/rejoin catch-up fidelity defect. `false` reverts to the legacy per-commit reconstruction path and exists as a rollback knob. This variable replaces the earlier enum `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_EXPERIMENT` (`off`/`snapshot`, default `off`), which never shipped in a release. No wire or API change.
- **The service is now enumerable by the v2 Discovery Kubernetes operator via a new Discovery v2alpha `CapabilitiesService`** (Issue `b.hv2`). The gRPC server exposes `nvidia.omniverse.discovery.capabilities.v2alpha.CapabilitiesService` alongside the existing `LiveService`; its single `ListServices` RPC returns a static response advertising the `nvidia.omniverse.live.v1alpha.LiveService` gRPC service under the fleet discovery type key `live-service` (the same `type` used by the v1 static discovery registry), with `rest_services` empty (the service is gRPC-only — the `/metrics` port is Prometheus scrape plumbing, not a service API) and pagination fields ignored. The RPC is **unauthenticated by design**: the operator scans k8s Services carrying the `discovery.omniverse.nvidia.com/enabled: "true"` annotation and dials their `grpc` port over plaintext h2c with no credentials; only the fully-qualified gRPC service name — already public via server reflection — is disclosed. The CapabilitiesService is marked `SERVING` in gRPC health statically at startup, independent of the dependency-aware readiness verdict (the operator retains previously discovered entries on a failed poll, so transient dependency blips do not evict the service from discovery output). **Operator action for discovery enablement**: the Helm chart gains a generic `service.annotations` value (default `{}`) rendered onto the k8s Service; per-environment opt-in is done by setting the discovery annotation in per-environment values — the chart itself bakes in no discovery-specific defaults, so existing deployments are unaffected until an environment opts in.
- OMPE-98174: live-edit metrics can now be exported over OTLP/gRPC via the standardized `observability.metrics` Helm block, in addition to the existing Prometheus scrape endpoint. Metrics are re-instrumented on the OpenTelemetry metrics API behind a dual-reader meter provider: the `/metrics` scrape endpoint is always served, and when `observability.metrics.enabled` is true its `endpoint.grpc` is exported as `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_ENDPOINT` and the same metrics are additionally pushed to a collector over OTLP/gRPC. Metric names, labels, types, and buckets are unchanged, so existing dashboards, alerts, and the `serviceMonitor` / `prometheus.io/scrape` path keep working. Metrics and tracing are independently configurable — enabling `observability.tracing` alone does not push metrics, and enabling `observability.metrics` alone does not export spans. When the metrics block is disabled (the default) no OTLP metrics env var is rendered and metrics are collected via the Prometheus scrape only (backward compatible).
- OMPE-98174: Add standardized `observability` block to the Helm chart for configuring OTEL telemetry via OTLP/gRPC. When `observability.tracing.enabled` is true, the tracing gRPC endpoint is exported as `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`, enabling OTLP span export by the service's OpenTelemetry SDK (a Helm `required` guard errors clearly if the endpoint is omitted while enabled). Metrics are exposed via the Prometheus scrape endpoint (`service.metrics` / `serviceMonitor`) and can additionally be pushed over OTLP/gRPC via the separate `observability.metrics` block (see the dedicated entry above). Fully backward compatible: without an enabled `observability` block no OTEL env var is set and the rendered manifest is unchanged.
- **New `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED` environment variable (default `false`) lets the service run without an external userinfo service by extracting caller identity from JWT-format bearer tokens in-process.** When `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` (the new default), the service no longer calls the userinfo backend — a new `JwtUserinfoClient` decodes the bearer token's JWT payload and walks a claim fallback chain (`name` → `preferred_username` → `sub` → `oid`) to produce the display identity. `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT` is not consulted on this path and may be omitted entirely. When `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=true`, the userinfo service handles identity resolution exactly as before via `RestUserinfoClient` (default) or `GrpcUserinfoClient` (`LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL=grpc`); `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT` is required on that path. **Opaque-token caveat (operator-visible).** When the userinfo service is disabled, clients MUST send JWT-format bearer tokens — opaque (non-JWT) tokens cannot have identity extracted in-process. Any RPC carrying a non-JWT bearer token under `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` fails the call. The verbatim message `"opaque bearer token requires the userinfo service; deploy the userinfo service or use JWT-format bearer tokens"` is the **operator-server-side** signal — it appears in the service's `tracing::error!` operator logs (which operators read in their log aggregator) and at the internal `AuthError` boundary; on the gRPC wire the client sees the redacted `INTERNAL` status with the fixed message `"internal error"` per the b.ohw security contract (backend detail is deliberately stripped so a buggy or malicious backend cannot leak token material into client-visible status messages). Operators discover the JWT-only-mode invariant violation by reading the service logs, not by inspecting the gRPC error returned to the client. The error is also memoized in the userinfo negative cache (`LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_USERINFO_NEGATIVE_TTL_SECS`, default 5 s) for the same TTL as other userinfo backend failures — semantically harmless because a malformed token stays malformed within the window, so the cache hit just skips a re-decode that would deterministically fail again. See `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Caller identity for the operator-facing setup. The new `JwtUserinfoClient` is a sibling implementation of `RestUserinfoClient` / `GrpcUserinfoClient` and goes through the same `UserinfoClient` trait, so the auth cache, retry, and singleflight machinery layered above the trait apply uniformly across all three backends.
- **New `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS` and `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` environment variables** (bug `b.w29`). Defaults `60` and `15` respectively, raised from the pre-fix hard-coded `30` / `10`. The bumped defaults give one missed refresh tick under tokio scheduler pressure ample headroom before the sweeper considers a connection stale. **Cross-validated at startup**: `Config::from_env` rejects any combination where `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS < 2 × LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` so a single dropped refresh cannot expire the lease prematurely. Zero is rejected on both knobs (`SET ... EX 0` is a Valkey syntax error; zero refresh would saturate the data pool). Operators upgrading from the pre-fix service inherit the new defaults transparently; the bump is forward-compatible (lease keys created against the new TTL remain valid under the old refresh cadence, just with more headroom). Deep-tuning knobs — not yet exposed on the Helm chart; operators who need to override can do so via the pod's raw `env:` block.
- **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_health_probe_failures_total{backend}`** (bug `b.u96` item 8). `backend` ∈ {`valkey`, `permissions`, `userinfo`}. Incremented once per readiness-supervisor probe call that the supervisor scored as a failure, BEFORE the per-backend consecutive-failure smoother (`LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_AUTH_FAILURE_THRESHOLD`) runs. The counter therefore captures every failed probe — including the ones the smoother absorbs without flipping readiness — so partial-degradation incidents (one backend flaking at 10 % below the smoother threshold) are visible to operators that pre-b.u96 readiness-only dashboards could not see. `AuthError::InvalidResponse` continues to be classified as **reachable** (the backend answered) and does NOT tick the counter. All three backend labels are pre-initialised at zero on process start so `rate(... [5m])` alerts see a continuous derivative from boot. The operator-facing entry is in `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?
- **New `LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_PROBE_INTERVAL_MS` and `LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_PROBE_TIMEOUT_MS` environment variables make the readiness health-supervisor probe cadence and per-probe deadline configurable** (bug `b.avd` item 9). Defaults are `5000` and `2000` respectively, matching the prior hardcoded values. Both knobs are rejected at startup if set to zero (the interval would spin; the timeout would synchronously fail every probe), and `Config::from_env` cross-validates that `LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_PROBE_TIMEOUT_MS < LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_PROBE_INTERVAL_MS` so a slow probe cannot queue past the next tick. Deep-tuning knobs — intentionally not exposed on the Helm chart; operators who need to lower probe cadence for faster outage detection (or raise it to shed Valkey / auth-backend probe traffic) can set the env vars on the Deployment pod directly.
- **New Prometheus gauge `live_edit_inflight_rpcs{endpoint, service}` exposes in-flight RPC count per endpoint** (bug `b.avd` item 5). Ticked up on entry into the metrics-layer wrapper and decremented via an RAII guard at the end of the response future, so it tracks real call lifetime including the timeout / cancellation paths where the response future is dropped before completing. Saturation signal: a gauge that climbs on a single endpoint without a corresponding climb in `live_endpoint_duration_microseconds` p99 means callers are arriving faster than they're being processed. Every (endpoint, service) cell is pre-initialised at zero on first scrape so dashboards see a continuous series from process start.
- **New `grpc_code` label on `live_endpoint_calls_total` and `live_endpoint_duration_microseconds`** (bug `b.avd` item 4). Per-code call rates and p99 latency are now queryable directly without joining against a separate error counter, e.g. `rate(live_endpoint_calls_total{grpc_code="DEADLINE_EXCEEDED"}[5m])` for client-deadline timeouts. The slug for `grpc_code` is `"OK"` on success and the canonical tonic name for errors (`"INVALID_ARGUMENT"`, `"NOT_FOUND"`, `"DEADLINE_EXCEEDED"`, …); values outside the known set collapse to `"UNKNOWN"`. Cardinality is bounded: 17 gRPC codes × 11 endpoint labels × the service label. Every (endpoint, service, grpc_code) cell is pre-initialised at zero on first scrape so `rate(... [5m])` alerts see a continuous derivative from boot rather than a step-change on the first call of a given code. **Operator action**: dashboards or alerts that grouped by `(endpoint, service)` continue to work unchanged — Prometheus aggregates over the new label by default; queries that pin a specific code now don't need a separate metric.
- **`live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total{pool}` and `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{pool, kind}` now cover all five pools** (bug `b.avd` item 7). `install_observability_hooks` now attaches fred reconnect / error broadcast subscribers to all five pools (`subscriber`, `data`, `txn`, `subscriber_pool`, `maintenance`); pre-b.avd only `subscriber` and `data` were hooked, so any reconnect or error on the three exclusive / maintenance pools was silently invisible and alert rules keyed off `rate(valkey_reconnects_total{pool="txn"}[5m])` could never fire. The `pool` label is also pre-initialised at zero for all five pools so `rate(...[5m])` sees a continuous derivative from boot instead of a step-change on first event. The `kind` dimension on `valkey_errors_total` remains opened lazily so an unknown fred `ErrorKind` does not materialise as a permanent zero series.

- **Helm chart now exposes the remaining operator-facing env vars as first-class chart values** (bug `b.39e`). Seven env vars that previously had to be set via the pod's raw `env:` block — `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_LIST_TIMEOUT_MS`, `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS`, `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_MAX_ATTEMPTS`, `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_RETRY_INITIAL_DELAY_MS`, `LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_AUTH_FAILURE_THRESHOLD`, `LIVE_EDIT_GC_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS`, and `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_LABEL` — now have dedicated `values.yaml` keys (`rpc.session_list_timeout_ms`, `rpc.deadline_ms`, `auth.max_attempts`, `auth.retry_initial_delay_ms`, `health.authFailureThreshold`, `gc.poll_interval_secs`, `serviceLabel`). Two of these (`health.authFailureThreshold`, `serviceLabel`) are emitted to the ConfigMap only when set to a non-empty / non-zero value so the binary's own defaults still apply when the chart key is left at its default; the rest are emitted unconditionally. No operator action required for deployments already happy with the binary defaults. Operators previously injecting any of these via raw `env:` should migrate to the new chart keys before redeploy.
- **CI: `cargo-binstall` is now version-pinned and SHA-256-verified in `.gitlab-ci.yml`** (bug `b.39e`). The `supply-chain:audit`, `supply-chain:deny`, and `supply-chain:attributions-drift` jobs now download a pinned `cargo-binstall` archive (`CARGO_BINSTALL_VERSION=1.10.13`) and verify its SHA-256 (`CARGO_BINSTALL_SHA256=3d3bb126…b13ac0bd`) before extracting, closing a silent supply-chain surface where any future `cargo-binstall` release would have been trusted implicitly. The bump procedure lives in the in-file comment block above `CARGO_BINSTALL_VERSION` — fetch the linux-gnu tarball via `gh release download` and compute the SHA-256 with `shasum -a 256`; cargo-binstall publishes a minisign `.tgz.sig` for optional cross-verification but no separate `.tgz.sha256` artifact.

- New `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_USERINFO_NEGATIVE_TTL_SECS` environment variable (default `5`, bug `b.ohw`). Caches userinfo backend failures for a short TTL so a long-running client holding an expired bearer token does not pound the userinfo backend on every RPC. Memoizes `AuthError::InvalidResponse("invalid or expired token")` (the expected response from userinfo for an expired bearer) plus transient `Timeout` / `Unreachable` errors (so a flapping backend cannot trigger a stampede). Independent of `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_NEGATIVE_TTL_SECS` because userinfo failures have a different recovery profile (token refresh, not policy revocation). Set to `0` to disable the negative cache (every error re-issues the lookup). Pre-b.ohw the userinfo cache only memoized successful lookups, so an expired token caused every RPC on that token to hit userinfo. Helm chart exposes it as `auth.userinfo_negative_ttl_secs`.
- New `ServiceError::Unavailable(String)` variant mapped to gRPC `UNAVAILABLE` (code 14, retriable; bug `b.ohw` H-grpc-1). Reserved for transient upstream-dependency outages — currently the auth backend (`AuthError::Timeout` and `AuthError::Unreachable` now route through it). `INTERNAL` continues to carry conditions that warrant operator attention (auth-backend protocol violations, internal invariants). Documented in SDD §18.1.

- New `LIVE_EDIT_MAINTENANCE_POOL_SIZE` environment variable (default `1`, bug `b.kf1`). Sizes a dedicated multiplexed Valkey pool used exclusively by the periodic external-value GC sweep and the orphan sweep so their SCAN / HGETALL / EVALSHA traffic cannot queue behind hot-path writers and readers on the data pool. One connection is sufficient because both sweeps are gated behind a shared `le:gc:leader` short-TTL lock (`SET le:gc:leader <replica_id> EX 30 NX`) — only one replica per fleet runs them at a time, and the sweep loop itself is serial inside the leader. Operators with very large keyspaces who want extra parallelism inside a single sweep can raise this; values above ~4 rarely help. Zero is rejected at startup.
- New Prometheus counter `live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total{kind}` (bug `b.kf1`). Exposes external-value GC sweep batch failures, labeled by `kind` (`transient_io`, `aborted`, `internal`, `other`, `leader_lock_error`, `ext_value_schedule`, or any fred `ErrorKind::to_str()` slug for orphan-sweep errors). Pre-b.kf1 these errors were tracing-only, which made "GC stopped collecting" silent until disk filled. Operators should alert on `rate(live_edit_gc_sweep_batch_errors_total[15m]) > 0` sustained for more than a few intervals — sustained non-zero rate is the signal that the GC pipeline has stalled and external-value disk usage is growing unboundedly.
- **Session lifecycle hardening** (bug `b.91j`). `CreateSession` duplicate-name uniqueness is now race-free on Valkey Cluster; `DeleteSession` cascade bails fast on backend outages (no more `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` amplification on multi-layer sessions); `base_path` is now validated for length (≤ 4,096 bytes), null bytes, and control characters. New env vars `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS` (default `32`) and `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_RETRY_INITIAL_DELAY_MS` (default `1`) cap the new `CreateSession` retry budget, mirroring the shape of the existing `UpdateLayer` retry knobs. Full-jitter backoff with the window capped at 50 ms. Zero is rejected at startup on `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS`.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_FORMAT` environment variable (default `json`, accepts `json` or `pretty` case-insensitive; bug `b.3z7`) selects the tracing fmt layer. `json` is the production default — structured records for log aggregators. `pretty` emits human-readable multi-line output intended for local development (`cargo run`, `kubectl port-forward` debugging); the Helm chart exposes it as `logging.format`. The OpenTelemetry span-export layer (active when `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` is set) is unaffected by the choice. Surrounding whitespace in the env-var value is now trimmed before parsing, so a quoted `" pretty "` in a Helm manifest parses correctly. Invalid values fail fast at startup.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_SWEEPER_INTERVAL_SECS` environment variable (default `15`, bug `b.3z7`) makes the presence crash-recovery sweeper scan cadence configurable. Previously hardcoded; operators who need faster stale-presence cleanup or who want to shed Valkey scan traffic can now tune without a rebuild. Zero is rejected at startup. Exposed on the Helm chart as `presence.sweeper_interval_secs`.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_SESSION_TOKENS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SECS` environment variable (default `60`, bug `b.j5t` item 5 / b.f36 A6) makes the background `session_tokens` crash-recovery sweep cadence configurable. The sweep probes each in-process `DashMap<session_id, CancellationToken>` entry against Valkey via `SessionStore::get_base_path` and removes + cancels entries whose session no longer exists — bounding the map against unbounded growth on long-running pods when sessions vanish without an explicit `DeleteSession` (abandoned clients, multi-day pub/sub outages, future deletes that bypass `le:session:deleted`). Previously hardcoded as `SESSION_TOKENS_SWEEP_INTERVAL`; operators can now tune cadence without a rebuild. Lower values reclaim stale entries faster at the cost of extra Valkey probe traffic; higher values reduce probe traffic at the cost of slightly slower reclamation. Zero is rejected at startup. Deep-tuning knob — most operators have no reason to change the default. See SDD §7.8 (background crash-recovery sweep).
- New `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS` environment variable (default `30`, bug `b.3z7`) caps the total post-gRPC-server shutdown sequence (layer cleanup + background-task joins + parallelised Valkey `quit()` + metrics-server abort) under a single `tokio::time::timeout`. Must fit inside `terminationGracePeriodSeconds - LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS`; operators raising either knob should raise the Helm chart's `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` accordingly. **Startup-validated**: the service refuses to boot if `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS < LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS + 10`, so a misconfigured pair fails fast at boot rather than silently logging "shutdown overall budget exceeded" on every restart. Exposed on the Helm chart as `shutdown.overall_budget_secs`.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_AUTH_FAILURE_THRESHOLD` environment variable (default `3`, bug `b.3z7`). When `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=true`, the readiness health supervisor probes the permissions and userinfo backends each tick (using a synthetic `__live_edit_health_probe_DO_NOT_USE__` token and `/__live_edit_health_probe__` path on the *uncached* instrumented clients — probing through the auth cache would short-circuit on hits and never observe an outage) and flips readiness to `NOT_SERVING` only after a backend fails this many consecutive probes. The counter resets on the first successful probe, so transient blips already absorbed by auth retry + singleflight cache (`b.nrt`) do not eject an otherwise-healthy replica from rotation. `Ok(_)` and `Err(InvalidResponse(_))` both count as reachable (the backend answered); `Err(Timeout|Unreachable)` and probe-timeout elapses count as failure. Zero is rejected at startup. Deep-tuning knob — intentionally not exposed on the Helm chart; operators who need to override can do so via the Deployment env block.
- New Prometheus counter `live_edit_valkey_reconnects_total{pool}` (`pool` ∈ {`subscriber`, `data`}) and counter `live_edit_valkey_errors_total{pool, kind}` (`kind` is the fred `ErrorKind::to_str()` value; bug `b.3z7`) provide a first-class "Valkey in trouble" signal. Sustained non-zero reconnect rate indicates a flapping backing store; `kind` lets operators tell timeouts from protocol errors without cross-referencing a separate table.
- New Prometheus gauge `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use{pool}` (`pool` ∈ {`txn`, `subscriber_pool`}; bug `b.3z7`) exposes exclusive-pool saturation. Populated by an RAII `PoolUseToken` returned alongside each `ValkeyClient::acquire_txn` / `acquire_subscriber_pool` guard — the token increments the gauge on acquire and decrements on Drop after any `WatchGuard` UNWATCH task completes, so the gauge tracks real pool release rather than synchronous `Drop::drop` return. A gauge that plateaus at the pool size means every concurrent writer is queueing on that pool — raise `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_POOL_SIZE` or `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE`.
- New Prometheus gauge `live_edit_storage_watch_unwatch_inflight` (bug `b.k1r`) exposes the number of `WatchGuard::Drop`-spawned UNWATCH cleanup tasks currently outstanding on the txn pool. Populated via a `/metrics` pre-scrape hook that reads the shared `tokio_util::task::TaskTracker` on every scrape (the tracker is the source of truth; the gauge is a snapshot). Non-zero at scrape time means at least one `update_layer` slow-path early-exit spawned an UNWATCH that hasn't completed yet — expected to be transient; a steady-state non-zero value across many scrapes points at an UNWATCH that is failing to complete (Valkey unreachable, command timeout) and warrants operator attention. `ValkeyClient::quit` drains the same tracker with a 2-second cap before closing the txn pool at shutdown.
- New Prometheus counter `live_edit_background_task_panics_total{task}` (`task` ∈ {`gc`, `orphan_sweep`, `presence_subscriber`, `presence_sweeper`, `health_supervisor`, `session_delete_subscriber`}; bug `b.3z7`) increments when the shutdown supervisor observes a spawned task's `JoinHandle::await` resolve to `Err(JoinError::is_panic())`. Any non-zero value is worth paging.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND` environment variable (default `lua`, accepts `lua` or `txn` case-insensitive; bug `b.67u`) selects the commit backend for `UpdateLayer`. `lua` dispatches field / time-sample / empty deltas through a single-RTT atomic server-side Lua EVAL; structural-command deltas use the legacy WATCH/MULTI/EXEC path regardless of the setting. `txn` forces every call down the legacy path — a no-redeploy rollback knob if the Lua path ever misbehaves in production. The gRPC contract is unchanged on both settings; `txn`-mode output is byte-for-byte identical to the pre-b.67u service.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS` environment variable (default `128`; default raised from `32` in `b.3gx` alongside the per-object admission semaphore so the slow-path single-call exhaustion probability at `K=16` is `(15/16)^128 ≈ 0.028 %`) caps the server-side retry budget for `UpdateLayer` WATCH/MULTI/EXEC conflicts. Zero is rejected at startup. Operators running contention-heavy workloads may raise this; operators who want the old fail-fast behavior can set it to `1`. Pair this env var with the two new retry metrics — `live_edit_update_layer_retries_total{outcome}` counter (`outcome` ∈ {`committed`, `exhausted`}) and `live_edit_update_layer_attempts_histogram` (attempts-per-successful-commit; buckets 1/2/4/8/16/32/64/128/+Inf — `64` and `128` added in `b.avd` item 6 to match the `b.3gx` retry-cap raise from 32 → 128) — to alert on retry-budget exhaustion (the `exhausted` label) and track steady-state contention (histogram p99).
- New `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL` environment variable (default `rest`, accepts `rest` or `grpc`, case-insensitive) selects the protocol used for both the storage (permissions) and userinfo service calls.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN` environment variable (default `4096`) caps the number of retained per-layer live-update entries in the new Valkey Streams transport.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY` environment variable (default `4096`) sizes the per-session `tokio::sync::broadcast` channel that fans presence events out to local `JoinSession` streams. Previously hard-coded at `256`, which was small enough that modest presence bursts on sessions with many peers could silently drop events. Operators seeing drops on the new `live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total` counter (see below) should raise this value.
- New Prometheus counter `live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total{service}` records the number of presence events dropped when the per-session broadcast channel overflows (incremented by `n` on every observed `broadcast::RecvError::Lagged(n)`, so the value reflects dropped-event count rather than lag-event count). A non-zero and growing value is the signal to raise `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_BROADCAST_CAPACITY`.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_NEGATIVE_TTL_SECS` environment variable (default `30`) caps revocation lag for negative auth decisions (`Denied` / `Unauthenticated` / `PathNotFound`). Previously every decision was cached for `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_TTL_SECS` (default 10 min), so a tightened permission or revoked token could remain denied-only-in-cache-but-allowed-in-truth (or allowed-in-cache-but-denied-in-truth inverted) for up to the full positive window. The positive TTL is unchanged — `Allowed` decisions still live for `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_TTL_SECS`.
- New `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_MAX_ENTRIES` environment variable (default `10000`) caps the size of each inner auth cache (positive-permissions, negative-permissions, userinfo — bounded independently). LRU-evicts when full; zero is rejected at startup. Pair with the TTL knobs to bound worst-case memory under token churn or flash-crowd traffic.
- New Prometheus auth-attribution metrics — `live_edit_auth_cache_hits_total{kind}` and `live_edit_auth_cache_misses_total{kind}` counters (`kind` ∈ {`permissions`, `userinfo`}), `live_edit_auth_call_duration_seconds{backend, protocol}` histogram (`backend` ∈ {`storage`, `userinfo`}, `protocol` ∈ {`rest`, `grpc`}, cache hits excluded), and `live_edit_auth_errors_total{backend, code}` counter (`code` ∈ {`timeout`, `unreachable`, `invalid_response`}) — give operators end-to-end attribution during auth-dependency incidents: whether latency or errors originate at the storage service, the userinfo service, or the cache path, and across which protocol.
- CI supply-chain gates on every MR and main-branch pipeline (bug `b.nko`). Four new GitLab CI jobs — `supply-chain:audit` (`cargo audit --deny warnings` over `Cargo.lock`), `supply-chain:deny` (`cargo deny check advisories licenses bans` against the new `deny.toml`), `supply-chain:helm-lint` (`helm lint helm/live-edit-service`), and `supply-chain:attributions-drift` (regenerates `ATTRIBUTIONS.md` from `Cargo.lock` and fails on drift) — make dependency advisories, license policy, chart validity, and attributions drift into gating checks instead of best-effort local discipline. Dev-facing only; no operator action required. Container image scanning remains covered by the platform-wide SBOM scanner consuming the existing `.build:sbom` output — a repo-local image-scan gate is deferred (see the in-file comment in `.gitlab-ci.yml`).
- New Prometheus counter `live_edit_delta_create_section_skipped_missing_parent_total` (bug `b.39x` medium) makes the legacy "`CommandCreateSection` with missing parent → silent skip" path visible to operators. The SDD §9.1 step 4c contract is preserved (an unknown `parent_id` is still ignored rather than rejected, to avoid breaking replay of older deltas), but each skipped command now bumps this counter and emits a structured `tracing::debug!(section_id, parent_id, …)` log line. Pre-b.39x the skip was silent with no log or metric; combined with the H4 "base_version unvalidated" footgun, an unexpectedly-empty layer was undiagnosable. Sustained non-zero rate is the actionable signal that a producer is emitting `CreateSection` against parents not yet present on the current object — either a buggy client or a delta-ordering bug. The counter exists precisely because this pattern DOES occur in practice; ops dashboards should add a panel for the rate and alert on sustained non-zero values. Unlabeled — the rate alone is the actionable signal; the companion `tracing::debug!` line carries `section_id` / `parent_id` for forensic follow-up.

- **Checkpoint-based disaster recovery for Valkey state — operator-selectable durability layer outside Valkey, with lazy first-access recovery on Valkey loss** (Bee `b.fgj`, Epics 1-6). The Live Edit service ships a checkpoint-based DR layer that durably persists each layer's materialized state (sections, fields, time samples, owning-session metadata, layer-to-object mappings, and the referenced external-value blobs) outside Valkey on an operator-configured cadence, and lazily recovers that state on the first RPC that needs it after a Valkey-loss event. The customer-facing surface is a small env-var matrix layered on top of the existing service; clients see the same gRPC API and continue to use the normal connection-drop-and-reconnect signal they already handle. Pre-`b.fgj` Valkey AOF was the service's sole durability layer — fine for routine pod restarts at a ~1 s loss window but with nothing covering catastrophic Valkey loss (PVC eviction, AOF corruption, namespace deletion, total cluster loss, accidental deletion of the Valkey instance) and nothing for operators who chose to run Valkey without AOF for cost, throughput, or platform-policy reasons. Azure Managed Redis offers RDB backups at a 60-minute minimum RPO and an active-geo-replication option that is mutually exclusive with AOF — neither covers tight-RPO catastrophic-loss recovery and neither helps self-hosted no-persistence deployments. Operators now choose between three supported durability postures at deployment time: **belt-and-suspenders mode** with Valkey AOF on and checkpoints on (routine-restart loss ≤ 1 s, catastrophic-loss recovery point ≤ 60 s — the recommended default); **today's mode** with AOF on and checkpoints off (identical to pre-b.fgj behavior, no checkpoint cost); **no-Valkey-persistence mode** with AOF off and checkpoints on (any-restart loss ≤ 60 s, for clusters that refuse AOF). The fourth combination — AOF off and checkpoints off — is refused at service startup so no deployment silently runs with zero durability; on Azure Managed Redis (where the platform blocks `CONFIG GET appendonly`) this guarantee degrades to a startup WARN rather than a hard refusal and is called out in the AMR deployment recipe.

  **Operator-selectable backend.** `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ADAPTER` (default `Disabled`, case-insensitive) selects between `Disabled`, `InMemory` (unit/integration testing), `LocalDisk` (development, integration testing, air-gapped deployments — requires `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_LOCAL_DISK_PATH`), and `StorageApi` (the production target — requires `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_STORAGE_API_ENDPOINT` with an explicit `https://` / `http://` scheme and `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_BASE_PATH`). `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ENABLED=false` (default) turns the feature off entirely and is the no-cost upgrade default. Backend choice is global per deployment — checkpoints are never mirrored across multiple backends and the choice is not a per-request decision. The abstraction is forward-extensible: future adapters (direct cloud blob storage, Postgres-backed checkpoints) can be added without touching the checkpoint or recovery code.

  **Cadence and retention knobs** — `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS` (default `60`, the upper bound on the gap between two backups **of the same layer**, measured from when the previous one landed, so a layer under continuous editing is still backed up at least this often — this is the knob that sets the catastrophic-loss recovery point), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_QUIESCE_SECS` (default `30`, how long a layer must go without an edit before it is backed up *early* rather than waiting out the rest of the interval — OR'd with the interval bound, not a precondition for it; zero rejected), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TICK_SECS` (default `10`, how often the loop wakes and re-evaluates which layers are due — the scheduler's evaluation resolution, not a per-layer cadence; zero rejected; cross-validated `< LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS`), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_RETAIN_GENERATIONS` (default `2`, the per-layer retained-generation count for the corrupt-newest-blob fallback path; zero rejected), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_SEQ_SAFETY_MARGIN` (default `1000`, the guard band of sequence numbers a restored layer skips past so a recovery never re-issues numbers clients already saw for edits lost in the recovery window; zero rejected), `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_MS` (default `5000`; zero rejected), and `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (default `30000`, larger than the auth-RPC pattern because checkpoint blobs can be multi-MB per object; zero rejected).

  **What the cadence knobs actually bound, and how to monitor backup freshness.** Eligibility is re-evaluated once per tick, so the recovery point a given layer observes is its interval plus up to one tick, plus the time taken to assemble and write that layer's backup; the service enforces only that the tick is strictly shorter than the interval. The `live_edit_checkpoint_pending_age_seconds` histogram reports how stale the newest backup is for each layer that still has un-backed-up edits — layers already fully backed up contribute nothing however long they sit idle, so a quiet fleet does not inflate the distribution. Because the same interval knob bounds the value, a healthy fleet's p99 sits near `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS` plus one tick (~70 s at the defaults), and the `5 × interval for 5 m` alert recipe in `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Recommended alerts is deployable as written on any interval (it reads `300 s` at the defaults, and scales itself via the `live_edit_checkpoint_config_*_seconds` gauges — see the dedicated entry above). Two per-tick gauges cover what a fleet-wide percentile cannot: `live_edit_checkpoint_oldest_pending_age_seconds` (the worst layer still awaiting a backup at the end of a pass — layers backed up during that pass are excluded, so on an active fleet the value rises and falls between `0` and roughly the interval, and only a value sustained above `interval + quiesce + tick` means a layer is genuinely stuck; a WARN log names it) and `live_edit_checkpoint_layers_never_written` (layers with no backup at all — expected `0`, and any sustained non-zero value means those layers are outside disaster recovery entirely). Aggregate both gauges across replicas with `max()`, never `sum()`. A third signal, the counter `live_edit_checkpoint_lease_total{outcome}` (`renewed` | `lost` | `refresh_error`), reports whether a backup pass got through all the layers it decided were due before its leader lease expired: `lost` means a pass was cut short and its remaining layers were deferred to the next one — deferred layers are retried, and a backup that has finished being written is never damaged by a concurrent maintenance pass, though a sustained `lost` rate is worth clearing rather than tolerating (see `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? — notably, do **not** lengthen `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TICK_SECS`) — and `refresh_error` distinguishes a backing-store fault from a genuine expiry. Backups are assembled worst-exposure-first — layers with no backup at all, then the stalest backup — so a pass cut short defers the freshest layers.

  **Recovery is lazy on first access.** Startup does not pre-rehydrate every layer; the service fetches a layer's checkpoint and applies it to Valkey when the first RPC against the missing state arrives, with sub-second recovery for typical layers. Sequence-number monotonicity is preserved across recovery — after a Valkey-loss event, the next acknowledged `UpdateLayer` returns a `sequence_num` strictly greater than any sequence number previously acknowledged for that object, with no client-visible rewind. That holds unconditionally when the layer's update stream survived the loss (the restore reads its exact high-water mark); when the whole keyspace is gone, it holds provided `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_SEQ_SAFETY_MARGIN` is sized at or above your peak commits-per-second × `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS`, which is a precondition rather than optional tuning. Object-ID collisions across Valkey restarts are eliminated under AOF-off operation via the persisted-counter safety margin. Snapshot point-in-time consistency holds under concurrent write load against a single layer; concurrent multi-replica recovery against the same layer converges to a single applied state with no double-apply, no interleaved apply, and no orphan keys. **Session-scoped recovery state — a session's metadata plus the list of layers it owns and which stored object each one is — is captured on session lifecycle events rather than on the cadence above**, and it is what makes a restored layer reachable at all, so it carries its own two guarantees: each capture is a single atomic read of that session's state, so a layer created or deleted while the capture runs cannot produce a capture naming a layer set the session never had; and when two replicas capture the same session concurrently, an older capture cannot durably overwrite a newer one — the service detects that it overwrote fresher work and rewrites from live state. The residual is that the older capture stays durable for roughly one capture-and-write cycle until the repair lands. Four narrow cases carry no repair and leave a capture stale until that session is next created, edited, or deleted: the service gave up after repeated repairs on one session; a write landed but its ordering could never be confirmed (deliberately not repaired on speculation, since a blind rewrite could resurrect a deleted session and a blind removal could destroy a live capture); a replica crashed between writing a capture and ordering it; or a repair was still queued in memory at process exit. Repairs are reported on the counter `live_edit_checkpoint_session_publish_superseded_total{result}` (`write` \| `delete` = repair scheduled, `exhausted` = repair abandoned), and the unconfirmed-ordering case on `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{result="error", kind="session"}` with a "durable but unordered" WARN; the last two cases are not reported on either counter. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? for `live_edit_checkpoint_writes_total{kind="session"}`; neither counter alone covers the whole condition.

  **Service identity for outbound storage-api calls (Epic 4 / `t1.fgj.dp`).** When `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ADAPTER=StorageApi`, the service authenticates its own outbound checkpoint reads and writes with a long-lived service-identity Bearer token — distinct from any per-request user token on the inbound side. A credential-provisioner sidecar writes a JSON token file (`{"expires_on": "<RFC3339>", "access_token": "<value>"}`) to the path named by `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE` via write-temp + atomic rename; the service polls the file at `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS` (default `15`s; zero rejected) and caches the parsed token in-process. Token rotation is picked up without a service restart. A cached token is presented for its whole real lifetime and is treated as unavailable only once it has actually expired — an approaching expiry never withholds the credential and never flips readiness; on a 401 from the upstream the outbound adapter forces an on-demand re-read of the file. Bearer-token bytes are never logged — operator-visible diagnostics carry only generic descriptors, never file contents or parsed token bytes. Authentication is degrade-friendly for local-dev workflows: when `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE` is unset, no provider is constructed and outbound storage-api calls go unauthenticated (intended for development; production `StorageApi` deployments must configure the sidecar). The in-process OAuth client-credentials flow (`LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_CLIENT_CREDENTIALS_ENABLED`) is **not** implemented in V1 — setting it to `true` emits a startup WARN and leaves outbound calls unauthenticated.
