The readiness probe consults a `service_token_available` dependency check when sidecar mode is enabled — sidecar-mode replicas stay `NOT_SERVING` until the sidecar writes the first parseable token and flip back to `NOT_SERVING` on cache loss thereafter (hard signal, no smoothing — matches the Valkey-probe posture; smoothing would mask a sidecar that has stopped writing the file). The companion Prometheus gauge `live_edit_service_token_available` (0/1, no labels; registered only when sidecar mode is enabled) is the per-replica view operators dashboard during a sidecar incident; the counter `live_edit_service_token_parse_errors_total` ticks on each parse failure and emits a WARN while the cache keeps the previous-good token across transient parse failures.

  **Operator-facing setup.** The durability matrix and what each mode loses are in `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Two protection layers; the Helm-chart wiring for the StorageApi sidecar mode is in `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Configure the token sidecar. `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Durability carries the AMR-specific durability-mode recommendation, and `docs/service/docs/deployment.md` § Create the values file an AMR outbound-auth example. The settings and their defaults are in `docs/service/docs/configuration.md` § Checkpoints. Wire and gRPC API are unchanged — recovery is server-side and transparent to clients beyond the existing connection-drop-and-reconnect signal. **Operator action required for Helm-managed deployments — chart default flip.** The Helm chart's `checkpoint.enabled` default flips from `false` to `true` while `checkpoint.adapter` stays `Disabled` — deliberately fail-loud against the SR-5.5 durability validator. A fresh `helm install` or chart upgrade with no checkpoint overrides will FAIL AT STARTUP with the SR-5.5 error, forcing operators to make an explicit adapter choice via `--set checkpoint.adapter=StorageApi` (or `LocalDisk`, or `InMemory` for tests) before the deployment can pass readiness. Operators who want to stay on the pre-feature AOF-only posture must explicitly opt out with `--set checkpoint.enabled=false`. Bare-binary deployments are unaffected: `Config::from_env`'s `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ENABLED` default remains `false`, so a deployment running `live-edit` directly without the Helm chart observes zero behavior change on upgrade. **Non-goals deliberately out of scope for V1:** replacing Valkey with a transactional SQL database as the live-edit source of truth, a gRPC API surface for querying historical checkpoint state or point-in-time rollback, a checkpoint-format versioning / migration tool, multi-adapter writes or cross-adapter replication, checkpointing of the per-object live-update stream (streams are transport not state, and subscribers receive a coalesced catch-up of the recovered materialized state via the existing path), presence checkpointing, eager fleet-wide rehydration at startup, and wire-protocol or client-SDK changes.

- **Helm chart now exposes outbound service-identity for storage-api calls as a first-class chart value via the new top-level `serviceIdentity:` block** (Epic `t1.fgj.dp`, Bee `b.fgj`). The `serviceIdentity` block is the declarative knob that opts a Helm-managed deployment into the credential-provisioner sidecar flow that authenticates the Live Edit service's outbound calls to the storage-api checkpoint endpoint (Epic 5, `t1.fgj.jd`). Two sub-modes are exposed: `serviceIdentity.accessTokenFile.*` is the V1-supported sidecar-provisioned flow, and `serviceIdentity.clientCredentials.*` is a forward-compat surface for a future in-process OAuth client-credentials flow that the V1 binary does not implement. Both default to `enabled: false`, so deployments upgrading to this chart version observe **byte-identical rendered manifests** to the pre-Epic-4 chart until an operator explicitly flips one of the `enabled` keys — verified by the new `supply-chain:helm-defaults-byte-identical` chart-render CI assertion. When `serviceIdentity.accessTokenFile.enabled: true`, the chart appends the operator-supplied sidecar container from `serviceIdentity.accessTokenFile.provisionerSidecar.container`, a shared `token-exchange` `emptyDir` volume mounted at `/var/run/oauth/` on BOTH the sidecar and the live-edit container, and the three new env vars `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE`, `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS`, and `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_CLIENT_CREDENTIALS_ENABLED` on the live-edit container — operators no longer hand-wire those env vars or the volume on the pod spec. Azure workload-identity wiring is auto-detected: when `serviceAccount.annotations."azure.workload.identity/client-id"` is set, the chart emits the matching `azure.workload.identity/use: "true"` pod label automatically (the webhook requires both annotation AND label to project the federated token). The readiness probe consults a fourth `service_token_available` dependency check, registered only when sidecar mode is enabled — operators of non-sidecar deployments see no readiness behavior change, and sidecar-mode replicas stay `NOT_SERVING` until the sidecar writes the first parseable token and flip back to `NOT_SERVING` on cache loss thereafter. The check is a hard signal with no smoothing (matches the Valkey probe; smoothing would mask a sidecar that has stopped writing the file), and the companion Prometheus gauge `live_edit_service_token_available` (0/1, no labels; registered only when sidecar mode is enabled) is the per-replica view operators dashboard during a sidecar incident. The `serviceIdentity.clientCredentials.enabled: true` setting is forward-compat only in V1: enabling it fires the SR-6.4 startup WARN and leaves outbound calls unauthenticated; operators who want to authenticate outbound calls in V1 must use the `accessTokenFile` sub-mode. Bare-binary deployments continue to set `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_FILE` directly on the pod's env block — the chart values are an ergonomic wrapper around the same env-var contract, not a parallel control plane. See `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Configure the token sidecar for the values and operator steps, and `docs/service/docs/deployment.md` § Create the values file for an AMR example.

- **The service can now connect to a TLS-enabled Valkey by using a `rediss://` or `rediss-cluster://` `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_URL` — including every managed Valkey offering, all of which mandate in-transit encryption** (Bee `b.tr5`, Epic 1). The shipped binary now compiles fred with a rustls (ring) TLS backend, so a TLS-scheme Valkey URL genuinely negotiates TLS and verifies the server certificate against the container's system trust store. **Operator action required before rolling forward — a `rediss://` URL that previously connected will now enforce TLS.** Before this change the shipped binary compiled fred with no TLS feature, so a `rediss://` URL connected in **plaintext with no error or warning**: any deployment that already set `rediss://` believed it had in-transit encryption and did not. After this change that same URL genuinely negotiates TLS, and consequently a deployment whose endpoint does not speak TLS on the configured port, or whose server certificate does not verify against the container's system trust store, will now **fail at startup** (repeated connect-retry WARN lines, then a `ConnectError::Timeout` crash — i.e. CrashLoopBackOff) where it previously came up. Before rolling a `rediss://` deployment forward, confirm: (1) the endpoint accepts TLS on the port named in `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_URL`; (2) the server certificate chains to a CA already present in the runtime image's system trust store — the most common self-hosted trip-hazard is a **private CA** whose root is not in the NVIDIA distroless/cc image's CA bundle; and (3) for a clustered `rediss-cluster://` endpoint, that the per-node addresses returned by cluster discovery match the certificate SANs — where discovery returns bare IPs, the service now pins fred's cluster host-mapping to the front-door host from `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_URL` so certificate verification succeeds against that hostname rather than a bare-IP SAN, which is what makes clustered TLS work against managed clusters that advertise private IPs. **A plaintext `redis://` URL is unaffected and requires no configuration change** — it connects exactly as before. A startup scheme guard additionally protects against a *future* build that drops the TLS feature: on such a build a TLS-scheme URL is rejected at startup rather than silently downgraded to plaintext. Every build this repo ships has the TLS feature on by default, so this guard is a compile-time backstop, not a state any shipped build can reach. See `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements and `docs/service/docs/backing-store-self-hosted.md` § TLS for TLS setup and certificate-trust guidance.
- **Managed-service Valkey connectivity — real TLS transport plus passwordless authentication for Azure Managed Redis, AWS MemoryDB, and AWS ElastiCache** (Bee `b.tr5`, feature-close). Rounds out the TLS transport shipped earlier (Epic 1 — the `rediss://` / `rediss-cluster://` / `valkeys://` entry above, including the startup guard that rejects a TLS-scheme URL on a build with no TLS compiled in, closing the silent-plaintext-downgrade hole) with passwordless auth, so the service can now run against every major managed Valkey offering using each cloud's recommended credential model while the default posture stays byte-identical to the prior service. **A new `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE` selects the backing-store credential model** (`static` default \| `accessTokenFile` \| `iam`, case-insensitive): `static` rides any password on `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_URL` exactly as before — no credential provider, **byte-identical to the prior service, no action required for existing deployments**; `accessTokenFile` reads a bearer token from a sidecar-written file and uses it as the Valkey `AUTH` credential — for Azure Managed Redis this is a Microsoft Entra ID managed-identity token minted by the sidecar, but the mode itself is issuer-agnostic; `iam` authenticates against AWS MemoryDB or ElastiCache with an in-process AWS SigV4 pre-signed token (production credentials resolve via IRSA / STS). Both passwordless modes require a TLS `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_URL` and fail closed at startup when their per-mode vars are missing. **Operator knobs.** `accessTokenFile` mode: `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_FILE`, `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_USERNAME` (conventionally the token issuer's identity — e.g. an Azure managed identity's **Object ID**, not its client ID), and `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_TOKEN_REFRESH_LEAD_SECS` (default `180`). `iam` mode: `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_IAM_USERNAME`, `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_IAM_REGION`, the new `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_IAM_SERVICE` selector (`memorydb` default \| `elasticache`, choosing the SigV4 service name and signing-host family), and `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_IAM_CLUSTER_HOST` — now **required when `iam`** (previously parsed-but-optional) and set to the cluster/cache **name**, not the endpoint FQDN. **Expected failover behavior on AWS.** During a MemoryDB / ElastiCache failover a replica may briefly report `NOT_SERVING` (~30–60 s; MemoryDB ~33 s, ElastiCache ~54 s) while its Valkey client rediscovers cluster topology and re-AUTHs — this is **expected, not a defect**: it self-heals with no operator action, and running multiple replicas keeps the fleet serving. See `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Authentication, `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § 5. Connect Live Edit, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store-aws.md` § 6. Connect Live Edit for per-platform setup (recommended passwordless mode + static fallback, certificate trust, and the IAM cluster-host form).
- **A rejected request now leaves a log line naming the request, instead of only a `+1` on a counter** (Issue `b.94g`). When the service turned a call away — a layer edit sent against a stale layer version, a request for a layer or session that does not exist, a malformed request, an authorization denial — it recorded nothing in the log at all. The rejection existed only as an increment on the `live_endpoint_calls_total` metric, labelled with the status code and nothing else, so the session, the layer, the client and the reason the client was told were recorded nowhere on the server and support had to reproduce the call or infer the cause from surrounding lines. Every rejected `CreateLayer`, `UpdateLayer`, `DeleteLayer`, `StatLayer`, `CreateSession`, `DeleteSession`, and `ListSessions` now emits **exactly one** log line with the message `RPC failed`, carrying the endpoint name, the status code and the exact message the client received, how far the call got before it failed, and — where the call got far enough for the service to have validated them — the session id, layer name, base path, client id, the layer version the client sent, and the layer version the service actually holds. That last pair identifies the most common rejection, a client editing against a layer version that has since been replaced, without any reproduction. The line's `endpoint` and status-code fields use the same names and spellings as the corresponding metric labels, so a log line and a metric series join without translation. **Level tells you whose problem it is**: `INFO` for client-caused rejections (unknown layer or session, stale layer version, malformed request, limit exceeded) and `WARN` for the ones worth attention (authorization and authentication denials, an unavailable backing store, and rejections raised against a layer the service had already resolved, which indicate damaged state rather than a client mistake). A rejected request never logs at `ERROR`, so a misbehaving client cannot raise a fleet's error rate. Separately, a call that exceeds the **server-side** deadline now logs `RPC deadline exceeded` with the endpoint, the deadline that applied and the elapsed time — previously also silent; a call cut short by a **client-imposed** per-call timeout is a different shape and still leaves no server-side line and no metric increment. Both new lines are visible at the default `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_LEVEL=info`. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no new or changed metric, and no change to any status code or message a client receives** — the only change is what the server writes to its own log. Two things are deliberately unchanged: bearer tokens and other credential material never appear (the fields are drawn only from validated request identifiers), and the per-edit success line stays at `debug` so a busy layer does not flood the log. Not covered: server-side faults, which continue to log `internal error` at `ERROR` from a different place and carry no request fields; calls refused for a missing, malformed, or non-`Bearer` `Authorization` header, which are turned away before the call reaches a handler and so still leave no line — unlike the authorization and authentication denials raised *inside* a call, which do; calls torn down before the server sees anything go wrong — an abandoned upload the server *does* observe leaves an `INFO` line with the status code `CANCELLED`, and only a call discarded before the server sees the dropped connection leaves none; calls cut short by a client-imposed per-call timeout; requests the server cannot decode, and requests exceeding the gRPC message-size limit, both of which the transport rejects before the call is processed and which therefore leave **no line anywhere** — the client sees `INTERNAL` for the first and `OUT_OF_RANGE` (not `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`) for the second, whose limit is the gRPC library's own 4 MB per-message default and is not configurable through this service; and the two streaming calls (`JoinSession`, `SubscribeReadLayer`), which keep their existing logging and whose setup rejections are unlogged for the same reason. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Logs.

### Changed (breaking)
- **`LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED` default flips from `false` to `true`.** The service now performs storage-service path-level authorization on every RPC by default. Any deployment that did **not** previously pin `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED` explicitly will inherit the new `true` default on upgrade and MUST take one of two operator actions before rolling forward, otherwise startup fails fast or the next authorized RPC fails:
  - **Path A — preserve the pre-upgrade behavior (no path-level authorization).** Set `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=false` explicitly in the deployment manifest's `env:` block (or the Helm chart's `auth.authz_enabled: false`). Combined with the new `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED` default `false` you must ALSO set `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=true` and provide `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT` — see the cross-validation entry below for why both-disabled is rejected at startup.
  - **Path B — adopt the new `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=true` default.** Provide `LIVE_EDIT_STORAGE_SERVICE_ENDPOINT` (with an explicit `https://` or `http://` scheme — bare hostnames are rejected at startup per b.ohw). The storage service is consulted for path-level read/write authorization on every RPC that gates on a path; deployments that previously ran without it must wire it in before flipping forward. `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL` (default `rest`) selects REST or gRPC for both the storage and userinfo backends.

  **Cross-validation invariant (SR-1.5): there is no "both off" mode.** `Config::from_env` rejects `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=false` AND `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` simultaneously at startup — the resulting rejection message names both env vars so operators see exactly which two knobs are in conflict. Rationale: with both disabled the service has no source of caller identity (no upstream identity validator from the permissions service and no JWT decode), so every request would be served under an anonymous identity with no auditable user attribution. The combinations that ARE accepted: (a) both `true` (full external dependency posture), (b) `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=true` + `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` (the new lightweight default — permissions service validates tokens, in-process JWT decode supplies display identity), and (c) `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=false` + `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=true` (preserves the historical no-authz behavior).

  **Opaque-token caveat under the new default (cross-references the `Added` entry).** Deployments that adopt Path B with `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=false` (the new default combination) require clients to send JWT-format bearer tokens. RPCs carrying non-JWT (opaque) bearer tokens are rejected with the verbatim error `"opaque bearer token requires the userinfo service; deploy the userinfo service or use JWT-format bearer tokens"`. Operators whose client population still emits opaque tokens must either keep the userinfo service deployed (set `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_SERVICE_ENABLED=true` and provide `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT`) or migrate clients to JWT-format tokens before upgrading.
- **`oodf_ffi::build_delta` now returns `Result<Vec<u8>, OodfError>` instead of `Vec<u8>`** (bug `b.vvs` item 8). The pre-b.vvs implementation called `std::abort()` on an unknown `CommandTag` discriminant or on `std::bad_alloc` from the `FlatBufferBuilder`; either shape blew away every concurrent in-flight RPC sharing the process when a single bridge caller emitted an out-of-range tag or hit allocator exhaustion while serializing a buffer. The function now returns a typed `OodfError` (new `BuildUnknownCommandTag(String)` for the bad-tag path; `AllocationFailed` for `std::bad_alloc`; `InternalError(String)` for any other `std::exception`) and never aborts the process. The C++ side returns a new `BuildResult` shared struct carrying `bytes: Vec<u8>`, a `BuildErrorKind` discriminant (`Ok` / `UnknownCommandTag` / `AllocationFailed` / `InternalError`), and an error message; `oodf_ffi::lib::build_delta` decodes that into the `Result` shape via the new `OodfError::from_build_ffi`. **Caller impact**: every in-tree `oodf_ffi::build_delta` call site must now handle the `Result` (the in-repo migration unwraps via `.expect(...)` at fixture-generation and proptest round-trip sites and propagates via `?` at production call sites in the diff-reconstruction and OMPF-serialize paths — see the b.vvs diff). The `oodf-ffi` crate is currently workspace-internal and not consumed by external clients outside this repo, so no downstream caller update is required; this entry is recorded under breaking changes for future external consumers and for anyone vendoring the crate.
- **Tightened input-validation surface across every RPC** (bug `b.39x`). Five previously-accepted input shapes now reject with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` before any auth, storage I/O, or log emission. Each is a contract change that operators should audit production clients for, even though no well-formed UUID / printable-ASCII / finite-f64 producer is expected to be affected. The `live_edit_delta_create_section_skipped_missing_parent_total` counter (see Added above) is the operator signal for one previously-silent pattern that DOES occur in practice — the rest of these new rejection rules cover threat-model gaps where the pre-b.39x behavior was either undefined or footgun-prone, not silent acceptance of common producer shapes. (1) `session_id` on every RPC that takes one off the wire (`CreateLayer`, `UpdateLayer`, `DeleteLayer`, `StatLayer`, `SubscribeReadLayer`, `JoinSession`, `DeleteSession`) is now validated via the new `validate_session_id` (≤ 256 bytes, no NUL, no other ASCII control characters `0x01..=0x1F`). Pre-fix only `is_empty()` was checked, so a 64 KB `session_id` with ANSI escapes could land in tracing output, Valkey hash tags (`le:{session_id}:sess`, `le:presence:events:{sid}`), and pub/sub channels. (2) `user_application` on `JoinSessionHeader` is now validated via the new `validate_user_application` (empty is still accepted; non-empty must be ≤ 256 bytes with no NUL or other ASCII control characters). Pre-fix the unsanitized string flowed straight into the `presence_user` HSET, the `PeerJoined` broadcast, and every membership replay payload — a single peer could poison the operator's tracing output and every co-tenant's `PeerJoined` view with multi-MB or control-char payloads. (3) Delta name fields (`CreateSection.section_name`, `MoveSection.new_name`, `DiffChangeSection.section_name`, `SetField.key_name`) are now rejected when they contain NUL (`0x00`) or other ASCII control characters (`0x01..=0x1F`) — caught at `validate_delta_bounds` with the new `DeltaError::NameContainsControlChar { field }`. Pre-fix only the 4,096-byte length cap was checked, so a name like `"\x00\x1b[31m"` would propagate into Valkey hash keys and structured-tracing spans. (4) Non-finite `TimeSample.time` values (NaN, +∞, −∞) are now rejected at `validate_delta_bounds` with the new `DeltaError::TimeSampleNonFiniteTime { index, time }`. Pre-fix the wrapper `OrderedFloat<f64>` accepted the value, but Valkey stringifies the f64 — round-tripping NaN produced the literal `"nan"`, which the Lua fast-path then compared as a string, breaking total-ordering semantics and producing unstable HashMap keys across re-parses. (5) Structural commands with `parent_id == section_id` (or `new_parent_id == section_id` on `MoveSection`) on a non-root parent are now rejected with the new `DeltaError::SelfParenting { command, section_id }`; `parent_id == 0` (the root marker) is still valid. Pre-fix self-parenting produced cycles in `state.children` that broke cascade-delete and diff-reconstruction DFS walkers — the iterative walker would exhaust the `MAX_SECTION_TREE_DEPTH = 10,000` budget before returning `SectionTreeTooDeep` to a downstream subscriber. All three new `DeltaError` variants map to gRPC `INVALID_ARGUMENT`. Operator action: review any client that constructs `session_id`s, delta names, or time samples from raw user input rather than internal opaque tokens, and ensure those callers reject or replace control bytes / non-finite floats / self-parenting structural commands client-side. Standard producers (UUID4 session IDs, printable-ASCII names, finite floating-point time keys, well-formed parent IDs) are unaffected.

### Changed
- **Auth client builders now bound the connect handshake at `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_TIMEOUT_MS` across both protocols; a DNS hang at startup no longer blocks indefinitely** (bug `b.j5t` item 3). Pre-b.j5t the gRPC permissions and userinfo client constructors built a tonic `Channel` with `Channel::from_shared(...).connect().await` and no `.connect_timeout()`, so a startup-time DNS hang against an unreachable or misconfigured endpoint blocked the boot path indefinitely while every other readiness signal piled up behind it. The fix calls `.connect_timeout(Duration::from_millis(auth_timeout_ms))` on the `Endpoint` builder for both `GrpcStorageServiceClient::connect` and `GrpcUserinfoClient::connect`, capping the full DNS + TCP + TLS handshake at the existing `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_TIMEOUT_MS` budget (default 5 s). Per-RPC deadlines continue to apply once connected. The REST clients (`RestStorageServiceClient::new`, `RestUserinfoClient::new`) also gained a `.connect_timeout(Duration::from_millis(auth_timeout_ms))` on the `reqwest::Client::builder()` plus the existing per-request `.timeout()` — pre-b.j5t the REST builder had neither, so the first call after a long-running DNS hang could block past the per-RPC deadline. Both protocols now wire the connect-phase ceiling to `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_TIMEOUT_MS` symmetrically, so a single operator knob shifts both the gRPC and REST connect ceilings and the per-request deadline together. No new env var; the connect-phase ceiling reuses the per-call knob deliberately so operators do not have to discover and tune a third auth-related timeout.
- **Auth singleflight inflight-map entries are now cleaned up via `scopeguard` so cancellation no longer leaks them** (bug `b.j5t` item 4). The `AuthCache::check_permission` / `get_current_user` singleflight (b.nrt) deduplicates concurrent backend lookups for the same key by installing a `Shared<Future>` in `permissions_inflight` / `userinfo_inflight`, awaiting it, and removing the entry post-await. Pre-b.j5t the `remove` ran unconditionally *after* `.await`, but a cancelled caller (tonic deadline drop, client disconnect, future-drop) returns from the function before `.await` resolves — the entry leaked indefinitely. DashMap has no eviction, so a long-running pod with sporadic mid-flight cancellations accumulated stale entries on the lookup hot path. Cleanup now goes through `scopeguard::guard((), move |()| inflight.remove_if(&key, |_, v| v.ptr_eq(&shared)))` which runs on every exit path — normal return, error return, or Drop on cancel. The `Shared::ptr_eq` check guarantees we never evict a follow-up caller's freshly-installed future at the same key. `ScopeGuard::into_inner` disarms the guard on the happy path so the b.ohw H-auth-2 cache-insert-then-remove ordering is preserved (cache-insert still runs before the explicit `remove_if` that mirrors the guard body); cancellation paths skip `into_inner` entirely and let the guard fire. No operator action required.
- **In-process `session_tokens` map is now bounded by a background crash-recovery sweeper; long-running pods can no longer accumulate dead entries** (bug `b.j5t` item 5 / b.f36 A6). `session_tokens` is a per-replica `DashMap<session_id, CancellationToken>` populated by `CreateSession` on the handling replica and lazily by `SubscribeReadLayer` / `JoinSession` via `acquire_session_cancel` on whichever replica load-balanced the streaming RPC (SDD §7.8 Step 4 mechanism #2). Removal previously relied on `DeleteSession` Step 5's `le:session:deleted` pub/sub fanout — every active code path was covered, but a session that was never explicitly deleted (clients abandoning sessions, deletes that bypass the pub/sub for any reason, multi-day pub/sub outages) left entries in the map until process exit. On a long-running pod with high session-creation churn the map grew unboundedly. `spawn_session_tokens_sweeper` runs every `SESSION_TOKENS_SWEEP_INTERVAL` (currently hardcoded — deep-tuning knob; operator-facing env var deferred) and probes each entry against Valkey via `SessionStore::get_base_path`; entries whose session no longer exists are removed and their token cancelled. Transient Valkey probe failures leave the entry in place for the next sweep. The sweeper joins the same shutdown supervision as the other background tasks under `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS`. No new env var; no client-visible behavior change.
- **Transient Valkey errors across the storage and gRPC layers now surface as gRPC `UNAVAILABLE`, not `INTERNAL`** (bug `b.fd9`). The b.ohw H-grpc-1 contract that mapped transient auth-backend outages to `UNAVAILABLE` (code 14, retriable per the gRPC retry contract) is now also enforced across every Valkey round-trip. Pre-b.fd9 several hot paths — `LayerStore::list_layer_names`, `LayerStore::get_layer_mapping`, `LayerStore::read_object_header`, the six standalone calls inside `LayerStore::delete_layer`, `SessionStore::get_base_path`, every `JoinSession` presence read/write (`src/grpc/presence.rs`), and the `process_pending` external-value GC EVALSHA — mapped any fred error to `ServiceError::Internal` → gRPC `INTERNAL` (code 13, **non**-retriable). A Valkey blip therefore fanned out to client-perceived hard failures on every authenticated RPC even though a retry on a fresh connection would have succeeded. The classification is now uniform: the shared `is_transient_fred_error` predicate (`Timeout` / `IO` / `Canceled` / `Cluster` / `Backpressure`) feeds two mappers in `src/storage/client.rs` — `map_transient_fred_err` (transient → `ServiceError::Aborted` for in-process retry inside `retry_aborted_with_jitter`) for sites that run inside a retry loop, and `map_fred_err_for_grpc` (transient → `ServiceError::Unavailable` → gRPC `UNAVAILABLE`) for sites that surface straight to a gRPC handler. Two further site-level changes ship in the same sweep: (1) `delete_layer`'s six-step cascade (DEL mapping, XADD deleted, SREM layers index, three PEXPIRE / UNLINK calls) now runs inside `retry_aborted_with_jitter` so a Valkey blip mid-cascade either completes or returns `ABORTED` (retriable), never `INTERNAL` with a half-cleaned layer; every cascade step is idempotent so retry is safe. (2) The `consume_layer_stream` `XREAD BLOCK` consumer no longer tears down the subscriber on the first transient I/O error — it now applies a full-jitter exponential backoff between 100 ms and 5 s (`XREAD_TRANSIENT_BACKOFF_INITIAL` → `XREAD_TRANSIENT_BACKOFF_CAP`) and continues, with the counter reset on the next successful XREAD. Subscribers should not be more sensitive than writers, and `update_layer` already retries the same fred kinds. **Client impact:** clients see fewer spurious `INTERNAL` errors during Valkey pod restarts, brief cluster reshards, or network blips on every Valkey-touching RPC (CreateSession, ListSessions, DeleteSession, CreateLayer, UpdateLayer, DeleteLayer, StatLayer, JoinSession, SubscribeReadLayer). Standard gRPC client retry policies pick `UNAVAILABLE` up automatically. Pre-b.fd9 clients that already retried `INTERNAL` will continue to work but the new code is the canonical retriable signal — strip any custom `INTERNAL`-retry from auth-gated and Valkey-gated call sites and rely on the standard `UNAVAILABLE` policy. Permanent fred errors (`Protocol` / `Parse` / `Auth` / `Config` / `NotFound` / etc.) keep the `INTERNAL` mapping because they signal a real bug or contract violation that warrants operator attention. No wire-format change.

  **Wire-contract note for `SubscribeReadLayer` consumers:** wrapping `delete_layer`'s six-step cascade in `retry_aborted_with_jitter` means each retry attempt's `XADD * deleted` produces a new auto-id stream entry (XADD with `*` is not idempotent at the stream-entry level). Under a transient Valkey blip mid-cascade, one logical `DeleteLayer` call may therefore emit 1..N `deleted` notifications on the per-object stream rather than exactly one. All duplicates carry the same `(object_id, sequence_num)` and resolve to the same terminal "layer deleted" state; the in-tree subscriber already treats them as idempotent. External clients implementing `SubscribeReadLayer` SHOULD treat duplicate `deleted` entries for the same `(object_id, sequence_num)` as idempotent — clients that already deduplicate or that act unconditionally on the first `deleted` are unaffected. This makes explicit a property the wire contract previously left implicit (pre-b.fd9 the cascade ran without retry, so a single `delete_layer` produced exactly one `deleted` entry in the success path). No proto-level change.

  **Follow-up — consecutive-transient-failure cap on `SubscribeReadLayer` (b.fd9 follow-up):** the `XREAD BLOCK` retry loop is now capped at `MAX_CONSECUTIVE_TRANSIENT_FAILURES = 60` consecutive transient errors. Without the cap, a prolonged Valkey outage (multi-minute) would pin the consumer's `subscriber_pool` slot indefinitely while it retried every ~5 s — on the 256-slot prod pool that is a resource-starvation risk under perma-flap. After the cap fires the consumer surfaces `ServiceError::Unavailable` → gRPC `Code::Unavailable` (retriable) and the standard client retry policy reconnects on a fresh slot. At MAX = 60 with the 5 s backoff cap the consumer rides out ≈5 minutes of continuous flapping before bailing — long enough for realistic ~30 s reconnect windows, short enough to bound worst-case slot occupancy on a true prolonged outage. Counter resets on the next successful XREAD; a single blip never counts toward subsequent ones. Clients observing `UNAVAILABLE` on `SubscribeReadLayer` should re-subscribe with the same `have_object_id` / `have_version`; the re-subscribe lands on a fresh `subscriber_pool` slot and resumes per-commit catch-up via the normal Regime-2 in-window path. No proto-level change.
- **Presence-broadcast lag log demoted from `WARN` to `DEBUG`; health-supervisor auth-backend probe-failure log is now throttled** (bug `b.avd` items 8 + 10). Two operator-visible log-volume changes. (1) The `JoinSession` server→client event-forwarding loop emits a log line each time the per-session `tokio::sync::broadcast` channel reports `RecvError::Lagged(n)` (the channel filled and the oldest events were dropped). Pre-b.avd that line was at `WARN`; a flapping subscriber could issue thousands of `Lagged` events per second and drown log aggregators, masking the actual incident signal. The line is now emitted at `DEBUG`; the actionable signal is the `live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total{service}` counter (incremented by `n` per event), and operators should wire `rate(live_edit_presence_broadcast_dropped_total[5m]) > N` alerts against the metric rather than grepping logs at `WARN`. **Operator action**: any aggregator-level alerting rule that grepped the lag line at `WARN` to detect presence-broadcast overflow must switch to the metric, or lower the aggregator's threshold to include `DEBUG`. (2) When `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=true`, the readiness health supervisor emits a log line on every failed auth-backend probe. Pre-b.avd each failure was at `WARN`; a multi-hour auth-backend outage at the default 5 s probe cadence produced ~720 `WARN` lines per hour per replica, drowning aggregators with the same "auth still down" message. The throttle now emits at `WARN` on failures up to and including the threshold boundary (`LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_AUTH_FAILURE_THRESHOLD`, default 3) — these are the failures that flip readiness, and remain at full fidelity. Past `threshold * 10` consecutive failures the per-failure log drops to `DEBUG` and one `WARN` is emitted per ~600 failures (≈ one log per ten minutes at 1 Hz), so the default log level still shows "auth still down" during a multi-hour outage without flooding. The `live_edit_health_*` metrics continue to carry the actionable signal. **Operator action**: aggregator alert rules that fire on a per-line basis ("any `WARN` from `health supervisor: auth backend probe failed`") should switch to rate-based thresholds on the metric or accept the reduced log fidelity during long outages — the in-aggregate "outage in progress" signal is unchanged, only the per-failure log fan-out is bounded.
- **Auth-backend transient outages now surface as gRPC `UNAVAILABLE`, not `INTERNAL`** (bug `b.ohw` H-grpc-1). `AuthError::Timeout` and `AuthError::Unreachable` — including the post-retry-exhaustion case after `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_MAX_ATTEMPTS` attempts — now map to the new `ServiceError::Unavailable` and reach clients as `UNAVAILABLE` (code 14, retriable) rather than `INTERNAL` (code 13, not retriable). Standard gRPC client retry policies pick `UNAVAILABLE` up automatically, so a brief auth-backend blip no longer fans out to client-perceived hard failures across every authenticated RPC. Protocol-level / contract violations (`AuthError::InvalidResponse` — malformed responses, unknown HTTP/gRPC status, TLS handshake failures) keep the `INTERNAL` mapping because they signal a real bug. Client impact: callers MAY retry `UNAVAILABLE` from any auth-gated RPC (CreateSession, ListSessions, CreateLayer, UpdateLayer, DeleteLayer, StatLayer, JoinSession, SubscribeReadLayer); pre-b.ohw clients that already retry `INTERNAL` will continue to work but the new code is the canonical retriable signal.
- **Auth gRPC clients now classify `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` / `ABORTED` / `INTERNAL` as transient, matching REST 429/500/503 parity** (bug `b.ohw` medium). Pre-b.ohw the gRPC permissions and userinfo clients only retried `UNAVAILABLE` and `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, while the REST clients already retried 429 / 500 / 502 / 503 / 504 — so a brief backend blip recovered transparently on REST and surfaced as a hard `INTERNAL` on gRPC. The two protocols now recover symmetrically. No operator action required; `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_MAX_ATTEMPTS` continues to bound the retry budget.
- **TLS handshake failures on the REST auth clients are now classified as permanent, not transient** (bug `b.ohw` H-auth-4). Pre-b.ohw any non-status, non-timeout error on the REST auth path fell through to `AuthError::Unreachable` and was retried `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_MAX_ATTEMPTS` times — a cert-rotation incident or CA mismatch then pounded the backend with failing handshakes for the full retry budget. The classifier now walks the `reqwest::Error::source()` chain looking for a `rustls::Error` (typed downcast, not log-format string-matching) and returns `AuthError::InvalidResponse("... TLS handshake failed (cert / trust mismatch — not retried)")`, surfaced as gRPC `INTERNAL` with the expected single-attempt count. Detection is implicitly pinned to `rustls = "0.23"`; a future `reqwest`/`rustls` major bump may require updating `auth::rest::is_tls_error`. Plain TCP / DNS / connection-reset errors stay transient as before.
- **Auth backend `status.message()` and HTTP reason phrases no longer flow into wrapped error strings or operator logs** (bug `b.ohw` H-auth-5). Both the gRPC and REST auth clients previously formatted the wrapped `AuthError::InvalidResponse` as `format!("storage service returned {code}: {message}", ...)` (gRPC) or with the `StatusCode` `Display` form including the reason phrase (REST). A buggy or malicious backend that echoed bearer-token material into `status.message()` or the HTTP reason phrase would then leak it through the wrapped error, into `ServiceError::Internal` log output, and into operator triage. The new contract carries only the numeric / textual code: `auth backend returned {code}` (gRPC) and `auth backend returned HTTP {code}` (REST, numeric only). Operator triage cost is unchanged — gRPC code and HTTP status are the high-signal triage axes; reason phrases are noise. No wire-format change.
- **Auth endpoints (`LIVE_EDIT_STORAGE_SERVICE_ENDPOINT` and `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT`) now require an explicit `https://` or `http://` scheme; bare hostnames are rejected at startup** (bug `b.ohw` C-6 / H-auth-1). Pre-b.ohw `normalize_endpoint("storage.prod.internal")` silently rewrote to `http://storage.prod.internal`, so an operator who omitted the scheme by accident silently downgraded the auth channel to plaintext and leaked bearer tokens on the wire. The new contract validates the value during `Config::from_env` and fails the boot with a clear error. Port `:443` parsing is also tightened: pre-b.ohw a `host:4430..host:4439` (or any `:443*` shape) tripped a `contains(":443")` check and was force-flipped to TLS, producing a confusing SNI-handshake error against a plaintext service; the helper now parses the right-most `:` (handling `[ipv6]:port` correctly) and TLS-flips only on exact equality with `443`. Operator action: any deployment whose `LIVE_EDIT_STORAGE_SERVICE_ENDPOINT` or `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT` lacks a scheme MUST be updated before redeploy. Test/dev only: `http://...` continues to work unchanged.
- **`LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_CACHE_MAX_ENTRIES` now has an explicit upper bound of `10_000_000`** (bug `b.ohw` medium). Values above the ceiling are rejected at startup with a parse error naming both the offending value and the ceiling, so a fat-fingered `100_000_000` fails fast instead of silently allocating four 100-million-entry mini-moka caches. Zero is still rejected (zero would disable the cache). Operators who legitimately need above ten million entries should re-architect; the ceiling is bug-prevention, not a soft hint.
- **`client_id` on `UpdateLayer` and `SubscribeReadLayer` now also rejects `|` (pipe)** (bug `b.k1r`). The server's live-update notification stream payload is pipe-delimited (`update|{object_id}|{sequence_num}|{client_id}|{client_ts}|{server_ts}`), so a `client_id` containing `|` previously corrupted the subscriber-side parse with a `splitn(6, '|')` that misaligned fields. The gRPC handler now rejects any `client_id` containing `|` with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` at the same point it enforces the existing 256-byte length cap and control-character rule (b.jr8). UUIDs, session tokens, and application-instance identifiers (the realistic shapes for `client_id`) are unaffected; client libraries that embed arbitrary user strings should reject or substitute `|` before sending. No wire format change.
- **`UpdateLayer` now also retries transient Valkey I/O during the EXEC reply, not only during WATCH / HGETALL / MULTI-queue** (bug `b.k1r` H-stor-6). Prior to b.k1r the slow-path EXEC reply had a local `Err(e) => Internal(...)` arm that bypassed the transient-vs-terminal classifier used elsewhere on the commit path — a Valkey restart that killed the connection as EXEC was dispatched surfaced as gRPC `INTERNAL` even though the same error kind during the snapshot phase already mapped to `ABORTED` (retried). EXEC errors now route through the same `map_transient_fred_err` classifier as the other three round-trip types, so the four commit-path sites (WATCH, HGETALL, MULTI-queue, EXEC) retry symmetrically. The `(last cause: ...)` suffix on the exhaustion message now also carries EXEC-site blips. **Client impact:** fewer spurious `INTERNAL` errors during Valkey pod restarts or brief cluster state transitions; no client code change required. The `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS` budget bounds the new trigger site exactly as it bounds the existing ones.
- **Client-streaming transport drops on `CreateLayer` / `UpdateLayer` now surface as gRPC `CANCELLED` instead of `INTERNAL`** (bug `b.jr8`). When the underlying HTTP/2 stream breaks mid-chunk — TCP reset, client process crash, load-balancer abort — the server previously collapsed the tonic transport error into `ServiceError::Internal` and returned `INTERNAL` to the caller. The server now classifies the transport-drop case as `ServiceError::Cancelled` → gRPC `CANCELLED`, matching the gRPC guidance for caller-initiated abandonment. Client libraries should treat `CANCELLED` as "the stream is gone" (retry the upload from scratch if appropriate) rather than a server fault. Server-side deadline-driven cancellation continues to surface as `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`; shutdown-driven stream termination is unchanged. No wire-format change.
- **`client_id` on `SubscribeReadLayer` and `UpdateLayer` is now validated** (bug `b.jr8`). Byte length capped at 256, control characters (`0x00..=0x1F`) rejected. Empty is still allowed and continues to disable self-echo suppression (the proto semantic is preserved). Violations reject with `INVALID_ARGUMENT` at the gRPC handler before any storage, auth, or logging work. The cap is load-bearing — `client_id` is embedded in the pipe-delimited stream-entry payload (`update|{object_id}|{sequence_num}|{client_id}|...`) and logged via `tracing`; an unbounded or control-character-laden value could corrupt the stream framing or inject ANSI escapes into operator terminals. Clients sending UUIDs, session tokens, or application-instance identifiers (all well within 256 printable-ASCII bytes) are unaffected. Advise client-library authors to validate at the caller side and treat empty as an explicit opt-out of self-echo suppression rather than an accidental default.
- **Client-streaming RPCs now enforce a chunk-count cap** (bug `b.jr8`). In addition to the pre-existing 100 MB byte cap, `CreateLayer` and `UpdateLayer` reject streams that exceed `LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE / 1024` chunk messages — at the 100 MB default that's 102,400 chunks; the cap tightens automatically on smaller configured max sizes. Violation → `INVALID_ARGUMENT` with message `"streaming chunk count exceeded N"`. The cap blocks a DoS shape where a client streams a well-formed byte total as millions of 1-byte chunks — per-chunk gRPC framing amplifies server CPU far beyond what the byte cap alone bounds. Uploads using normal-sized chunks (tens of KB to 1 MB) are unaffected; a 100 MB upload at 1 MB chunks is only 100 messages. No client code change required unless you were deliberately uploading >100,000 chunks.
- **`SubscribeReadLayer` streams now self-terminate on unresponsive subscribers** (bug `b.jr8`). The internal channel between the `XREAD BLOCK` task and the gRPC send task is now bounded at 4,096 entries with a 5-second full-channel watchdog. If a subscriber's read loop has not drained the channel within 5 seconds after it fills, the consumer task exits and the RPC ends with the stream closing cleanly — the server reclaims the `subscriber_pool` slot and the XREAD-BLOCK connection within seconds of the subscriber going dark. *(The clean close is superseded later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.8z2`, which makes the tear-down surface `UNAVAILABLE` so the client re-subscribes, and by Issue `b.f4f`, which guarantees the status is enqueued rather than queued behind the backlog that caused the tear-down.)* The consumer also exits immediately if the gRPC writer task has already closed (client disconnected), rather than waiting for the 5-second watchdog. Clients reading the stream faithfully are unaffected; clients that stop polling but hold the stream open will now see the stream close from the server side rather than keeping a zombie subscription alive indefinitely. No wire-format change.
- **`CreateSession` now rejects `base_path` values that contain control characters or null bytes, or whose UTF-8 byte length exceeds 4,096** (bug `b.91j`). `ListSessions` applies the same validation. Previously only empty-string was rejected. Clients sending pathological paths — multi-MB values, raw ANSI escapes, embedded null bytes — now receive `INVALID_ARGUMENT` at the gRPC handler before the value reaches auth, Valkey key formatting, or structured logs. Ordinary paths (repository-root-relative, POSIX-style) are unaffected.
- **Build now requires a `flatc` whose major version is in `23..=25`** (bug `b.8uk`). `oodf-ffi/build.rs` previously invoked `flatc --cpp` without any version assertion, so a dev machine with a `flatc` whose `--cpp` codegen differs from the 24.3.25 the vendored schema was pinned against could silently produce incompatible headers. The build script now shells out `flatc --version` and hard-fails outside the `23..=25` wire-compatibility window for the `--cpp` output we consume, with an actionable error message naming the observed version and recommended install commands. Majors inside the window but not exactly `24` succeed with a `cargo:warning` pointing at the pinned release. Hard-pinning to exactly `24.3.25` was rejected because it would break dev machines whose package manager installs a newer `flatc` by default — the `23..=25` range is the tested-compatible set. No operator action required on pre-built images (CI and the container builder already use the pinned version).
- **Build: staticlib no longer pulls an extraneous archive into downstream binaries.** `oodf-ffi/build.rs`'s sibling `cc::Build` for the `header_compiles.cpp` self-containment check now sets `.cargo_metadata(false)` so its output archive is not advertised as a link input. The archive was a build-time artefact of the header self-containment check, not a runtime dependency; the previous setup would otherwise drag an empty archive into every binary that depended on `oodf-ffi`. No behavior change.
- **Session and layer name length is now enforced in bytes, not code points** (bug `b.55p`). Both names were previously capped at `name.chars().count() > 255`; the limit is now `name.len() > 255` (byte length). Downstream storage — Valkey keys, hash fields, Rust `HashMap` buckets — treats names as bytes, so a character-count check allowed 255-codepoint names of multi-byte characters (emojis cost 4 bytes each) to exceed every byte-aligned budget downstream. **Operator-visible side effect:** a name that previously passed validation at 255 code points of multi-byte characters (up to ~1,020 bytes) now rejects with `{kind} name exceeds 255 byte limit`. The rejection happens at the gRPC handler before any storage operation; no storage migration is required. Names of 1-byte characters (ASCII) are unaffected — the cap coincides exactly with the old one.
- **Readiness probe is now dependency-aware; Helm chart `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` raised from 30 to 40 seconds** (bug `b.3z7`). A health-supervisor background task pings Valkey every 5 seconds (under a 2-second per-probe timeout) and edge-triggers the `nvidia.omniverse.live.v1alpha.LiveService` readiness service: `NOT_SERVING` on the first failure after a run of successes, `SERVING` on the first success after a run of failures. Valkey is treated as a hard dependency — a single failed ping contributes immediately. When `LIVE_EDIT_AUTHZ_ENABLED=true` the supervisor also probes the permissions and userinfo backends on the same tick, smoothed through a per-backend consecutive-failure counter sized by the new `LIVE_EDIT_HEALTH_AUTH_FAILURE_THRESHOLD` env var (default 3) so a transient auth blip already absorbed by retry + cache doesn't evict an otherwise-healthy replica. Kubernetes routes traffic away from replicas whose backing dependencies are unreachable for the duration of the outage and restores routing automatically on recovery — no manual intervention required. The grace-period bump gives the new bounded post-server shutdown sequence (layer cleanup + background-task joins + parallelised Valkey `quit()` + metrics-server abort, capped at `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS` = 30 s) enough headroom after the 5-second HTTP/2 drain to finish cleanly before the kubelet sends SIGKILL. Operators on non-default deployments should align their `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` to `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS + LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_OVERALL_BUDGET_SECS` plus a few seconds of headroom; the service cross-validates the two env vars at startup (`overall >= timeout + 10`) so a misconfigured pair fails fast rather than silently hitting the grace window.
- **Default auth protocol is now REST.** Previously the service called the storage and userinfo services via gRPC. Operators who previously relied on the implicit gRPC behavior must explicitly set `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL=grpc` to preserve the prior behavior, and `LIVE_EDIT_STORAGE_SERVICE_ENDPOINT` / `LIVE_EDIT_USERINFO_ENDPOINT` must point at the gRPC ports. Without this change, the service will attempt REST calls against gRPC endpoints and fail at auth time.
- **`SubscribeReadLayer` now delivers per-commit updates by default.** The internal live-update transport was migrated from Valkey pub/sub to Valkey Streams, so any subscriber whose read loop keeps up with the stream retention window (default 4096 entries per layer, configurable via `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN`) now receives one update per `UpdateLayer` commit rather than a server-coalesced diff. This is the change client libraries previously worked around by synthesizing "no-op" gaps for collapsed sequence numbers — that workaround is no longer needed for in-window subscribers. Subscribers that fall off the retention window still transparently fall through to a single coalesced catch-up diff and resume per-commit delivery afterward. The wire API (proto messages, RPC shape) is unchanged.

### Fixed
- **A live subscription that cannot be served is now refused promptly with a retryable error instead of waiting indefinitely, and a subscription the server tears down with an error now always enqueues the reason instead of ending silently** (Issue `b.f4f`). Three related problems on `SubscribeReadLayer`, all of which presented to a user as a live-editing session that hung or went quiet with no error. **First, waiting without end.** A replica serves at most `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` concurrent subscriptions (Helm default 256 — a count of *subscriptions*, not of people, so one user with ten layers open occupies ten). Past that ceiling a new subscribe queued behind the existing ones and, because a live subscription can legitimately last hours, could wait until one of them ended. It now waits a bounded `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_ADMISSION_WAIT_MS` (new, default 2 seconds) and is then refused with the standard retryable `UNAVAILABLE`, which client libraries re-issue automatically; a second new setting, `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_ADMISSION_MAX_WAITERS`, caps how many calls may be queued at once and defaults to the replica's own pool size, so the queue cannot grow past one turnover's worth of waiting. **The refusal is delivered before any response message**, so a client receives either a working stream or a clean error — never a stream that opens and then silently stalls — and it is issued only after the caller's read permission has been checked, so replica load is not observable to a caller who is not entitled to the layer. Retrying a refused call no longer leaves an abandoned attempt waiting on the server, either. **Second, a connection-pool defect underneath it.** The pooling library the service uses picks one connection slot to wait on and stays on it, so a waiter could remain blocked while other slots in the same pool went idle. The service now schedules its own next-free slot selection, so a waiter proceeds as soon as **any** eligible slot frees. This also removes an unnecessary latency skew on the transaction pool under saturation; no configuration changes as a result. **Third, tear-downs that looked like a clean finish.** A client that stopped reading its stream could park the server on the send indefinitely, holding its buffers and its slot; that send is now bounded at 5 seconds. More importantly, a server-driven tear-down could end the stream with no status at all — which a client reads as "this subscription is over" and does not retry — because the status had to queue behind the very backlog that caused the tear-down. The server now reserves room for that status before it sends anything, so a server-driven **error** ending — a slow reader torn down, a backing-store failure, a cancellation that landed part-way through delivery — always **enqueues** one, and the server's own cleanup (the slot, the task, the buffers) completes without waiting for it. **Enqueued is not the same as seen, and the distinction matters when reading the behaviour:** the status queues behind whatever your client has not yet read, so a client that resumes reading gets the already-queued updates in order and then the status, while a client that never reads again never sees it — harmless, because that client is also no longer consuming anything and the server has already let the subscription go. Two endings carry no status at all, and both are intended: a client that has already disconnected, where there is nobody left to tell, and a cancellation arriving while the subscription is **idle** with nothing half-delivered, which still ends cleanly. For a `DeleteSession` that clean ending is the correct answer — the session is gone and there is nothing to reconnect to — and it is the same path that produces the known gap below when the canceller is a pod shutdown instead. (A third is possible only as a server fault: if an internal consistency check fails, a stream ends with nothing delivered. That is surfaced on the service's own metrics as an anomaly to investigate and should never be observed in normal operation.) A refusal *before* the stream starts is different again and is seen immediately, because the call simply completes as a failed RPC. A session deletion or a pod shutdown that lands part-way through delivery likewise now ends `UNAVAILABLE` rather than as a clean end, because the client holds a partial update at that point and must re-establish. **Known gap, tracked separately:** an **idle** subscription at the moment a pod shuts down still ends cleanly and is not automatically re-established (issue `b.e1h`) — expect a rolling restart to leave idle sessions detached until something client-side re-subscribes; nothing is lost when it does. **New operator-visible surface:** five Prometheus series — `live_edit_subscriber_admission_total{result}` (`admitted` / `refused_saturated` / `refused_waiter_cap` / `cancelled` — the only way to tell the three refusal causes apart, since all reach the client as `UNAVAILABLE`), `live_edit_subscriber_admission_wait_seconds`, `live_edit_subscriber_stream_exits_total{reason}` (complete over every ending, so `sum by (reason)` is total exits), `live_edit_valkey_pool_waiters{pool}` and `live_edit_valkey_pool_checkout_seconds{pool}`. The alert recipe carries a warning worth repeating here: **a replica that is turning subscriptions away shows flat CPU and flat memory**, so utilization-based autoscaling cannot see this condition and a utilization dashboard will read healthy throughout — watch the refusal counter instead. **What is explicitly unchanged:** upload size limits (`LIVE_EDIT_MAX_LAYER_SIZE`, 100 MB) and the chunk-count cap, the on-the-wire and stored formats, the default `LIVE_EDIT_SUBSCRIBER_POOL_SIZE` of 256, container resource requests and limits, and every other status code and message. **This fixes waiting and cleanup; it is not a memory limit.** A single slow subscriber can still accumulate a large in-memory backlog before any of these timers fires — per-subscriber and fleet-wide byte budgets are issue `b.ntm` and are not in this change. See [`docs/service/docs/scaling.md`](docs/service/docs/scaling.md) § Subscriber capacity and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md`.
- Helm autoscaling excludes test runners from server pod selection and rejects missing or zero credential-sidecar requests for enabled CPU/memory utilization metrics. Existing Deployment selectors remain upgrade-compatible. Credential sidecars inherit overridable requests of `20m` CPU and `32Mi` memory and render only when token-file mode is enabled and an image is supplied; see `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Configure the token sidecar.
- **A disaster-recovery restore no longer fails when one incremental backup is slow and the full backup underneath it is healthy** (Issue `b.bu8`). With incremental backups enabled (`LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_TAIL_ENABLED=true`), a restore fetches a full backup plus the incrementals resting on it, all inside one fixed time budget — the larger of `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` and twice `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`, 60 seconds at the defaults. That budget was meant to hold back enough time for the full backup's own fetch so that a single unresponsive incremental would cost only the newer content resting on it, and the restore would step down to an older recovery point. **At the shipped defaults it did not**: one unresponsive incremental consumed its whole share plus the real time already spent listing the store, which left fractionally too little for the full backup's fetch to be admitted at all, so the restore was declined outright and the layer was reported as having nothing to recover from — with a healthy full backup sitting one generation below it, which is the exact situation the step-down exists for. The time the full backup needs is now held back at the point each fetch is issued rather than checked against a threshold beforehand, so an incremental is fetched only while doing so still leaves the full backup funded, and one that would eat into that is cut short or skipped un-fetched. However many incrementals are unresponsive, the restore still reaches the full backup and lands. **The time budget itself is unchanged** — this is an accounting fix, not a larger number; enlarging it would reintroduce the queuing delay that budget exists to cap for every other client of the same layer. **One edge case moved in the other direction and briefly shipped that way:** a backup store slow enough to spend most of that budget on merely *listing* a layer's backups can run out of time before any backup answers, and the layer was then reported `NOT_FOUND` — terminal, and not picked up by standard client retry policies — where it previously surfaced a retryable failure. *(Corrected later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.hxk`, which makes that case retryable again — see the entry below. It still needs a store spending most of a minute on one listing, and should be treated as a backup-store latency incident.)* **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed default, no new or renamed metric, and no wire or API change.** The existing `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_lineage_total{outcome="stepped_down_budget"}` counter keeps its meaning, and its warning now reports the time actually left rather than a flat zero. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore.
- **A slow backup store is now reported as a retryable failure instead of as a layer with no backup, and a subscriber is no longer told its layer "was deleted" when nothing observed a deletion** (Issue `b.hxk`). Two places where the service made a claim it had not established. **The restore classification.** When a disaster-recovery restore ran out of its fetch time budget, the layer was reported `NOT_FOUND` — the same terminal answer given to a layer that genuinely has no usable backup, and one that standard client retry policies do not retry — even when the service had never managed to read the layer's backups at all. The realistic way to hit that is a store slow enough to spend most of the budget on merely listing a layer's backups (the edge case the `b.bu8` entry above states). The service now distinguishes the two: it answers `NOT_FOUND` only when the store holds nothing at all for that layer, or it holds backups and the service read every full one of them and none of them can be restored from — and answers a retryable `UNAVAILABLE` whenever it ran out of time with any of them still unread — because a backup it never read is not evidence that no backup exists. The practical effect: a client whose layer was written off during a store slowdown now retries and gets the layer back once the store keeps up, instead of giving up on it. **What this does not fix:** while the store stays slow the restore still cannot reach the backup, so the layer remains unavailable — the difference is that clients are told to retry rather than told the layer is gone. **The subscriber's terminal message.** A subscription that ended because the layer's stored content was gone and could not be rebuilt reported `layer '<name>' was deleted` — a claim about durable data that nothing had observed, and one that also fired an internal "this should not happen" warning as a matter of routine. That wording is now reserved for a real deletion signal; the state-loss case ends with the same `NOT_FOUND` status but says the layer's state was lost and could not be recovered (and, when the session's record of the layer is gone too, says that as well). **Status codes, retry behaviour and every other part of the subscription flow are unchanged**, including re-attaching automatically when a layer has been replaced by a fresh upload. **New operator-visible surface:** the `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_lineage_total{outcome}` counter gains a tenth cell, `exhausted_budget`, for the retryable case, kept separate from `exhausted` because the two call for opposite responses — restore your backups versus fix backup-store latency — and because `exhausted_budget` says nothing at all about your stored backups. A restore ending that way now counts as `error` rather than `not_recoverable` on `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_attempts_total`, so a backup-store latency incident legitimately moves the `error` rate; the metrics guide's baseline guidance is updated accordingly. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed default, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore.
- **A disaster-recovery restore now rebuilds a layer instead of merging the backup into whatever survived, and a rebuild that no retry can fix is reported as non-retriable instead of retriable** (Issue `b.f4t`). Three related corrections to what happens when a layer is rebuilt from a backup after a **partial** Valkey loss — the case where the record that says a layer exists is gone while the entries naming its prims, attributes, time samples, deletions and child ordering are still there. **First, a restore is now an authoritative rebuild.** Previously the backup was written *into* those leftovers: stale entries joined the restored set and were read back as though the backup had contained them, deletions the backup does not carry were resurrected, and child ordering under a prim the backup does not know about survived intact. The layer that came back was therefore a union of the backup and the debris, silently — nothing distinguished the restored content from the leftovers, at either end. The service now discards the leftovers before rebuilding, so the layer that comes back is exactly what the backup holds and nothing else. A structurally incomplete layer record is replaced rather than patched, which also closes two ways such a record could poison the rebuilt layer — one that would have made its large-attribute storage permanently unreclaimable, and one that would have left the rebuilt layer **permanently unwritable** with every edit refused. The layer's update stream is deliberately left alone; it is handled separately and earlier in the same restore. On the ordinary catastrophic case — the whole keyspace gone, nothing to discard — this costs a single existence check and nothing else. A restore that does find leftovers logs a warning naming the layer and what survived, and counts on the **new Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_residue_wiped_total`**, whose expected reading is zero unless a loss was partial. The storage accounted to any large attribute values those leftovers referenced is released along the way: the clearing step deliberately keeps the small record of which such values the layer was holding, and the rebuild that immediately follows compares that record against what the backup actually carries and gives up exactly the difference, inside the same write. Releasing all of them at the clearing step instead would give up values the rebuilt layer still uses — which would delete storage out from under a live layer — so the accounting is settled where the rebuilt contents are known. **Second, a restore that another replica finishes at the same moment can no longer have its work deleted while the guard holds.** The clearing step runs under a guard on the layer's own record, so a replica that lands its rebuild while another is clearing cancels that clear rather than losing the layer it just rebuilt; if the guard cannot be honoured the event is reported on the existing `live_edit_storage_watch_voided_total`, which gains a third `path` value, `restore_residue_wipe`. **The guard covers the clear, not the whole restore**, and both ways it comes apart are documented rather than elided: a backing store that runs the clear on a connection that never held the guard lets the clear go ahead unguarded, which replaces the other replica's rebuild wholesale and is what the counter above reports; and the guard's protection ends when the clear commits rather than when the rebuild is written, so a rebuild landing in the window between those two moments is added to rather than replaced — that second window was counted by nothing, so a zero on the counter did not rule it out. *(That second window is closed later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.h4m`, which puts the rebuild itself under an equivalent condition so exactly one rebuild lands and the loser is served the winner's layer — see the entry below. The first way, a clear that runs unguarded, is unchanged and this counter is still what reports it.)* See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Self-checks that should read zero. **Third, a rebuild the keyspace itself blocks is now reported as permanent.** If a key the rebuild must read or write holds a foreign Valkey data type, no retry can change that — only an operator removing the key can — yet the service classified it as a transient failure and answered the client with a retriable `UNAVAILABLE`, so the client retried into the identical error indefinitely while the service re-drove the whole rebuild and re-logged the error on every attempt. It now answers **non-retriable `INTERNAL`** and logs one ERROR naming the exact key and the remediation: the rebuild did not run, nothing was deleted, so establish what wrote that key, remove it, and let recovery retry. The same classification now applies to every read the restore makes, wherever the wrong-typed key is first touched. **Also fixed: a rebuild that never ran is no longer reported as one.** The check for "has another replica already rebuilt this layer" was a bare existence test on the layer's record, so a record that existed but was structurally incomplete read as "already restored" — while the read paths that route such a layer into recovery in the first place treat exactly that record as missing. `StatLayer` and `SubscribeReadLayer` therefore reported a successful recovery, counted it on `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_attempts_total{result="restored"}`, and handed the caller its one retry back with the identical error. The check now requires the record to actually carry the fields its readers need, so such a layer drives a real rebuild — which repairs the record — and the counter only moves for a rebuild that ran. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed default, and no wire or API change**; the only new client-visible outcome is the retriable-to-non-retriable reclassification above. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore.
- **Two service instances restoring the same layer at the same moment now land exactly one rebuild, and the instance that loses the race is served the layer the other rebuilt** (Issue `b.h4m`). Recovery from a backup is lazy and per-layer, so two clients asking two instances for a layer whose backing-store state is gone start two restores of the same layer. Nothing held one instance off while the other wrote the layer back: if the other instance's rebuild landed during this one's write, this instance's content was **added** to that rebuild rather than replacing it, and the layer came back holding a **mixture of two backups** — internally plausible, with no error to any client and no counter moving. That was reachable on **any** concurrent restore of one layer, and the exposure was **widest on a total loss** — the common catastrophic case, where nothing survives to be discarded and so no earlier check could catch it. The rebuild is now written under a condition the backing store itself enforces: the layer's record must still be absent at the instant of the write. The store serializes the two writes, so whichever lands first creates the record and cancels the other outright. **Exactly one rebuild lands, whole and from a single backup generation**, and neither ordering can produce a mixture. **The losing instance's rebuild writes nothing, and the loser is served the winner's layer** — a `StatLayer` re-reads and returns it, an `UpdateLayer` re-issues its edit once against the restored layer, a subscription retries its read once — so the client that triggered the restore gets its layer rather than an error, and neither `NOT_FOUND` nor a non-retriable failure can come out of the race. Where the layer's record is present but incomplete — a concurrent re-upload marking the layer as replaced is the concrete case — the rebuild is refused with a **retryable `UNAVAILABLE`** instead of being written over it, which would have left the layer permanently unwritable; the next attempt clears the partial record first and converges. **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_fenced_total{stage}`** counts every restore attempt that found another instance had already rebuilt the layer, with `stage` (`presence_probe` \| `residue_wipe` \| `object_write`) saying how late it was noticed; all three cells are pre-initialised at zero, and `presence_probe` is deliberately a partial count of the earliest discoveries. **None of the three is an error**: a rising `object_write` means instances were racing right up to the write, which is the expected shape during a fleet-wide recovery or a DR drill and calls for no action. `live_edit_storage_watch_voided_total` gains a fourth `path` value, `restore_write_object`, which is where a rebuild that committed *without* that condition being enforced is reported — the only remaining way a layer can end up holding a mixture, and expected to read zero. **The operational remedy previously published is withdrawn**: the README no longer asks you to avoid driving two instances at one layer during a recovery or a DR drill, because doing so is now safe. **Two residuals are stated rather than elided, and neither needs operator action.** *An accounting one* — a losing instance has already reserved storage for the large attribute values its own generation would have referenced, and that reservation is not given back: a bounded storage overhead per lost race, never data loss and never client-visible, with symmetric release tracked as a follow-up. *And one narrow race on the layer's update stream* — earlier in the same restore, before it can discover it has lost, an instance adjudicates that stream, and it now re-checks for the layer's record immediately before deleting a stream it has judged untrustworthy, so the common shape of the race is a skip rather than a deletion. If the winner's rebuild lands after that check — inside a single round trip in the common case, and up to whatever remains of that step's own 10-second time limit when the stream removal itself has to be retried — the loser deletes what has just become the **live** stream. **Subscribers are not disconnected and do not start the layer over**: they receive nothing until the layer's next edit recreates the stream, and the edits in between appear as a sequence gap their client library fills by re-syncing; new subscribers are unaffected and the rebuilt content is untouched. It reads as `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_stream_refused_total` moving alongside `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_fenced_total{stage="presence_probe"}` during a recovery, and closing it entirely (holding the same kind of condition across that deletion) is tracked as a follow-up. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed default, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore.
- **Two clients that spell the same base layer path differently now see the same sessions: base paths are canonicalized before they are stored, indexed, or authorized** (Issue `b.uoc`). `CreateSession` and `ListSessions` previously used the caller's path string byte-for-byte as the identity of a session's base path, so two RFC 3986-equivalent spellings of the same file produced two unrelated session sets. Kit sends reserved characters literally (`.../user@example.com/scene.usd`); an ordinary URL encoder — `encodeURIComponent`, Python's `quote`, Postman's "URL encode" — sends `%40`. Both were authorized by the storage API and both could create sessions, but neither could see the other's, and `ListSessions` reported the mismatch as an **empty list rather than an error**, so the failure was invisible. E-mail-shaped folder names make `@` present in essentially every real path, and the same `(path, name)` pair could be created twice under two spellings, defeating the duplicate-name check. The service now normalizes the path to a single canonical percent-encoding at those two RPCs — the only two that take a path off the wire — and uses only that form for the permission check, the session indexes, the stored session metadata, the session backup, and the logs; every other RPC already reads the stored value back. In the **path component**, every byte outside `A-Z a-z 0-9 - _ . ~ /` becomes an uppercase `%XX` escape and an encoded slash becomes a real separator, which is the same rule the storage API applies to its own resource identities — so the path this service stores is the string the storage API and the permission service compare against, and the authorization outcome is unchanged by the rewrite. The **scheme** is lowercased as well, and so is the **host** under the `ftp`, `file`, `http`, `https`, `ws` and `wss` schemes, matching what those upstream services do with the same spellings: `HTTPS://Host/x` and `https://host/x` are one path. A host under any other scheme (`omniverse://Server/x`), the user-info and port parts of the authority, query-parameter order, and percent-encoding inside those parts or inside a `?query` / `#fragment` suffix are deliberately **not** normalized — a query or fragment is preserved exactly as sent, neither stripped nor rejected.
