Everything the service folds is something the upstream services already fold, so the rule can never merge two paths they treat as different; at worst it leaves two spellings of one path in separate session sets. **New `INVALID_ARGUMENT` rejections** on those two RPCs: percent-escapes that do not decode to valid UTF-8, escapes that decode to a null byte or a control character (`Product%00Pound`), and `.` / `..` path segments including encoded ones (`..%2Fkey`); the existing 4,096-byte limit now also applies to the canonical form, whose error names the post-encoding length. **New diagnostic log surface:** whenever the service rewrites a path you sent, it logs both spellings — the one received and the canonical one it stored — as `CreateSession canonicalized base_path` at `info` (visible at the default log level, since a create durably fixes the spelling the session is keyed under) and as `ListSessions canonicalized base_path` at `debug` (`ListSessions` is a polled call, so set `LIVE_EDIT_LOG_LEVEL=debug` to see it); neither is logged when the path was already canonical, so the empty list that used to be the only symptom is now confirmable as a spelling rewrite. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** This service is pre-release, so no migration path is provided for sessions created under a non-canonical spelling — they are simply no longer found under that spelling. The same applies to a session restored from a backup taken before this change: the restore rebuilds that session's indexes from the path recorded in the backup exactly as recorded, so the session comes back under its original spelling and is likewise not found under the canonical one. See `docs/api/docs/validation.md` § Base path canonicalization.
- **The permission check now uses the storage service's stat endpoint (`HEAD`) instead of its read endpoint (`GET`)** (Issue `b.uoc`). Authorizing a path meant issuing a read-by-address `GET` whose response body — the layer's contents — was downloaded and discarded, and whose paging parameter the endpoint ignored. Two consequences are fixed: the wasted transfer on every permission check that missed the cache, and a hard failure mode in deployments where that endpoint answers with a redirect to blob storage rather than the object itself, which the service classified as a protocol violation and which therefore failed **every** authorization check on such a deployment. The check is now a `HEAD` against the stat-by-address endpoint, which carries the same authorization meaning (stat and read map to one upstream permission), returns no body, and matches what the gRPC authorization backend already did — so the two protocols now probe the same upstream operation. `204 No Content`, the stat endpoint's success answer, joins `200 OK` as an allow; every other status keeps its existing meaning. **Deployment prerequisite:** the storage service must route `HEAD` on its by-address endpoint. A backend that answers `405 Method Not Allowed` fails authorization closed and is not retried, so verify the storage service version in the target cluster before rolling this out. **No configuration change and no new environment variable.**
- **A disaster-recovery restore no longer rebuilds a layer underneath an update stream it could not read: it refuses with a retriable `UNAVAILABLE` instead, and a stale stream it does clear is now verified rather than assumed** (Issue `b.4et`). When a layer is rebuilt from a backup, the service checks whether that layer's live update stream — a separate key that a partial Valkey loss can spare — outlived the layer's state, so it can resume above whatever position the stream had reached. Previously **every** way that check could fail was treated as "there is no stream": a backing-store blip during the restore, an unexpected reply, or a key holding some other kind of value all collapsed into the same answer, and the delete of a stream judged untrustworthy was best-effort and unchecked. On a layer that had taken more edits since its last backup than the restore's guard band covers, that meant a layer rebuilt underneath a stream sitting above it — which is a **full write outage on that layer**: every subsequent edit is refused (loudly, on `live_edit_stream_id_collision_total`) until an operator clears the stream by hand, and nothing says so until the first client tries to edit. The service now distinguishes the three cases instead of conflating them, retrying each read a few times first so an ordinary blip costs nothing. **No stream survived** — the common whole-keyspace-loss case — restores exactly as before, including when the check itself failed, because the key's absence is proof enough. **The stream is there and its position cannot be established** now **refuses the restore**: the caller gets a retriable `UNAVAILABLE`, nothing is written, and the remediation is self-serve (retry once the backing store is healthy; if it persists, delete that layer's stream key `le:{object_id}:obj:stream` to unblock the restore — attached subscribers are not disconnected by that; they resume on the layer's next edit and their client library re-syncs across the gap). **The key holds a value the service did not write** proceeds deliberately — the service never deletes a value it does not own — so the layer comes back readable but **not writable** until an operator removes that value, which is now reported **at restore time** with a warning naming the key and its type rather than being discovered by the first refused edit. Separately, when the service does clear a stream whose position it has judged untrustworthy, that delete is now **verified** (and retried), and is authorised first by the same identity check the restore itself uses — which also fixes a case where a restore racing a `DeleteLayer` destroyed the short-lived stream that lets attached subscribers see the delete and disconnect cleanly, for a restore that was going to be declined anyway. If the delete cannot be verified, the restore is refused rather than run. **New Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_stream_unresolved_total{reason}`** with three pre-initialised cells — `probe_unreadable` and `unlink_unverified` (a restore that was refused) and `wrongtype_key` (a restore that landed on a foreign value and is unwritable until it is removed) — all expected to read zero forever, so **any increment is actionable**; an alert recipe and per-reason remediation ship with it. The existing `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_stream_refused_total` is **unchanged** and keeps its narrower meaning (the stream's position was read, judged untrustworthy, and the stream was cleared, on a restore that landed). **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed default, and no wire or API change** — the only new client-visible outcome is a restore answering the retriable `UNAVAILABLE` it already used for other transient recovery failures. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § After a disaster-recovery restore.
- **A backup of a session's layer list can no longer be silently overwritten with older contents after the backing store loses data, a backup whose identity file is missing now falls back to the previous recovery point instead of reporting the layer as unrecoverable, and an upload that runs short of time during an internal repair now gets a retryable answer instead of a bare deadline expiry** (Issue `b.hka`). Three operator-visible pieces, all in the disaster-recovery path, plus a set of internal hygiene changes with no observable behaviour (noted at the end).

  **Session backups survive a backing-store data loss.** Each session backup carries a freshness stamp, and the service uses those stamps to decide which of two concurrent writes stays durable. The stamps live in a small key in the backing store, and if that key was lost — a wipe or flush, an eviction, a failover onto a replica that had fallen behind, or a delete from outside the service — the stamps restarted from the beginning, so a write that was already in flight when the loss happened carried a *higher* stamp than everything written afterwards and was accepted as the newest. The result was a durable session backup silently replaced with pre-loss contents, plus a poisoned watermark that suppressed the next several legitimate backups. Each session's key now also carries a random token identifying that key's lifetime; a write minted against a key that no longer exists is refused outright and the backup is rebuilt from live state instead. **No operator action, no configuration change**, and nothing to migrate — a key that predates the token adopts one the first time it is touched.

  **New metric cell:** `live_edit_checkpoint_session_publish_superseded_total` gains `result="stale_incarnation"` (the label set is now `write`, `delete`, `stale_incarnation`, `exhausted`, all pre-initialised at zero as before). It is deliberately not folded into the existing `write` cell: those mean two replicas raced and the service repaired it, which is routine, while this one means the **backing store lost a key underneath a running pod** — the same event the object-id counter-repair metric reports from the other side, and worth investigating on the platform. It repairs itself, so the cell is a detector rather than an outage. One benign cause is worth ruling out first: the key self-expires after 24 hours, so a session left completely untouched for a day and then given exactly one more lifecycle event can produce a single isolated tick with nothing wrong. A dashboard row and an alert recipe ship with it.

  **A backup missing its identity file now steps down instead of failing the layer.** Every backup is written with a small companion file recording which layer it belongs to. If that companion was missing, the service used to invent an empty one, carry it three layers up, and refuse it there — reporting the layer as **not found** even though a perfectly restorable older backup was sitting right behind it. The backup store now refuses such a generation at the source, with a message naming both files, and recovery falls back to the next-older backup it can prove, exactly as it already does for a broken chain. This applies to full backups as much as to incremental ones. **New metric cell:** `live_edit_checkpoint_restore_lineage_total` gains `outcome="stepped_down_missing_meta"` (nine cells now, all pre-initialised at zero), which also narrows the existing `stepped_down_decode` to mean strictly "the bytes could not be read". Expected reading is zero forever: the service writes the companion **before** the payload and deletes the payload **before** the companion, so this state cannot arise from normal operation in either direction — an increment means the backup store is corrupt, a write was torn and rolled back, or something outside the service removed a file. Remediation is to let the backup loop write a current generation with its companion and to investigate the store; do not relax the guard.

  **Uploads that run short of time during a counter repair now get a meaningful error.** When a `CreateLayer` finds the internal layer-id counter missing it repairs it first and waits, and that wait is bounded so the *service* — not the request deadline — ends it, which is the difference between a retryable "a repair is in progress" answer and a bare deadline expiry that says nothing. The bound was computed from the configured deadline rather than from the time the request actually had left, so an upload that reached that step late (allocation is the last thing a `CreateLayer` does — receiving, authorizing and parsing the upload all happen first) or one whose client set a shorter per-call timeout was still dropped by the deadline. The bound is now two-thirds of whatever is genuinely left, so the retryable answer is the outcome in every case. **The `wait_timeout` cell on `live_edit_object_id_counter_repair_total` therefore no longer under-counts**, and dashboards that compared it against the request-deadline error rate can drop that cross-reference. No configuration change and no new setting — the fraction is derived, and the repair itself is unaffected.

  **Also in this change, invisible to clients:** the service no longer writes a backup for a layer whose deletion has already landed, no longer writes one that carries no layer identity, and refuses to run retention cleanup against a listing that describes a different incarnation of a layer id than the one currently live — all three are storage hygiene and safety-of-deletion, with no observable behaviour change. **No new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Incremental backups and retention.
- **A property whose value was replaced by a large one could keep reading back as the old, smaller value for every client that joined, caught up, or was rebuilt from a backup afterwards** (Issue `b.zvd`). The service stores a property's value in one of two ways depending on its size: small values are held with the property itself, large ones are stored separately and referenced. When an edit replaced a small value with a large one, the old small value was left behind alongside the new reference, and every subsequent read of the stored layer preferred the leftover — so a client joining the layer or catching up after a disconnection, and any copy of the layer rebuilt from a backup, saw the value from before the edit while the writer had been told the edit succeeded. Clients already subscribed at the time were unaffected: they receive the writer's own update directly and saw the new value. Nothing reported an error. The service now clears the previous form of the value as part of the same write, so a property carries exactly one value, always. Two consequences worth knowing. **Existing affected properties are not migrated**: this is a pre-release service, and a property in that state is corrected the next time it is written; the service now logs a warning whenever it reads one — carrying the layer's internal object id and one example property, not the layer name — so any that exist are visible rather than silent. And the separately-stored value such an edit *wrote* was never counted as being in use by the layer, because the layer read back as still holding the old small value — so **nothing releases it, and nothing will**: not a later edit to the property, and not deleting the layer — deleting a layer releases only the value each of its properties currently holds, which a superseded value by definition is not. Storage occupied by those values stays occupied. Correcting the read fixes this going forward only in the narrow sense that a value written from now on is the one the layer is seen to hold; it does not recover the storage already stranded. *(Both of those statements are retracted by later entries in this same unreleased set. **"Nothing releases it, and nothing will" is closed**: Issue `b.qpf` — see the entry below — makes an edit that supersedes a large value release the copy it replaced, on every path that writes a layer, so a superseded value is no longer stranded in the first place. **"Not recovered" is closed too**: recovering storage stranded before these changes is the job of the background reconciler added by Issue `b.yda`, and the upload-timestamp recording that reconciler was waiting on landed with Issue `b.rej`. Both are in this same unreleased set, so such storage is reclaimed on the reconciler's schedule, with no operator action.)*
- **A momentary backing-store hiccup while reading a layer could silently delete the ordering of a section's children** (Issue `b.zvd`). Reading a layer's stored state fans out into many small reads, and one of them — the read of a parent's child ordering — treated *any* failure as the answer "this parent has no children" instead of as a failure. A brief timeout or a dropped connection on that one read was therefore indistinguishable from an empty list, and a structural edit applied immediately afterwards would write that emptiness back as fact, discarding the real ordering with no error to the client and nothing in the logs. That read is now reported as a failure like every other read beside it: an edit that hits it is retried against fresh state, and a client subscribing or catching up receives a retriable `ABORTED` rather than a layer with pieces quietly missing.
- **Creating a layer now checks the keys it is about to write and refuses if one holds foreign data, instead of writing part of the layer and reporting failure** (Issue `b.zvd`). Edits to an existing layer already did this; creating a layer — including the service rebuilding a layer from a backup — did not, so a Valkey key left holding a value of the wrong kind by something outside the service caused a half-written layer rather than a clean refusal. Creation now fails closed, writes nothing, and names the offending key in an error log line, alongside which of the two cases it was: a client creating or re-uploading a layer, or a rebuild from a backup. **Operator-visible surface:** the existing counter `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total` gains its third cell, `backend="txn", site="create"`, pre-initialised to zero like the others; a related fix means a rebuild from a backup also replaces a layer's child orderings rather than appending to whatever survived. Same expectation as before — this counter should read zero forever, and any increment means something outside the service wrote into its Valkey keyspace. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? under `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total`. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.**
- **A replica no longer takes itself out of service while holding a perfectly good credential, and calls to the backup store made shortly before a credential rotation no longer fail on a round trip that could not have succeeded** (Issue `b.e49`). The service authenticates its own calls to the backup store with a long-lived service-identity token that a credential sidecar writes to a file and replaces periodically. It used to treat a cached token as unusable for the last 60 seconds before it expired, even though the token was still perfectly valid — the same mistake that was corrected on the *backing-store* credential earlier in this release, at one third the width. That produced two symptoms, both once per token lifetime, on any deployment whose sidecar writes the replacement less than about 75 seconds ahead of expiry. **A healthy replica reported `NOT_SERVING`.** Readiness is a routing signal, so this took the pod out of rotation for *all* traffic — not just the backup-store calls the token is for — while a working credential sat in memory, and because token expiry tends to line up across a rolling deployment, it could take a large share of the fleet out at once. **And a backup-store call made in that window went out with no credential at all**, was rejected as unauthenticated by construction, re-read the same not-yet-replaced file, retried, and was rejected again — a wholly failed call manufactured from a working token. Both are fixed the same way: a cached token is now presented for its whole real lifetime, and the service treats it as unavailable only when it has genuinely expired or has never been read. **This is never worse than the old behaviour**: if the far side really would reject a token close to expiry, presenting it fails exactly as withholding it did. **A third change, visible to clients:** when a call to the backup store is rejected twice during a credential rotation, the service now reports it as **retriable** unless the rejected retry actually carried a credential. The deciding question is what the rejected request put on the wire — not whether the token file happened to change, and not how much life the token had left when the retry began: the common case — the sidecar has not written the replacement yet, so the request went out with no credential at all — clears itself within seconds, but it was previously reported as a permanent failure, which (since the change described in the next entry) told clients to stop retrying something that was about to start working. A retry can span several attempts over a minute or more, so a token that was still valid when the retry started may have expired before the attempt that was finally rejected; the service therefore judges each rejection by what that request carried. A genuinely rejected live credential is still reported as permanent and still needs an operator. **Early warning is unchanged and does not live in the readiness state**: watch `live_edit_service_token_expires_in_seconds{role}` and `live_edit_service_token_refresh_headroom_seconds{role}`, both already shipped. On the `storage-api` series the actionable threshold for the headroom gauge is simply the **zero crossing** — that reader holds no long-lived authenticated connection and repairs itself on a rejection, so there is no window above zero for it to miss. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, no new or removed metric, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/authentication.md` § Service identity for outbound calls and `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are credentials and auth services healthy? under `live_edit_service_token_available` and `live_edit_service_token_refresh_headroom_seconds`.
- **A layer whose rebuild from a backup can never succeed is now reported as a permanent failure instead of a temporary one, so clients stop retrying it and operators get a log line naming the cause** (Issue `b.33e`). When a client touches a layer whose state is missing, the service rebuilds it from the backup store on the spot. If that rebuild failed, the client was always told the service was temporarily unavailable — whatever the reason. Standard client retry policies treat that as "try again shortly", so a rebuild that could never succeed — a corrupt or unreadable backup file, a backup store answering in a way its own contract forbids — was retried in a loop by every client touching the layer, and the failure produced no log line at all, only a metric increment, which made a data-integrity problem look like a passing outage. The service now distinguishes the two: a genuinely transient failure (the backup store is slow, unreachable, or briefly erroring) is still reported as retriable and clients recover on their own, while a failure that no retry can clear is reported as a **non-retriable error** and written to the pod log at ERROR with the full detail. The client-visible message for the non-retriable case is the generic `internal error` — as with every other non-retriable failure, the service does not put internal detail on the wire, so the pod log is the diagnostic surface. **Client action:** none for the retriable case; a client receiving the non-retriable error should surface it rather than retry, which is what standard retry policies already do. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change** — only which status code a failing rebuild reports and whether it is logged at ERROR. Session rebuilds already behaved this way and are unchanged; the two paths now share one rule. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? under `live_edit_checkpoint_recovery_attempts_total`, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Logs for reading a failure against its ERROR line.
- **After a layer was rebuilt from a backup, clients reconnecting to it received none of the restored content — they were told they were already up to date while holding content the restore had rolled back** (Issue `b.2ti`). A restore rebuilds the layer under its original identity, so a client that reconnects afterwards asks to be caught up from the last edit it saw rather than starting over. The restored content was internally labelled as older than anything such a client had already seen, so the catch-up filtered all of it out: the client got an empty catch-up and an "up to date" acknowledgement, or in some cases a message that only told it to discard prims. Restored content is now labelled at the sequence the layer resumes from, which is above every edit any client can still be holding, so a reconnecting client receives the restored state — with the default catch-up path, and on the legacy path (`LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false`) for a client whose gap has already aged out of the layer's retained update window. **Expect one larger catch-up per reconnecting client after a restore**: it receives the whole layer rather than a small delta covering its gap, visible as a one-off spike on `live_subscribe_catch_up_delivered_bytes` — see `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is it serving?, and `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost for what does and does not rewind after a restore. **Two limits are worth knowing, both tracked separately.** On the legacy path, a client whose gap is still *inside* the retained update window receives none of the restored content and must start a fresh session on the layer to converge — a reason to leave that setting at its default where disaster recovery matters. And on every path, a prim the restore rolled back and does not carry at all — created after the backup was taken and lost with the failure — is not retracted from a client that still holds it, so such a client keeps showing it until it starts a fresh session on that layer. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.**
- **Large layers could not be backed up or restored through the `StorageApi` backup store: the service treated the backup store's "fetch this from blob storage instead" answer as a permanent failure** (Issue `b.z22`). A backup store declines to move large files through its own API — above a size threshold it answers with a short-lived pre-signed URL pointing at the underlying blob storage and expects the client to use it. The service had no handling for that answer and classified it as a permanent error, with three consequences, all of them scaling with how big a layer is. **Restoring any layer whose backup file exceeded the read threshold failed outright** (256 KiB in the reference deployment) — which is to say disaster recovery did not work for exactly the large documents it exists to protect, and the failure only surfaced at the moment a restore was attempted. **Backups of any layer whose file reached the write threshold were never written at all** (2 MiB in the reference deployment), silently capping how large a layer could be and still have a recovery point. And with incremental backups enabled, a hot layer's chain could not be composed into a fresh full backup once its incrementals grew past the read threshold, so the chain hit its limit and stopped capturing — visible as `live_edit_checkpoint_tail_total{outcome="refused_chain_full"}` climbing with no matching `rebased`, and as a backup age that rose without bound for that layer. **Reads are fixed by not needing the redirect at all.** Every backup file the service reads now explicitly asks the backup store to send it inline, which the store honours at any size and streams rather than buffering, so a restore stays a single request to the endpoint you already configured and disaster recovery gains no dependency on a network path it has never used. If a store answers with a redirect anyway, the service follows it once, succeeds, and warns loudly — that fallback is a contract-drift signal, not the design. **Writes complete the redirect handshake**: the file is uploaded to the pre-signed URL and then acknowledged back to the backup store. **Large writes ask the store how to upload before sending anything**: for files at or above 256 KiB the service asks the backup store once which upload method it wants at that size and caches the answer. Only a size the store says it wants by redirect — or by multipart, which the service converts to a redirect — is sent with no payload at all and uploaded straight to the pre-signed URL, so a multi-megabyte file is never streamed at a store that was never going to read it. Any size the store does not answer for is sent inline exactly as before: guessing the other way could turn a writable backup into an unwritable one, so the fallback deliberately points at the path that always works. If the store cannot answer — an older version without the endpoint, or a service identity lacking permission on it — the service logs one warning per five-minute window and falls back to sending the payload and following the redirect when it arrives. The same fallback covers a store whose size settings change under a running pod: the service re-asks once and stops asking for the rest of a five-minute window if contradicted again. **Backups publish correctly either way.** Files at or above the size where the store would propose a *multipart* upload (64 MiB in the reference deployment) are routed to an ordinary pre-signed upload as well; where the store cannot answer the sizing question, a multipart proposal is converted away in one extra request. Multipart itself is not implemented and is not needed. **A failure at the pre-signed URL is retried within the same backup attempt, against a freshly issued URL** — the whole exchange (ask, upload, acknowledge) is one attempt of the existing retry ladder, so an expired or rejected pre-signed URL, a blob-storage failure, or a failed acknowledgement costs one attempt and is re-driven from the beginning rather than waiting for the next backup pass. An expired URL is never re-presented. **Operator action, for `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_ADAPTER=StorageApi` only: confirm the pod can reach blob storage.** Because large writes now go to a pre-signed URL, the live-edit pod needs DNS, TLS trust, and outbound network policy for the blob-storage endpoint the backup store hands out — a *different host* from the backup-store API, so a rule that permits one does not permit the other. This is a prerequisite for backing up layers whose backup file reaches ~2 MiB, and it was not previously required because those backups simply never happened. Reads deliberately do not need it. See `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § Network access to the backup store. **Credentials never travel to blob storage**: a pre-signed URL is itself the credential for the file it names, so the service attaches no service-identity token to that hop and never writes such a URL to a log line or a metric label. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change**; the existing per-call time ceiling covers the extra hop rather than being added to, so every published timing bound is unchanged. **New operator-visible surface:** the Prometheus counter `live_edit_checkpoint_adapter_redirect_total{verb, outcome}`. Its `verb="put"` cells are the *expected* path for large backups and should be busy on any fleet with realistically-sized layers; its `verb="get"` cells should stay at zero, and a non-zero reading there means your backup store is ignoring the inline read request and every large restore is taking an extra hop through blob storage. Three `verb="put"` cells report the upload-sizing question described above: `preflight_redirect` (the expected steady state for large backups — it should track the delivered/acknowledged pair one-for-one), `preflight_unavailable` (the store could not be consulted, so uploads fall back to streaming their payload — an optimisation loss, not a failure) and `preflight_stale` (the store was consulted and then contradicted its own answer on a file it had classified). See **`live_edit_checkpoint_adapter_redirect_total`** in `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current?, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for its alert rules.
- **Deleting a layer immediately after creating it no longer leaves a broken record behind, and a deleted layer's backups are now cleaned up instead of kept forever** (Issue `b.vn6`). Two problems, one fix, and neither ever lost data. **First**, when a layer was deleted while its first backup was still being written, the service finished by writing that backup's position onto the layer's record — and because the record had just been removed, writing it **recreated** the record, as a stub with none of the layer's actual details. Observed on a development cluster where a client created and deleted eight layers in about a second each. Nothing a client could see was affected, but the backup loop then rejected that stub on every pass and logged a warning about it, once per layer per pass, until an internal cleanup swept it up about five minutes later — and while it sat there it distorted the "how far behind is our backup coverage?" measurement, which is a metric operators are told to alert on. The service now updates a layer's record only if the layer still exists; when it does not, the update is refused instead, and the backup that was written for the by-then-deleted layer is deleted rather than left on the backup store. All four places that update this record were changed, so the same race cannot reappear on the periodic backup path. **Second, and larger in practice**, deleting a layer never removed its backups at all: the ordinary retention cleanup only ever runs for a layer that is still being backed up, and the housekeeping pass only looked for backups that had failed part-way, so a deleted layer's completed backups stayed on the backup store forever, along with the external-value blobs they referenced. Every deletion leaked, whether or not it raced anything. The service now records each deleted or replaced layer on a durable list and the housekeeping pass reclaims their backups, giving that job a guaranteed share of each pass's time budget without reducing the share guaranteed to blob deletion. The store-wide listing that opens each pass is additionally given its own, larger bound — half the pass's budget rather than the quarter the new job gets — because a listing that runs out of time costs the job it feeds entirely rather than merely cutting it short, and tying it to the smaller share would have left a large enough store reclaiming no leftovers at all on every pass. A failed or timed-out listing costs **only** that one job: the reclamation of deleted layers' backups and the deletion of unreferenced blobs both still run on such a pass, and both backlog readings are still published, so a growing backlog can never be mistaken for a pass that did not run. **The reclamation is driven by the deletion itself, never by inference** — the service never goes looking for backups that merely *look* abandoned, because after a total loss of the backing store no layer looks alive and a rule of that shape would delete the entire backup set at precisely the moment it is the only copy. It is also re-armed from the other direction: a backup that lands for a layer deleted while the backup was being written puts that layer back on the list, so a backup arriving after the pass had already cleaned up is not missed. **Before deleting anything it re-checks whether the layer has come back** (a disaster-recovery restore does exactly that) and spares it if so — a check that runs once per layer *and* again immediately before each individual backup is deleted, because a slow backup store can put minutes between the two. That check has a third answer as well: a layer whose record is still present but marked as replaced by a fresh upload is a dead layer with a stale record rather than a layer that came back, so the pass deletes nothing and also keeps the entry on its list, rather than dropping it and losing the only record that those backups are still owed a cleanup. **Two limits worth knowing.** Layers deleted by an earlier version were never recorded and are **not** reclaimed retroactively; stub records left behind by an earlier version clear themselves within about five minutes. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** **New operator-visible surface:** one new outcome on `live_edit_checkpoint_base_bootstrap_total` (`unstamped_header_absent`, the deleted-mid-write race, expected to be small and self-clearing), three on `live_edit_checkpoint_reconcile_total` (`dead_object_reaped`, `dead_deferred`, `dead_spared`), and a new gauge `live_edit_checkpoint_dead_object_backlog{service}` reporting how many deleted layers are still awaiting reclamation — aggregate it with `max()` and read it as a trend, since it is what makes the new job falling behind visible at all. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? for how to read each (`dead_spared` above zero is the safety check working rather than a fault), and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule that ships for the new gauge.
- **Intermittent write failures and request deadlines on token-authenticated clustered deployments, clustered around the moments the service refreshed its backing-store credential** (Issue `b.7td`). On a clustered Azure Managed Redis deployment using `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile`, the service's periodic credential re-authentication was intermittently rejected by the cache with "not allowed in transactions", followed by refresh timeouts, connections reported as unresponsive, and requests failing with `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` — on callers that had done nothing wrong. The re-authentication was not the cause; it was the first thing to touch a pooled connection that an *earlier* request had already left mid-transaction, and it was the most likely thing to touch one because it reaches every shard. Once a connection is in that state neither the service nor its client library could previously detect or repair it, so a single such connection went on failing every request that borrowed it until the pod restarted — and because the failures surfaced as cross-slot or deadline errors on unrelated requests, they pointed nowhere useful. Two changes fix it. **Connection health is now proven at checkout.** Before every transactional write the service checks the connection it just took from the pool with two constant-time commands that ask the cache whether a transaction is already open on it, and clears any leftover watch in passing. A connection that fails that check — or does not answer it — is normally closed and rebuilt on the spot, before the request runs on it, so a single bad connection can no longer poison everything that follows. Every failed check is recorded; the *rebuild* is paced, in two situations where rebuilding indiscriminately would cause more harm than it prevents. A cache having a bad minute makes every connection fail the check at once, so rebuilds are spaced out rather than answering a slow cache with a reconnect storm. And if rebuilding a connection repeatedly fails to change the verdict, the service stops rebuilding for that reason until some connection passes the check again — a rebuilt connection that answers identically is evidence the problem is not on the connection at all, and the counter below is what makes that state visible. The check runs on **every** deployment shape, not just clustered or token-authenticated ones. It costs roughly two fast round trips per transactional write and cannot itself fail a request: if the rebuild fails, the connection is used anyway and the request's normal retries take over. **The credential is re-sent only when it has actually changed.** Previously the service re-authenticated every pooled connection on a five-to-six-minute timer whether or not the token had changed — a permanent, cluster-wide stream of authentication traffic carrying a credential that rotates about once a day, and the traffic whose stalls produced the cascade above. It now checks more often (every 60–90 seconds) but sends a credential only on a real token rotation, plus a short window afterwards so every connection picks the new token up. Recurring authentication traffic to the cache drops by roughly **two orders of magnitude**, and rotation pickup **improves** rather than degrading, because the shorter check interval lands inside the window where the new token is available and the old one has not yet expired far more often than the old one did. **The AWS `iam` mode is deliberately unchanged** — it mints a fresh token on every authentication by design, so it is already "only when it changed", and its periodic re-authentication is what keeps connections alive against AWS's 12-hour forced disconnect. **The sidecar sizing recommendation gets looser, not tighter:** the token source's refresh lead should now be at least **195 seconds** ahead of expiry at the defaults (180 seconds for a rotation to reach every pooled connection, plus the 15-second token-file poll interval), down from 375 seconds — so any deployment sized against the previous guidance already clears the new one and **needs no change**. The paired warning threshold on `live_edit_service_token_refresh_headroom_seconds{role="valkey"}` moves from 360 to 180 seconds to match. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.** **New operator-visible surface:** two Prometheus counters. `live_edit_valkey_txn_dirty_checkouts_total{reason}` records each connection found unfit — the verdict, not the rebuild, per the pacing above — expected to stay at zero, with the `leaked_multi` and `unexpected_reply` cells worth an alert, since those are the ones that confirm connection state is being corrupted rather than merely a slow or unreachable cache. `live_edit_valkey_txn_repair_ineffective_total{reason}` records the connections the service declined to rebuild because rebuilding them had stopped helping; also expected to stay at zero, and worth a **higher-severity** alert than its sibling, because a sustained rate means this protection is switched off for that reason and will not resume on its own — most often a proxy or endpoint in front of the cache whose error wording the service cannot classify, which the accompanying error log names along with the reply it rejected. See `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Connection repair disabled, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Token refresh for the revised sizing rule. **Honest scope:** the mechanisms are covered by tests against a single-node backing store; that they remove this specific failure on a clustered managed cache is expected but is confirmed only by the next run against that environment.
- **On a clustered backing store, requests could fail with a cross-slot error naming keys they never touched — a fault left behind by an *earlier, unrelated* request on the same pooled connection** (Issue `b.ji8`). Some writes use an optimistic transaction: the service asks the backing store to watch the keys it is about to change, then releases that watch when the transaction commits or is abandoned. A watch belongs to the connection it was placed on, and the release command carries no key of its own — so on a clustered store the client library had nothing to route it by and sent it to an arbitrary shard, which is frequently not the shard holding the watch. The watch was left standing, the connection went back into the pool still carrying it, and the cost was paid by whichever request borrowed that connection next. On a clustered store fronted by a proxy (Azure Managed Redis, Redis Enterprise) that request was rejected outright with a cross-slot error citing the *previous* request's key alongside its own — reported to the client as a non-retriable internal error — which made the failure look like a bug at a call site that was in fact correct. Observed on a dev Azure Managed Redis cluster as failures of structural-edit writes and of a layer-creation probe. **Single-node backing stores were never at risk of the hard failure**, but the same leftover watch could cancel an unrelated transaction there, which the service already retried transparently. The service now records which shard each watch was placed on and routes every release to that shard explicitly. A release that fails is retried once at the last chance the service has — as the connection is handed back to the pool — with each attempt bounded at 0.65 seconds and the release as a whole at 1.5 seconds, so the retry reliably gets to run and a stuck release can never outlast the drain the service performs while shutting down. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no wire or API change, and no change to how keys are named or transactions are committed.** (The per-attempt bound applies to this release path only and deliberately overrides `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` there, so raising that setting cannot push a release past the shutdown drain.) **New operator-visible surface:** the Prometheus counter `live_edit_storage_watch_unwatch_failures_total{site}` records a release the service gave up on — expected to stay at zero, and worth an alert on the `site="drop"` cell, which is the one that means a connection went back to the pool still holding a watch. A release that failed because **the connection itself was already gone is not counted** and is not logged as an error: a watch cannot outlive its connection, so nothing was left behind — which keeps ordinary connection recycling and failover from raising a false alarm. Genuine give-ups are logged at error level, replacing an earlier warning that described the condition as self-healing on the next reconnect; it does clear for that connection, but not before the next request to borrow it has already failed. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Self-checks that should read zero, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule.
- **A configuration-only chart upgrade now actually takes effect — the pods restart and pick up the new settings** (Issue `b.pfu`). Previously a `helm upgrade` that changed only settings held in the chart's ConfigMap updated that ConfigMap but restarted nothing, and the service reads its configuration once at startup — so every replica kept running the old settings, with no error and no event, until some unrelated change (an image bump, a manual restart) happened to roll the deployment and applied the earlier edit retroactively. This was the routine upgrade path for anyone who pins the chart version and changes only values. The pod template now carries a checksum of the rendered settings ConfigMap, so changing any ConfigMap-backed value changes the pod template and Kubernetes performs a rolling restart; the roll is staggered by the chart's default rolling-update strategy — no `strategy:` is set, so Kubernetes' defaults apply (`maxUnavailable: 25%` / `maxSurge: 25%`), which at 3 replicas means one surged replacement at a time, gated on the new pod becoming ready before an old one is terminated, with the available count never dropping below 3. There is no opt-out. (The chart's PodDisruptionBudget plays no part in this: it governs voluntary disruptions from outside the deployment, such as node drains and evictions.) **Secret content is not covered:** rotating the value inside the Kubernetes Secret named by `envSecretName` still does not restart pods, so trigger that restart deliberately (change it through the Helm path, or restart the deployment yourself). This includes `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_URL` under the chart's default source — with neither `valkey.url.value` nor `valkey.url.valueFrom` set it is a reference to that Secret's `valkey-url` key, not a value in the pod template, so rewriting the key rolls nothing. Note also that `kubectl rollout undo` reverts the pod template but leaves the new ConfigMap in place, so roll a configuration change back through Helm — revert the values change, or `helm rollback` — rather than with `rollout undo`.
- **A second kind of small bookkeeping record left behind when a layer is retired is now reclaimed too, closing the last known instance of that slow memory creep** (Issue `b.jdr`). This completes the `b.9fn` fix in this same release (entry below), which reclaimed the record holding the order of a layer's *top-level* items. Layers keep one such ordering record per item that has children, and a layer can legitimately end up with an ordering record attached to an item that is not itself present in the layer — an accepted state that arises from the way partial updates are expressed, and one the service itself produces when bringing a reconnecting client up to date. Those records were reachable from nothing the retirement cleanup could enumerate, so like the top-level record before them they were left behind every time a layer was overwritten, deleted, removed with its session, or reclaimed by the background sweep — and, because the backing store is deliberately configured never to evict data, nothing else would ever reclaim them. The service now keeps an explicit list of which ordering records a layer owns, updated in the same atomic write as the records themselves, and retirement deletes everything on that list in addition to everything it could already work out. **No correctness impact**: as with `b.9fn`, nothing a client could read, write, or subscribe to was affected and no edit was lost or mis-ordered — the leftovers were inert and a few bytes each. The accumulation rate was lower than `b.9fn`'s, since it required layers that actually carry such records rather than every layer with any top-level item. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, no new metric, and no wire or API change. Layers written before this release carry no such list, and retirement still reclaims only what it can work out for itself; layers written from this release on are cleaned up completely. **Already-leaked records**: as with `b.9fn`, no cleanup pass is added for leftovers accumulated by earlier builds — they cannot be discovered after the fact, and on a pre-release service the residue is not worth a dedicated pass. They disappear the next time the backing store for an environment is reset or redeployed.
- **An edit that the service could not announce to live subscribers is now rejected outright instead of being applied anyway** (Issue `b.ph9`). Every committed edit appends one entry to the layer's update stream — the stream live subscribers read — under an ID derived from the layer's own edit counter, so a healthy layer's stream top and its edit counter always agree. If they ever diverged (the realistic cause is a layer rebuilt from a backup underneath an update stream that outlived the layer's own state; the other is something outside the service writing to that stream), the service would **apply the edit to the layer and advance its counter, then fail to append the stream entry, and return an error to the client** — because neither of the two mechanisms the service commits with rolls back a failure that happens mid-execution. The result was the worst possible split: the client was told the write failed, the layer said it succeeded, and every live subscriber missed that edit permanently with no error surfaced at either end. Both commit paths now decide every possible rejection **before** anything is written, so a write in this situation fails whole — the layer's contents are untouched, its counter does not advance, and the client receives a non-retriable `INTERNAL` error. **What operators see changes, and for the better**: the condition is now loud and localised rather than silent. Writes to the affected layer keep failing until the layer's update stream is cleared (reads and subscriptions are unaffected), each refusal logs a warning naming the session, layer, and stream ID involved along with remediation specific to the operation that was refused, and a new counter reports the rate. **The detail is server-side only**: as with every internal error, the message the client receives is the generic `internal error`, so the warning log and the counter — not any client-side log — are what identify the condition. Ordinary contention between clients editing the same layer at the same time is unaffected and is still absorbed by the internal retry loop — that is a different condition and does not touch this counter. Neither does a write that races a deletion or a re-upload of the same layer: it hits the same internal refusal for a harmless reason, and the service re-checks the layer before reporting, so the client gets the ordinary "layer is gone / was replaced" error instead. When that re-check cannot be completed because the backing store blipped, the write is retried against fresh state instead of being reported as a collision — so a backing-store outage surfaces as the usual retriable exhaustion error, never as a false "this layer is corrupt" verdict. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule.
- **On a clustered backing store, routine maintenance — a failover, a resharding, or a managed-cache maintenance window — dropped every live subscription, and under sustained churn could also fail an ordinary read with a non-retriable error; both are now treated as the temporary conditions they are** (Issue `b.cv4`). When a clustered backing store moves a slice of its keyspace from one node to another, it tells the client "that data is over there now" and the client follows the redirect. For one specific command the service issues — the read that keeps a live `SubscribeReadLayer` subscription supplied with updates — the service deliberately allows the client library exactly one attempt, so that a read which times out can never be silently re-sent onto another connection and steal updates from its replacement. That safeguard is correct and is kept. What was not understood is that following a redirect *costs* that one attempt, so the client library could never complete the hop and reported a budget-exhaustion failure instead. The service classified those failures as permanent. In practice that meant every ordinary topology change tore down every live subscription on every replica — clients saw `UNAVAILABLE` and re-subscribed, so editing continued, but every subscriber paid a full reconnect and catch-up. **Ordinary reads and writes were affected far less**, because that one-attempt restriction applies only to the subscription read described above: every other command the service issues gets three attempts, and following a redirect costs two, so a single hop is followed transparently and never reaches this classification at all. Such a command failed with `INTERNAL` — which clients are not expected to retry — only when it exhausted all three attempts, meaning it was redirected repeatedly or hit connection churn on top of a redirect, as happens during a resharding rather than a brief failover. The service now recognises all of these failures as the temporary redirect they really are: a subscription backs off and resumes from its exact position on the layer's update stream, and a read that is not on a retry path returns retriable `UNAVAILABLE` instead of `INTERNAL`. **Who was affected**: clustered deployments only, which includes both managed targets (Amazon MemoryDB and Azure Managed Redis both require cluster mode). A single-primary self-hosted backing store never issues these redirects and was never affected. **Client-visible change**: a read that previously failed with `INTERNAL` during a topology change now fails with `UNAVAILABLE`; clients following the standard gRPC retry policy recover automatically where they previously surfaced a hard error. Live subscriptions that previously dropped now pause briefly and resume — the retry backoff starts under 100 ms and widens to at most 5 s while the topology settles. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change. **Operator note on an existing counter**: `live_edit_valkey_consumer_permanent_exits_total{phase}` is documented as expected to read zero on every deployment. On a clustered store it did not — it ticked once per live subscriber on every topology change, and any historical non-zero reading on such a deployment is likely to have this cause rather than the credential or endpoint misconfiguration the counter is meant to flag. It should now genuinely read zero.
- **A backing-store data loss that left the service running could hand a new layer the identity of an old one; the service now refuses to allocate an identity it cannot prove is unused** (Issue `b.b2j`). If the backing store lost its data while the service pods kept running — a wipe, a failover onto an empty replica, an eviction of the whole keyspace — nothing restarted the service: the process is healthy and the backing store answers normally against an empty dataset. The internal counter that mints layer identities was gone with everything else, and the next upload silently restarted numbering from the beginning, handing new layers the identities that layers already backed up on the backup store still held. Three things could then go wrong, all silent: restoring the old backup overwrote the **new** layer's live content; a restore could serve one layer's content under another layer's name; and retention could keep the old layer's backups while deleting the new layer's. The service now **refuses to allocate an identity from a missing counter**. On the first upload after such a loss it re-establishes the counter above every identity any retained backup can still claim — read from the backup store's own counters record plus `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_COUNTER_SAFETY_MARGIN` (default 1000, unchanged) — and only then allocates. If the backup store cannot be read at that moment the upload fails with a retriable `UNAVAILABLE` rather than guessing; clients retry. **A slow backup store now generally produces `UNAVAILABLE` rather than a deadline expiry.** The repair runs in the background, so it is never abandoned half-finished by the request that triggered it, and an upload waits on it only up to two-thirds of `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` before returning that retriable error — the repair keeps going, records what happened, and the client's retry joins the same repair instead of starting another. Previously a slow backup store simply ran the request out of time: the client saw a bare `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` with nothing logged or counted about the counter, and each retry restarted the same doomed read from scratch. **The `UNAVAILABLE` shape depends on the repair starting early in the request's deadline window.** The deadline covers the whole request, and identity allocation is the last step of an upload — the content has already been received, authorized, and parsed by then — so an upload that has already spent more than a third of its deadline getting that far, or one whose client set a shorter per-call timeout of its own, still ends in `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED` mid-wait. That case is not a regression and loses nothing: the repair is running in the background regardless, so it still completes, still logs, and is still counted; only the `wait_timeout` tick is absent, and the client's retry joins the same repair as before. In short, the two-thirds budget makes the common case report itself properly rather than promising the deadline can never win the race. There is no new environment variable for the wait — it is derived from the existing RPC deadline. As a second line of defence, a stored backup can now only be restored under the exact session and layer name it was captured for, so one layer's content can never be materialized under another layer's identity even if an identity were somehow reused; that check also no longer gives up on very large datasets, where it previously stopped scanning and refused legitimate restores. **One backup-compatibility consequence**: because the check reads the session and layer name recorded alongside each backup, a backup whose companion metadata record is missing can no longer be restored — the service refuses it rather than materializing content under an identity it cannot verify, and reports it as "not found". Two shapes reach this. The first is pre-release **local-disk** backup directories written by a very early build that recorded no such metadata at all. The second, and the one not to overlook on a production deployment, is a **`StorageApi`** backup generation whose metadata companion object was lost out-of-band (it returns "not found" while the content object is still there) — the service treats it identically and refuses it. In both cases the remedy is to let the periodic backup pass write a current generation, not to work around the check. Backups written intact by any build that records the metadata — which is every build that also ships this change — are unaffected; it is only the loss of the companion record, on either backing target, that produces the refusal. **Operator action: none** in the ordinary case — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change; the only exception is the missing-metadata case just described, where the action is to confirm a current, complete backup generation exists before relying on those backups for recovery. **New operator-visible surface**: a counter `live_edit_object_id_counter_repair_total{outcome}` reports each repair, with the `outcome` label separating the two cases that reach it. Every cell is pre-initialised at zero, which matters more than usual here: this counter is expected to read zero forever on a healthy deployment, so without it "no repair has ever fired" would be indistinguishable from a missing series. **An increment on `repaired_seeded` or `repaired_noop` is evidence your backing store lost its data while the service was running** — the backup store held a counters record, so backups genuinely claim identities the missing counter was protecting — and that event also destroyed every live session, layer, and presence record, so it is worth paging on in its own right, as are `failed` and `failed_after_repair`. `failed` means the repair finished without leaving a usable counter behind and the upload was rejected — a deliberately broad cell covering the backup-store read failing *or* running out of time, a counters record that is present but corrupt, the backing-store write that seeds the counter failing, the read-back of the counter failing, and a repair that ended without reporting at all; the accompanying error log names which, so triage starts there rather than at "the backup store must be unreachable". `failed_after_repair` is the most severe of the four and means something different: the counter was re-established successfully and was **missing again** immediately afterwards, so the backing store is losing writes it has already acknowledged — the upload fails with a retriable `UNAVAILABLE` without retrying harder, and the backing store is what needs investigating. The remaining outcomes are logged at warning level and are deliberately **not** paged. `repaired_initialized` and `unhooked_initialized` are the expected one-off on the first upload against a brand-new backing store, where no backup claims any identity and nothing can collide — though "one-off" describes the event and not necessarily the count, since several uploads arriving together can each observe the missing counter and `unhooked_initialized` (the no-backup-store case) ticks once for each of them; the writes are conditional, so they converge harmlessly on the same counter. The caveat is that the same outcome against a store you know is *not* new means the backup store's counters record itself has gone missing, which is worth investigating separately. `wait_timeout` means an upload stopped waiting on a repair that was still running and returned the same retriable `UNAVAILABLE`; the repair carries on in the background, records its own outcome, and the client's retry joins it rather than starting a second one — so a single occurrence is not a verdict on anything, while a *sustained* rate means the backup store is slow enough that no upload can outlast a repair and has a warn-level recipe of its own. `already_repaired` means a repair found the counter already restored by a peer and had nothing to do. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy? for the meaning of each outcome, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Keyspace lost and § Additional alert rule definitions for the runbook and alert rules, and `docs/service/docs/durability.md` § When the backing store is lost for the operator-facing description. **Also changed**: the existing `live_edit_checkpoint_counter_seed_secs` histogram now carries a `phase` label (`startup` for the once-per-process routine, `repair` for this new runtime path), because the two have very different latency profiles and an unlabelled series mixed them. Dashboards and rules that read this histogram — including the "counter-seed did not complete" alert, whose canonical form is now scoped to `{phase="startup"}` — should add the selector; on a deployment that has never taken a repair the old and new forms agree, which is exactly why the difference is easy to miss until it matters.
- **Live updates no longer stop silently after a burst of backing-store reconnects — a subscription the service cannot keep alive now ends with a retriable error so the client reconnects, instead of ending as though it had finished normally** (Issue `b.c7a`). When many connections to the backing store are re-established at once — most commonly right after a credential rotation — the client library can briefly have no usable connection at all, and any command issued in that gap fails with a routing error. The service classified that error as permanent. For a write it meant returning a non-retriable failure for a condition that clears itself within seconds. For a live `SubscribeReadLayer` subscription it was worse: the subscription was torn down and the stream was closed **cleanly**, which a gRPC client reads as "the server has nothing more to send" and does not retry. Editing sessions therefore went quiet with no error surfaced anywhere on the client, and stayed quiet until the user reconnected by hand. The service now treats that routing gap as the temporary condition it is: subscriptions back off and resume from their exact position on the layer's update stream, and a write that hits the gap is retried internally and — if the gap outlasts those retries — fails with a retriable status the client can safely reissue (`ABORTED` from the retried write path, `UNAVAILABLE` from calls that are not retried). **The tear-down path is fixed more broadly than that one error.** Every way a subscription can end for a server-side reason — a routing gap, an authentication or protocol failure, a reply the client library cannot interpret, a prolonged backing-store outage, or a client that stopped draining its stream long enough to wedge delivery — now ends the stream with `UNAVAILABLE`. A clean stream close is reserved for the two cases where the subscription really is over: the client cancelled it, or the client went away. A deleted layer is not a clean close either — the subscription ends with `NOT_FOUND`, or, when the layer was replaced by a fresh upload rather than removed, transparently continues on the new content. **Client-visible change**: a client that previously saw an occasional clean end-of-stream in these situations now sees `UNAVAILABLE` instead and should re-subscribe, which the standard gRPC retry policy does automatically. Clients that already retry on `UNAVAILABLE` need no change; a client that treats end-of-stream as "session over" will now correctly recover instead of going quiet. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change. **New operator-visible surface**: a counter `live_edit_valkey_consumer_permanent_exits_total{phase}` reports subscriptions ended because of an error retrying cannot fix, labelled by whether it was caught while setting the subscription up (`checkout_probe`) or while it was running (`xread`). Unlike the other counters in this area, **zero is the expected value on every deployment**: it does not count transient failures or the routing gap above, so any sustained non-zero rate means clients are in a reconnect loop against a real problem — usually a credential or endpoint misconfiguration — and the service's warning logs name the underlying error. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up?
- **Azure Managed Redis deployment guide: added a section on how far ahead of expiry the token-provisioner sidecar should refresh the Valkey access token** (Issue `b.c7a`). This change added the section; it did not change any behavior. At the time it was written the service refused to use a cached token once it came within `valkey.credentials.accessTokenFile.refreshLeadSecs` (default 180 seconds) of expiry, so the section published a hard requirement — a sidecar refresh lead of roughly 195 seconds or more — to keep that refusal from recurring daily. **That requirement no longer applies**: Issue `b.d6b`, in this same release, removed the refusal (the service now uses a cached token for its whole real lifetime) and replaced the requirement with a differently-derived *recommendation* — refresh at least 360 seconds ahead of expiry plus the service's token-file poll interval, 375 seconds at the defaults — which Issue `b.7td`, also in this release, subsequently relaxed to **195 seconds at the defaults** (see the `### Fixed` entry below). `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Token refresh carries the current rule and the symptoms to look for; see the `b.d6b` entry above for the full description. Documentation only; no code or configuration change in this service.
- **The service no longer leaves a small bookkeeping record behind in its backing store every time a layer is overwritten or deleted, which caused memory use to creep upward indefinitely** (Issue `b.9fn`). Every layer the service stores keeps a record of the order of its top-level items. When a layer was retired — overwritten by a fresh upload of the same layer, deleted outright, removed as part of deleting its session, or reclaimed by the background cleanup sweep — the service deleted everything else belonging to that layer but always missed that one record. Because the backing store is deliberately configured never to evict data (so that no edit is ever silently lost), nothing else would ever reclaim it: the background cleanup sweep finds abandoned layers by looking for their main record, which had already been deleted, so a leftover of this kind was invisible to it, and the separate cleanup that reclaims large attribute values only ever handled those values. The result was one small orphaned record per retired layer, accumulating for the lifetime of the deployment. **No correctness impact**: nothing a client could read, write, or subscribe to was ever affected, and no edit was lost or mis-ordered — the leftover records were inert, and only ever consumed memory. The service now deletes that record on all four retirement paths, using a single shared list of what a retired layer owns so the paths cannot drift apart again. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, no new metric, and no wire or API change. **Already-leaked records**: no cleanup pass is added for leftovers accumulated by earlier builds. As above, the background cleanup sweep cannot discover them — it finds abandoned layers by looking for their main record, which these leftovers no longer have — and on a pre-release service the residue is not worth a dedicated pass: the records are inert and a few bytes each. They disappear the next time the backing store for an environment is reset or redeployed.
- **Non-clustered deployments using a passwordless Valkey authentication mode no longer log a warning and pause delivery for every live subscriber every ~5 minutes** (Issue `b.9yf`). Under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` or `iam`, the service re-presents its credential on each backing-store connection every 5–6 minutes so those connections survive token rotation. A connection carrying a live `SubscribeReadLayer` subscription spends nearly all of its time parked in a blocking read, and on a **non-clustered** backing store that read has to be briefly interrupted for the credential refresh to run. The service already resumed the read from the exact position it held — no update was ever lost or reordered — but it treated the interruption as a generic backing-store failure: it logged `XREAD transient failure; backing off and retrying` at **warning** level, waited out a short random back-off (up to 100 ms) before resuming, and counted the event against the budget that decides when to give up on a subscription entirely. Operators therefore saw a recurring, alarming warning — one per live subscriber roughly every 5 minutes, indefinitely — describing a healthy, self-inflicted, scheduled event, plus a small delivery gap on every affected subscription each cycle. The service now recognises its own interruption and resumes the read **immediately**, with no back-off, without counting it against the give-up budget, and logging it at debug level instead of warning. **Clustered deployments were never affected** — including the production Azure Managed Redis and Amazon MemoryDB targets, where in-flight reads are allowed to finish rather than being interrupted — and neither was `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=static`. **Genuine problems are still loud**: if the same subscription is interrupted repeatedly without the read succeeding in between (four consecutive times), the service falls back to exactly the previous behaviour — warning, back-off, and eventually giving up so the client reconnects. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change. **New operator-visible surface**: an unlabelled counter `live_edit_valkey_consumer_unblocked_retries_total` reports how often this happened. Unlike the other counters in this area it is **not** an error signal: it reads zero on clustered deployments and on the `static` authentication mode, and is expected to sit at a steady, periodic non-zero rate — roughly one per active subscription per credential refresh — on non-clustered passwordless deployments. What is worth investigating is a rate well above that shape, or any non-zero rate on a clustered deployment. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up? **Log alerts note**: if you built an alert or filter around the `XREAD transient failure; backing off and retrying` warning, expect its volume on non-clustered passwordless deployments to drop to near zero — keep the alert, because the same warning still fires for genuine backing-store trouble.
- **A pod using file-based Valkey authentication now connects about a second after its credential sidecar writes the token, instead of waiting up to a full poll interval and risking being killed and restarted before it ever starts serving** (Issue `b.o98`). Under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` the service cannot open a single connection to its backing store until the sidecar's token file has been read, and the sidecar typically writes that file a fraction of a second after the container starts. The service read the file once at startup — normally a moment too early, before the sidecar had written it — and then not again until its next scheduled poll, `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS` (default 15) seconds later. The token was sitting on disk the whole time. The resulting startup delay pushed the first successful connect past the container liveness probe's grace window, so Kubernetes killed the pod before it finished starting and the same race repeated on the restart. The service now re-reads the token file roughly four times a second until the first token loads, then reverts to the configured poll interval for the rest of the process lifetime. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change; `LIVE_EDIT_SERVICE_TOKEN_POLL_INTERVAL_SECS` keeps its meaning as the steady-state refresh cadence and no longer affects how quickly a token is picked up at startup. **New operator-visible surface**: if no token has loaded 20 seconds after startup the service logs a single warning naming which token reader fired, the configured path, and the elapsed time, then falls back to the steady poll interval. That warning means the sidecar is not writing the file, is writing it somewhere other than the path the corresponding environment variable names, or is writing content the service cannot parse — previously this condition produced no log line at all and had to be diagnosed by inspecting a running pod. No token material appears in the warning. The same startup behavior applies to the separate service-identity token used for outbound checkpoint calls, so the warning identifies the reader as either `valkey` or `storage-api`: the first means the pod is about to be killed and restarted (the service cannot reach its backing store without that token), the second means only outbound checkpoint calls lose authentication while the service keeps serving. The 20-second window is deliberately shorter than the 30-second default `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS`, so on the Valkey path the warning is written before the failed connect ends the process — a longer window would mean the pod was already gone by the time the warning was due. Relatedly, during those rapid startup re-reads a token file that is present but malformed now produces at most one warning and one `live_edit_service_token_parse_errors_total` increment per poll interval rather than one per read, which keeps a single bad file from flooding the log and inflating that counter; the first failure is always reported. Once the service has reverted to its steady poll cadence — and for on-demand re-reads after an upstream authentication rejection — every failed read is still reported individually.
- **Disaster-recovery backups are now provably complete at the point in time they claim to cover; a backup racing a concurrent edit is deferred to the next pass instead of silently omitting that edit** (Issue `b.pw6`). When checkpoints are enabled, the service periodically snapshots each layer's state to the configured backup store and records which edit that snapshot covers. Reading a layer's state takes several round-trips to the backing store, and previously the service determined the covered version *after* that read — which put an upper bound on what the snapshot contained but did not prove the snapshot contained everything up to that version. An edit committed by a user *during* the read could therefore be left out of the backup while the backup's own metadata claimed to include it. Two consequences made this worse than a one-off miss.
