First, the gap did not repair itself: the service recorded the layer as backed up at that version, so the next pass saw nothing new to do and skipped the layer — the omission survived until somebody edited that layer again, by which time the retention policy (two generations by default) could have deleted the older, correct backups behind it. Second, the same race could produce a structurally broken backup — an attribute persisted without the prim it belongs to. **Neither was visible from outside**: the backup was written, no error was raised, and the omission would only surface as missing work during an actual restore. The service now proves the read captured a single clean point in the layer's history before it writes anything — it records the layer's edit counter before the read and re-checks it after, and accepts the snapshot only if nothing moved. If it cannot obtain that proof for a layer (which requires the layer to be under near-continuous write), it **writes nothing at all for that layer on that pass** — no backup, and no record of having backed it up — so the previous good backup stays the newest one and the next pass tries again. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change. **RPO impact, bounded but cumulative**: for a layer in that state the catastrophic-loss recovery point stretches from "≤ `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS`" to that plus however many consecutive passes the layer keeps deferring — each deferral leaves the newest usable backup one pass older, so the exposure grows with the duration of the write contention rather than stopping at one pass. It stays narrow in practice: once a layer is past its interval, a deferral does not push its next attempt out by another interval — the layer stays due and is re-attempted on **every** subsequent pass until a backup lands. (A layer deferred by the early-quiesce trigger *before* its interval has elapsed is not re-attempted every pass; it becomes due again when its interval elapses.) The completeness check itself needs no quiet period either, only that no edit lands during a single read of the layer, so sustained accumulation takes sustained write pressure. It is self-correcting and needs no operator action. **New operator-visible surface**: two counters, `live_edit_checkpoint_stable_cut_total{outcome}` (`outcome` ∈ {`verified`, `unverified`}) and `live_edit_checkpoint_stable_cut_retries_total`. `verified` should be very close to 100% of the volume. **A sustained `unverified` rate is the new DR-exposure signal** — it means those layers' newest usable backups are not advancing on schedule, and it is *not* a sign of a failing backup store (the store is never contacted on that path), so it is deliberately reported separately from the backup-write error counter. The retries counter rises earlier and is the advance warning. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are backups current? — the useful levers are write pressure and `LIVE_EDIT_CHECKPOINT_QUIESCE_SECS`, not the backup interval.
- **Deployments using a passwordless Valkey authentication mode no longer emit a recurring credential-refresh warning every ~5 minutes from the connection reserved for presence and session notifications** (Issue `b.j64`). Under `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_AUTH_MODE=accessTokenFile` or `iam`, the service periodically re-presents its credential on each of its Valkey connections to keep them alive across token rotation. One of those connections — the single connection reserved for presence and session notifications — is permanently in subscribe mode, and Valkey does not permit re-authentication on a connection in that state. The attempt was rejected every cycle and logged as a warning: roughly 288 warnings per replica per day, indefinitely, on every passwordless deployment. Nothing was actually broken — the connection stayed up, notifications kept flowing, and that connection has always renewed its credential by reconnecting rather than by re-authenticating in place — but the constant warning stream polluted log-based triage and trained operators to ignore warnings on the Valkey authentication surface, which is exactly where a real problem would surface. The service no longer schedules a refresh it cannot perform on that one connection. In its place, a single informational line is logged once at startup recording that the connection renews its credential by reconnecting. **Nothing else changes**: the credential presented on that connection is identical to the one every other connection presents, all other connections keep their periodic refresh unchanged, and credential rotation continues to be picked up automatically everywhere. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change. **What still warns:** this change removes exactly one source of the `Failed to refresh credentials` warning — the one that fired unconditionally, every cycle, on every passwordless deployment. The other four Valkey connections keep their periodic credential refresh and can still emit that same warning, including when a token or credential is genuinely broken, which is worth investigating. One known-benign source also remains: on a non-clustered Valkey deployment the service can still emit the same warning text with nothing actually wrong. Log alerts and filters built around that warning text should therefore be kept rather than retired; expect its volume to drop, not to reach zero.
- **A client joining or rejoining a session while others are actively editing no longer risks having one of those edits applied twice** (Issue `b.vd8`). When a client subscribes to a layer, the service reads the layer's current state, sends it as a catch-up, and then tells the client which edit that catch-up brought it up to. Because reading the state takes several round-trips to the backing store, an edit committed by another user *during* that read could end up both included in the catch-up and re-sent afterwards as a live update — so the joining client applied it twice. For ordinary property and value edits a repeat application is harmless (the second application is identical), but for **structural** edits it is not: creating a prim is applied by tearing down and rebuilding it, so a repeated prim-creation immediately followed by a move of an older prim underneath it could permanently drop that older prim from the joining client's scene. The service now proves that its state read captured a single clean point in the layer's history — it re-checks the layer's edit counter after the read, and if nothing moved, the read is known to be consistent — and acknowledges exactly that point, which lets it filter the duplicate out before it reaches the client. If the layer is being written to continuously enough that the check keeps being invalidated, the service falls back to exactly its previous behavior, so the worst case is unchanged rather than made worse. The retry is bounded twice over — at most three attempts and at most 250 ms of wall clock, whichever comes first — so subscribing to a large, continuously-written layer cannot become slower than it was; a layer that is quiet at the moment a client subscribes pays nothing at all. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change; the fix is on by default and applies whichever catch-up mode is selected. **New operator-visible surface**: two counters, `live_subscribe_stable_cut_total{outcome, regime}` (`outcome` ∈ {`verified`, `unverified`}) and `live_subscribe_stable_cut_retries_total{regime}`, report how often the check succeeds, broken down by which kind of catch-up the subscriber took (`regime` ∈ {`brand_new`, `in_window`, `out_of_window`}). `verified` should be very close to 100% of the volume; a sustained `unverified` rate means subscribers on that replica are still exposed to the duplicate and is worth investigating.
- **A reconnecting subscriber now receives the edits it missed in the order their author applied them, instead of an arbitrary order** (Issue `b.3pe`). When a client reconnects to `SubscribeReadLayer`, the service brings it up to date with a single catch-up diff covering everything that changed since the version it last held — the same path whether its gap is still inside the layer's retained update window (`LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN`, default 4096 entries per layer) or has already been trimmed past it. That diff took each field's ordering number from the server's internal write counter, which advances in the order fields arrive on the wire; writing clients serialize the fields of a single commit out of a hash map, so wire order is effectively arbitrary and any commit touching more than one field could be replayed to the reconnecting client in an order its author never used. Where the resulting scene depends on the order edits are applied — transform-op ordering on a prim being the common case — a reconnected client could converge on a different result than a peer that never disconnected. The service now orders those fields by the authoring rank the writing client declared, which is the ordering brand-new and late-joining subscribers already received, so all three catch-up paths agree; catch-up output is also now fully deterministic, meaning two clients reconnecting against the same layer state receive identical content in an identical order. **This is an ordering-fidelity fix, not a data-loss fix — no edit was ever dropped on this path.** Operators additionally get a warning log naming the affected layer, prim, and attribute if a stored field is ever missing its ordering or version metadata, rather than that field silently sorting to the front. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, no new metric, and no wire or API change; the fix is on by default. `LIVE_EDIT_CATCHUP_SNAPSHOT_ENABLED=false` remains the rollback knob to the legacy catch-up path, which does not carry this correction.
- **`SubscribeReadLayer` no longer stops delivering live updates when a managed cache silently invalidates a subscription's backing-store connection; the service now detects and rebuilds those connections itself, and reports each self-heal on the new `live_edit_valkey_consumer_forced_reconnects_total{phase}` counter** (Issue `b.9uq`). Managed cache frontends — Azure Managed Redis is the observed case — can invalidate a client connection during maintenance or at credential rotation **without closing it**. Each live subscription holds one dedicated connection that spends nearly all of its time in a blocking read, so an invalidated subscriber connection had no traffic to fail on: live delta delivery stopped, no error was reported, and the affected connection was returned to the pool still dead, spreading the condition to later subscribers. Writes and initial catch-up kept working throughout (they ride connections with constant traffic), so the deployment looked healthy while real-time collaboration was broken until the pod was restarted. The service now runs a fast health check on every connection it picks up for a new subscription, pins each subscription's resume position to an exact stream entry before it reads any of the layer's state (so a rebuilt connection resumes precisely where it left off instead of skipping everything that arrived while it was dead), forces a reconnect when a read times out on a connection that should have answered, and filters out updates a subscription has already been given. **The change also closes a long-standing gap where an edit could be skipped entirely**: a commit landing in the brief window between a subscription pinning its resume position and reading the layer's current state used to be delivered by neither path, and is now delivered by both. That left a redundant second delivery, which the separate `b.vd8` fix described above now removes. **No data is lost across a self-heal, as long as the subscription resumes inside the layer's stream retention window** (bounded by `LIVE_EDIT_LAYER_STREAM_MAXLEN`, default 4096 entries per layer): the subscription resumes from its exact position on that stream, so every commit still inside the window is delivered. A heal that takes longer than the layer's write rate keeps in the window is handled exactly like any other out-of-window reconnect — the client re-subscribes and the service serves a single coalesced catch-up diff bringing it to current state. **Operator-visible surface**: the new counter `live_edit_valkey_consumer_forced_reconnects_total{phase}` (`phase` ∈ {`checkout_probe`, `xread`}, both pre-initialised at zero) reports how often the service healed such a connection and at which point it was caught. A `checkout_probe` detection happens before delivery starts and costs a subscriber a few seconds of setup; an `xread` detection happens mid-subscription and costs roughly 35–50 seconds of live updates, long enough that most clients will hit their own read deadline and re-subscribe. Occasional ticks around a maintenance or credential-rotation window are expected and are a success signal; a sustained rate means backing-store connectivity is genuinely unhealthy. **Operator action: none** — no configuration change, no new environment variable, and no wire or API change; the fix is on by default. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is live delivery keeping up?, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store-azure.md` § Monitoring and maintenance for the AMR-specific note.
- **Slow-path `SetChildren` now keys each parent's children list by its own `section_id` instead of an always-zero id, fixing silent corruption of nested scenes built via `UpdateLayer`** (bug `b.mq1`). Pre-fix the WATCH/MULTI/EXEC (slow-path) `SetChildren` handler wrote every parent's children list under a single always-zero key, so any layer with more than one parent-that-has-children constructed via `UpdateLayer` structural commands had its nested hierarchy silently collapsed onto one key — corrupting the reconstructed scene for subscribers. The handler now keys each list by its own `section_id`, so nested hierarchies materialize correctly. This corrects production behavior on the default (slow) commit path regardless of any catch-up setting. **Client impact**: clients building nested scenes via `UpdateLayer` now see the correct hierarchy on subscribe; flat single-parent layers were unaffected. No wire or API change.
- **Checkpoint recovery now reliably stamps the `last_checkpoint_seq` audit field on the recovered `obj:hdr`** (Issue `b.4fh`). Pre-fix `apply_checkpoint` wrote the 12 canonical header fields inside one MULTI/EXEC and then issued a separate trailing `HSET last_checkpoint_seq <snap_seq>` outside the transaction; a crash between the two left a header missing the audit field, and the recovery-path EXISTS short-circuit then mis-fired on the next attempt — the layer was correctly materialized but an operator running `HGET le:{oid}:obj:hdr last_checkpoint_seq` against the recovered layer saw `nil` instead of the seeding checkpoint's snapshot version. The fix folds the audit stamp into the same MULTI/EXEC that writes the 12 canonical fields, so either the entire header lands (audit field included) or none of it does. No client-visible behavior change; no wire-format change. **Operator action**: none required. Operators who consume `last_checkpoint_seq` for recovery audit / dashboards will now reliably see the stamped value on every recovered layer rather than occasionally observing `nil`.
- **`LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` is now honored end-to-end into fred's per-attempt connect timeout** (Issue `b.1kf`). Pre-fix the env var was wired only into the outer connect-retry budget; fred's `ConnectionConfig::connection_timeout` kept its hardcoded `10 s` default, so any operator who set the env var **below** 10 s would have observed the per-attempt TCP/TLS handshake taking up to 10 s regardless of the configured value. The fix wires the configured value into fred at pool-construction time across all five Valkey clients (`data_pool`, `txn_pool`, `subscriber_pool`, `maintenance_pool`, and the dedicated pub/sub `subscriber`), so the env var honors its documented contract end-to-end across the full operator-tunable range. **Operator action**: none required. The default (`30` s) is unchanged and deployments running on the default observe no behavior change. Deployments that explicitly pinned `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` to a value below 10 s for fail-fast-on-dead-Valkey behavior will now actually get that fast-fail; deployments pinning it at or above 10 s see no change. No wire-format change.
- **`SubscribeReadLayer` watchdog tear-down now surfaces gRPC `UNAVAILABLE` instead of a clean EOF** (bug `b.8z2`). When a subscriber falls behind beyond the 5-second `CONSUMER_SEND_WATCHDOG` (`src/storage/notifications.rs`), the consumer task now stashes `ServiceError::Unavailable("subscriber for {stream_key} fell behind beyond {watchdog_ms}ms watchdog; tearing down")` into `terminal_error` before bailing, so the gRPC handler emits `Code::Unavailable` (code 14, retriable) rather than closing the stream with a clean EOF. Pre-fix the bare `return;` dropped `tx` without touching `terminal_error`, so `run_steady_state` returned `Ok(())` and the client's standard retry policy never reconnected — under a real overload (sustained writer rate exceeds the bounded mpsc drain rate for > 5 s) the subscriber would silently miss every commit after tear-down. The fix mirrors the existing XREAD transient-retry-budget-exhaustion path (b.fd9 follow-up). The watchdog itself is retained as the primitive that bounds how long an undrained backlog is held and reclaims the `subscriber_pool` slot — only the close-code observability changed. *(It is an entry-count bound, not a byte bound, and was never a memory ceiling; see Issue `b.f4f` earlier in this same unreleased set, which says so explicitly and records the remaining memory exposure.)* **Client impact**: callers MAY treat `UNAVAILABLE` on `SubscribeReadLayer` as the canonical retriable signal and re-subscribe with the same `have_object_id` / `have_version`; gRPC client libraries with default retry policies (`gRPC service config retryableStatusCodes` including `UNAVAILABLE`) recover automatically. Clients that previously interpreted the clean EOF as "server completed the stream normally" should switch to the new code — pre-b.8z2 the close was indistinguishable from a normal stream end so any client that did not periodically re-subscribe was silently dropping updates. No wire-format change.
- **Presence sweeper now crash-recovers across replica failures, and graceful shutdown drains peer-cleanup before closing Valkey** (bug `b.w29`). Two latent gaps fixed together. (1) The presence crash-recovery sweep pre-fix enumerated each replica's `LocalPresenceRouter` for sessions with *local* connections — if every replica routing for a session crashed simultaneously the session vanished from every router's local view and the sweeper never re-visited it, leaking the orphan presence keys until the session itself was deleted. The sweeper now reads a new fleet-wide `le:presence:active_sessions` SET (SADDed by JoinSession's first connection and SREMed by the disconnect-cleanup path when the per-session presence members set goes empty), so crash-recovery survives any individual replica's death. Each tick is leader-gated via the dedicated `le:presence:sweeper:leader` lock — distinct from `le:gc:leader` so the two maintenance loops can pick different leader replicas in steady state (different tick cadences and workload profiles, see SDD §13.3). (2) Graceful shutdown pre-fix dispatched the JoinSession disconnect-cleanup body (SREM presence members, DEL info hash + lease, publish `PeerLeft`) onto a best-effort `tokio::spawn` that the post-server Valkey-pool tear-down could race past — dropping `PeerLeft` for every peer the draining pod was still routing, so peers on other replicas saw the user appear connected indefinitely. The cleanup is now tracked on a shared `tokio_util::task::TaskTracker` that the shutdown sequence (§5.6 step 4.1) `close()`s and `wait()`s under the `LIVE_EDIT_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECS` per-task budget before firing the cancellation tokens for the other background tasks; `PeerLeft` now reaches peers on other replicas before the draining pod's Valkey connections close. Companion default-bump on the lease budgets — `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_LEASE_TTL_SECS` raised from a hard-coded 30 s to a configurable default of 60 s and `LIVE_EDIT_PRESENCE_REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` raised from 10 s to 15 s — gives a single missed refresh tick under tokio scheduling pressure 4× headroom before the sweeper considers a connection stale, with a startup-time cross-validation rule rejecting `TTL < 2 × refresh` so future tuning cannot regress the invariant. **Operator action**: none required to inherit the fix; the new TTL/refresh defaults are forward-compatible (lease keys created against the new TTL remain valid under the old refresh cadence). Operators who currently set the older `LIVE_EDIT_SWEEPER_INTERVAL_SECS` continue to set sweep cadence the same way; the new env vars are deep-tuning knobs (see Added entry above). Pre-fix wire contract for `PeerLeft` is preserved — clients still receive `PeerLeft` for every peer leaving a session, but on the failure modes that pre-fix dropped them silently they now arrive reliably.
- **GC ownership protocol now uses Valkey-side time across the claim and drain paths; `cleanup_old_object` enumerate calls propagate transient I/O errors; CI gates Helm placeholder + port-invariant drift** (bug `b.u96` post-bundle follow-ups). Seven internal hardening items closing carry-forwards from the b.j9j / b.39e / b.bm8 / b.fd9 / b.avd review bundles. (1) `LayerStore::cleanup_old_object`'s four enumerate calls (`SMEMBERS obj sections index`, `ZRANGE obj fields index`, `ZRANGE obj time samples index`, `SMEMBERS obj deleted sections index`) now propagate transient fred errors through `map_transient_fred_err` instead of `unwrap_or_default()`; a Valkey blip mid-enumerate no longer silently shrinks the to-UNLINK key set and strands per-section / per-field / per-time-sample keys with no `obj:hdr` left for the orphan sweep to re-discover (permanent disk leak). Closes the carry-forward to the b.j9j item 3 fix that previously only covered `cleanup_orphaned_object` / `sweep_orphaned_layers` on the orphan-sweep side. (2) `process_pending` gained a `with_process_pending_fault_injector_for_test` builder so the b.j9j per-hash transient-tolerance regression test can exercise the EVALSHA error-classification path end-to-end without a real Valkey outage. (3) The `supply-chain:helm-lint` CI job now exercises the chart's `nodomain.none` placeholder guard. (4) New `supply-chain:port-invariant` CI job asserts Dockerfile `EXPOSE` matches `helm/live-edit-service/values.yaml` `service.grpc.port` and the liveness/readiness probe ports — drift now fails the pipeline rather than surfacing as a confusing readiness flap after deploy. (5) The cxx-bridge mapper-routing test rewritten as a workspace-wide scan with a small allowlist, so adding a new conversion site without registering it in the allowlist fails the test rather than silently bypassing the mapper. (6) The internal test harnesses' default port corrected `50051` → `50001` (matches the service's `LIVE_EDIT_PORT` default). Tools-side ergonomics only; explicit `--endpoint` / `LIVE_EDIT_LOCAL_ENDPOINT` overrides were unaffected. (7) The external-value GC ownership protocol now samples Valkey-side time on both the claim and drain paths. `gc_claim_pending.lua` calls `redis.call("TIME")` inline rather than reading a caller-supplied `now_ms` ARGV from `SystemTime::now()`, and `drain_inflight_to_pending` is a new single Lua script (`drain_inflight_to_pending.lua`) that samples `redis.call("TIME")`, computes `cutoff_ms = now_ms − LEADER_LOCK_TTL_MS` inline, and collapses the prior `SADD pending + ZREM inflight` Rust-side pipeline into one atomic Valkey block. The Valkey-side clock is consistent across the fleet, so a replica with NTP skew can no longer stamp claims that its peers' drain treats as stale (regressing the b.j9j double-decrement scenario the ZSET timestamp guard was designed to prevent). Verified safe under Valkey 7.0+ effects-replication: `redis.call("TIME")` is sampled once on the master and the resulting ZADD is replicated verbatim. SDD §11.3 and §11.4 rewritten to reflect the Valkey-TIME watermark and the Lua collapse. (8) See the `Added` entry above for the new `live_edit_health_probe_failures_total{backend}` counter that landed alongside this bundle.
- **Two writes could go through without the safety check they depend on, if the connection carrying that check was replaced at the wrong moment** (Issue `b.bhb`). Two operations guard themselves the same way: a structural edit to a layer — one that adds, moves, deletes or re-orders items rather than just setting values — and creating a session. Each reads the current state, decides based on it, and then commits, asking the backing store to *watch* the thing it read so the commit is cancelled if anything changed in between. A watch belongs to the **connection** it was placed on. If that connection was replaced between the read and the commit — a failover, a managed proxy retiring a backend session, or an ordinary reconnect — the watch was gone and the store committed **unconditionally**, with nothing in the reply to say so. Two things made this more likely than it sounds. Holding a connection exclusively for the whole operation, which the service already did, prevents a *different* request interleaving on it but does nothing about the connection itself being rebuilt underneath. And the client library, by default, quietly re-sends a whole transaction on a fresh connection after a reconnect, and on a different node after the store moves a slice of its keyspace — up to three times and five times respectively. Every one of those re-sends was a commit with no watch behind it. **What could go wrong differed by operation, and neither was theoretical.** Creating a session could produce **two sessions sharing one name at one path** — the exact uniqueness guarantee the check exists to provide — with no error to anyone. A structural edit fared better, because the entry it appends to the layer's update stream is written at an explicit position that the store itself refuses to hand out twice: two editors racing could not both take the same position. So instead of one edit silently overwriting another, the edit came apart — the layer's stored contents changed while the stream entry was refused, meaning live subscribers never saw that edit — and the client was told the write failed. **The fix is three layers, and only the last of them is a guarantee.** First, the automatic re-sending is gone: the connection pool used for these writes never re-attempts a command that failed to reach the wire and never follows a redirect, and the commit is sent as one batch rather than as a transaction the client library is willing to replay wholesale. A command that fails on its way out now surfaces as an ordinary retry — a fresh connection, a fresh watch, and the write goes through a moment later with the client seeing nothing. **What that does not cover is the case where nothing fails**: if the client library decides to rebuild the connection while the commit is still sitting in its outbound queue, it rebuilds first and then sends the commit successfully on the *new* connection, with no error anywhere to notice. Second, the service records which connection the watch was placed on and re-checks — immediately before committing — both that identity and whether the connection is being rebuilt at that very moment, using information the client library already holds locally, so the check costs no round trip and does not widen the window it measures. That narrows the window; it cannot close it, for the reason just given. Third, and this is the layer that actually closes the gap, the transaction now **asks the store, from inside itself, which connection actually ran it** — and compares the answer against a reference obtained the same way, from inside a throwaway transaction on that same connection immediately before the watch is placed, rather than against the client library's own record. That distinction matters on a managed or proxied endpoint, which may number connections differently from what the client library sees: comparing two readings taken the same way means an ordinary write still matches, where comparing across the two views would have reported **every** write as a failure. A mismatch is proof rather than suspicion, and is acted on: a session creation that committed unwatched is **undone and retried** under a fresh watch, so the correct answer is still reached; a structural edit re-checks whether the layer was deleted or replaced underneath it, and either accepts the edit (the stream position it took proves no other editor claimed that edit) or returns the ordinary "layer is gone / was replaced" error.

  **Both of the last two protections depend on the backing store answering a question about connection identity, and not every store does.** Some managed and proxied endpoints refuse or restrict that query. Where that happens the affected protection is **switched off for the write rather than allowed to fail it** — asking anyway would have made every structural layer edit and every session creation fail permanently on such an endpoint — and the fact is recorded on the counter below so it is visible rather than silent. The service detects this for itself, per write, with no configuration: nothing to set, and nothing changes on a store that answers normally. **A commit whose reply never arrives is now reconciled instead of blindly retried.** If the connection dies with the transaction in flight, whether it ran is genuinely unknown, and structural edits are not safe to repeat — so the service asks the store directly whether its own edit landed (each edit carries an identifier unique to that attempt) and reports the truth, rather than retrying and applying the same edit twice. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.** **New operator-visible surface:** the Prometheus counter `live_edit_storage_watch_voided_total{path, disposition}`. Read the `disposition` label first, because the five values mean different things. Three describe an event: `aborted_pre_exec` is the protection working — nothing was written, the write was retried, the client saw nothing — and is **expected to be non-zero** on any fleet that sees failovers or proxy recycles, so do not alert on it; `compensated` is a session creation that committed unwatched and was successfully undone, also not an alert; and `committed_unwatched` is the residual worth alerting on, meaning the store confirmed a commit ran on a connection that never held the watch and it could not be undone. That last cell is expected to read zero indefinitely, and when it ticks the accompanying error log names exactly what to inspect. The remaining two describe **a property of your backing store rather than an event**, and each reads either zero or roughly once per guarded write, with nothing in between: `witness_unavailable` means the store would not say which connection ran a transaction, so the strongest of the three protections is switched off — though not all detection goes with it, and the difference is the one thing to know before trusting a flat `committed_unwatched`: a structural layer edit still claims a position on the layer's update stream that the store itself refuses to hand out twice, so a torn edit still registers, while session creation has no equivalent backstop and an unwatched one goes unseen. Zero on such a store therefore still means "never happened" for a torn layer edit, and only "never observed" for an unwatched session creation or a layer edit that went through unwatched and landed intact; `fingerprint_unavailable` means the client library never learned the connection's identity, so the pre-commit re-check is skipped and the write goes ahead, which is much less serious because the strongest protection still works. Neither is an alert — read them once when you bring a backing store up, to learn which protections that store supports. A backing store addressed directly reads zero on both. Each also logs a warning, at most one every five minutes, naming what was unavailable and why. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Self-checks that should read zero, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule. **Honest scope:** the mechanisms are covered by tests that kill the connection carrying a watch mid-transaction against a single-node backing store; the residual — the client library rebuilding the connection in the instant between the last check and the commit going out, including the case where the rebuild happens first and the commit then lands cleanly on the replacement connection — is *detected and reported* rather than prevented, and closing it requires moving structural edits to a different commit mechanism, which is tracked separately as Issue `b.2wo`. How a proxied managed endpoint answers the connection-identity question has likewise not been observed — the design copes with either answer without needing to know which in advance, and the two `*_unavailable` cells above are how a given deployment finds out.
- **If something outside the service left a stray value on one of the keys a layer is stored under, some edits could be applied only in part instead of being refused** (Issue `b.459`). The service stores a layer as a set of Valkey keys, each holding a specific kind of value. If an unrelated writer puts, say, a plain string on one of those keys, the backing store rejects the write that touches it — and how that rejection was handled depended on which of the two internal commit paths the edit took. Ordinary value edits already checked every key they were about to write **before** writing any of them, so such an edit was refused whole and nothing changed. **Structural edits** — adding, moving, deleting or re-ordering items — did not, and their commit is a batch the backing store does not roll back: the rejected write failed while the rest of the batch went through, so the layer could end up partly updated while the client was told the edit failed. Deployments running `LIVE_EDIT_UPDATE_LAYER_BACKEND=txn` took that path for **all** edits. Structural edits now run the same check first, so both paths refuse whole, write nothing, and return the same error — the layer's contents are untouched, its edit counter does not advance, and no partial update reaches subscribers. Keys the edit only *deletes* are deliberately not checked, because a delete clears a stray value of any kind and refusing there would block the very edit that fixes the problem. A backing-store blip while the check runs is not treated as a verdict: the edit is retried against fresh state, so a Valkey wobble cannot turn into a write outage. **One narrow case remains and is worth stating plainly:** the check runs just before the batch is sent, so a stray value written into that instant — rather than one already sitting there, which is what this actually looks like in practice — still produces the old partial-update behaviour on structural edits. Closing it entirely requires moving structural edits to a different commit mechanism, which is tracked separately as Issue `b.2wo`. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.** **New operator-visible surface:** the Prometheus counter `live_edit_keyspace_type_error_total{backend, site}` reports edits refused for this reason, on both commit paths (`backend` is `lua` or `txn`; `site` is always `update`). Both cells are pre-initialised at zero, and **zero is the expected reading forever** — nothing in the service ever replaces one of its own keys with a value of a different kind, so any increment means an out-of-band writer touched the service's keyspace and that layer is unwritable until the key is removed. Reads and subscriptions to that layer usually stop as well, because most of the keys checked here are also read when the service loads the layer's current state — that refusal is reported separately, on `live_edit_snapshot_read_type_error_total`. Every other layer is unaffected. The **error** log line names the affected key, what it holds, and what it should hold, which is the whole remediation. Note that the remedy is **not** the one that goes with `live_edit_stream_id_collision_total`: that counter's advice is to clear the layer's update stream, which here would disrupt subscribers without fixing anything. Per-layer identifiers are deliberately not metric labels. See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Is the backing store healthy?, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Damaged layer keys, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule.
- **Large values that are edited repeatedly no longer grow the backing store without bound: replacing a large value now releases the copy it replaced, instead of holding it forever** (Issue `b.qpf`). Values above the inline threshold — materials, meshes, time-sampled arrays — are stored once, addressed by their content, and kept alive by a count of the layers referring to them. Only two things ever gave a reference up: deleting a layer, and overwriting one with a new `CreateLayer` — and even then only for the values each field held *at that moment*. Every superseded copy was therefore stranded permanently: changing a field's large value to a different one, changing it to a small inline value, deleting the field, and even re-writing a field with the value it already had all left a reference nothing would ever release. The store grew in proportion to how often large values were edited, with no error, no log, and no metric to see it by — precisely the workload the feature exists for. A second, independent leak sat beside it: two references given up for the same value before the cleanup task next ran — two layers deleted in quick succession, or an edit and a deletion of the same value — were collapsed into a single release, so one of the two was lost. Both are fixed. A reference is now given up exactly once for every change that gives one up, on both internal commit paths, on the disaster-recovery restore path, and at layer deletion; a value's storage is reclaimed once the last field in the last layer referring to it lets go. **Operator action: none — but storage already lost is not recovered.** This service is pre-release, so no migration is provided: references stranded before this change are not found or reclaimed, and layers that already existed keep leaking on one shape of edit (re-writing a field with the value it already holds) for as long as they exist. Deleting or replacing such a layer stops the leak growing and gives back one reference per value the layer still holds, but it does **not** reclaim what has already piled up: ten such edits to the same field strand ten references, and deleting the layer returns one of them. *(Both of those gaps are addressed later in this same unreleased set by Issue `b.yda` — see the entry below — but they close on different schedules. **"Keeps leaking" is closed outright**: `b.yda` stops the one remaining edit shape — re-saving a field with the large value it already holds — at source, on both internal commit paths, so that statement describes only the state between the two changes. **"Not recovered" is now closed too**: recovering already-stranded storage is the job of the background reconciler `b.yda` adds, and the upload-timestamp recording that reconciler was waiting on landed in this same unreleased set — see the `b.rej` entry below. Storage stranded before these changes is reclaimed on the reconciler's schedule, with no operator action.)* What has no safe repair is the stored count itself. Layers written after the upgrade are correct from their first edit. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.** **New operator-visible surface:** two Prometheus counters. `live_edit_ext_ref_released_total{reason}` counts references given up, by cause — `rewrite` (the last field holding the value stopped holding it), `surplus_acquire` (the layer already referred to that value, so an edit's provisional claim was handed straight back — expect this to be the busiest one on a workload that re-saves the same value), and `retirement` (the layer itself was deleted or replaced). It is **not** an alert on its own; a healthy editing workload moves it constantly. Read it against the number of stored values instead: a store whose stored-value count climbs while this stays flat is this bug reappearing. `live_edit_ext_ref_release_enqueue_failed_total` **is** the alert — `increase(live_edit_ext_ref_release_enqueue_failed_total[1h]) > 0`. It counts **references rather than failures — one failed recording covering three references adds three — and is an upper bound**, since a store that applied the recording but answered with a timeout is counted as though it had not, and since a deletion that is retried after a transient error can count the same references again on each attempt. Every tick is a reference the service knows it owes and could not record, and the edit or deletion it belongs to has already been accepted and is never failed for it; where the reference truly was not recorded, nothing at that time gave it back. *(The reconciler added by Issue `b.yda`, below, does — and the upload-timestamp recording it waited on landed in this same unreleased set; see the `b.rej` entry.)* Which later event *could* have returned it depends on the change that owed it, and none does: where an edit stopped the layer holding a value, deleting or replacing the layer later does not release it, because the layer's record of the values it holds was already updated by that same edit and nothing the service looks at afterwards still names it; where an edit re-claimed a value the layer already held, the layer does still hold that value and a later deletion gives back the one reference it legitimately holds, but the surplus claim is not something any later event ever owes. A failure to record what a layer owed **as it was being deleted, replaced, or swept up in the background** is counted here too. Every recording — an edit's and a retiring layer's alike — is retried once immediately before the counter moves, and the retry does not change what is counted: still one tick per reference the service ended up unable to record, never one per attempt. Past that retry, a retiring layer's recording is re-attempted only if the cleanup that follows it also fails and is itself retried; otherwise the layer's storage is removed and nothing can re-derive which values it held. **A layer being swept up in the background is now marked as retiring before its references are read**, so an edit that was already in flight against it is refused rather than allowed to give back the same reference a second time — the one shape in this area that could have deleted a value another layer still referred to. Expected reading is zero. One smaller consequence worth knowing: the cleanup task's queue is now sized by the number of releases owed rather than by the number of distinct values, so on an edit-heavy fleet it is normally longer than before and worth a dashboard (it is drained by a single background connection by default, `LIVE_EDIT_MAINTENANCE_POOL_SIZE`). See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly?, and `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the alert rule.
- **Resharding a clustered backing store no longer looks like a fault to the connection check, and the check's give-up rule now works when only one shard misbehaves** (Issue `b.289`). Before every transactional write the service checks the pooled connection it just took (see the `b.7td` entry above). Three corrections to that check, none of them changing what the service writes or how any request behaves. **(1) Replies that mean "this node will not serve this command right now" are recognised as such.** A slot that has moved, a shard that is down, a replica still loading its dataset, a demoted primary, a cluster asking the client to try again — these were previously read as *"this connection is corrupted"*, which is the class the service rebuilds immediately and, after three of them in a row, gives up on with an ERROR and a page-level counter. So a routine reshard, failover or replica promotion could put your fleet into a state whose alert accuses the service of misreading your endpoint. Those replies now have their own reading, `topology_reply`, on the existing counter `live_edit_valkey_txn_dirty_checkouts_total{reason}` — **the first cell on that counter that is expected to be non-zero**, since it ticks while a topology change is in progress. The premise this depended on turned out to be wrong in the service's favour: the client library was assumed to intercept such replies before the service ever saw them, and it does so for only three of the seven, each in one exact spelling, so the rest genuinely reach the check. **The existing alert on that counter is unchanged and correctly excludes the new cell** — it would otherwise page you for maintenance you performed on purpose. In its place a **new alert recipe ships**, on a *sustained* `topology_reply` rate that outlives the topology change: that is now the only signal for an endpoint which permanently renders these replies in a shape the client library does not act on (a proxy that always answers `-READONLY`, or rewrites `-MOVED` into a non-standard form), because such an endpoint no longer escalates itself through the give-up rule and instead pays a quiet, permanent tax of one paced rebuild in eight on every transactional write. **Add that recipe if you run a clustered or proxied backing store.** **(2) The give-up rule is now per node of the backing store rather than per pool.** It stops rebuilding after three unfit verdicts with no clean check in between — but a clean check against *any* node used to clear that run, so when a single shard (or a single proxy shard) was the one misbehaving, checks aimed at the healthy shards kept re-arming the rule, it never fired, and every request aimed at the bad shard rebuilt its connection forever: exactly the unbounded loop the rule exists to bound. Runs are now counted per node, so the rule fires for the node that is actually failing. A tick on `live_edit_valkey_txn_repair_ineffective_total` therefore now means "connection hygiene is switched off for **one node**", not for the whole store; **the node is named in the accompanying ERROR log's `node` field and deliberately not as a metric label** (node identity is unbounded over a deployment's lifetime), and **no metric names or labels changed**. Two consequences worth knowing: a healthy shard can no longer vouch for a failing one, so hygiene stays off for that node until it answers a check correctly; and on a **single-endpoint or proxied store — where there is no per-node identity — the counter reads exactly as it did before**, because every unattributable check shares one bucket that any clean check clears. That same bucket is what keeps a brief window during a reshard, when the client library is rebuilding its view of which node owns what, from leaving a run nothing can clear. **(3) A metrics-only gap is closed:** if closing a connection succeeded but rebuilding it then timed out, that pool slot's own error and reconnect counters stayed dead until some later rebuild happened to succeed. They are now re-armed after every rebuild attempt. **Also in this change, with no runtime effect:** an audit of every command the service sends that carries no key of its own — the class that a clustered client routes to an arbitrary node, and the cause of the `b.ji8` defect above — found no new mis-routed command, and the resulting inventory is now recorded internally with build-time checks behind the two claims that rest on a negative. Three node-scoped surfaces were confirmed and are **not** changed here, all three tracked in follow-up Issue `b.kvd`: two of them genuinely reach an arbitrary node — the readiness check's `PING`, which samples one shard rather than all of them, and the check for whether append-only persistence is enabled, which reads one node's setting — while the third, the pub/sub pair, is precisely routed on both sides but to two *different* nodes, because the service publishes on the channel's own node and subscribes on another. That last one is correct on a real Valkey Cluster because the cluster bus forwards published messages to every node, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements now states it as an explicit requirement on any managed or proxied endpoint. Moving the structural-write commit off its optimistic-transaction path — the strategic follow-up that would remove this whole class of connection-state hazard — is out of scope here and is tracked as a separate plan-scale Issue, `b.2wo`. **No configuration change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults, and no wire or API change.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Backing-store diagnostics for the new cell, `docs/service/docs/monitoring.md` § Additional alert rule definitions for the new alert rule, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Requirements for the pub/sub requirement.
- **Large-value storage now reclaims itself: the counts that decide when a stored value can be deleted are checked against reality on a schedule, storage stranded by a momentary backing-store failure is given back automatically, and storage stranded by an earlier defect follows on the same schedule** (Issue `b.yda`). Values above the inline threshold are kept alive by a count of the layers referring to them, and until now that count could only ever drift **upward** — every way of losing a release left storage held forever, with no operator action that could recover it. Three changes close that, and none of them requires configuration.

  **Re-saving a layer with a large value it already holds no longer strands a copy.** An edit takes a provisional claim on a large value before committing, and hands it back when the layer turns out to have already referred to that value. For layers created before reference tracking existed, the service had no record proving the layer already held it, so the claim was kept — every such save, for the whole life of the layer. It now recognises the proof directly: a field re-written with the value it already holds is by itself evidence the layer held it, so the claim is handed straight back. Both internal commit paths do this identically. **This is the shape the previous release described as leaking at the full pre-upgrade rate for a layer's whole lifetime; it is now closed at source.** One narrow imprecision remains and is deliberate: a value written and then overwritten again *within the same edit* still strands its claim, and is reclaimed by the reconciler below rather than at commit time.

  **When a layer is deleted, replaced, or swept up in the background and the backing store will not accept the releases it owes, the work is now parked rather than lost.** Previously the layer's storage was removed immediately afterwards, which destroyed the only record of which values it had held — so a single momentary store failure at that instant stranded every large value the layer referred to, permanently. The service now writes the owed releases onto a durable note attached to that layer before the cleanup proceeds, and a background pass finishes the job within a few minutes. If the layer identity turns out to be in use again by then — most often because disaster recovery rebuilt that layer — the note is **dropped rather than acted on**, because the rebuilt layer has inherited the very references it was about to give back; dropping can leave a reference standing, which the reconciler reclaims, whereas acting would have deleted a value a live layer still refers to.

  **A periodic reconciler now checks every stored value's count against the layers that actually refer to it, and hands back the excess.** It runs hourly by default on one replica, and it is the backstop for every remaining way a release can be lost — a replica dying between an edit and its bookkeeping, references stranded before this accounting existed, a disaster-recovery restore that stored a value the restored layer no longer holds, and the two cases named above. It is deliberately cautious: it repairs nothing on a single look (a difference must hold at exactly the same amount across two observations a fixed safety window apart, and is re-checked once more immediately before acting), it abandons a whole pass rather than acting on a partial reading, and where the arithmetic says a count is too **low** — the direction that means a value may already have been deleted while a layer still refers to it — it reports and never repairs, because raising the count would claim bytes that are gone. **New environment variable `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS`**, defaulting to hourly (precisely, the larger of 3600 seconds and the derived safety window, so leaving it unset is valid at any `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS`); `0` switches the reconciler off and logs a startup warning; a value below the safety window is rejected at startup rather than clamped, because such a run could never act on what it observed. The safety window itself — the larger of 600 seconds and ten times `LIVE_EDIT_RPC_DEADLINE_MS` — is **derived and not configurable**: it is what keeps an upload still in flight from being mistaken for an excess. Helm exposes the interval as `gc.ext_reconcile_interval_secs`.

  **One thing to know about when the reconciler repairs.** To tell a genuine excess from an upload still in progress, it needs to know when a value's bytes were last uploaded. Recording that timestamp shipped separately, in this same unreleased set — see the `b.rej` entry below, which also flipped the pass from reporting-only to actively repairing and renamed one of the outcome readings described here.

  **New operator-visible surface**: a fourth cause `reconcile` on the existing `live_edit_ext_ref_released_total{reason}`; three counters covering parked releases (`live_edit_ext_ref_release_deferred_total`, `..._recovered_total`, `..._cancelled_total`, which balance as parked ≈ re-driven + dropped) and a `live_edit_ext_retire_retry_backlog` gauge; `live_edit_ext_ref_undecodable_total{site}` for references discarded because corrupt stored data could not be interpreted; and the reconciler's own `live_edit_ext_reconcile_total{outcome}` (nine cells) with four gauges — `live_edit_ext_meta_total`, `live_edit_ext_referenced_hashes_total`, `live_edit_ext_ref_surplus_total` and `live_edit_ext_ref_deficit_total`. Three new alert recipes ship: parked releases not draining, the reconciler failing to complete a pass, and — the one worth paging on — **a recorded count standing below the layers that refer to that value**, which means a stored value may have been reclaimed while a layer still needs it. **Read that last one over several reconciler intervals rather than on a single report** (`max_over_time`): the reconciler's own accounting produces an occasional one-off on a value whose references churn quickly, and what a genuine problem does that churn does not is persist. **The meaning of `live_edit_ext_ref_release_enqueue_failed_total` narrows** as a result — on the deletion path it now moves only when the parked note could not be written either — and its existing alert, its zero baseline and its remediation are all unchanged. One caveat narrows: a transient failure the service can **park** no longer counts the same references again on each retry of a deletion, because parking also marks the layer as already handled. A failure it cannot park at all still does — those are the deletion-path ticks that remain on this counter, so read a large number as attempts as much as references.

  **No wire or API change, no change to how layers, sessions or subscriptions behave, and no changed defaults** beyond the new variable. **Operator action: none.** Storage stranded by earlier versions is reclaimed without intervention; there is nothing to migrate and nothing to run. The one thing worth checking is `LIVE_EDIT_MAINTENANCE_POOL_SIZE`, which now has three background users sharing its default single connection — raise it to `2` only if the reconciler's pass duration visibly delays the other two on a large store.
- **Uploading a large value no longer occupies an exclusive backing-store connection, and the reference-count reconciler is now actively reclaiming storage instead of only reporting on it** (Issue `b.rej`). Two changes that ship together because the second depends on the first.

  **Storing a large value is one server-side operation on the shared connection pool.** Each distinct large value used to be written as a transaction on the service's small pool of exclusive connections — one connection per value, up to 8 at a time — which made uploads by far the largest consumer of that pool and made every one of them pay the connection-hygiene check the service runs when it borrows such a connection: about two extra round trips per value, turning a one-round-trip store into roughly three. The store is now a single atomic server-side script on the general-purpose multiplexed pool, which needs no exclusive connection because the backing store already runs such a script atomically. The write it performs is byte-for-byte the same, in the same order, with the same failure behaviour. **What you will see on a dashboard:** `live_edit_valkey_pool_in_use{pool="txn"}` and `live_edit_valkey_txn_dirty_checkouts_total` both drop sharply, in proportion to how blob-heavy your workload is — **that is the change landing, not a regression**; nothing stopped being checked and neither series changed its meaning. Re-baseline any threshold written against the old volume. **One cost is real and stated plainly:** large-value transfers now share connections with ordinary reads and edits, so a burst of them can make those wait. `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_POOL_SIZE` is therefore a sizing knob for uploads as well as reads. The service refuses to start with it below `8`, but **8 is a hard floor and not a value to deploy** — at exactly 8 a single upload of 8 large values can momentarily occupy every connection in the pool. **Production should sit well above it; the shipped Helm chart sets `64`**, and leaving the variable unset resolves to twice the container's CPU count, floored at 8. No configuration change is required to inherit any of this.

  **The reconciler now repairs.** Every upload records, using the backing store's own clock, when a value's bytes last landed — which is exactly what the periodic reconciler added by Issue `b.yda` was waiting for, and it is now on. It reads that timestamp three ways: a value uploaded within its safety window is skipped as too busy to judge; a value with no timestamp at all is eligible, because every upload now records one and its absence is positive evidence that nothing has uploaded that value since this build was deployed; and a timestamp that is present but unreadable makes the value ineligible, because acting on a value the pass could not interpret is exactly the guess this design refuses. **One reading is renamed:** `live_edit_ext_reconcile_total{outcome="skipped_no_stamp"}` is now `{outcome="skipped_unreadable_stamp"}`, and its meaning narrowed with it — it used to mean "cannot date this value at all" and now means only "the timestamp is there and unreadable". **Re-point any dashboard or alert that names the old spelling**; the counter keeps its nine cells and gains no new label. Its expected reading is **zero forever**, and any sustained rate is an integrity signal that something outside the service is writing into the service's keyspace. **The reading to expect instead is `skipped_recent_store`**, and one consequence is worth knowing before it surprises you: a value re-uploaded more often than once per safety window stays in that cell and is never repaired while that traffic continues. That is the pass declining to race a live upload, so `repaired` sitting at zero on a hot fleet while `skipped_recent_store` carries the traffic is correct behaviour, not a stalled reconciler.

  **Upgrade note, and it applies only to a deployment coming from a build older than this one.** Such a build uploads without recording the timestamp, so during a rolling deployment an already-upgraded replica could in principle read an older replica's in-flight upload as excess and hand back one reference. The exposure is bounded to a single reconciler interval — a value is repaired only on a second observation a full safety window later, passes run one interval apart, and an interval shorter than the window is rejected at startup — so the earliest such repair is the second pass after an upgraded replica first takes maintenance leadership: at least 10 minutes, an hour at the defaults. It additionally requires the older replicas to still be accepting uploads a full interval after that point. If your rollout can take that long, set `LIVE_EDIT_EXT_RECONCILE_INTERVAL_SECS=0` for its duration and restore the value afterwards. **No released build can produce this situation**, and there is no configuration flag and no two-step rollout: the recording and the flip ship together.

  **One residual is recorded rather than fixed.** Because the store is now retried transparently by the client library on the shared pool, a connection that drops after the store has already run but before its answer arrives can pay a second reference. The direction is always excess, never a reference given back that someone still needs, and the reconciler drains it — the duplicate carries the same timestamp every other upload does. The previous arrangement was not exactly-once either; it surfaced the same shape as a retryable error, which the client then retried at a higher level for the identical result.

  **No wire or API change, no new environment variable, no changed defaults beyond the `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_POOL_SIZE` floor described above, and no change to how layers, sessions or subscriptions behave.** **Operator action: re-point dashboards that name `skipped_no_stamp`, re-baseline transaction-pool thresholds, and check that `LIVE_EDIT_VALKEY_POOL_SIZE` is comfortably above 8.** See `docs/service/docs/metrics-reference.md` § Are large values handled correctly? and § Backing-store diagnostics, and `docs/service/docs/backing-store.md` § Memory.

### Security
- **Runtime base image bumped to NVIDIA distroless/cc `v4.1.2`, clearing all 16 base-image CVEs reported by the container scan; no behavior change** (Issue `b.kn7`). Every finding in the scan of the previous image was in the Debian packages of the `distroless/cc:v4.0.3` base — 14 in OpenSSL 3.5.5-1~deb13u1 (`libssl3t64` / `openssl-provider-fips`, including two Critical) and 2 in glibc 2.41-12+deb13u2 (`libc6`) — and none in the service or its Rust dependency graph. None was reachable in practice: the service's TLS is rustls (pure Rust), so the OpenSSL library is never loaded, and the two affected glibc functions are not called. The `v4.1.2` base carries OpenSSL 3.5.7-1~deb13u2 and glibc 2.41-12+deb13u3, which clear every finding. The image is otherwise unchanged: same non-root `nvs` user (UID/GID 1000), same system CA bundle, and the same statically linked service binary. **No operator action required.**
- **OODF parse path hardened against multiple DoS shapes, build path no longer aborts the process, and the cxx bridge module is no longer reachable by downstream consumers** (bug `b.vvs`). Ten interlocking hardening items shipped on the `oodf-ffi` crate plus one auth-error scrub. (1) *Tightened `Verifier::Options` (b.vvs item 2)*: `max_depth = 8` (library default 64) and `max_tables = 100_000` (library default 1_000_000). The deepest legal OODF nesting bottoms out at 3 levels of table nesting, so a `max_depth = 8` bound rejects deep-fanned-table verifier-stack-walk DoS shapes with margin; the `max_tables` cap rejects oversized-vector DoS shapes in O(1) instead of letting the verifier walk a million tables before rejecting. (2) *Per-vector reservation cap `kMaxCommands = 1_000_000` (b.vvs item 3)*: applied to attacker-controlled vector lengths (`structCommands`, `setFields`, `timeSamples`, per-`CommandReorderChildren` `childrenList`) post-verify. A verifier-accepted million-element vector inside an otherwise compact buffer would otherwise commit hundreds of MB per parse on the consumer side because `reserve(count)` runs before any per-element bridge marshalling — the previous `reserve(count)` calls are dropped (amortized growth via `push_back` handles legitimate sizes; the cap removes the adversarial worst case). (3) *Bulk-copy u64-vector bridge helper (b.vvs item 4)*: `extend_u64_vec` collapses the per-element `push_back` loop on `CommandReorderChildren.children_list` into a single bridge call (mirrors the b.8uk `extend_byte_vec` fix that already collapsed byte-vector marshalling). A million-element `childrenList` previously cost ~1 M bridge round-trips with ~1 M `reserve_total` calls; now one. (4) *Defensive null-pointer guards on bridge bulk-copy helpers (b.vvs item 1)*: `to_rust_byte_vec` and `to_rust_u64_vec` skip the bridge slice construction entirely when the FlatBuffers vector is absent or empty-with-null-data, even though `rust::Slice<(nullptr, 0)>` is defined behavior in cxx 1.0.194 — defense in depth against a future cxx upgrade tightening that contract. (5) *Direct-write `SetField.value_or_ext_hash` (b.vvs item 5)*: parse-path marshalling writes directly into the bridge `Vec<u8>` instead of through an intermediate `std::vector<uint8_t>` copy. (6) *Build path drops a redundant `delta.clone()` (b.vvs item 6)*: the conversion layer now takes `&OwnedDelta` and copies owned fields directly into bridge values, so a 100 MB external-value payload is no longer double-copied on the build path. (7) *`bridge` and `convert` modules are now `pub(crate)` by default (b.vvs item 7)*: downstream consumers (e.g. `live-edit`) cannot import `oodf_ffi::bridge::ffi` or cxx shared structs directly and must go through `crate::types` + the public wrappers. The opt-in `internal-bridge` cargo feature re-exposes both modules as `#[doc(hidden)] pub` for the crate's own integration tests; the feature is NOT a default feature and is NOT enabled by `live-edit`'s dependency edge, so production code cannot accidentally widen its API surface. Workspace-wide `cargo clippy` / `cargo test` invocations now pass `--features oodf-ffi/internal-bridge`. (8) *`build_delta` no longer calls `std::abort()` (b.vvs item 8)*: the previous abort blew away every concurrent in-flight RPC sharing the process when a bridge caller emitted an out-of-range `CommandTag` or hit `std::bad_alloc` on the `FlatBufferBuilder`. Unknown-tag / OOM / unexpected-exception conditions now surface as typed `OodfError` values — see the Changed (breaking) entry above for the API impact. (9) *Auth REST `reqwest::Error` URL scrub (b.vvs item 9)*: `reqwest::Error::Display` includes the request URL by default; the URL embeds the encoded resource address (full blob URL with container + filename) which is unnecessary for diagnostics and would leak into operator logs and cached error strings. `auth::rest` now applies `err.without_url()` before wrapping into `AuthError::InvalidResponse`, keeping the underlying status / body explanation while stripping the URL. Defense in depth against operator-log exposure of resource paths. (10) *Two new fuzz targets `fuzz_round_trip` and `fuzz_mutate_built` (b.vvs item 10)*: the pre-b.vvs `oodf-ffi/fuzz/` directory hosted a single `fuzz_parse_delta` random-byte target. `fuzz_round_trip` is structure-aware — generates an arbitrary `OwnedDelta` from the libfuzzer byte stream and asserts `parse(build(x)) == x`, catching build → parse asymmetries and schema-drift regressions. `fuzz_mutate_built` shares `fuzz_parse_delta`'s shape but the recommended seed corpus is `tests/fixtures/oodf/*.oodf`, so the libfuzzer mutator produces inputs that share the layout of legitimate OODF traffic and stress the post-verifier accessor path. See `oodf-ffi/fuzz/README.md` for invocation. **Operator action**: none. The verifier and reservation caps are conservative — well-formed OODF buffers in production fit comfortably under all three bounds (max_depth = 8 vs. observed 3, max_tables = 100 K vs. observed << 1 K, kMaxCommands = 1 M vs. observed << 100 K). Existing wire-format clients are unaffected. The `Changed (breaking)` entry above covers the only externally-visible API change (`build_delta` now returns `Result`). No wire-format change.
- **Auth gRPC backend now classifies TLS handshake failures as permanent, not transient — REST/gRPC parity for the b.ohw hardening surface** (bug `b.j5t` item 1). Pre-b.j5t the gRPC `Channel::from_shared(...).connect().await.map_err(|_| AuthError::Unreachable)` arm discarded the source-chain and classified every connect failure transient, so a cert-rotation incident or CA mismatch on the gRPC auth path pounded the backend for the full `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_MAX_ATTEMPTS` budget with failing handshakes — the same failure mode b.ohw H-auth-4 closed on REST. The fix mirrors `auth::rest::is_tls_error` for gRPC: `auth::is_tls_error_in_chain` walks the `connect()` error's source chain (and per-RPC `tonic::Status` source chain on subsequent calls) for a typed `rustls::Error` downcast and short-circuits to `AuthError::InvalidResponse("auth backend TLS handshake failed (cert / trust mismatch — not retried)")` — surfaced as gRPC `INTERNAL` with a single-attempt count. Detection is implicitly pinned to `rustls = "0.23"`; a future tonic/rustls major bump may require updating `auth::is_tls_error_in_chain` and its `io::Error::get_ref()` inner helper. Operators running `LIVE_EDIT_AUTH_PROTOCOL=grpc` now get the same fail-fast TLS posture as REST; no operator action required.
- **REST userinfo response body is now capped at 64 KiB; a malicious or broken backend can no longer OOM the service** (bug `b.j5t` item 2). Pre-b.j5t `RestUserinfoClient::get_current_user` called `reqwest::Response::json::<UserInfoDto>()`, which buffers arbitrary response sizes into memory before deserialization — a userinfo backend that streamed a 100 MB JSON body (well-formed or otherwise) would consume the corresponding heap on every coalesced caller. The new path consults `Content-Length` as a fast-path rejection and then reads the body incrementally via a chunked `bytes_stream` that bails as soon as the accumulated size exceeds `USERINFO_MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES = 64 * 1024` — three orders of magnitude of headroom above the four-string `UserInfoDto` payload (well under 1 KiB in practice) while preventing unbounded allocation. The streaming cap is the correctness primitive; the `Content-Length` check is an optimization (a backend can omit the header on chunked transfer-encoding). Violations surface as `AuthError::InvalidResponse` and reach the gRPC boundary as `INTERNAL`. Permanent classification — not retried. No wire-format change.
- **`BearerToken` field is now private; the redacted `Debug` impl can no longer be bypassed via direct field access** (bug `b.j5t` item 7). Pre-b.j5t `pub struct BearerToken(pub Arc<str>)` exposed the raw `Arc<str>` to any in-crate caller, so `format!("{}", bearer.0)` (`Display` on `Arc<str>`) or `bearer.0.to_string()` could smuggle the plaintext bearer-token bytes past the `Debug` impl's `"***"` redaction into a log line, error string, or trace span. The field is now private; all access channels through `BearerToken::as_str()` (`&str` borrow for backend client trait methods), `BearerToken::arc()` (cheap `Arc` clone for cache-key construction), and `BearerToken::new()` (interceptor-time construction from raw bytes). Defense-in-depth — no current call site was leaking, but the surface is no longer reachable by future contributors who unknowingly touch `bearer.0`. No behavior change for compliant callers; the access pattern is identical in the few in-crate sites that needed updating.
- **`.dockerignore` now excludes `.env`, `.env.*`, `*.pem`, `*.key`, and `secret*` from the Docker build context** (bug `b.bm8`). The Dockerfile's builder stage performs `COPY . .` over the entire repository root, so any file present in the build context lands in an intermediate builder-stage layer. Pre-b.bm8 `.dockerignore` did not exclude `.env`, which is gitignored at the repo level but routinely populated in local development with a real bearer token, Valkey URL, and other secrets for `cargo run` smoke tests. Any `docker build` run from a developer's checkout would bake that `.env` into the builder layer; the layer is discarded for the final runtime image but is fully recoverable from any registry that retains build cache, any local `docker save` of the builder stage, or any CI pipeline that pushes the intermediate. The expanded ignore list now closes the same hole for arbitrary `.env.*` shapes (`.env.local`, `.env.development`, …), PEM-encoded keys and certs (`*.pem`, `*.key`), and the catch-all `secret*` for files that operators may have hand-named. **Operator action**: rotate any bearer token, Valkey credentials, or other secret that was committed to a local `.env` *and* had a `docker build` run against the same working tree at any point. Pushing an image built from such a checkout to a registry should be treated as a credential disclosure for the duration the image was reachable; rotate and audit access logs accordingly. The fix takes effect on the next `docker build`; no code path or runtime behavior changed.
- **Auth-cache HMAC seed is now zeroized on Drop, and the keyed HMAC template is built once instead of per-call** (bug `b.ohw` medium). The 32-byte per-process random seed used by `AuthCache::hash_token` is now wrapped in `zeroize::Zeroizing<[u8; 32]>` so its bytes are scrubbed from memory when the `AuthCache` is dropped — a memory disclosure on a long-running process (core dump, debug container, sidecar tracer) cannot recover the seed from a freed allocation. The hot-path hash also no longer re-runs the HMAC keying schedule per call: a `HmacSha256` template is built once at construction and `clone()`d per `hash_token` invocation, so each cache lookup runs one clone + one update + one finalize instead of a fresh keying step. No wire-format or behavior change; takes effect on redeploy.
- **Auth-backend `status.message()` and HTTP reason phrases no longer reach operator logs through wrapped errors** (bug `b.ohw` H-auth-5; also covered under Changed above for completeness). Mentioned here because the security posture is the meaningful change: a buggy or malicious backend that echoed the bearer token into `status.message()` would otherwise leak the token through the wrapped `AuthError::InvalidResponse` into `ServiceError::Internal` logs. Operator-side log audit may apply to deployments where the auth backend was known to echo request material into responses; remediation is the same as for any token-in-logs incident (rotate, audit access).

- **Auth cache tokens are now HMAC-SHA256-hashed with a per-process random seed** (bug `b.nrt`). Bearer tokens in the auth cache were previously keyed by an unsalted `SHA-256(token)`, which is deterministic across replicas and deployments — a memory disclosure (core dump, sidecar tracer, debug container) plus a candidate token list would let an attacker verify cache hits offline. The cache now hashes tokens with `HMAC-SHA256(seed, token)` where `seed` is a 32-byte random value read once from the OS CSPRNG at `AuthCache` construction and held only in-process (never logged, never exported, never serialized). The same bearer token now hashes to a different digest in every replica and every restart, so an attacker must extract both the seed and the candidate list to brute-force. No wire change; no operator action required.
- **Auth retry now uses full jitter; concurrent auth lookups are coalesced into a single backend RPC** (bug `b.nrt`). Transient-error retries in the auth path previously slept exactly `current_delay` between attempts, so concurrent callers retried in lockstep and amplified load into a just-recovered auth dependency. Sleeps are now sampled from `uniform(0, current_delay)` (full jitter). In addition, a per-process singleflight deduplicates in-flight backend lookups by cache key: a flash crowd of N concurrent lookups for the same `(token_hash, path, permission)` (or `token_hash` for userinfo) now produces one backend RPC plus N cache fills instead of N independent RPCs. No operator action required; both changes take effect on redeploy.
- **OMPF parser hardened against a trivial OOM denial of service** (bug `b.jcd`). An 8-byte OMPF header claiming `num_parts = u32::MAX` previously drove a ~34 GB `Vec::with_capacity` before any validation ran, so any authenticated caller of `CreateLayer` / `UpdateLayer` could OOM-kill a replica with an 8-byte payload. The parser now caps `num_parts` against the actual input buffer size *before* any allocation and uses checked arithmetic on the offset-table layout, so malformed headers surface as a new `NumPartsTooLarge` error rather than an out-of-memory abort. Two additional hardening items shipped in the same change: (1) `offsets[0]` is now rejected when it points inside the header/offset-table region (new `OffsetOverlapsTable` error), closing a crafted-OMPF shape where part bodies could alias header bytes and confuse downstream parsers; (2) three `.try_into().unwrap()` conversions on untrusted input in the header and offset-table readers were replaced with `copy_from_slice` into fixed buffers so a future refactor cannot reintroduce a panicking-on-untrusted-input codepath. No client-visible wire changes — both new error variants map to `INVALID_ARGUMENT` on the gRPC boundary like the other OMPF parse errors.

### Fixed
- **An edit that touches many children of the same prim now writes that prim's child ordering once instead of once per change; where two siblings share a name, the service now picks the same one every time; and replacing a layer's contents no longer leaves that layer's top-level child ordering behind** (Issue `b.ot3`). Three fixes in the code that applies an incoming edit to a layer. **First, child-ordering writes are coalesced.** Every structural change to a prim's children — adding one, removing one, reordering — used to rewrite that prim's entire child ordering, so an edit that added N children under one parent wrote the ordering N times and the service built up all N versions of it in memory before committing. Uploading a layer with 8,000 sibling prims under one parent therefore held 32,004,000 child references (about 256 MB) across 8,000 writes; the same upload now holds 8,000 (about 64 KB) and writes the ordering once. **The ordering itself is unchanged** — same rule (by the client's ordering value ascending, ties in the order the client sent them), same committed result — and the same is true of every other observable part of the edit; only the number of times the ordering is written changed.
